# All findings

164 findings on record after cycle 33, in-scope ones first.

## F-012. Rebasing stETH held by the PoolManager gains and loses balance outside pool accounting

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Ethereum.

What differs on the network: stETH balances are recalculated on each Lido oracle report, normally daily, with no transfer. The change is positive on staking rewards and can be negative after slashing or penalties. Lido's integration guide lists Uniswap among applications that are not designed for rebasing tokens and recommends wstETH.

How it meets the code: Pool accounting never reads token balances. Balances are read only in sync and in _settle, and the only credit from a balance is the increase between a sync and the settle that follows in the same transaction (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288, 349-364). A positive rebase raises balanceOf(PoolManager) with no sync and settle around it, so the increase belongs to no pool, no position and no claim. The only outflows are take and collectProtocolFees (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57), and both debit an accounted amount, so the surplus cannot be paid out to LPs. Someone who can run sync, the rebase and settle inside one transaction would be credited the whole rebase on the PoolManager's balance. For stETH the rebase happens inside submitReportData, which only an oracle committee member or a SUBMIT_DATA_ROLE holder can call. A negative rebase lowers the balance below the sum of pool and claim accounting, and the last withdrawals of stETH revert in Currency.transfer. Transfer rounding is safe for the PoolManager: a take debits the full amount while stETH moves at most that amount, and settle credits what arrived.

What is at risk: The PoolManager holds 80.4539 stETH at block 26470360. LPs in stETH pools earn no staking yield on it; the yield accumulates as an unclaimable surplus. After a negative rebase the shortfall falls on whoever withdraws stETH last. wstETH (2,295 held) is not affected because its balances do not rebase.

What it takes: A pool must use stETH or another elastic supply token as a currency. The loss case needs a negative Lido report. Capturing the positive rebase needs an oracle committee member acting through a contract or an EIP-7702 delegated account. I derived the rounding argument from the share formula in the Lido guide and did not test it on chain. I did not compute how much of the 80.4539 stETH is surplus.

What can be done: Use wstETH in pools. Interfaces can warn on pools whose currency is stETH or another rebasing token. A hook cannot fix this because the surplus is not reachable through any delta.

Second reviewer's corrections: I raise the severity from low to medium. Low is for hazards with no direct loss path. Here the staking yield on stETH held by the PoolManager is stranded for good, the increase is visible on chain, and the loss is limited to stETH pools and claims, which is the medium definition. The effect is closer to a permanent freeze of the yield than to degrade. The principal shortfall case needs a negative report, which the Lido guide says had not happened when it was written. The same sync, trigger, settle sequence applies to any token whose balance at the PoolManager a third party can raise inside one transaction, which is recorded as a lead.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/lidofinance/docs/main/docs/guides/lido-tokens-integration-guide.md (stETH rebases daily on the oracle report, rebases can be negative, transfers can move 1 to 2 wei less than requested, and Uniswap is named as not designed for rebasing tokens)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/lidofinance/docs/main/docs/contracts/accounting-oracle.md (submitReportData reverts with SenderNotAllowed unless the caller holds SUBMIT_DATA_ROLE or is a consensus member, and the rebase is applied within that transaction)
- https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com (eth_call at blocks 26470360 and 26470380: stETH.balanceOf(PoolManager) = 80453883612211151739 and sharesOf = 64372946869317648418; wstETH.balanceOf = 2295.05; AMPL.balanceOf = 16 base units)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ampleforth/ampleforth-contracts/master/contracts/Orchestrator.sol (Ampleforth Orchestrator.rebase() in the project repository requires msg.sender == tx.origin, a check that EIP-7702 delegated accounts can satisfy while running code; I did not confirm that the deployed orchestrator matches this file, and the PoolManager holds only dust AMPL)

## F-033. A take to a recipient whose receive policy rejects the PoolManager succeeds and strands the funds in ReceivePolicyGuard

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Tempo.

What differs on the network: Since T6 any Tempo account can set a receive policy in the TIP-403 registry: a sender list, a token filter and a recovery authority. When a TIP-20 transfer fails the recipient's receive policy, the transfer does not revert. The token credits ReceivePolicyGuard at 0xB10C000000000000000000000000000000000000, records a receipt and returns true. Only the recovery authority captured in the receipt can claim the amount. With recovery authority address(0) the claimer is the originator, which is the from address of the transfer. An ERC-20 transfer on Ethereum either credits the recipient or reverts.

How it meets the code: 1. A user or a router calls take(currency, to, amount) (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297). The caller's delta is debited and the PoolManager calls transfer(to, amount) on the TIP-20 (src/types/Currency.sol:60-75). 2. The recipient to has a receive policy that rejects the PoolManager as sender, or filters out the token. The TIP-20 precompile moves the amount from the PoolManager to ReceivePolicyGuard with the PoolManager as originator and returns true. 3. The return value is 32 bytes of 1, so the success test at src/types/Currency.sol:64-74 passes and take completes. The recipient's balance is unchanged. 4. If the recipient's recovery authority is its own address or a third party, that party can claim. If it is address(0), only the originator can claim. The originator is the PoolManager, which has no function that calls the guard, so the receipt can never be claimed. 5. PoolManager accounting stays consistent, because the delta was debited and the balance fell by the same amount. The loss is the withdrawing party's.

What is at risk: The amount of each affected take is lost for good when the recipient's recovery authority is address(0), and delayed until a claim otherwise. This hits LP withdrawals and swap outputs sent to accounts that restrict senders, for example custodial deposit accounts with a sender whitelist. Pool reserves and other users are not affected.

What it takes: The recipient must have set a receive policy that rejects the PoolManager address or the token, and must have chosen address(0) as recovery authority for the loss to be permanent. The party that names the recipient in take must not check the policy first. An attacker cannot set a policy for someone else. receivePolicy(PoolManager) is unset on chain, and the PoolManager cannot set one, so payments into the PoolManager are not redirected.

What can be done: Routers and interfaces on Tempo should call validateReceivePolicy(token, PoolManager, recipient) before a take and refuse or warn when it would block. Account owners who use sender whitelists should add the PoolManager address or set themselves as recovery authority. The deployer can document that withdrawals originate from the PoolManager address.

Second reviewer's corrections: The mechanism holds as written. Two points on reach. First, both accounts that reject all senders today name themselves as recovery authority, so they could claim a redirected amount. Three accounts have chosen address(0) as recovery authority, and all three currently allow every sender and every token. I found no account that is in the permanent loss configuration today. Second, a router that takes to itself and then forwards becomes the originator, and a router has no claim function either. The Universal Router and PositionManager on Tempo were not read. The severity stays medium. No third party can trigger the loss, pool reserves are not in reach, and the amount is limited to takes sent to accounts that set such a policy.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-75`.

Sources:

- https://tempo.com/developers/docs/protocol/tip403/receive-policies.md (a blocked inbound transfer still succeeds, delivery goes to ReceivePolicyGuard, recovery authority table)
- https://tempo.com/developers/docs/protocol/tip403/spec.md (setReceivePolicy is set by the account itself, address(0) gives recovery control to the original sender, claim(to, receipt) is called by the captured authority)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/tempoxyz/tempo/main/crates/precompiles/src/tip20/mod.rs (transfer returns Ok(true) when validate_transfer reports a blocked inbound, and validate_inbound_or_block moves funds to the guard with originator set to the transfer's msg.sender)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/tempoxyz/tempo/main/crates/precompiles/src/receive_policy_guard/mod.rs (claim reverts with unauthorized_claimer unless msg_sender equals the recovery authority, which is the receipt originator in originator mode)
- https://rpc.mainnet.tempo.xyz (eth_call receivePolicy(PoolManager) on 0x403c000000000000000000000000000000000000 returns all zeros at block 50,260,093)

## F-041. Any account can book a balance increase it triggers at the PoolManager as its own payment, and EIP-7702 removes the origin check that guards Ampleforth's public rebase

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Ethereum.

What differs on the network: Mainnet is the reference chain, so the difference is one that arrived after the Dencun rules the build targets. Since Pectra (EIP-7702) an EOA can run code and still satisfy msg.sender == tx.origin. The Ampleforth Orchestrator on mainnet uses that check as its only guard on the public rebase() function, and its source says the guard exists to protect liquidity providers from flash loan attacks. Mainnet also has reward contracts that pay a holder when any third party asks, such as Compound's claimComp(holder).

How it meets the code: sync has no lock check and records balanceOf(PoolManager) for any currency (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288). _settle credits the named recipient with the whole rise in that balance since the sync (src/PoolManager.sol:349-364). Pool accounting never reads balances, so a rise that comes from a rebase or a reward payment belongs to no pool, position or claim until someone settles it. The steps for AMPL are these. The attacker is an EOA that has delegated to its own contract code and sends a transaction to itself. Its code calls unlock, and the PoolManager calls unlockCallback on the same EOA (src/PoolManager.sol:104-114). Inside the callback the code calls sync(AMPL), then Orchestrator.rebase(), which passes the origin check because the caller is the transaction sender. When the day's supply change is positive, the AMPL balance of every holder grows, including the PoolManager's. The code then calls settle, which credits the attacker with the growth on the PoolManager's entire AMPL balance, and take(AMPL, attacker, growth), which pays it out (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297). All deltas are zero and unlock returns. The attacker paid gas only. The same bracket works for any token where an ordinary account can cause a transfer or a balance increase to the PoolManager: a reward claim on behalf of a holder, an airdrop claim that pays a named account, or a rebase with a public trigger. A negative rebase is never bracketed, so liquidity providers keep the losses and lose the gains. This is the unprivileged form of the stETH finding from cycle 1, where the trigger needs an oracle member.

What is at risk: The gain of each positive rebase or reward payment on the full balance the PoolManager holds in that token, across all pools, claims and accrued protocol fees. The existing mainnet PoolManager holds 16 base units of AMPL, and the public triggers I simulated moved nothing at block 26471590 (0 COMP from claimComp, no sOHM change), so the amount at risk today is close to zero. The new deployment starts empty. The exposure grows with any pool that lists a token of this kind. Without an attacker the same increase stays in the PoolManager as a surplus that core has no function to pay out, so liquidity providers do not receive it in either case; the attacker turns a stranded amount into a taken one.

What it takes: A pool or claim balance in a token whose balanceOf(PoolManager) an ordinary account can raise without paying the same amount: AMPL when a positive rebase is due and the attacker is first in the rebase window, or a token with a public reward or airdrop push to holders. For AMPL the attacker needs an EIP-7702 delegation, which any EOA can set. The rebase window is 1,200 seconds per day (offset 7,200 seconds into each 86,400), and the policy reported inRebaseWindow() = 0 at block 26471631, so my simulated rebase() call reverted. I did not execute the full sequence and did not simulate a delegated EOA; the origin check claim rests on the EIP text and the verified source. I checked 12 rebasing or reward-bearing tokens out of 66,554 token rows the explorer lists for the PoolManager.

What can be done: Liquidity providers and interfaces can avoid or flag pools in elastic supply and reward-pushing tokens and use wrapped, non-rebasing forms. A keeper or a hook can run the same bracket first and pass the proceeds to the pool with donate (src/PoolManager.sol:256-276), which returns the increase to in-range liquidity providers. Token projects cannot rely on msg.sender == tx.origin to keep contracts out. The deployer cannot close this in core without tracking reserves per currency.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor value unprivileged is right: every step is open to any account, and the pooled token and the pool are not made by the attacker. The assumptions should also list A5, because a take of the pooled balance before the rebase followed by a nominal repayment gives the same gain without a sync before the rebase. A change that only restricted sync would leave that route open. The supports text for the EIP source says the broken invariant is that msg.sender == tx.origin implies an account without code. The EIP words it as the check being true only in the topmost execution frame, and it says the use of the check to ensure that msg.sender is an EOA is unaffected. The conclusion for the Orchestrator guard is the same. The preconditions should say that recent rebases changed supply by zero (block 26467574, and my simulation of the next window), so a gain needs a market rate outside the policy's deviation band as well as a pool that holds AMPL. With 16 base units held, the gain rounds to zero today. The Blockscout row count was 66,563 at my fetch. Severity stays medium on the scale: the loss is limited to pools that hold an elastic supply or reward pushing token, and no such pool holds a material balance today.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:104-114`, `src/PoolManager.sol:256-276`.

Sources:

- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/1/0x6fb00a180781e75f87e2b690af0196baa77c7e7c?fields=sources,compilation (verified source of the Orchestrator at 0x6fb00a180781e75f87e2b690af0196baa77c7e7c: rebase() is external, begins with require(msg.sender == tx.origin) and then calls policy.rebase(); the comment says contracts are guarded from calling to avoid flash loan attacks on liquidity providers)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum/EIPs/master/EIPS/eip-7702.md (a delegated EOA may originate transactions and run its delegated code, and the EIP breaks the invariant that msg.sender == tx.origin implies an account without code (lines 299 to 310))
- https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com (eth_call at block 26471525: AMPL.monetaryPolicy() = 0x1b228a749077b8e307c5856ce62ef35d96dca2ea, policy.orchestrator() = 0x6fb00a180781e75f87e2b690af0196baa77c7e7c, AMPL.balanceOf(PoolManager) = 16. eth_call at block 26471631 on the policy: inRebaseWindow() = 0, minRebaseTimeIntervalSec() = 86400, rebaseWindowOffsetSec() = 7200, rebaseWindowLengthSec() = 1200, lastRebaseTimestampSec() = 1795312800; AMPL.totalSupply() = 1,527,834. eth_simulateV1 at blocks 26471565 and 26471590: Comptroller.claimComp(PoolManager) from an unrelated address succeeds and leaves COMP.balanceOf(PoolManager) unchanged; Olympus staking rebase() succeeds and leaves the sOHM balance unchanged; CometRewards.claim for the PoolManager reverts for lack of COMP in the rewards contract; eth_config reports fork id 0x07c9462e, a fork later than Pectra)
- https://eth.blockscout.com/api/v2/addresses/0x000000000004444c5dc75cB358380D2e3dE08A90/token-balances (token rows for the PoolManager address fetched 2026-11-22: 66,554 rows, including the stETH, OUSD, aToken, cToken, sOHM and AMPL balances quoted in the profile)

## F-066. Anyone can run the staking rebase of five Olympus forks on Polygon and BNB Smart Chain, and on Polygon bots already book each sLGNS and sATH rebase on the PoolManager balance as ERC-6909 claims

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Polygon, BNB Smart Chain.

What differs on the network: The cause is a token design, and Polygon and BNB Smart Chain are where pools hold such tokens. Staked Longinus (sLGNS), Staked Aegis (sATH) and Staked Klima (sKLIMA) on Polygon, and Staked NovaBank (sNVB) and sShadowsClaw (sSAC) on BNB Smart Chain, raise every holder's balance when their staking contract runs rebase(). That function has no caller check and takes effect once per epoch. F-041 recorded this class on Ethereum for AMPL, where an origin check stands in the way. These five tokens have no such check, and the Polygon PoolManager shows the path in use since May 2026.

How it meets the code: 1. sync has no lock check and stores balanceOf(PoolManager) for any currency (src/PoolManager.sol:279-289). _settle credits the caller with the whole rise of that balance since the sync (src/PoolManager.sol:349-365). Pool accounting never reads balances, so a rise that comes from a rebase is booked as a payment by whoever brackets it. 2. The Longinus staking contract 0x1964ca90474b11ffd08af387b110ba6c96251bfc exposes rebase() to any caller. When epoch.endBlock <= block.number it calls sLGNS.rebase(epoch.distribute, epoch.number), which raises the total supply and lowers the gons per fragment, so every balance grows inside that call (Staking source lines 672 to 693, sLGNS source lines 1068 to 1090 and 1121 to 1123). 3. Observed on chain: transaction 0xfe645530665d1f424807e4ef99fc8cd3569d3fbaab75af4dca893749098eae11 in Polygon block 97,799,112, the block in which epoch 3018 became due, at transaction index 0. An externally owned account calls a contract of 851 bytes. The trace shows unlock, then inside unlockCallback sync(sLGNS), Staking.rebase(), settle() and mint(). The PoolManager emitted an ERC-6909 Transfer from the zero address with the sLGNS id for 9.015568747 sLGNS. The sender paid 3.39 POL at 5,250 gwei to be first in the block. 4. The same gain needs no settle credit: a caller can take the whole pooled balance (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297), call rebase() while holding it, pay the principal back and keep the growth. 5. Read-only tests through Multicall3 with the call list balanceOf(PoolManager), rebase(), balanceOf(PoolManager): KlimaStaking.rebase() raised the PoolManager's sKLIMA balance by 1,276,529 base units at the latest block, with about 1,232 epochs outstanding. The NovaBank StakingV2.rebase() raised its sNVB balance by 82,488,131 base units on BNB Smart Chain. The same call for sLGNS, sATH and sSAC succeeded and changed nothing, because their epoch was not yet due at the time of the call; the sATH and sSAC staking contracts contain the rebase() selector.

What is at risk: sLGNS: the PoolManager held 36,908.56 sLGNS, about 5,900 USD at 0.16 USD, in 267 pools of which 265 have no hook. An epoch is 14,400 blocks and the next rebase adds 8.78 sLGNS on that balance, four times a day. From block 87,258,312 (22 May 2026) to block 97,799,112, 661 ERC-6909 mints with the sLGNS id went to six recipients for 20,418.69 sLGNS in total, about 3,300 USD at today's price, and every one of them sits in a block where an epoch became due. In the one day window the sLGNS balance rose 36.92 beyond its Transfer logs. sATH: 150 mints for 328.86 sATH to two recipients against a balance of 389.04. sKLIMA: 8 mints for 0.0102 sKLIMA against a balance of 247.67. BNB Smart Chain: the PoolManager holds 38,313.74 sNVB and 2,287.62 sSAC; the endpoint returned no claim mint for either id in the last 1,344,000 blocks, and a payout through take would not appear in that search. Who loses: liquidity providers in these pools would have earned this growth by holding the token outside the pool. Inside the PoolManager the growth belongs to no pool, position or claim, and core has no function that pays it to them. The amount taken was therefore never an accounted balance of any user, and principal is not reduced. The balance does not fall back afterwards.

What it takes: A pool or claim balance in a token whose rebase any account can start. The taker must be the first caller after the epoch becomes due. No role, no hook and no token of the taker's making is needed. I did not run the sequence. The call order comes from debug_traceTransaction of one of the 661 transactions; the other 660 were checked by their mint logs and block numbers only. The sLGNS price is the figure the DexScreener API returned for the LGNS and sLGNS pairs. The sATH, sNVB and sSAC staking sources are not on Sourcify and I read their selectors from bytecode.

What can be done: Core cannot tell a rebase from a payment and keeps no total of what is owed per currency, so it cannot compute or redirect the surplus. Liquidity providers should pool a wrapped form with a fixed balance where the project offers one. Interfaces and routers should warn on or hide pools whose currency changes balance without transfers; reconcile.py in the work directory finds them from chain data. A deployer who wants the growth to reach liquidity providers needs a wrapper or a vault held by a hook around the token, outside core.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor value unprivileged is right: the sender needs no role, no hook and no token of its own, and the pools involved have no hook. Three details need correction. First, the preconditions say the sNVB staking source is not on Sourcify, while the sources list cites the verified StakingV2 record at 0x6bcd1273eba684ff62614e41eff87123113112d1; I fetched it, Sourcify reports a match, and rebase() is public at line 716. Only the sATH staking contract 0x98d21b8e6bf0c1d2dfb0e3fcb82035095dcdd7de and the sSAC staking contract 0x99954eb0b159514d608f64c89a2c5bbe9f42120b were read from bytecode. Second, the sATH epoch is counted in seconds: epoch() returned length 28,800 and an end value of 1795417200, which is a timestamp. Third, balanceOf(PoolManager) for sLGNS was 34,658.405133592 at block 97,804,424, below the 36,908.56 in the finding, so the next rebase adds about 8.24 sLGNS on today's balance. The finding states the scope limit correctly: the cause is the token design and would be the same on any network, the amount taken is unaccounted surplus, and no accounted balance of a liquidity provider or claim holder falls. This is a second and third token for the class in F-041 with live use, and it is unrelated to any difference between Polygon or BNB Smart Chain and Ethereum.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-289`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-365`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:322-329`, `src/PoolManager.sol:104-114`.

## F-067. Interest Protocol lets any caller accrue interest, which raised the PoolManager's USDI balance by 399,373 in one transaction and can raise it again today

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Ethereum.

What differs on the network: This is a second token for the class in F-041 and it needs no EIP-7702 delegation. USDI at 0x2a54ba2964c8cd459dc568853f79813a60761b58 is a rebasing token. Its VaultController at 0x4aae9823fb4c70490f1d802fc697f3fff8d5cbe3 has calculateInterest(), which any contract can call and which distributes accrued interest to all holders by raising their balances.

How it meets the code: 1. sync, settle and take work as in F-041 (src/PoolManager.sol:279-297, 349-365). 2. Transaction 0xad36440ffe69062fe8b57599f3d5e77ae69f38b81bf19667c2e39957464ebc4d in block 26,469,396 was sent through Multicall3 by an ordinary account. It made the VaultController donate 874,691 USDI of interest, which moved the supply from about 1.53 million to 2.40 million. The sender took 158,347 USDI out of a v2 style pair in the same transaction and sold 118,000 USDI through v4 pools. 3. In that block the PoolManager's USDI balance rose from 697,167.03 to 1,214,540.56. Of the rise 118,000 arrived by Transfer and 399,373.53 came from the rebase. The sender did not bracket the rebase with sync and settle, so the 399,373.53 USDI stayed in the PoolManager and belongs to no pool. 4. Read-only test through Multicall3 with balanceOf(PoolManager), calculateInterest(), balanceOf(PoolManager): at block 26,469,395 the balance rises by 399,373.08, and at the latest block it rises by 4,636.71. A caller who runs sync before and settle after that call, or who takes the pooled USDI, makes the call and pays the principal back, is credited that amount.

What is at risk: The PoolManager holds 1,214,540.56 USDI, which is 47.5 percent of the supply, in six pools without a hook. Any account can book 4,636.71 USDI today and more as interest accrues. The market value is small: the 118,000 USDI sold through v4 pools in the observed transaction returned 9.04 USDC. Liquidity providers do not receive this growth in either case, because core has no function that pays out an unaccounted balance. The amount was never an accounted balance of any user. The balance does not fall back afterwards.

What it takes: A pool or claim balance in USDI and interest that has accrued since the last call. The VaultController implementation at 0xffc8c49c5b38bc9bafd0bb0f5f189eecd35e35e2 is not on Sourcify, so the statement that anyone may call calculateInterest() rests on the two simulations from Multicall3 and on the observed transaction, which also came from Multicall3. I did not run the bracket.

What can be done: Same as for the Polygon finding. Core cannot separate this growth from a payment. Liquidity providers should not pool USDI, and interfaces should warn on currencies whose balance changes without transfers.

This entry adds to F-041.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor value unprivileged is right. The amount an account can book today is 366.76 USDI, where the finding says 4,636.71, because a third party accrued the interest at block 26,475,328 after the researcher's read. The market value is close to nothing: the observed sale of 118,000 USDI returned 9.04 USDC, so 366.76 USDI is worth about 0.03 USD and the roughly 404,000 USDI of unaccounted surplus now in the PoolManager is worth about 31 USD. The six USDI pools carry fees of 18 to 90 percent according to the researcher's Initialize rows, which I did not recheck. The severity medium follows F-041 as a class rating; by value this instance is negligible. No accounted balance of a liquidity provider or claim holder is reduced.

## F-072. ViciCoin on Polygon counts every PoolManager transfer against a daily net flow limit of 6 VCNT, and the PoolManager is not exempt

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Polygon.

What differs on the network: No chain rule is involved. ViciCoin (VCNT, 0x8a16d4bf8a0a716017e8d2262c4ac32927797a2f) is a pooled token on Polygon whose owner installed the plugin TransferLimitHybridPluginV04 at 0xf58fed40fba1ab7aa7ffc160b863bedd998386a7. The plugin runs before every transfer and keeps one signed running total per address and per day. A transfer out lowers the total of the sender and a transfer in raises the total of the recipient. A transfer reverts with TransferTooLarge() when the sender total would fall below minus the limit or the recipient total would rise above the limit. On chain the limit is 6 VCNT (about 109 USD at 18.12 USD per VCNT), the period is 86,400 seconds counted from the Unix epoch, 57 addresses are exempt, and the PoolManager 0x67366782805870060151383f4bbff9dab53e5cd6 is not one of them. The source comment of the plugin says that DeFi contracts must be added as exemptions and that otherwise the limit restricts total volume.

How it meets the code: 1. take(VCNT, to, amount) makes the PoolManager call VCNT.transfer(to, amount) (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:60-75). The plugin subtracts the amount from dailyTotals[PoolManager][day] and reverts when the result is below minus 6 VCNT. The revert reaches the caller as ERC20TransferFailed (src/types/Currency.sol:82-86). 2. A payment of VCNT into the PoolManager before settle adds to the same total and reverts when the result is above plus 6 VCNT, so settle cannot be reached with that payment (src/PoolManager.sol:349-364). 3. A take that is repaid in the same unlock moves the total down and back up by the same amount. I simulated it: after the round trip transferLimitAmountUsed(PoolManager) and transferLimitAmountRemaining(PoolManager) were unchanged. The free round trip therefore does not use up the limit. 4. A net outflow does. At block 97,801,741 the PoolManager had a total of plus 0.1256 VCNT and transferLimitAmountRemaining returned 6.1256 VCNT. In simulation, after transfers of 6 VCNT and 0.1256 VCNT to two recipients the view returned 0, and a further transfer of 0.01 VCNT and of 1 base unit reverted with 0x1b97a875 (TransferTooLarge). A payment of 1 VCNT into the PoolManager reopened exactly 1 VCNT. 5. An account that holds about 6.13 VCNT of ERC-6909 claims or of pool liquidity can burn or remove it at the start of a UTC day (src/PoolManager.sol:331-336 and 291-297). From then on every take of VCNT by any other user reverts until someone pays VCNT in or the day ends. The account can pay the same VCNT back in at the end of the day, when the total returns to zero, and repeat the next day. A buy of the same size through a swap has the same effect and costs the swap fee. 6. The recipient is limited as well, so one take of more than 6 VCNT to a recipient that is not exempt reverts even when the PoolManager has room. A take of zero VCNT reverts with the string invalid transfer.

What is at risk: Withdrawals of VCNT from every v4 pool on Polygon share one allowance of 6 VCNT of net outflow per UTC day. The PoolManager held 11.35 VCNT (about 206 USD) at block 97,801,699, so the liquidity providers of these pools cannot withdraw all of it in one day without matching inflows, and one account can keep the allowance at zero for the cost of gas and about 111 USD of VCNT that it keeps. No funds are taken. The amount at risk today is small. The hazard grows with the VCNT that pools hold, because the cap does not grow with it.

What it takes: The PoolManager stays outside the exemption list of the plugin. The blocking account needs about 6.13 VCNT of net outflow per day, which means it holds VCNT claims, VCNT liquidity or the other currency to buy with. It needs no role. A round trip without capital does not trip the limit.

What can be done: The token owner can add the PoolManager with transferLimitAddExemption, as the plugin source asks for DeFi contracts. Until then liquidity providers should treat VCNT in v4 pools on Polygon as withdrawable at 6 VCNT per day at most, and interfaces can read transferLimitAmountRemaining(PoolManager) before building a take. Pool creators should check any token for a transfer limit plugin and for its exemption list before pairing it.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor value unprivileged stands. Any account can bring the allowance to zero, and the victims use a hookless WETH and VCNT pool that the attacker did not create. The cause is an issuer setting, and the issuer can end it with one exemption. Three refinements. First, at the start of a UTC day the running total is zero, so the amount needed is exactly 6 VCNT of net outflow. The figure 6.13 applies on a day that already had 0.1256 VCNT of inflow. Each recipient that is not exempt can receive at most 6 VCNT on that day, so an outflow above 6 VCNT needs two recipients or an exempt one. Second, the cost is not fixed at the value of 6 VCNT held overnight. An account can also buy the VCNT in the pool and sell it on a venue whose contracts are exempt, which costs fees and price impact. I did not measure the depth of either venue. Third, the limit binds without any hostile account. Net withdrawals of all v4 pools together cannot exceed 6 VCNT per UTC day plus the inflows of that day, and the same holds in the other direction for payments in. Blocked users can leave with ERC-6909 claims. This hazard is a token setting on Polygon. It does not come from a difference between Polygon and Ethereum, and it gives no path to take funds. The amount exposed today is 11.35 VCNT. The severity is medium on the scale because liquidity operations are blocked without loss.

## F-078. QANX and seventeen tokens of one generator family count locked units in balanceOf and let any holder assign them, so the PoolManager's transferable balance can be swapped for units it can never send

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Ethereum, BNB Smart Chain, Polygon.

What differs on the network: The cause is a token design and the three networks are where pools hold such tokens today. Two contracts named QANX (0xaaa7a10a8ee237ea61e8ac46c50a8db8bcc1baaa on Ethereum, 0xaaa9214f675316182eaa21c85f0ca99160cc3aaa on Ethereum and BNB Smart Chain) return _balances plus the locked amount from balanceOf, and their transferLocked function lets any holder send an amount to any recipient under a lock whose end date the sender chooses. Seventeen tokens built with one token generator (contract name MainToken with FreezableToken: WOZX, IZI, RKME, EXO, EAT, MNTO, ZITI, LED, GHD, YF4 and DXF on Ethereum, ELV on BNB Smart Chain, CARR, WELT, BLITZ, ALGB and HITT on Polygon) return the plain balance plus freezingBalance from balanceOf, and their freezeTo function lets any holder move its own tokens into the frozen balance of any address. In both designs transfer spends the unlocked part only. The trial sweep of this unit found no such token on the other eighteen networks.

How it meets the code: 1. sync stores balanceOf(PoolManager) for the currency without any lock check (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288). _settle credits the caller with balanceOf now minus the stored figure (src/PoolManager.sol:358-364). take pays out with a plain transfer call (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:60-75). The credit is therefore counted in units of balanceOf and the payout is made in transferable units. 2. Inside one unlock (src/PoolManager.sol:104-114) a caller takes an amount X of the token, which the PoolManager pays from its transferable balance, then calls sync, then calls transferLocked(PoolManager, X, far date, far date, 0) on QANX or freezeTo(PoolManager, X, far date) on a generator token, then calls settle. balanceOf(PoolManager) is back at its old value, so settle credits X and the debt from the take is cleared. All deltas are zero and unlock returns. The caller needs no tokens of its own and pays gas only. X can be the whole transferable balance. 3. After that the PoolManager reports the same balance and can send X less. Every take of that currency beyond the remaining transferable balance reverts with the wrapped ERC20TransferFailed error, for every pool, every ERC-6909 claim and the protocol fees of that currency, because all of them share one holder address. 4. How long it lasts. In the generator tokens only the holder can release frozen units (releaseOnce and releaseAll act on msg.sender, MainToken.sol lines 479 to 516 in the WOZX source), and the PoolManager has no function that calls them, so the units stay frozen whatever date was chosen. In the old QANX contract unlock(account) can be called by anyone after hardLockUntil, a uint32 the sender chooses, so the lock can run to the year 2106. In the newer QANX contract the dates are uint64. 5. Read-only test. I placed a measuring stub at the PoolManager address by state override and ran one eth_call per token: the PoolManager address sends an amount to an ordinary account A, A calls the function with the PoolManager as recipient, and the stub then reads balances and tries two transfers. Old QANX on Ethereum at block 26,476,590 with the whole balance of 21,210,277.639 QANX: balanceOf(PoolManager) is 21,210,277.639 after the call, A holds 0, a transfer of the full balance is refused and the reported balance stays in place. With one third of the balance the transfer of balance minus amount is accepted and the transfer of the full balance is refused. The newer QANX gave the same result with 3,512.31 QANX on Ethereum and 10,297.83 QANX on BNB Smart Chain (block 135,237,759). freezeTo gave the same result on DXF, WOZX and ZITI on Ethereum, on ELV on BNB Smart Chain and on all five Polygon tokens. The other eight Ethereum generator tokens share the selector set of WOZX and were not run one by one. I did not send the sequence through the PoolManager itself.

What is at risk: Liquidity providers and claim holders of every pool in the affected token lose access to the amount that was swapped for locked units, up to the whole pooled balance, and nobody gains it. Amounts today: 21,210,277.64 old QANX at the Ethereum PoolManager in pools from 6 Initialize logs. coins.llama.fi values that at about 204,500 USD because it gives both QANX contracts the price 0.00964 USD, and this figure should not be relied on: QANplatform replaced the old contract after the bridge wallet attack of 11 October 2022, distributed the new token by a snapshot of that date and states that only the new token is supported, and DexScreener lists no pair for the old contract. The old balance is therefore worth little or nothing. Newer QANX: 3,512.31 on Ethereum (about 34 USD) and 10,297.83 on BNB Smart Chain (about 99 USD), in pools from 21 Initialize logs on Ethereum. Generator tokens: 2,158,038.8 EXO (about 182 USD at the DexScreener price), 2,297,431 CARR (about 70 USD at the coins.llama.fi price), 3,062.3 YF4, which is 10.2 percent of that token's supply, and fourteen others without a price. The freezingBalanceOf(PoolManager) and lockedBalanceOf(PoolManager) readings are zero for all of them today, so nobody has done this yet. The new deployment starts empty and the same exposure appears as soon as a pool lists a token of this kind.

What it takes: A pool or claim balance in a token whose balanceOf includes units that transfer cannot move and whose lock function is open to any holder. No role, hook or attacker made token is needed. For the old QANX contract the recipient must have no lock yet, because the contract allows one lock per address. Link I could not confirm: the behaviour of the eight Ethereum generator tokens that were covered by their family representative only. The search used function names from a public signature database, which named 85,092 of 137,912 selectors, and a fixed set of argument patterns, so a lock function with an unlisted name or unusual arguments would have been missed.

What can be done: Core cannot tell locked units from transferable ones, because it reads balanceOf only (src/types/Currency.sol:90-96). Interfaces and routers should refuse or flag currencies that expose transferLocked, freezeTo, freezingBalanceOf, lockedBalanceOf or actualBalanceOf. Liquidity providers should not pool these tokens. For the old QANX contract anyone can protect the PoolManager by sending it a lock of one base unit with a far end date, since a second lock on the same address is refused while the first exists. The newer QANX contract adds to an existing lock and has no such protection. A hook cannot prevent the sequence, because take, sync and settle do not call hooks.

Rated high by the verifier. The review rates it medium, in line with F-041 and F-066: the loss is limited to pools of the named tokens, and the amounts at risk today are a few hundred USD.

Second reviewer's corrections: The statement that nobody gains is incomplete for both QANX contracts. transferLocked lets a sender whose unlocked balance is too small pay from its own locked balance when the new lock is the same or stricter and allowedHops is lower (old QANX lines 471 to 495, newer QANX _transferLocked). A holder of locked QANX with allowedHops of at least 1 can therefore send locked units to the PoolManager between sync and settle and take liquid units for them, which is a gain for that holder at the expense of the pools. I read this in the source and did not search for holders with such locks. The WOZX balance at the PoolManager is 4,364 base units, which is dust. The cause is a token design and has no link to a network difference, so this entry does not answer the question about a network specific weakness. Severity high is kept because an ordinary account can freeze accounted funds for good in pools that exist; the amounts at risk today are a few hundred USD.

Sources:

Same result for freezeTo on ELV 0x20a2ee8d26aa6a7ce4890c2a44b788c4283356eb.)

## F-079. Two pooled tokens on Ethereum let any holder grant revocable vesting tokens to the PoolManager, book the grant as a payment and then revoke it, which removes pooled tokens without a debit

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Ethereum.

What differs on the network: The cause is a token design. The old AdEx token ADX (0x4470bb87d77b963a013db939be332f927f2b992e, contract ADXToken, 4 decimals) and CDT (0x177d39ac676ed1c67a2b268ad7f1e58826e5b0af, contract CDTToken) inherit an early library contract named VestedToken. grantVestedTokens is public: it records a grant for the recipient and transfers the amount to it. The grant can be made revocable, and revokeTokenGrant lets the granter take the unvested part back out of the recipient's balance. This is a balance that an ordinary account can raise for the PoolManager and later pull back. District0x DNT and ESCB carry the same function names and reject the call from an ordinary account, ESCB through a whitelist in its source.

How it meets the code: 1. sync, settle and take work as described in the QANX finding (src/PoolManager.sol:279-297, 349-365). 2. Inside one unlock a caller takes X of the token from the PoolManager, calls sync, then calls grantVestedTokens(PoolManager, X, start, cliff, vesting, revokable = true, burnsOnRevoke = false) with dates in the future. The token transfers X to the PoolManager (ADXToken.sol lines 275 to 307), so balanceOf(PoolManager) is back at its old value and settle credits X, which clears the debt of the take. 3. Still inside the callback, or in any later transaction, the caller calls revokeTokenGrant(PoolManager, grantId). The token adds the unvested amount, which is all of X before the cliff, to the caller and subtracts it from the PoolManager (lines 314 to 338). Core records nothing, because no PoolManager function runs. 4. The caller now holds X and owes nothing. The PoolManager balance is lower by X while pools, claims and protocol fees still record the old amounts. X can be the whole pooled balance. 5. Read-only test at Ethereum blocks 26,476,597 to 26,476,600 with a measuring stub at the PoolManager address. ADX with the whole balance of 165,963 base units: after the grant alone balanceOf(PoolManager) is 165,963, the full transfer is refused and the account holds 0; after grant and revoke in one call balanceOf(PoolManager) is 0 and the account holds 165,963. CDT with half of 310.02 CDT: after the grant alone the balance is back at 310.02 and the full transfer is refused; after grant and revoke the PoolManager holds 155.01 and the account 155.01. tokenGrantsCount(PoolManager) is 0 on both today. I did not send the sequence through the PoolManager itself.

What is at risk: The whole pooled balance of the token, taken from liquidity providers, claim holders and accrued protocol fees of every pool in it. Today that is 16.5963 old ADX and 310.02 CDT, both without a market price that I could find, so the loss available now is negligible. The size follows the token: any pool that lists a token with open revocable grants can be emptied of that token by anyone, and the new deployment has the same exposure once such a pool exists. If the caller does not revoke, the result is the freeze of the QANX finding, because granted tokens are not transferable before they vest.

What it takes: A pool or claim balance in a token with a public grant function whose grants the granter can revoke. The recipient may hold at most 20 grants in this library, which does not hinder a single grant. No role, hook or attacker made token is needed. I searched by function name and by trial, so a clawback function under another name (revoke, cancelVesting, reclaim) on a token that the trial arguments did not fit would have been missed.

What can be done: None in core, which cannot see that a balance increase carries a revocation right. Interfaces should refuse or flag currencies that expose grantVestedTokens, revokeTokenGrant or transferableTokens. Liquidity providers should not pool such tokens.

Rated high by the verifier. The review rates it medium, in line with F-041 and F-066: the loss is limited to pools of two tokens, and 16.6 ADX and 310 CDT are at risk today.

Second reviewer's corrections: No error found in the mechanism. The amounts at risk today are 16.5963 old ADX and 310.02 CDT with no price at coins.llama.fi, so the loss available now is negligible. The cause is a token design and has no link to a network difference. Severity high is kept on the ground that an ordinary account can remove accounted funds of pools that exist; the rating describes the class and not the value at stake today.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-365`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-75`.

Sources:

- src/PoolManager.sol:349-365 (_settle credits the rise of balanceOf(PoolManager) since sync to the caller, with no view of how the rise came about.)
- src/PoolManager.sol:291-297 (take debits the caller and transfers before anything is paid in.)

## F-083. The deployed PositionManager refuses to mint claims, so a position with one currency that cannot leave the PoolManager closes only by forfeiting that side or by placing it in a new position, and the published SDK builds neither call

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: all 21 networks.

What differs on the network: Several of the 21 networks hold pooled tokens that refuse a transfer from the PoolManager address under a rule that Ethereum pools rarely meet. ViciCoin on Polygon counts every PoolManager transfer against a daily net outflow limit that any account can use up (F-072). G$ on Celo blocks the PoolManager as sender (F-056). Ten wrapped stock tokens on Monad can be paused by a two signature Safe (F-061). TIP-20 tokens on Tempo carry transfer policies set by their issuers. The PoolManager core can still pay such a currency out as an ERC-6909 claim, but the PositionManager that holds the positions of ordinary users does not expose that route on any of the 21 networks.

How it meets the code: 1. A position made through the PositionManager belongs to the PositionManager inside the PoolManager, so its owner can act only through PositionManager.modifyLiquidities and the action list of _handleAction. 2. A removal creates a positive delta in both currencies for the PositionManager. Every delta must be zero when unlock returns (src/PoolManager.sol:104-114). 3. TAKE, TAKE_PAIR and CLOSE_CURRENCY end in PoolManager.take, which calls transfer on the token (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:60-86). When the token refuses, the whole call reverts and the other currency stays in the pool as well. 4. Core offers mint(to, id, amount) to turn a positive delta into a claim without touching the token (src/PoolManager.sol:322-329). The PositionManager does not handle action 0x17 (MINT_6909) or 0x18 (BURN_6909). A read-only modifyLiquidities call with action 0x17 reverts with UnsupportedAction(0x17), data 0x5cda29d7 followed by 0x17, on all 21 networks. SWEEP does not help because the tokens never reach the PositionManager. 5. Two sequences succeed in simulation from the position owner. Sequence A is DECREASE_LIQUIDITY or BURN_POSITION, TAKE of the other currency, then CLEAR_OR_TAKE of the blocked currency with amountMax set to the maximum uint256. That calls PoolManager.clear (src/PoolManager.sol:310-319), which erases the delta. The tokens stay in the PoolManager balance with no delta and no claim behind them, so the provider loses that side for good. Sequence B is DECREASE_LIQUIDITY, TAKE of the other currency, MINT_POSITION_FROM_DELTAS (action 0x05) into a range on the side of the current price that holds only the blocked currency, then CLEAR_OR_TAKE for the rounding remainder. The provider leaves with the other currency and a new position that holds the blocked currency. No sequence leaves the provider with an ERC-6909 claim. 6. The v4 SDK that Uniswap publishes for building these calls ends every removal and every fee collection with TAKE_PAIR, and its Actions enum has the lines for MINT_POSITION_FROM_DELTAS and CLEAR_OR_TAKE commented out, so a front end built on it cannot produce sequence A or B. Simulations: Celo position 178 (G$ and cUSD) at block 83,530,712 and Polygon position 60652 (WETH and VCNT) at block 97,817,338 with the tokens as they are today, Monad position 651334 at block 124,698,657 and Arc position 333954 at block 32,722,324 with one currency replaced by reverting code through an eth_call state override. In all four the six sequences that pay or close the blocked side revert, MINT_6909 reverts with UnsupportedAction, and sequences A and B succeed.

What is at risk: For a user who relies on a front end, a block on one token of a pool also locks the other currency of every position in that pool, although core could release it. On Polygon an ordinary account can cause this at will: F-072 shows that one account can keep the shared ViciCoin allowance of 6 VCNT per day at zero, and 8 of the 15 ViciCoin pools have an open position held through the PositionManager. On Celo 5 of 8 G$ pools have such a position and the standard removal reverts today. On Monad a pause of the wrapped stock tokens would put the USDC side of about 7.9 million dollars of pools (figure from F-061) in the same state. A provider who learns of sequence A loses the blocked side. A provider who uses sequence B keeps it as a range position that converts to the other currency if the price crosses the range. No funds move to the attacker.

What it takes: A pooled token refuses transfers from the PoolManager. On Polygon any account can bring this about for VCNT by using up the daily allowance. On the other networks it needs the token issuer or its transfer policy. The provider holds the position through the PositionManager and uses a front end that builds removals with TAKE_PAIR. Not confirmed: which calls the hosted Uniswap web application sends for a removal. I read the published SDK, and the application may build its transactions through a server interface whose output I did not capture.

What can be done: Add MINT_6909 and BURN_6909 handling with the caller as recipient and payer to the PositionManager that ships with this deployment, or publish a documented exit that uses MINT_POSITION_FROM_DELTAS. Front ends can fall back to sequence B when a simulation of TAKE_PAIR fails, and should warn before sequence A. Providers in pools with a token that has issuer controls or a transfer quota can hold positions through their own contract that calls PoolManager.mint for a side that cannot be paid.

This entry adds to F-072.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor value unprivileged is right for Polygon (chain 137) alone, where F-072 lets any account use up the ViciCoin allowance. On the other 20 networks in chainIds a token must first refuse transfers from the PoolManager, and that needs the token issuer (G$ on Celo, the wrapped stock tokens on Monad, TIP-20 policies on Tempo). The chainIds list should be read as where the PositionManager lacks the action, and the unprivileged trigger exists on one network. The Polygon evidence needs one change. Position 60652 fails to close in full today because its VCNT side exceeds the 6 VCNT daily allowance, with 6.1256 VCNT of allowance still open. Removals of 50, 10 and 2 percent succeed with TAKE_PAIR, which the SDK can build through liquidityPercentage. The block for every size appears only after the allowance is used up, which I reproduced with a state override. The freeze applies to providers who rely on interface-built calldata. An owner who sends modifyLiquidities by hand can leave with the other currency through sequence B at any time, or by INCREASE_LIQUIDITY_FROM_DELTAS into another position of the same pool. No funds reach the attacker. The amounts under the unprivileged trigger are small: the PoolManager on Polygon held 11.35 VCNT, about 206 USD. The finding is about periphery code and a third party token rule. It does not follow from a difference between a network and Ethereum, and the same PositionManager behaviour exists on Ethereum. It overlaps F-072, F-056 and F-061 and adds to them the fact that the paired currency is locked for interface users. SDK line numbers: removeCallParameters ends at line 399 and collectCallParameters spans lines 407 to 430 in the file I fetched. Unconfirmed link: what the hosted Uniswap interface sends for a removal.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:104-114`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:310-319`, `src/PoolManager.sol:322-329`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-86`.

Sources:

- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/1/0xbD216513d74C8cf14cf4747E6AaA6420FF64ee9e?fields=sources,compilation (Verified source of the Ethereum PositionManager (match). src/PositionManager.sol:197-286 lists the handled actions: 0x00 to 0x05, SETTLE_PAIR, TAKE_PAIR, SETTLE, TAKE, CLOSE_CURRENCY, CLEAR_OR_TAKE, SWEEP, WRAP, UNWRAP. Lines 474-484 show _clearOrTake calling poolManager.clear when the credit is at most amountMax. The Actions library defines MINT_6909 as 0x17 and BURN_6909 as 0x18 and the handler has no branch for them.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Uniswap/sdks/main/sdks/v4-sdk/src/PositionManager.ts (removeCallParameters (lines 334-391) encodes BURN_POSITION or DECREASE_LIQUIDITY and then addTakePair. collectCallParameters (lines 413-423) encodes a decrease by zero and addTakePair.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/Uniswap/sdks/main/sdks/v4-sdk/src/utils/v4Planner.ts (The Actions enum (lines 30-66) has MINT_POSITION_FROM_DELTAS = 0x05 and CLEAR_OR_TAKE = 0x13 as comments only and has no entry for 0x17 or 0x18.)
- https://forno.celo.org eth_call (From owner 0xf18f712b760732cde4d1835673b98b2c7978b3a8 to PositionManager 0xf7965f3981e4d5bc383bfbcb61501763e9068ca9 at block 83,530,712, position 178: decrease then TAKE_PAIR reverts with 0x90bfb865 wrapping the G$ transfer, MINT_6909 reverts with 0x5cda29d7 and 0x17, sequences A and B return without error. Output in research/network-risk/cycles/005/theme-claim-operators.work/raw/exit_42220_178.json.)
- https://polygon-bor-rpc.publicnode.com eth_call (From owner 0xb2d3f2324061c3a0acf0453f01a1045ebad1647b to PositionManager 0x1ec2ebf4f37e7363fdfe3551602425af0b3ceef9 at block 97,817,338, position 60652: same result with the ViciCoin transfer as the failing call. Output in research/network-risk/cycles/005/theme-claim-operators.work/raw/exit_137_60652.json. Position 14963 in another ViciCoin pool exits with the normal sequence at block 97,817,667, which shows that the block depends on the amount and the allowance left that day (raw/exit_137_14963.json).)
- https://rpc.monad.xyz eth_call with state override (Position 651334 at block 124,698,657 with the code of currency1 0xe7cd86e13ac4309349f30b3435a9d337750fc82d replaced by 0x5f5ffd: same pattern of reverts and the same two working sequences. Output in research/network-risk/cycles/005/theme-claim-operators.work/raw/exitovr_143_651334.json. Arc position 333954 at block 32,722,324 gave the same result (raw/exitovr_5042_333954.json).)

The PositionManager has 23,877 bytes on 19 networks and 19,965 bytes on Elysium and HyperEVM.)

## F-084. The Klik fee hooks keep one pending fee amount per token and burn it in the currency of whichever pool of that token swaps next, and they accept a pool of the token against any currency from any account

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Ethereum, Base, Unichain, Ink, Arbitrum One, Robinhood Chain, Avalanche, Monad, Tempo.

What differs on the network: None. The behaviour does not depend on the network. It comes from one family of third party fee hooks (VERSION strings klik-multichain-1 and klik-anypair-1) that launch pads deployed on nine of the 21 networks. The PoolManager code behaves as written.

How it meets the code: 1. The hook charges a fee on swaps of a launched token and holds it as ERC-6909 claims at the PoolManager. It records the amount in pendingClaims(token), one number per token. In Tempo transaction 0x36bf56c18219aeb53b5f8a04f6f2fa4c062ccf81c67c1966c202635fc7073fbc the hook mints 0x2d5067 claims of pathUSD to itself inside beforeSwap. 2. At the next swap the hook pays the pending amount out. In Tempo transaction 0xa23224d5f477539161486a82ab2430469d6c08ec74e56ae67800d949595f913d beforeSwap calls PoolManager.burn(hook, pathUSD, 0x2d5067), then take(pathUSD, hook, 0x2d5067), then transfers 60 percent to the platform treasury and calls transfer for the rest to the factory. The trace marks that last inner call as out of gas and the swap continues. 3. The currency of that burn is read from the PoolKey of the swap. I called beforeSwap from the PoolManager address with a PoolKey that pairs a launched token with the codeless address 0x1111111111111111111111111111111111111112 and traced it with debug_traceCall. On six deployments the hook calls PoolManager.burn(hook, 0x1111111111111111111111111111111111111112, amount), and the amount equals pendingClaims(token), which was accrued in another currency: 23876031656686502 and 2079850173284467176091326 on the two Ethereum hooks, 143394835225437444 on Unichain, 333714925697516922 on Robinhood Chain, 54489710721685566614 on Monad and 230765 on Tempo. The afterSwap fee is minted in the same way: on Tempo the trace shows mint(hook, 0x1111111111111111111111111111111111111112, 10000000000000000). 4. The hook does not restrict who creates pools. PoolManager.initialize accepts any PoolKey that names the hook (src/PoolManager.sol:117-142) and calls beforeInitialize (src/libraries/Hooks.sol:178-182). An eth_call of initialize from an ordinary account with a launched token and the foreign currency succeeds on all eleven hook addresses listed under sources. The hooks have no liquidity flags, so anyone can add liquidity to such a pool. 5. First consequence, a swap freeze that exists today. When a token has pools against two currencies, fees accrued in the first are looked for in the second. If the hook holds fewer claims of the second currency than the pending amount, burn reverts on the checked subtraction (src/ERC6909.sol:85-87, src/PoolManager.sol:332-336) and beforeSwap reverts, so every swap of that pool reverts. The deployed Quoter on Ethereum returns the PoolManager error wrapped around Panic(0x11) from beforeSwap for seven pools: 0x227260acd3c0fa351a1360f11e8408f5fca8f099ce3b34e0dd8d886f61fdc228, 0x02ae580f824b1e9407c9063e08e7d2e29262a73917db4da9ac9acc23155403e8, 0x2256de11213c69f6a404cd2b973d79782e5a888114ef86b932768c99deb288e6, 0x9415295f90547e1b1ec7bed8e5396158003ca5889ad0fc9687a1014171d1fb29 and 0x1dc6439eb8e3e653d053533fd0a14c5a67b9aae60369885807cc0a8f5e2971a8 on hook 0x1d06faa7ff674ccfd8a5736c0c0973cb0a8fa0cc, and 0x1d3f06543054f9035578cf564802fd565fd424c1812dba91d841f61d0098246b and 0x8699a47da36461ce75bff3cdf0e6370bfb054203d1ebc7aae1a858f784b264fe on hook 0x6d01769ab90674d0d56b155f57003284e1b460cc. For the last one the token 0xe07befffcc37771dd3a5250fdd3b9a0798e415cc has 39205855192377918360 pending, the hook holds 39205855192377928360 claims of currency 0x29ef66e020e391aad7a8b1b5897af599251893b5 from the token's other pool, and only 2551319276869624230 claims of WLFI, the currency of the pool that fails. The same Quoter call succeeds for 63 other pools of the two hooks, and five pools without liquidity fail with the Quoter's own NotEnoughLiquidity or price limit errors. 6. Second consequence, which I derived from the traces and did not run. An account that creates a pool of token T against a currency it issues can grow pendingClaims(T) by any amount at no cost, because the fee is a share of its own swap in its own currency. A later swap in a pool of T against a valuable currency then burns that amount from claims the hook holds for the pending fees of other tokens and pays it to the platform treasury and to the fee receiver of T through the factory. The other tokens keep their pendingClaims entries, the hook no longer holds the claims, and their pools fail as in step 5. In the opposite direction, a swap in a pool of T against a worthless currency after the hook was given that many worthless claims (PoolManager.mint accepts any recipient, src/PoolManager.sol:322-329) clears pendingClaims(T) and pays T's receivers in the worthless currency. 7. The books already disagree on two hooks. On Ethereum hook 0x1d06faa7ff674ccfd8a5736c0c0973cb0a8fa0cc the pending amounts of tokens whose pool uses SPCXon (0xc9eef266834730340a55b6cc24621b31baf55581) sum to 79607629840677756 and the hook holds 7798353546842757 claims of it. On Unichain the pending amounts in UNI sum to 272597197961328745 against 262597197961328745 claims. I did not trace which transactions caused the difference.

What is at risk: Fees that the hooks hold as ERC-6909 claims for token creators and platforms, and the ability to swap in the affected pools. The claims are small today: about 16 USD and 17 USD at DexScreener prices on the two Ethereum hooks (44 and 23 tokens with pending fees), 0.26 UNI on Unichain, claims in 14 tokenised stock currencies on Robinhood Chain worth about 12 USD for the six that have a price, 54.49 MON on Monad and 0.230765 pathUSD on Tempo, where the factory holds a further 2.946234 pathUSD of creator fees. Seven Ethereum pools cannot be swapped today. Three of the five on the first hook have liquidity (153845604096252485200300 units each), the other two have none, and the liquidity of the two on the second hook was not read. Liquidity providers are not locked in, because the hooks have no liquidity callbacks and removal does not call them. Holders of the launched tokens lose the route through the frozen pool. The hooks serve 83 and 59 pools on Ethereum, 46 on Base, 30 on Unichain, 14 on Ink, 7 on Arbitrum One, 34 on Robinhood Chain, 11 on Avalanche, 15 on Monad and 2 on Tempo.

What it takes: None for the freeze: it follows from a second pool of a token against another currency, which any account can create with initialize, and it has already happened on Ethereum. To gain from the payout the account must be the fee receiver of a token launched through the same factory. On Tempo launchEnabled() is true, but I did not confirm that launch has no caller restriction, and the path from creditCreatorFee to collectFees in the factory was read from selectors only. The burn was traced on Ethereum, Unichain, Robinhood Chain, Monad and Tempo. On Base, Ink, Arbitrum One and Avalanche no token of the sampled pools had pending fees, so only the open initialize was confirmed there. The Base hook 0x5b0a864213d49f23ed2a6635cc42b3b4fe7720cc has the code length of the traced Ethereum hook (8082 bytes, 232 bytes differ), and the Ink, Arbitrum One and Avalanche hooks have the code length of the traced Unichain and Monad hooks (9123 bytes). The newer Ethereum hook 0xcba49142a75da64a679f32a159f4316f11a7e0cc (klik-anypair-direct-dividends-2, 254 pools) reverts a foreign initialize with "only factory initializes" and returned a zero delta for the foreign PoolKey.

What can be done: The PoolManager deployer cannot change this, and the PoolManager code is not at fault. The hook author can record pending fees per pool id or per token and currency, burn only in the recorded currency, and reject initialize from any caller other than the factory, as the newer hook does. A frozen pool swaps again once someone mints the missing claims to the hook. Interfaces should not route through a second pool of a Klik token. Liquidity providers can withdraw at any time.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor value unprivileged is right: every step (public factory launch, initialize, mint of claims to the hook, swap) was done by an ordinary account in the transactions cited, and the affected pools use a hook and tokens the attacker did not write. Severity medium is right on the SCHEMA scale: the loss is confined to the fee claims and the swaps of one hook family, the claims are worth tens of USD, and liquidity removal does not call the hook. Corrections. (1) Step 6 is no longer a derivation. It ran on Ethereum in transaction 0xd903d0ad at block 26330140 and moved 85325615158209763 SPCXon claims (about 11 USD) that backed the pending fees of other tokens. Evidence for that step is primary. (2) Step 7 can name the cause: that transaction explains the whole Ethereum shortfall, and transaction 0x2b6aab13 explains the 0.01 UNI on Unichain. (3) The three frozen Ethereum pools that hold liquidity (0x227260ac, 0x02ae580f, 0x9415295f on the first hook) are frozen as a result of that transaction, because they share the SPCXon claim balance it drained. (4) The two frozen pools on the second Ethereum hook have zero liquidity (StateView getLiquidity returns 0 for 0x1d3f0654 and 0x8699a47d). They were initialized inside contract creation transactions sent by 0x02dcd01d534477b5e76628a7284e7fa71c7a7ed7 and 0x3a15cf422307e922645d671cb247e76e8b1ec50d, so no user funds sit in them. (5) The preconditions understate the freeze. An account does not need liquidity or a token of its own launch to freeze the pool of another creator's token T: it initializes a pool of T against a currency it issues, mints that currency's claims to the hook for the current pendingClaims(T), and swaps there with a large specified amount. pendingClaims(T) then exceeds the hook's claims in the real quote currency and the real pool reverts with Panic(0x11) until someone mints the missing claims to the hook or resets the entry through another pool. I did not simulate this sequence end to end. Each step is shown separately by the transactions above. (6) The doubt about launch on Tempo can be narrowed: on Ethereum the launch function is called by many unrelated senders. The Tempo factory was not tested. (7) The Base hook 0x64746dca (b20-stock-1) should stay marked as open initialize only.

## F-085. Any holder of 1,000,000 DEAD can take the PoolManager's whole DEAD balance on Base after 90 days without a payout, and one account did so on 17 November 2026

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Base.

What differs on the network: The cause is a token rule, and Base is where the token and its pools are. DeadToken at 0xf0197f10ea542a67914ecc0ec5304dc9df1faf6f lets any caller who holds 1,000,000 DEAD call claimIdleTokens(target) and move the whole balance of an address that has sent no DEAD for 90 days: half to the caller, 40 percent burned and 10 percent to three platform addresses. The token keeps a whitelist of pool addresses that are exempt, and the Uniswap v4 PoolManager is not on it. Earlier cycles recorded balances that rise through a public trigger (F-041, F-066, F-067). This is a public trigger that lowers a balance that pools have recorded.

How it meets the code: 1. Core keeps no per currency total. A pool's reserves are numbers in pool state, and the only place the token balance is read is sync and settle (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288, 349-365). When the balance falls without a take, every later take of that currency draws on less than pools record, and the transfer in take reverts once the balance is too low (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:60-86). 2. DeadToken updates lastTransferTime only for the sender of a transfer, and for a receiver whose balance was zero (verified source Dead.sol lines 563 to 578). The PoolManager is a sender only when a take pays DEAD out. A quiet period of 90 days without a DEAD payout makes the whole pooled balance claimable (lines 388 to 420: balance check of the caller at 390, whitelist check at 394, idle check at 398, 50 percent to the caller at 405 and 413, 40 percent burned at 406 and 414). 3. Observed: transaction 0xc32384b25be1e5d29cd5e978fc5f531359ddd8dcae67a1ff89204e98c27d0d2b in Base block 58,594,779 (17 November 2026, 09:55:31 UTC) is a contract creation by 0x5f21bfbe94a4774fc6fb88ac84d47538422f7976. The new contract 0x25bead0f62a7c234e89691e4519ca5e74079cf3f receives 1,000,000 DEAD from the pair 0x098a1a39a006ea83c86fd8da991c0e7134eaf68c, emits three TokensClaimed logs (targets 0x50ddc37e9060fda26cc5fe2f521fe9d01bc29810 for 18,583.25 DEAD, the PoolManager for 14,899.78 DEAD and 0xaa2e244d93fd4d0d8a6c6d525de06468d77be7b3 for 13,444.75 DEAD) and returns 1,003,009.03 DEAD to the pair. The PoolManager emitted no log in that transaction. Its DEAD balance was 14,899.783090229956530365 at block 58,594,778 and 0 at block 58,594,779. lastTransferTime(PoolManager) one block earlier was 1784165391, which is 10,743,940 seconds (124 days) before the block. 4. The PoolManager took no part in the call. No sync, settle, take or unlock is needed, so no check in core can see it. 5. The balance does not come back. Three later swaps paid 88.41, 699.16 and 872.46 DEAD into the PoolManager, which holds 1,660.03 DEAD today. Those tokens are recorded by pools as well, so the gap of 14,899.78 DEAD stays.

What is at risk: 14,899.78 DEAD left the PoolManager: 7,449.89 to the claimer, 5,959.91 burned and 1,489.98 to the three platform addresses. I found no price source for DEAD and did not value it. The liquidity of the pools on the routes of the DEAD swaps before and after the claim is unchanged across the claim block (for example pool id 0x18fca1729428c26f554c4f431a551e05404b0d6b8ae9ae518966e5a9e9371121 holds liquidity 319,322,205,084,229 at block 58,594,778 and today), so pools still record the DEAD that was taken. Liquidity providers and buyers in the DEAD pools lose: a swap or a removal that must pay out more DEAD than the PoolManager holds reverts. The loss is limited to currency DEAD, because deltas, takes and settles are kept per currency (src/PoolManager.sol:368-378). Pools in other tokens are not touched. The token saw 136 TokensClaimed logs since block 56,000,000, so claiming idle holders is routine for this token.

What it takes: A DEAD balance of 1,000,000 for the length of one call, which the observed account borrowed from a pair inside its creation transaction. No DEAD payout from the PoolManager for 90 days. The PoolManager stays off the token's pool whitelist (whiteListedPool(PoolManager) returns 0 and getPoolWhitelistStatus(PoolManager) returns false, false and 0). After the claim the timer restarted when the PoolManager received DEAD into a zero balance at timestamp 1795149430 (20 November 2026), and getTimeUntilClaimable(PoolManager) returned 7,502,995 seconds at my read, so the balance becomes claimable again on about 18 February 2027 unless a take pays DEAD out before then. I did not read the PoolKey of the DEAD pools (the Base log endpoint serves 1,000 blocks per request), so the list of pools comes from the Swap logs of the transactions that moved DEAD, and I did not send any transaction.

What can be done: The token admin can put the PoolManager on the pool whitelist with setWhiteListPool, which takes effect after a 60 minute timelock (Dead.sol lines 315 to 324 and 355 to 382). Any account can restart the timer by causing a DEAD payout from the PoolManager, for example a swap that buys a small amount of DEAD. Liquidity providers should not pool a token that lets third parties move idle balances. Interfaces can find such tokens with the search of this unit: an ERC-20 Transfer log with the PoolManager as sender in a transaction where the PoolManager emitted no log. Core cannot prevent it, because the token moves the balance without calling the PoolManager.

Second reviewer's corrections: Actor unprivileged is right: the claimer needs 1,000,000 DEAD for one call, borrowed it from a pair inside one transaction, and is neither the token owner 0x485f77d6362f5922fcb63a40aaa4fd79d26ec905 nor the admin 0x6f8b4f05434df0f1af51f3e27a34b36dc8fbbac3. Four points need fixing. First, the contract created by the transaction is 0x120df14514851c4fbec63d374703b0b7ef7fd71e according to the receipt; the claimer 0x25bead0f62a7c234e89691e4519ca5e74079cf3f is a second contract deployed inside the same transaction. Second, the nine pool ids in the raw file and the pool named in the impact text are the pools on the swap routes, and five of them do not hold DEAD according to PositionManager.poolKeys (0x9f6013f1 pairs USDC with 0xc1a36ca099c37ded68f0d6be608fb00767238aa4, 0x90333bb0 and 0xec746b81 pair WETH with USDC, 0xbd976d4c and 0x24662c76 pair USDC with two other tokens); a sixth, 0xcfd23695, has no PositionManager record and I could not read its currencies. The DEAD pools are the three listed in the fact check, and 0x18fca172 is one of them, so the conclusion holds. Third, the finding gives no value. At the pool price the amount taken is about 4 USD, and the shortfall against what the three pools would pay out is about 10,900 DEAD today, under 3 USD. Fourth, the 136 TokensClaimed logs come from two transactions by two claimers (3 logs in block 58,594,779 and 133 logs in block 58,617,833), so the word routine overstates it. The title says the claimer takes the whole balance; the claimer receives half and the whole balance leaves the PoolManager. The cause is a rule of one token and has nothing to do with how Base differs from Ethereum, as the finding says. Medium stands because the loss is confined to the pools of one token and the path is closed until about 18 February 2027 unless no DEAD payout happens before then.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-365`, `src/PoolManager.sol:368-378`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-86`.

Sources:

- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/8453/0xf0197f10ea542a67914ecc0ec5304dc9df1faf6f?fields=sources,compilation,abi (Exact match record for DeadToken (solc 0.8.30). Dead.sol: IDLE_PERIOD 7,776,000 seconds (line 58), MIN_CLAIMER_BALANCE 1e6 * 10**18 (line 61), claimIdleTokens(address) at lines 388 to 420, _update at lines 563 to 578 sets lastTransferTime for the sender and for a receiver with a zero balance.)
- https://base.gateway.tenderly.co (eth_getTransactionReceipt and eth_getTransactionByHash for 0xc32384b25be1e5d29cd5e978fc5f531359ddd8dcae67a1ff89204e98c27d0d2b: creation transaction, block 58,594,779, five DEAD Transfer logs with the PoolManager as sender (7,449.89 to the new contract, 5,959.91 to the zero address, 744.99, 446.99 and 298.00 to three addresses), TokensClaimed topic 0xa86305abc2db271df4609aa86a8d044bc11fb36939841bfdad6c1ab2b26e9471 with target PoolManager and amount 14,899.783090229956530365, no log of the PoolManager. eth_call at blocks 58,594,778 and 58,594,779: balanceOf(PoolManager) 14,899.78 and 0, lastTransferTime(PoolManager) 1784165391, block timestamp 1794909331. eth_call at the latest block: whiteListedPool(PoolManager) 0, isClaimable(PoolManager) 0, getTimeUntilClaimable(PoolManager) 7,502,995, balanceOf(PoolManager) 1,660.03, StateView.getLiquidity for nine pool ids at block 58,594,778 and latest.)
- https://base-rpc.publicnode.com (eth_getLogs on the DEAD token from block 56,000,000: 136 TokensClaimed logs, three of them in the transaction above; nine Transfer logs that name the PoolManager since block 57,000,000 (one payment in before the claim, five out in the claim, three payments in after it).)

- research/network-risk/cycles/005/theme-unbacked-claims.work/raw/base_DEAD_pools.json:1 (DEAD transfers that touch the PoolManager, the pool ids in those transactions and their liquidity before the claim and today.)
