## F-090. Any account can call claimFor on Alpaca ibBUSD for the PoolManager on BNB Smart Chain, which burns the whole pooled ibBUSD balance and stakes the replacement where the PoolManager cannot reach it

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: BNB Smart Chain.

What differs on the network: ibBUSD 0x7c9e73d4c71dae564d41f78d56439bb4ba87592f is the share token of the Alpaca Finance BUSD vault on BNB Smart Chain (proxy, implementation 0xf19c0106fad0892b6d9f5db0ae1c9a2cfd14635b). Alpaca converted the BUSD of that vault to USDT under its proposal AIP-29 and told holders in February 2024 to claim the converted assets. Its function with selector 0xddeae033, claimFor(address), converts the ibBUSD of any holder, and the caller does not have to be that holder. The token is not attacker made and the PoolManager holds it for 16 hookless pools.

How it meets the code: 1. The PoolManager keeps no total per currency. It reads the token balance only in sync and settle and pays with transfer in take (src/PoolManager.sol:279-297, 349-365, src/types/Currency.sol:60-80). A balance that a third party removes stays recorded in pool state. 2. An eth_call of claimFor(PoolManager) from the unrelated address 0x00000000000000000000000000000000000a11ce returns success on three endpoints at blocks 135,293,814 to 135,293,817. The same call for an address without shares reverts with the text no shares. 3. The call trace shows what the function does for the PoolManager: ibBUSD logs Transfer(PoolManager, zero address, 56658476151267410600), which is the whole balance. It then approves 55.48 units of the token 0x90476bfef61f190b54a439e2e98f8e43fb9b4a45 (symbol ibUSDT) to 0xd20b887654db8dc476007bdca83d22fa51e93407, and that contract deposits them into 0x4579587ae043131999ce3d9c66199726972e3fb7 with the PoolManager as the account and pool number 5 (selector 0x0efe6a8b). 4. After the call the PoolManager holds no ibBUSD and no ibUSDT. The ibUSDT sits in a staking contract under the PoolManager address. The PoolManager can only call transfer and balanceOf on a currency, the hook selectors on a hook and unlockCallback on its caller, so it has no way to withdraw that position. 5. I did not read the source of the staking contract and did not test whether a third party can withdraw the position for the PoolManager.

What is at risk: 56.658476151267410600 ibBUSD, about 56 USD at the coins.llama.fi price, held for 16 pools that were created between blocks 48,048,145 and 133,218,725. Nine of them pair ibBUSD with USDT, and their LP fees lie between 88 and 99.9996 percent. Once any account sends the call, every take of ibBUSD reverts in the transfer and the liquidity providers of those pools cannot withdraw that side. The amount is small. The class is the same as the DEAD case on Base (F-085): an ordinary account empties the PoolManager balance of a token that the victims did not get from the attacker.

What it takes: None beyond gas. The call has not been sent yet: the PoolManager balance was 188.70 ibBUSD at block 60,000,000, 18.87 at block 120,000,000 and is 56.66 today.

What can be done: Liquidity providers should withdraw ibBUSD from v4 pools now. Interfaces should stop listing ibBUSD pools. The assigned balance sweep tests one token per selector family, so vault tokens with a conversion or migration function that names the holder as an argument should be listed per token on every network.

This entry adds to F-085.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor and the effect are right. Two details to add: only five of the 16 pools show liquidity in range today, and the pool fees of 88 to 99.9996 percent suggest bait pools more than ordinary liquidity, so the 56.70 USD at stake may belong to very few parties. The claim that the staked ibUSDT is out of reach is now supported by the verified MiniFL source and by the selector list of the unverified account manager.

Sources:

The same two results came from https://bsc.rpc.sentio.xyz and https://bsc-dataseed1.bnbchain.org.)
- https://bsc-mainnet.nodereal.io/v1/64a9df0874fb4a93b9d0a3849de012d3 (debug_traceCall of the same call with callTracer and logs: burn of the whole PoolManager balance, approval of ibUSDT and deposit for the PoolManager into 0x4579587ae043131999ce3d9c66199726972e3fb7. A second trace is saved in research/network-risk/cycles/006/theme-currency-lists.work/raw/ibbusd_claimfor_trace.json.)

- src/types/Currency.sol:60-80 (take pays with transfer and reverts when the token refuses, so a missing balance blocks every payout of that currency.)

## F-091. The sSAC rebase on the PoolManager balance has been paid out to ordinary accounts on BNB Smart Chain in 119 transactions since block 115,986,214, and on Polygon the sLGNS and sATH rebases were booked 193 times in 30 days

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: BNB Smart Chain, Polygon.

What differs on the network: The staked tokens of these Olympus forks raise every balance at a rebase that any account can start. Ethereum pools of the main assets hold no token whose balance an ordinary caller can raise at will.

How it meets the code: 1. settle credits the caller with balanceOf(PoolManager) now minus the balance stored by sync (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288, 349-365). 2. I read every log with topic 0x917acfbe39be6509ccf7fecb66a7e42ce2be1083c2d7dd3b9b7491dabddb8da4 that sSAC 0x0ae7cd10f94fd16a6f9a29a372708718144c46f8 emitted from block 104,200,000 (the PoolManager balance is zero before block 104,200,560) to block 135,290,559: 191 rebase transactions, epochs 45 to 367. 3. Each of them was classified with one rule from its receipt and its call trace. 119 are payouts: the trace shows sync(sSAC), the rebase call 0xaf14052c on the staking contract 0x99954eb0b159514d608f64c89a2c5bbe9f42120b, settle returning more than was transferred in, and take or mint of that amount. The other 72 rebase transactions hold no call from the PoolManager to sSAC. 4. The payouts sum to 210.068614 sSAC (9 decimals). 119 were taken out as tokens and 0 were minted as claims. The senders: 0x2d68a551e7c8ecf7312ee083a16e736dce2347d5 through 0x0b675fb382e0c986d724fd6cbb51837b3ae30e8a in 117; 0x00000000004ef5098c6a84d085a2e9d69e332b2f through 0x0000000000be226afde21672a5e4adc45692e69d in 1; 0x000000000062b66705d912df5940ffacbbec6a82 through 0x0000000000be226afde21672a5e4adc45692e69d in 1. 5. The list with transaction hash, block, epoch, amount, sender and called contract is in the work directory. 6. Polygon, 30 day census: 109 sLGNS bookings that sum to 1,349.946362 sLGNS and 84 sATH bookings that sum to 78.935549 sATH, each a claim mint after sync, rebase and settle with no transfer in.

What is at risk: The amounts are the rebase on the tokens that pools hold. Pool accounting never records them, so no pool becomes short. The liquidity providers lose the rebase that their staked tokens earned: about 0.11 percent of the pooled sSAC per epoch on BNB Smart Chain, where the PoolManager holds 2,287 sSAC. The loss repeats every epoch for as long as the tokens sit in the PoolManager.

What it takes: A rebase that is due and that any account can start. The taker needs no funds.

What can be done: Holders of rebasing staked tokens should pool the wrapped, non rebasing form. Interfaces should mark pools whose currency balance grows without transfers.

This entry adds to F-066.

Second reviewer's corrections: The BNB Smart Chain count is 188 payout transactions and 271.921622232 sSAC, where the finding has 119 and 210.068614. The first payout is in block 115,890,216, which is 95,998 blocks before the one named. 135 of 323 rebase transactions hold no PoolManager transfer, where the finding has 72 of 191. The log scan through bsc.api.pocket.network silently lost 132 of 323 logs, so every other whole history scan through that endpoint needs a second source. The 69 missed payouts are saved in theme-currency-lists.work/raw/ssac_rebase_missed_verifier.json. The value taken is rebase surplus that no pool records, so the effect is lost yield for liquidity providers, as the finding says.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-365`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:322-329`.

Sources:

- https://bsc.api.pocket.network (eth_getLogs for the sSAC rebase topic in windows of 5,000 blocks from block 104,200,000, then eth_getTransactionReceipt and debug_traceTransaction with callTracer for each of the 191 transactions. Old balances were read through https://bsc.rpc.sentio.xyz.)
- research/network-risk/cycles/006/theme-currency-lists.work/raw/ssac_payout_list.json:1 (One entry per payout: transaction, block, epoch, credited amount, sender, called contract and the calls made between sync and settle.)
- https://polygon.gateway.tenderly.co (Census of blocks 96,099,090 to 97,827,090 on Polygon and debug_traceTransaction of every flagged row: the sLGNS and sATH rows show sync, the rebase call 0xaf14052c, settle and a claim mint.)
- src/PoolManager.sol:349-365 (The credit is the balance rise since sync, whoever caused it.)

## F-092. WHITE on Ethereum and ARGUS on Arc charge the sender on top of a transfer to a listed pair, so a take that names such a recipient costs the PoolManager more than the caller is debited

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Ethereum, Arc, BNB Smart Chain.

What differs on the network: Both tokens keep a list of trading pairs. A transfer to a listed address moves the amount to the recipient and a further share from the sender to a tax address: 10 percent for WHITE 0x9cdf242ef7975d8c68d5c1f5b6905801699b1940 (to 0x287ecebd1223e235b929217fe596c905a11752ae) and 1 percent for ARGUS 0xece5ca8bf9220718e5727754026757512212cb3c (to the token contract). The main assets of Ethereum pools take nothing from the sender beyond the amount.

How it meets the code: 1. take debits the caller by amount and then calls transfer(to, amount) on the token (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:60-80). Core assumes that the call lowers its balance by amount. 2. The census flagged these rows because the logged outflow exceeds the Swap amounts of the transaction. Ethereum transaction 0x36869e0c74164dfaf399507b77a3d4398d90bb3b0f53d3bb52f47085f381c3fc: the trace shows one transfer call from the PoolManager for 27,819.93 WHITE to 0x6ec94f50cadcc79984463688de42a0ca696ec2db, and the receipt shows a second Transfer log from the PoolManager for 2,781.99 WHITE to the tax address. Arc transaction 0xaf96d29fbbc703117f44d34945c9e8b04fc768ed235c2cd93d66bd0c4289ff4c: 176.74 ARGUS to the pair 0x6a3bacaa6493734c1ac221ebf42cf530a96c1e02 and 1.767 ARGUS to the token contract. 3. Simulation with a measuring stub at the PoolManager address (state override, eth_call): a transfer of one third of the balance to the listed pair lowers the PoolManager balance by 110.0000 percent of the amount for WHITE and 101.0000 percent for ARGUS. The same transfer to an address without code lowers it by the amount only. A transfer from the WHITE pair to the PoolManager arrives in full, and one from the ARGUS pair arrives at 99 percent. 4. Whole history of Transfer logs from the PoolManager to the tax address: 766 transfers that sum to 6,129,406.68 WHITE between blocks 23,150,160 and 26,459,964, and 1,295 transfers that sum to 188,007.27 ARGUS between blocks 21,078,826 and 32,673,463. 5. The recipient of take is chosen by the caller, so any account can make the PoolManager pay the charge on any amount it takes to a listed pair. The caller owes only the amount. I did not test whether the caller can get the amount back out of the pair, so the cost of doing this on purpose is unknown. 6. BNB Smart Chain shows the same pattern once in the 3 day census: in transaction 0x82b6ad131efc04d9ee34211e458f107a1a94f9d093fd679e1554edcf7ebfaf06 the pool owed 212,712.18 units of the token with symbol T 0x4ef42e4cf51cd779e9947db65b629ecd8c92b1da and the PoolManager paid a further 6,381.37 units, 3 percent, to 0xfe34d3505cc8a835f7efe54bf8ff4d680d2bd6b5. I did not simulate that token.

What is at risk: The pools of these two tokens together record 6,129,406.68 WHITE and 188,007.27 ARGUS more than the PoolManager holds, assuming no other flow outside pool accounting. The PoolManager holds 48,265,470.02 WHITE and 46,697,370.97 ARGUS. DexScreener prices WHITE at 0.0000303 USD, so the WHITE shortfall is about 186 USD. I found no price for ARGUS. The last liquidity providers to withdraw bear the shortfall, and it grows with every routed sale into a listed pair. The same test on the new lists found 13 currencies on BNB Smart Chain, 12 on Base and 2 on X Layer where the PoolManager loses more than the amount it sends. The priced ones are receipt tokens of lending markets that lose between 1 and 537 base units by rounding.

What it takes: A pool of such a token and a route or a caller that names a listed pair as the recipient of take. The list is set by the token owner.

What can be done: Routers should take such tokens to their own address first. Interfaces should flag tokens whose transfer lowers the sender balance by more than the amount. Liquidity providers in WHITE and ARGUS pools should expect a shortfall.

Second reviewer's corrections: Severity rises from low to medium. For WHITE the cost of doing this on purpose is gas only: one unlock with take to the v2 pair, sync, skim to the PoolManager and settle moves 10 percent of the taken amount from the pooled balance to the token owner, and repeating it empties the PoolManager balance of WHITE. The caller gains nothing unless it is the token owner, so for an ordinary account the effect is a loss and freeze for the liquidity providers of the hookless WHITE pools, and for the owner 0x287ecebd1223e235b929217fe596c905a11752ae it is a siphon. The actor unprivileged is right. For ARGUS and for T the round trip is not free on the pairs I tried, so they stay at the level the finding describes. I did not rerun the count of 13, 12 and 2 currencies on the other lists.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-80`.

Sources:

- https://mainnet.gateway.tenderly.co (Census of Ethereum blocks 26,377,966 to 26,478,434 (8 WHITE rows), receipt and debug_traceTransaction of 0x36869e0c74164dfaf399507b77a3d4398d90bb3b0f53d3bb52f47085f381c3fc, eth_getLogs for WHITE Transfer logs from the PoolManager to 0x287ecebd1223e235b929217fe596c905a11752ae from block 21,688,329, and the two stub simulations.)
- https://arc.gateway.tenderly.co (Census of Arc blocks 30,362,860 to 32,750,542 (24 ARGUS rows) and eth_getLogs for ARGUS Transfer logs from the PoolManager to the token contract from block 1,948,056.)

- https://api.dexscreener.com/latest/dex/tokens/0x9cdf242ef7975d8c68d5c1f5b6905801699b1940 (WHITE price 0.00003031 USD and 1,879 USD of liquidity in one v4 pair.)

## F-096. The Klik factories pay creator fees in a token's listed pair currency whatever currency the hook delivered, and two Ethereum transactions used this to take the fees of other creators

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Ethereum, Base, Unichain, Ink, Arbitrum One, Robinhood Chain, Avalanche, Monad, Tempo.

What differs on the network: None. The behaviour does not depend on the network. It comes from the Klik launch pad contracts (KlikLauncher, AnyLauncher, KlikHook, AnyHook) that third parties deployed on nine of the 21 networks. The PoolManager behaves as written.

How it meets the code: This answers lead L-378 and adds a direct gain to the finding. 1. Verified source of KlikLauncher 0xb6da931df2ed2c80db47923d4eb46fd913ee01c3 on Ethereum: launch (lines 310 to 328) has no caller check. _launch (lines 362 to 404) requires launchEnabled and an enabled pair, deploys a new KlikToken, and records creatorOf[token] = msg.sender and pairOf[token] = pair. An eth_call of launch from an ordinary account returns a token address on Ethereum and on the unverified Tempo factory 0x20839c453a0ed8bcab1095ea068669469098297c. 2. creditCreatorFee(token, amount) (lines 559 to 564) is callable by the hook and only adds amount to creatorFees[token]. It takes no currency argument. collectFees(token) (lines 566 to 574) lets the creator withdraw creatorFees[token] and pays it with _safeTransfer(pairOf[token], creator, amount), that is in the pair recorded at launch. AnyLauncher 0x95e0da371711502eed78bcc3f17856b4415120ca has the same two functions (lines 596 to 609). Its launch has no caller list either, and it needs a price quote signed by the platform priceSigner (_verifyQuote, lines 296 to 301). 3. Verified source of KlikHook 0x1d06faa7ff674ccfd8a5736c0c0973cb0a8fa0cc: _tokenOf (lines 373 to 378) takes the token from currency1 of the PoolKey when the factory knows it, and takes the pair from currency0 of the PoolKey, without comparing it with pairOf(token). _beforeSwap (lines 446 to 485) mints the buy fee as claims of currency0 and adds it to pendingClaims[token], pendingPlatform[token] and pendingCreator[token]. At the next swap _flushClaims (lines 245 to 266) burns pendingClaims[token] in currency0 of that PoolKey, takes the currency, sends the platform share to the treasury and the creator share to the factory in that currency, and calls creditCreatorFee(token, share). 4. PoolManager.initialize has no access control (src/PoolManager.sol:117-142) and these hooks accept any caller, so an account that launched token T can open a pool that pairs T with a currency X it issues. A swap there accrues a fee in X at no cost. The next swap makes the hook send X to the factory and credit creatorFees[T] by the same number. collectFees(T) then pays that number of units of the real pair from the factory balance, which is the uncollected fee money of every creator who launched against that pair. 5. This has run on Ethereum. Transaction 0xe91af1e1dfb2e1bc9d8026fa275275b16f40c94512207eb3b6936b52b12a9a11 (block 26078723, sender 0x690193287a9f3e0095adae116f4260818332ce0a through contract 0xdb9b1e94b5b69df7e401ddbede43491141047db3): the hook mints and burns 2439181612486875092 claims of currency 0x8b74867835f2207cd61710fc3fd7ffdc6c5d1ca8 for token 0xec2d248beff7f0424fd5d6c602dfd3e6a11c8ce6, whose listed pair is SPCXon. The factory logs a credit of 975672644994750037 and then transfers 975672644994750037 SPCXon to the token creator 0x1ec0e76d1a7f023995cf603dc2802863e5821ec3, which forwards 975770212259253512 SPCXon to the sender. No SPCXon enters the factory in that transaction. Transaction 0x341ecc661e5a52595f908573ae1d560ae8964990ea7c57ab229fc93b8504ceb9 (block 26151960, sender 0x888ddf6a86fd6e6da39395cd3f333ecba55ff364 through contract 0x6730d584c0e5f84b6af8698a16a11d6f82089b1e) repeats it for seven tokens created by that contract. The fees arrive at the factory in token 0x43c6b27489139debfdb71463f83552cc7264968b, which is minted from the zero address in the same transaction, and the factory pays out 36032480969017805 units of 0x2d1f7226bd1f780af6b9a49dcc0ae00e8df4bdee, 15311085289905665 of 0xf6b1117ec07684d3958cad8beb1b302bfd21103f, 8548104401245769 SPCXon, 6797353818778040 of 0xba47214edd2bb43099611b208f75e4b42fdcfedc, 4284309310775460 of 0xfedc5f4a6c38211c1338aa411018dfaf26612c08, 2836402531014289 of 0x14c3abf95cb9c93a8b82c1cdcb76d72cb87b2d4c and 470446068834558 of 0x59644165402b611b350645555b50afb581c71eb2. 6. The factory books no longer match its holdings. For the 16 tokens launched against SPCXon the factory logged credits of 2873155228010260120, the hook sent it 1888836911349760839 SPCXon, and the difference of 984318316660499281 is exactly the two transactions above. Creators collected 1884499543919557792, creatorFees still records 988655684090702328, and the factory holds 4337367430203047 SPCXon. In an eth_simulateV1 run collectFees for token 0xe413ebda258f05cd12aea2aaf9348b1045a136e4 from its creator, with 752539572571985445 recorded, reverts with "transfer failed". The pairs 0xf6b1117e, 0xfedc5f4a, 0x59644165 and 0xba47214e show owed amounts equal to what the second transaction took, against a factory balance of zero. 7. On Tempo an eth_simulateV1 run of creditCreatorFee(DUCK, 100000) from the hook address followed by collectFees(DUCK) from the creator makes the factory transfer 2946234 pathUSD, its whole balance, where creatorFees(DUCK) was 2846234 before.

What is at risk: Creator fees that the Klik factories hold between collection calls, and the ability of honest creators to collect. On Ethereum about 0.984 SPCXon (about 130 USD at the 132 USD price recorded in cycle 5) and small amounts of six other pair tokens were taken, and at least 0.9887 SPCXon of recorded creator fees can no longer be paid. What the factories hold today is small: 0.0043 SPCXon, 0.0040 AAPLon, 0.0050 NVDAon and two other tokens at 0xb6da931df2ed2c80db47923d4eb46fd913ee01c3 (about 3 USD), about 2 USD of tokens at 0x95e0da371711502eed78bcc3f17856b4415120ca, 2.946234 pathUSD on Tempo, 0.212 UNI on Unichain, 20.65 MON on Monad, about 5 USD of stock tokens on Robinhood Chain, 0.0012 ETH on Ink, 0.2868 AVAX on Avalanche and 14.99 units of one token on Base. Every later fee payment into these factories is exposed in the same way. No liquidity in the PoolManager is touched by this step. The swap freeze of the earlier finding still applies.

What it takes: None beyond what any account has: launch a token through the factory (open on Ethereum and Tempo by eth_call), initialize a second pool, swap twice, call collectFees. The source was read for the two Ethereum factories and the Ethereum and Unichain hooks. The Tempo factory is unverified and was tested by simulation. The factories on Base, Ink, Arbitrum One, Robinhood Chain, Avalanche and Monad were not read. launchEnabled() returns 1 on every one of them, including the Avalanche factory 0x5e91ca49f5e94319755634539bd9a1d2f15467a2, which holds 0.2868 AVAX. On Robinhood Chain a launch call from an ordinary account fails only with "pair not enabled" for the pair I chose. The AnyLauncher factories (second Ethereum factory, Unichain, and by the hook version string also Base, Ink, Arbitrum One, Avalanche and Monad) need the signed price quote for a launch. I did not examine who can obtain such a quote, so open launch is unconfirmed there. The newer Ethereum hook 0xcba49142a75da64a679f32a159f4316f11a7e0cc rejects a foreign initialize, and its factory 0xfe4a23bbe27be16955f4d8599c73bac88ef647d1 (0.118 ETH and about 480 USD of tokens) was not read.

What can be done: The PoolManager deployer cannot change this. The factory author can record the currency with each credit and pay each credit in the currency received, and the hook author can compare currency0 with pairOf(token) and reject initialize from any caller other than the factory. Creators should collect often. Interfaces should treat a second pool of a Klik token as hostile.

This entry adds to F-084.

Second reviewer's corrections: 1. Four transactions used the path on Ethereum, and the finding names two. The same sender 0x690193287a9f3e0095adae116f4260818332ce0a also sent 0x6f7afaab99a4ad2ab4f7c77cf1953b649e1e69d7da2a521f57a1c2eb4ad4b266 (block 26078715), which took 146203994947546868 AAPLon (0x14c3abf95cb9c93a8b82c1cdcb76d72cb87b2d4c) for token 0xa220196e6d2051595beb8ddb66e6d37f21898b17, and 0xf1dc644ddf2356ab7d9f400208b34a73bd33a4329b1cadcede3c8428d77a4fba (block 26078731), which took 189761423183711877 NVDAon (0x2d1f7226bd1f780af6b9a49dcc0ae00e8df4bdee) for token 0xc5584905cde5376e55d1714c57ec2cb22065ef0f. 2. The shortfall per pair at block 26478691, summed over the 71 tokens of the factory, is 984318316660499281 SPCXon, 225793904152729682 NVDAon, 149040397478561157 AAPLon, 15311085289905665 of 0xf6b1117e, 6797353818778040 of 0xba47214e, 4284309310775460 of 0xfedc5f4a and 470446068834558 of 0x59644165. Each figure equals what the four transactions took in that pair, so the reconciliation is complete for this factory. The impact text should add about 0.149 AAPLon and 0.226 NVDAon to the loss. 3. 25 tokens have pairOf equal to SPCXon today, where the finding says 16. 4. The attacker has to wait one block after initialize: the hook charges no fee in the block a pool was created and rejects buys in that block (KlikHook lines 361 and 403). 5. Scope: the money taken sat in the Klik factory, outside the PoolManager, and the fault does not depend on any network. It does not answer the question about a weakness that follows from a network difference. The claims that the hook holds at the PoolManager are the subject of the earlier part of F-084. 6. Chains: I confirmed the open launch on Ethereum (KlikLauncher) and Tempo only. For Base, Unichain, Ink, Arbitrum One, Robinhood Chain, Avalanche and Monad the factory step is unconfirmed, as the finding states, because AnyLauncher needs a signed price quote and the other factories were not read. An existing creator on those chains needs no new launch.

## F-109. A sale of WHITE or ARGUS that pays the listed pool through take leaves the sell charge to the PoolManager, the Arc PoolManager holds about 107,000 USD of ARGUS, and no other priced currency on the 21 networks charges the sender

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Ethereum, Arc.

What differs on the network: The cause is the token contract in both cases. Neither Ethereum nor Arc adds a network property. WHITE 0x9cdf242ef7975d8c68d5c1f5b6905801699b1940 on Ethereum debits the sender 10 percent on top of the amount when the recipient is its Uniswap v2 pair with USDC 0x6ec94f50cadcc79984463688de42a0ca696ec2db or its Uniswap v3 pool with WETH 0xf8f6191561997010870056fc265d0f7ddaf105bf (fee 0.3 percent). The v3 pool is a second charged recipient that F-092 did not list. ARGUS 0xece5ca8bf9220718e5727754026757512212cb3c on Arc debits the sender 1 percent on top when the recipient is its mainPool() 0x6a3bacaa6493734c1ac221ebf42cf530a96c1e02, a Uniswap v3 pool with USDC at fee 1 percent. ARGUS reports currentTaxes() = (100, 100) basis points, taxExpiry() = 0 and isExempt(PoolManager) = false.

How it meets the code: 1. take debits the caller by amount and then calls transfer(to, amount) on the token (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297). The transfer routine checks only that the call did not revert and returned nothing or true (src/types/Currency.sol:60-88). No line compares the fall of the PoolManager balance with the amount. 2. Sweep of this cycle: a measuring stub placed at the PoolManager address by state override (eth_call only) sent one third of the balance of every priced currency to every candidate recipient and read the balances before and after. 13,001 priced currencies on 21 networks, 12,751 with a balance of at least 3 base units, 128,687 recipient trials, 127,554 accepted, 43,808 of them to a v2 style pair, a v3 style pool or a pair listed by DexScreener. Recipients came from getPair and getPool of 83 factories (v2 style, Solidly style and v3 style, each verified on chain) against up to 13 base tokens per network, from every address that a zero argument call of any selector of the token returns, from DexScreener pair lists for currencies above 50 USD, and from four fixed recipients (a plain account, the token itself, the dead address, the wrapped native contract). 3. Result: the PoolManager balance fell by more than the amount for 86 currencies. For 84 of them the excess is 1 to 536 base units (Aave and Compound receipt tokens and valTUSD), which is rounding. The two others are WHITE and ARGUS. 4. WHITE at Ethereum block 26,479,738 on two endpoints: a transfer of 16,088,490.01 WHITE from the PoolManager to the v2 pair lowers its balance by 17,697,339.01 (110.0000 percent) and the pair receives 100 percent. skim(PoolManager) on the pair then returns 16,088,490.01 in full, so the net loss is 1,608,849.00 WHITE. The same transfer to the v3 pool also costs 110.0000 percent. A transfer from either pool to the PoolManager arrives in full, and a transfer from a plain holder to the PoolManager carries no charge. 5. ARGUS at Arc block 32,779,722 on two endpoints: a transfer of 15,539,568.77 ARGUS to the v3 pool lowers the PoolManager balance by 101.0000 percent of the amount. The pool has no skim. A transfer from the pool to the PoolManager arrives at 99.0000 percent. A transfer from a plain holder to the PoolManager carries no charge. 6. Who gains. A holder that sells into the listed pool from its own address pays the amount plus the charge: in the trial a plain holder that sent WHITE to either pool paid 110.0000 percent of the amount, and one that sent ARGUS to its pool paid 101.0000 percent. A caller that pays the amount into the PoolManager, or buys it in a v4 pool, and names the listed pool as recipient of take owes the PoolManager the amount only, and the token takes the charge from the pooled balance. The caller keeps the charge it would have paid: 10 percent of a WHITE sale and 1 percent of an ARGUS sale. Arc transaction 0x471fafc3a4761f749412e3856fac067b8f95e73d1d7bf6240dee396e9fc3e9fb (block 32,766,474) shows the pattern: the contract 0x4d680e3a362f64005b4f71004fdfebe203b0ea59 swaps in v4, the PoolManager sends 3,161.80 ARGUS to the v3 pool and 31.62 ARGUS to the token contract, and the pool pays 7.200687 USDC to the PoolManager. 7. Cost of doing it without a real sale. WHITE: take to the v2 pair, sync, skim to the PoolManager and settle costs gas only and moves 10 percent of the taken amount to the token owner 0x287ecebd1223e235b929217fe596c905a11752ae. One round can take at most 10/11 of the balance, so each round removes at most 1/11 of it and 48 rounds leave about 1 percent ((10/11)^48 = 0.0103). The caller gains nothing from this unless it is the token owner. ARGUS: the return leg loses 1 percent to the buy charge and each v3 swap pays the 1 percent pool fee, so a round trip costs the caller about 3 percent of the amount to remove 1 percent from the PoolManager. 8. History. The cycle 6 scan counted 766 charge transfers and 6,129,406.68 WHITE up to block 26,459,964. No charge transfer followed up to block 26,479,742 (eth_getLogs on one endpoint; the second endpoint refused the range), and balanceOf(PoolManager) is still 48,265,470.022830203421280226 WHITE, the value cycle 6 recorded. For ARGUS two more charge transfers of 59.24 ARGUS in total followed between blocks 32,673,464 and 32,779,791 on two endpoints, which brings the total to 1,297 transfers and 188,066.52 ARGUS.

What is at risk: ARGUS: the PoolManager holds 46,618,706.31 ARGUS, about 107,300 USD at the DefiLlama price of 0.002302 USD (DexScreener: 0.002293 USD, 222,906 USD of liquidity in the listed v3 pool, 53,316 USD of volume in 24 hours). The v4 pools of ARGUS, among them the hookless pool 0xb3f441e8871840ecfcb284e5cbb5b9c21a50e961e1c50109bdced25e429f00cc (USDC and ARGUS, fee 0.985 percent, tick spacing 99, no hook, 90,823 USD of liquidity at DexScreener), together record 188,066.52 ARGUS (about 433 USD) more than the PoolManager holds, assuming no other flow outside pool accounting, and the gap grows by 1 percent of every amount taken straight to the listed pool. The gain goes to the caller of each such route as a saved charge, and the loss falls on the liquidity providers who withdraw last. WHITE: the PoolManager holds 48,265,470.02 WHITE, about 1,495 USD at the DefiLlama price of 0.00003098 USD. Any account can move nearly all of it to the token owner at the cost of gas, and any seller into the two listed pools can let the PoolManager pay the 10 percent charge. Nothing else was found: 19 networks have no priced currency with a sender charge above rounding. The sweep covers priced currencies only. The unpriced token T on BNB Smart Chain that F-092 mentions was not retested.

What it takes: A v4 pool of the token with a balance at the PoolManager, and a caller that names a listed pool as recipient of take. Both hold today. The lists of charged recipients are set by the token owners. I did not check which deployed routers pass a pool address as the recipient of take on their own.

What can be done: Routers and interfaces should take these two tokens to their own address and pay the pool from there, so that the sender of the sale carries the charge. Interfaces should flag a currency when a simulated transfer from the PoolManager to one of its pools lowers the PoolManager balance by more than the amount. Liquidity providers in WHITE and ARGUS pools should expect a shortfall on withdrawal. The deployer cannot close this in core without a balance check around the transfer in take.

This entry adds to F-092.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor unprivileged and the severity medium stand. Six points need correction or addition. 1. The gain of the caller is overstated for ARGUS. The verified implementation source of ARGUS (ArgusTaxToken.sol at Sourcify, lines 430-435) clamps the sell surcharge to the balance the sender has left after the transfer. My simulation confirms it: an account that holds exactly 1,000,000 ARGUS and sends all of it to the main pool pays no surcharge, and an account that holds 1,004,000 pays 4,000. A seller can therefore avoid the 1 percent charge with any helper contract that holds exactly the amount, and the route through take saves the seller nothing beyond that. The loss to the PoolManager on ARGUS comes from routes that name the pool as recipient without need. For WHITE the saving is real: a holder with 100 or 105 percent of the amount gets the revert text Insufficient balance for amount + tax, so only the route through take lets a seller pay 100 percent. 2. The cost of doing it on purpose for ARGUS is lower than the 3 percent the finding states. The source charges third party liquidity adds on the main pool like sells (comment at lines 376-377). A one sided liquidity add paid by take inside the v3 mint callback, followed by burn and collect to the PoolManager, would cost the caller the 1 percent buy charge and no swap fee, for a loss of 1 percent to the PoolManager. I derived this from the token source and the standard v3 pool interface and did not simulate it, because it needs a contract. 3. Who receives the ARGUS charge. config() returns creatorBps 10000, burnBps 0 and dividendBps 0, with creatorWallet 0x7d613c6316ede9d257f3bf512777cb4ea4a6bee4, which is also owner(). swapBack is open to any caller and pays a bounty of 0.5 percent, and the last three TaxReceived logs of the processor 0x863a810de5d1b9ab8e853dbb6218fabfa5fc6d61 show a protocol fee of 1 percent. The creator wallet therefore receives about 98.5 percent of the proceeds of both charges, and for that wallet the round in point 2 gains about 1 percent of the amount per round out of the pooled balance. That part has actor issuer. It is inferred from source and logs and was not simulated. 4. The title says no other priced currency charges the sender. Priced means a DefiLlama price in the sweep. Token T 0x4ef42e4cf51cd779e9947db65b629ecd8c92b1da on BNB Smart Chain has no DefiLlama price and has a DexScreener price of 0.000002139 USD with a PancakeSwap pair of 56 USD liquidity. The PoolManager holds 19,136,577.6 T, about 41 USD. The title should say no other currency with a DefiLlama price. 5. Figures have moved. balanceOf(PoolManager) is 46,617,370.19 ARGUS at block 32,784,916, about 106,200 USD, against 46,618,706.31 in the finding. 6. The portal 0x0f1c7cb26d6cd36bd4189e41947658b39437587a has nonce 7 and its logs show two launches: ARGUS and BBASRUS 0x6de0323c4bf764849df01a82e307c24dda18c6cf, a clone of the same implementation with charges of 200 basis points and main pool 0xf21f82a7125d6a4e1db00a1ffde1f056a8b95f77. The PoolManager holds 0 BBASRUS. This answers the third lead of the research file for this portal.

## F-008. Polygon system token at 0x1010 mirrors the PoolManager native balance and cannot be paid out by take

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Polygon.

What differs on the network: Polygon has a genesis contract at 0x0000000000000000000000000000000000001010 that presents native POL as a token. Its balanceOf returns the native balance of the account. Its transfer is payable and returns false unless msg.value equals the amount. Ethereum has no ERC-20 face of ETH at the protocol level.

How it meets the code: 1. sync(0x1010) stores balanceOf(PoolManager) as reserves (src/PoolManager.sol:285-286, src/types/Currency.sol:94). That value is the full native POL balance of the PoolManager, which already backs pools that use address(0). 2. settle with 0x1010 synced credits the caller with any rise in that native balance since sync (src/PoolManager.sol:358-360). msg.value is rejected at src/PoolManager.sol:356 and the PoolManager has no receive function, so the balance can rise only through a forced transfer such as SELFDESTRUCT. 3. take(0x1010, to, amount) calls transfer on 0x1010 with zero value (src/types/Currency.sol:73). The token returns a 32 byte zero. The success test at src/types/Currency.sol:68 fails and the call reverts at src/types/Currency.sol:82-86. collectProtocolFees for this currency reverts the same way (src/ProtocolFees.sol:56). 4. A positive delta in currency 0x1010 can therefore only be cleared, minted as an ERC-6909 claim, or swapped. The POL that paid for it stays in the PoolManager native balance and no account holds a native delta to take it. 5. Double credit was checked and is not possible. A native settle while 0x1010 is synced reverts at src/PoolManager.sol:356, and sync(address(0)) clears the synced currency (src/PoolManager.sol:281-283), so one POL is never credited under both currencies.

What is at risk: POL that a contract pays into a pool or claim denominated in 0x1010 cannot leave the PoolManager as that currency and is not claimable as native POL either. Pools that hold POL under address(0) are not affected. The PoolManager held about 3.1 million native POL at the time of the query. Protocol fees accrued in currency 0x1010 would also be stuck.

What it takes: Someone initializes a pool with 0x1010 as a currency, and a custom contract forces POL into the PoolManager between sync and settle. Standard periphery cannot do this because approve and transferFrom on 0x1010 revert. The actor loses its own funds. No path was found that takes funds from other users.

What can be done: Interfaces, routers and pool indexers on Polygon should refuse 0x0000000000000000000000000000000000001010 as a pool currency and direct users to address(0) or WPOL. Block explorers label 0x1010 as the POL token, so the warning should be explicit in deployment notes.

Second reviewer's corrections: Nothing wrong in the mechanism. One detail to add: a take of amount zero on 0x1010 would see transfer return true, and it changes nothing because a zero delta is ignored (src/PoolManager.sol:369). The frozen funds belong only to a party that chose to fund a 0x1010 position through a forced transfer, so low is the right level.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-365`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-75`, `src/types/Currency.sol:82-86`, `src/types/Currency.sol:90-96`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57`.

Sources:

- rpc https://polygon.drpc.org, queries at block 97759148 to 97759199 on 2026-11-22 (balanceOf(PoolManager) on 0x1010 equals eth_getBalance(PoolManager); transfer(0xdead, 1) from the PoolManager with zero value returns 0; transfer(PoolManager, 1) with value 1 reverts; a 1 wei call with empty calldata to the PoolManager reverts; approve and transferFrom on 0x1010 revert)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/maticnetwork/contracts/main/contracts/child/MRC20.sol (Published source: balanceOf returns account.balance, transfer returns false when msg.value differs from value, deposit and _transfer use address.transfer)

## F-021. Three more recipients on HyperEVM accept a PoolManager payout and strand it: the L1 block number precompile, the ERC-4337 EntryPoints and a system address reached through the stHYPE contract

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: HyperEVM.

What differs on the network: HyperEVM has a precompile at 0x0809 that answers any call, with or without value, with the L1 block number. The ERC-4337 EntryPoints v0.6 and v0.7 are deployed and book incoming value as a deposit of the sender. HyperCore credits an ERC-20 sent to a token's system address only from the Transfer log of the linked contract, and for STHYPE the linked contract is wstHYPE, one of two contracts that move the same balance.

How it meets the code: take debits the caller and then pays (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297). Read-only calls from the PoolManager address show what three recipients do with the payout. A native payout of 1 wei to 0x0000000000000000000000000000000000000809 returns success, so by the EVM's call rules the value is then held by a precompile address that cannot send it on (src/types/Currency.sol:44-53). A native payout to either EntryPoint succeeds and is booked as the PoolManager's deposit, which only the PoolManager could withdraw. Both EntryPoints answer balanceOf(PoolManager) with that deposit. A caller can therefore sync the EntryPoint address, make the payout and call settle, and is credited the same amount under the EntryPoint address as currency id (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288 and 349-365). That credit can be minted as an ERC-6909 claim and can never be paid out, because the EntryPoints revert on transfer(address,uint256). The caller still owes the native amount. A take of stHYPE to 0x2000000000000000000000000000000000000077 moves shares to the STHYPE system address while the linked contract, wstHYPE, emits no Transfer log, so by the documented rule HyperCore credits nobody. A take of wstHYPE to the same address is credited to the PoolManager's HyperCore account, as F-021 describes. In each case the caller pays the full amount and no other user loses anything.

What is at risk: The loss falls on the caller who chose the recipient, or on the user of a router that lets the user name any recipient. Pool reserves stay whole. Claims under an EntryPoint id are worthless and cannot affect another currency.

What it takes: A caller or a router that passes one of these addresses as recipient. The stHYPE case needs stHYPE in the PoolManager, which holds none today, and the statement that HyperCore credits nothing rests on the docs and was not tested.

What can be done: Routers and interfaces on HyperEVM should refuse recipients in 0x0800 to 0x08ff, the EntryPoint addresses, 0x2222222222222222222222222222222222222222 and addresses that start with 0x20 followed by zeros, unless the payer chose them knowingly. The 0x settler contracts added a similar rule for HyperCore system addresses.

Second reviewer's corrections: The stHYPE part is a consequence of the documented crediting rule and needs stHYPE in the PoolManager, which holds none. The actor value unprivileged is right in the sense that any caller can do it, and the loser is that caller or the user of a router that passes the recipient through. No other user loses funds.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:44-87`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-365`.

Sources:

- https://rpc.hyperliquid.xyz/evm eth_call from 0x12D4Fd9C5DeDd00ab8a0bCe2CF0167bbf94b6B1F to 0x0809 with empty calldata and value 1; to both EntryPoints with empty calldata and value 1, balanceOf and transfer (0x0809 and both EntryPoints accept the value call; the EntryPoints return 32 bytes for balanceOf and revert on transfer)
- https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/for-developers/hyperevm/hypercore-less-than-greater-than-hyperevm-transfers (EVM to HyperCore credits are based on the Transfer log emitted by the linked contract)
- https://api.hyperliquid.xyz/info spotMeta (STHYPE index 119 is linked to 0x94e8396e0869c9f2200760af0621afd240e1cf38, the wstHYPE contract)
- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/999/0xe71caf5c1fe56d8897c7b604295d23968049e057?fields=sources (A transfer through stHYPE emits Transfer from the stHYPE contract only)
- https://github.com/0xProject/0x-settler/blob/master/CHANGELOG.md (Another protocol reverts on HyperEVM when a payout names a HyperCore system address as recipient unless the payer funds the transfer)

## F-025. The OP Stack wrapped native predeploy on Celo calls itself Wrapped Ether and WETH but wraps CELO

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Celo.

What differs on the network: On OP Stack chains that use ETH for gas, 0x4200000000000000000000000000000000000006 is canonical WETH. On Celo the same address is a live wrapper of the native asset, which is CELO, and it still returns name 'Wrapped Ether' and symbol 'WETH' because the L2 L1Block predeploy reports the gas token as Ether. The bridged ETH token on Celo is a different contract at 0xD221812de1BD094f35587EE8E174B07B6167D9Af.

How it meets the code: The PoolManager accepts any address as a currency and reads no metadata (src/PoolManager.sol:117-142). It learns balances only through balanceOf (src/types/Currency.sol:90-96). Anyone can obtain the predeploy token one for one by depositing CELO. A pool, hook, router table or price source that treats this address as ETH backed WETH values a CELO backed token at the ETH price. Liquidity placed against it at that price is bought with tokens that cost one CELO each, and the paired asset leaves the PoolManager through ordinary swaps. Swappers who buy the token in such a pool receive a claim on CELO.

What is at risk: Funds of LPs and swappers in pools that list 0x4200...0006 as a currency, and integrations that map that address to WETH across OP Stack chains. Pools on the bridged WETH or on CELO are not affected. The PoolManager holds none of this token today, and its total supply is 0.143 CELO.

What it takes: Someone must create or join a pool with the predeploy as a currency while pricing it as ETH, or an integration must hardcode the address as WETH. Any account can do the rest.

What can be done: Interfaces and token lists for Celo should hide or relabel 0x4200...0006 and point to 0xD221...D9Af for ETH. Deployment scripts and routers should take the wrapped native address from Celo specific configuration. Pool creators should check that remoteToken() on a WETH candidate returns the Ethereum WETH address.

Second reviewer's corrections: 1. Severity lowered from medium to low. No path in the PoolManager mishandles the token. A loss needs an LP or an integration to price a CELO backed token as ETH. The PoolManager holds none of it, its whole supply is 0.143 CELO, and the deployed Uniswap periphery on Celo does not reference it. 2. The finding does not say which wrapped native address the periphery uses. PositionManager reports the CELO token itself as WETH9, which is a different question and is listed as a lead.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:117-142`, `src/types/Currency.sol:90-96`.

Sources:

- https://forno.celo.org eth_call name(), symbol(), decimals(), version(), totalSupply() on 0x4200000000000000000000000000000000000006 and eth_getBalance of the same address (name is 'Wrapped Ether', symbol is 'WETH', decimals 18, version 1.1.0-beta.3, and totalSupply equals the contract's native balance of 143112547216520198 wei.)
- https://forno.celo.org eth_call deposit() with 1 CELO of value on 0x4200000000000000000000000000000000000006 (simulation only) (deposit() accepts native CELO.)
- https://forno.celo.org eth_call isCustomGasToken(), gasPayingTokenName() on 0x4200000000000000000000000000000000000015 (The L2 L1Block predeploy returns false and 'Ether'.)
- https://forno.celo.org eth_call name(), remoteToken(), bridge() on 0xD221812de1BD094f35587EE8E174B07B6167D9Af (The bridged token is named 'Wrapped Ether (Celo native bridge)', its remote token is 0xC02aaA39b223FE8D0A0e5C4F27eAD9083C756Cc2 and its bridge is the L2StandardBridge.)
- https://docs.celo.org/operate/specification/native-bridge.md (Native ETH bridging is not supported on Celo and WETH is bridged as a standard ERC-20. The L1 bridge treats L1 CELO as the native token of Celo.)

## F-037. Aave aTokens held by the Arbitrum One PoolManager grow outside pool accounting and the growth cannot be claimed

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Arbitrum One.

What differs on the network: This is the rebasing token case that the Ethereum unit recorded for stETH, found here for Aave aTokens. No chain rule is involved. An aToken balance is a stored scaled amount multiplied by the reserve's liquidity index, so balanceOf(PoolManager) rises with time without any transfer.

How it meets the code: Step 1: a liquidity provider adds liquidity in a pool whose currency is an aToken. The pool records a fixed amount and the PoolManager receives the aTokens. Step 2: the index grows with time and balanceOf(PoolManager) grows with it. For aArbUSDT the PoolManager has a scaled balance of 1,214.98 and a balance of 1,504.61. Step 3: a credit exists only for a balance increase between a sync and a settle in the same transaction (PoolManager.sol:279-288 and 358-360). The index does not move inside a transaction as far as I could establish: interest accrues with time and the Aave pool sends the whole flash loan premium to the protocol. I checked the premium setting on chain and did not read the Aave pool code for other paths. A sync therefore always includes the interest accrued so far in the reserve figure, and no settle is ever credited for it. Step 4: take, burn and fee collection pay out only recorded amounts, so the accrued interest stays in the PoolManager balance and belongs to no pool, claim or fee. The provider gets back the recorded amount and the pool fees. I found no way for an ordinary account to collect the surplus, and none to reduce the balance below the recorded amounts.

What is at risk: Liquidity providers in aToken pools lose the lending interest on their deposit for as long as it stays in the pool. Balances today are small: 1,504.6 aArbUSDT, 443.3 aArbUSDCn, 34.9 aArbUSDC and 0.41 aArbWETH. Principal is not affected.

What it takes: A pool must use an aToken or another token whose balance rises without transfers. Nobody has to act. If a future Aave version again added value to the supplier index inside a transaction, an account that calls sync before it and settle after it would be credited the PoolManager's share of that addition.

What can be done: Use a non-rebasing wrapper of the aToken as the pool currency. Interfaces can warn when a pool currency reports a scaled balance that differs from its balance.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor value unprivileged is acceptable only in the sense that no privilege is needed. No account acts and no account gains. Nothing is taken from the PoolManager and nothing is frozen, so this item is outside the goal of the review and should be reported as lost yield for providers who choose a rebasing currency. The scaled balance of aArbUSDT read 1,214.96 at my query against 1,214.98 in the finding. The warning in the preconditions is the part worth keeping: a token whose balanceOf(PoolManager) can be raised by a call that any account may make inside a transaction would let that account collect the PoolManager's share through sync and settle. Lead 5 of the research file covers the search for such a token and I add what I found on xUSD as a lead.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`.

Sources:

- https://arb1.arbitrum.io/rpc eth_call scaledBalanceOf(PoolManager), balanceOf(PoolManager) and POOL() on aArbUSDT 0x6ab707Aca953eDAeFBc4fD23bA73294241490620 and aArbUSDCn 0x724dc807b04555b71ed48a6896b6F41593b8C637, and FLASHLOAN_PREMIUM_TOTAL() and FLASHLOAN_PREMIUM_TO_PROTOCOL() on 0x794a61358D6845594F94dc1DB02A252b5b4814aD, queried 2026-11-22 (The PoolManager holds aTokens whose balance exceeds the scaled balance, and the Aave pool directs 100 percent of the flash loan premium to the protocol.)
- https://aave.com/docs/aave-v3/smart-contracts/tokenization (Yield is distributed to aToken holders by continuously increasing their balance, and balanceOf returns principal plus yield.)
- https://governance.aave.com/t/arfc-bgd-aave-v3-4/21572 (Aave v3.4 removed the part of the flash loan fee that was added to the supplier index, which leaves time based accrual as the only way the index rises.)

## F-038. Any account can fill a boundary tick to the per tick liquidity cap for a dust payment, which blocks later deposits that use that tick

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: all 21 networks.

What differs on the network: None. The cap and the amount formula are part of the pool math and give the same numbers on every network. Read-only probes against the PoolManager on 20 of the 21 networks returned identical results. Tempo could not be probed with a native payment because the node rejects calls that carry value.

How it meets the code: Pool.modifyLiquidity adds liquidityDelta to liquidityGross of the lower and the upper tick of the range (src/libraries/Pool.sol:160-162, 524-531). For a delta of zero or more it reverts with TickLiquidityOverflow when liquidityGross of either tick is above tickSpacingToMaxLiquidityPerTick (Pool.sol:165-173). The cap is (2^128 - 1) divided by the number of ticks (Pool.sol:565-582): 191757530477355301479181766273477 for spacing 1, 1917559095893846719543856547154045 for spacing 10, 11505354575363080317263139282924270 for spacing 60 and 38345995821606768476828330790147420 for spacing 200, which are the values in test/Tick.t.sol:115-141. A range that lies above the current tick is paid in currency0 only, with getAmount0Delta(sqrtLower, sqrtUpper, liquidity) rounded up (Pool.sol:209-217, src/libraries/SqrtPriceMath.sol:188-211, 261-271). That amount is liquidity times the difference of the inverse sqrt prices, and at the highest usable ticks the sqrt prices are close to 2^160, so the amount is tiny. Filling the highest usable tick to the cap with a range one spacing wide costs 519,766,372 base units of currency0 at spacing 1, 51,993,092,462 at spacing 10, 1,878,783,905,959 at spacing 60 and 20,966,766,746,469 at spacing 200. The mirror case at the lowest usable tick is paid in currency1 and costs 519,766,206, 51,993,074,932, 1,878,783,895,257 and 20,966,766,629,403 base units. The cost does not depend on where the pool trades as long as the range is out of range. After the fill, every add whose range starts or ends on that tick reverts, whoever the owner is and however small the amount. An owner who removes liquidity from that tick can add back at most what it removed. Removals never run the cap check, because the check sits inside the branch for a delta of zero or more (Pool.sol:165), so nothing is locked. The filler can remove its position at any time and gets its payment back, so the fill costs gas and idle dust. Flash accounting does not make it free: the position has to stay, so the debit has to be settled. I confirmed the numbers three ways. A Python transcription of Pool, Position, TickBitmap, SqrtPriceMath and TickMath gives them. An eth_call through the PositionManager on Ethereum for the ETH/USDC 0.05 percent pool (spacing 10) minted 1917559095893846714755826590044058 units of liquidity on the range 887260 to 887270 with a value of 51,993,092,462 wei, reverted with one wei less, and reverted with TickLiquidityOverflow(887270) for one more unit of liquidity. The same three calls on a pool created inside the call gave the same outcome on 20 networks for spacings 1, 60 and 200.

What is at risk: New deposits that use the filled tick stop in that pool. This covers the usual full range position and every position that ends on the lowest or highest usable tick. Principal and fees of existing positions are not touched and can be withdrawn. A liquidity provider can avoid the block by choosing an outer tick further inside, at the price of a position that front ends do not show as full range. A contract that always adds on the filled tick, such as a full range vault or a hook that holds one fixed full range position, cannot take deposits or reinvest fees while the fill stands. If such a contract has to add liquidity on that tick before it can pay out, its withdrawals stop too. I looked for one deployed contract of that kind and did not find it, so the freeze variant is unconfirmed. For a currency with 18 decimals the cost is below 0.00003 of one token for every spacing up to 200. For a currency with 6 decimals it is about 520 tokens at spacing 1 and about 1.88 million tokens at spacing 60, so the attacker fills the end whose currency is cheap per base unit. On the Ethereum ETH/USDC 0.05 percent pool the liquidityGross of tick 887270 was 4788029957109987 at block 26471794, 18 orders of magnitude under the cap, and the fill costs about 0.00000005 ETH.

What it takes: An initialized pool in which at least one of the two currencies is cheap per base unit. Any account can act. No hook, no role and no ordering advantage is needed. The fill lasts until the filler removes its position.

What can be done: Core has no switch for this. Front ends and liquidity providers can fall back to an outer tick one or more spacings inside the usable range when the boundary tick is full. Authors of vaults and hooks should not hard-code the lowest and highest usable ticks in any path that must succeed for a withdrawal, and should let the range be changed. Operators can watch liquidityGross of the two boundary ticks of important pools through extsload and compare it with the cap.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor is correct: any account can add liquidity to a pool without a hook, and the affected users did nothing unusual. The severity stays low. The medium row of the scale mentions blocked liquidity operations. Here only adds that touch the one filled tick are blocked, removal and fee collection keep working, and a range that ends one spacing further inside is accepted. No funds are taken or frozen. The freeze variant for a contract that must add on that tick in its exit path remains unconfirmed, as the finding says. The finding states that the hazard is the same on every network. It does not follow from a network difference, so it is a general property of this code and does not answer the search for a network-specific weakness. The cap applies to every tick. The finding treats the two boundary ticks because the cost there is dust. The cost rises steeply toward the current price. The side remark about Soneium is out of date: eth_getBalance for the PositionManager 0x1b35d13a2e2528f192637f14b05f0dc0e7deb566 returned 0 at block 31118892, and the one wei less probe reverted there like on the other networks. The source entries call the on-chain probes primary evidence for the formulas. They are primary evidence for the deployed contracts. The link from src/ to the numbers is the transcription, which I rebuilt independently with the same result.

Code: `src/libraries/Pool.sol:159-181`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:206-237`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:520-531`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:565-582`, `src/libraries/SqrtPriceMath.sol:188-211`, `src/libraries/SqrtPriceMath.sol:261-271`, `src/PoolManager.sol:145-184`.

Sources:

- src/libraries/Pool.sol:165-173 (The cap is checked only when liquidityDelta is zero or more, on liquidityGross of both ticks.)
- src/libraries/Pool.sol:209-217 (A range above the current tick is paid in currency0 only, from the sqrt prices of its two ticks.)
- src/libraries/Pool.sol:565-582 (The cap is (2^128 - 1) divided by the number of ticks for the spacing.)
- test/Tick.t.sol:115-141 (The repository tests state the same cap values for spacings 1, 10, 60 and 200 that the cost figures use.)
- https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com (extsload on PoolManager 0x000000000004444c5dc75cB358380D2e3dE08A90 at block 26471794: pool id 21c67e77068de97969ba93d4aab21826d33ca12bb9f565d8496e8fda8a82ca27 (ETH/USDC, fee 500, spacing 10) has liquidityGross 4788029957109987 at tick 887270 and 0 at tick 887260. eth_call at the latest block (about 26471905) from an account with an overridden balance to PositionManager 0xbd216513d74c8cf14cf4747e6aaa6420ff64ee9e, modifyLiquidities with MINT_POSITION and SETTLE_PAIR on ticks 887260 to 887270: liquidity 1917559095893846714755826590044058 succeeds with value 51993092462 wei, reverts with 51993092461 wei, and liquidity one unit higher reverts with data 0xb8e3c385 followed by 0xd89e6, which is TickLiquidityOverflow(887270). The ETH/USDC fee 3000 spacing 60 pool gives the same pattern at tick 887220 with 1878783905959 wei.)
- rpc endpoints listed in research/network-risk/state/networks.json (On 20 networks (all except Tempo) an eth_call to the PositionManager multicall that initializes a new native pool and mints on the top one spacing range succeeds with a value of exactly 519766372, 1878783905959 and 20966766746469 wei for spacings 1, 60 and 200, reverts with one wei less, and reverts with TickLiquidityOverflow for one liquidity unit above the cap. On Soneium the PositionManager already holds 176652317140 wei that the settlement draws on, so the lower value was reduced by that balance and the one wei less check could not be made for spacing 1.)

## F-039. The swap loop pays for every bitmap word and every initialized tick on its way, and a HyperEVM small block holds 3,000,000 gas

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: HyperEVM.

What differs on the network: HyperEVM produces small blocks with a 3,000,000 gas limit every second and big blocks with a 30,000,000 gas limit about once a minute. A sender reaches big blocks only after setting the usingBigBlocks flag on its HyperCore account. I sampled blocks 51958402 to 51958471 through the public RPC: 69 had gasLimit 3,000,000 and one (51958443) had 30,000,000. For comparison, state/networks.json records an Ethereum block gas limit of 59,999,943, and EIP-7825 specifies a cap of 16,777,216 gas per transaction. I did not confirm in this session that the cap is active on Ethereum.

How it meets the code: Pool.swap runs one loop iteration per step (src/libraries/Pool.sol:344-437). Each iteration reads one tick bitmap word from storage (Pool.sol:347-348, src/libraries/TickBitmap.sol:85-121), computes the price of the next tick and runs one swap step. When the step ends on an initialized tick, crossTick reads the tick slot and reads and rewrites both fee growth slots of that tick (Pool.sol:413-428, 602-612). The repository gas snapshots give 2,242 to 2,287 gas for the bitmap search, which includes the cold storage read, and 919 to 1,565 gas for a swap step with liquidity. I use 2,242 gas as the floor for one iteration and leave out the swap step, TickMath and loop bookkeeping. A crossing adds one cold read (2,100) and two read and write pairs. Each pair costs 5,000 gas when the slot changes from one nonzero value to another and 22,100 gas when it is written for the first time. That gives about 14,342 gas per crossing when both fee growth slots change, and about 48,542 when both are written for the first time. With the repository figure of 123,144 gas for a simple swap as fixed cost, a 3,000,000 gas transaction fits at most about 200 crossings, about 59 when the fee growth slots are written for the first time, and about 1,283 empty bitmap words. The real numbers are lower, because the floor leaves out the computation. I did not measure gas on HyperEVM. Three things an ordinary account controls raise the iteration count of other people's swaps. First, modifyLiquidity lets anyone add a position of one liquidity unit on any aligned tick pair of a pool without a hook (src/PoolManager.sol:145-184, Pool.sol:159-181). In my transcription of the source, 200 such positions on [t, t+1) below the price of a pool with tick spacing 1 made every tick initialized, each cost 1 base unit of one currency, and a swap that moved the price by 100, 200 and 400 ticks ran 101, 201 and 401 iterations, each with a crossing. Second, a step with zero liquidity in range consumes nothing and moves the price to the next word boundary (src/libraries/SwapMath.sol:63-75), so a swap that runs past the last position toward its price limit visits every word on the way. From tick 0 to the lower limit that is 3,468 iterations at tick spacing 1, 349 at spacing 10, 60 at spacing 60 and 20 at spacing 200, which is at least 7,775,256 gas at spacing 1 from the bitmap reads alone. Third, initialize has no access control and accepts any price from MIN_SQRT_PRICE up to one unit below MAX_SQRT_PRICE (PoolManager.sol:117-142, Pool.sol:100-107), so a third party can create a pool id at an extreme price before anyone else uses it. The gap to the market price then has to be walked with swaps that take no tokens and still pay for every word.

What is at risk: No funds leave the PoolManager and no position is frozen. Pool.modifyLiquidity has no loop (Pool.sol:146-238), so removals fit in a small block in every case I looked at. The cost falls on swappers and arbitrageurs in pools with tick spacing 1 or other small spacings. In a pool where every tick carries a dust position, one small block transaction can move the price by at most about 59 to 200 ticks, which is 0.6 to 2.0 percent at spacing 1. A larger move needs several transactions with nearer price limits, or a big block. A swap that names the extreme price limit and exceeds the liquidity on its side runs out of gas in a small block where it would otherwise end with a partial fill. A contract that must complete one large swap in one transaction, for example a liquidation, can stall until it is sent to a big block. The dust positions cost the attacker about 161,276 gas each by the repository snapshot for a simple add, plus one base unit, and stay until the attacker removes them.

What it takes: The pool has a small tick spacing, since at spacing 60 a move of 59 crossings is already more than 40 percent. The attacker pays gas for each dust position. The victim sends the swap to a small block, or the swap sits inside a contract that cannot split it. Any account can undo the third case by walking the price in several transactions.

What can be done: Routers and keepers on HyperEVM can pass a price limit close to the expected end price and split large swaps. Contracts that depend on one atomic swap can use pools with a wider tick spacing or send the transaction to a big block. Pool creators can initialize the pool ids they intend to use at the market price before announcing them. Liquidity providers do not need to act, because removal does not loop.

Second reviewer's corrections: The finding says it did not confirm that the EIP-7825 cap is active on Ethereum. It is active since Fusaka on 2025-12-03. The run with 200 dust positions cannot give 401 passes for a 400 tick move: my transcription gives 203 passes (201 crossings of the ticks from 0 down to -200 and two further passes to the word end and to the limit), and 401 passes need about 400 dusted ticks. My pass counts for the empty walk are one lower than the finding's (3,467, 348, 59 and 19), which changes the bitmap floor to 7,773,014 gas and changes no conclusion. The 48,542 gas case needs both fee growth outside slots of a tick to go from zero to nonzero. That happens for a tick above the current price when it is crossed upward for the first time while both global fee growth values are nonzero (Pool.sol:533-539, 608-609). Dust placed below the price starts with both slots equal to the global values, so those crossings fall in the 14,342 gas case or lower. The attacker can bring a tick back to the first time state by removing and adding the dust position again. Refunds for slots set to zero are paid after execution and do not lower the gas limit a swap needs. The evidence grade can move from inferred to primary for the block limits and the cold read cost. The per pass total on HyperEVM stays an upper bound on the number of crossings until someone measures it.

Code: `src/libraries/Pool.sol:344-437`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:602-612`, `src/libraries/TickBitmap.sol:85-121`, `src/libraries/SwapMath.sol:63-75`, `src/PoolManager.sol:117-142`, `src/PoolManager.sol:145-184`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:146-238`.

Sources:

- https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/for-developers/hyperevm/dual-block-architecture (Fast blocks last 1 second with a 3M gas limit and slow blocks last 1 minute with a 30M gas limit. A sender is directed to big blocks by the HyperCore action evmUserModify with usingBigBlocks true.)
- https://rpc.hyperliquid.xyz/evm eth_getBlockByNumber for blocks 51958402 to 51958471 (69 of the 70 sampled blocks report gasLimit 3,000,000 and block 51958443 reports 30,000,000.)
- https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/for-developers/hyperevm (The HyperEVM uses the Cancun hardfork without blobs. That the gas schedule equals Ethereum's Cancun schedule is my inference from this sentence.)
- https://eips.ethereum.org/EIPS/eip-2929 (COLD_SLOAD_COST is 2100 and SSTORE_RESET_GAS becomes 5000 minus COLD_SLOAD_COST, which is 2900.)
- https://eips.ethereum.org/EIPS/eip-2200 (SSTORE_SET_GAS is 20000.)
- https://eips.ethereum.org/EIPS/eip-7825 (The cap of 16,777,216 gas per transaction that I quote for Ethereum as comparison.)
- snapshots/TickBitmapTest.json:5-10 (nextInitializedTickWithinOneWord costs 2,242 to 2,287 gas in the repository snapshots.)
- snapshots/SwapMathTest.json:2-9 (computeSwapStep costs 919 to 1,565 gas in the repository snapshots.)
- snapshots/PoolManagerTest.json:12-16 (A simple add of liquidity costs 161,276 gas and a simple swap 123,144 gas through the test routers.)
- src/libraries/Pool.sol:344-437 (One loop iteration per bitmap word or initialized tick, with the tick crossing at lines 413 to 428.)

## F-042. One transaction can use the whole per-block budget for new state on MegaETH, and the node skips every later candidate of that block

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: MegaETH.

What differs on the network: MegaETH counts new accounts and new storage slots per transaction and per block, and both limits are 1,000 entries. Ethereum has no such counter. The block limiter admits the transaction that brings the block counter to the limit and then rejects every further candidate before execution, whatever that candidate would do. The base fee has stayed at its floor of 1,000,000 wei in every block sampled.

How it meets the code: A sender submits one transaction that creates 1,000 new state entries, for example a contract creation that writes 999 fresh storage slots. eth_estimateGas accepts that creation at 22,559,525 gas, which at the base fee of 1,000,000 wei is about 0.0000226 ETH, and rejects a creation with 1,000 slots. Storage gas is zero for slots in buckets of minimum size, so the cost is the compute gas of the writes. After this transaction the block counter for state growth equals the block limit. BlockLimiter::pre_execution_check then returns StateGrowthLimit for every later candidate of that block, including a liquidity removal or a take that creates no new state. Those candidates are skipped and can enter a later block. A sender who repeats this in every one second block spends about 1.95 ETH per day. Calls to modifyLiquidity and take on the PoolManager have no path that avoids the block limiter. What the sender achieves depends on where its transaction lands inside each block. Candidates placed before it are included. The ordering rule of the sequencer was not confirmed, so the share of each block that the sender can close was not established. Cycle 19 addition: the claim was measured again on 2026-11-25 against the replica build mega-reth/v2.3.1-4499c42 at block 32,832,912 (base fee 1,000,000 wei). eth_estimateGas accepts a contract creation that writes 999 fresh slots at 22,559,525 gas and rejects one with 1,000, because the new account counts as one entry. A call to an existing contract can write 1,000. The files block/limit.rs, constants.rs, block/executor.rs and block/chain.rs are byte identical between mega-evm main and tag v1.7.4. Deposits are exempt only from the two data availability tests in pre_execution_check (limit.rs lines 821 to 845) and not from the state growth test, so a deposit avoids the skip only if it is placed before the filling transaction. No block in the 20,001 blocks from 32,806,000 to 32,826,000 came near the limit (largest estimate 58 entries). Cycle 21 addition: the MegaEVM resource limit specification (https://docs.megaeth.com/spec/megaevm/resource-limits) now states the rule in normative form. The first transaction that brings a block to a limit is still included, later candidates must be skipped before execution, a skipped transaction gets no receipt and may be reconsidered in a later block. Both state growth limits are still 1,000, and limit.rs and constants.rs have equal hashes on mega-evm main and v1.7.4. The endpoint answered mega-reth/v2.3.1-4499c42 at block 32,849,640. A delayed swap or liquidity change runs under the same checks, at whatever pool price the later block has and within the caller's own limits.

What is at risk: Removals, swaps and claims on the PoolManager can be delayed block by block while the sender keeps paying. No balance moves and no position changes. The delay ends when the sender stops or when users' transactions are ordered ahead of the sender's.

What it takes: The base fee stays near its floor, and the sender's transaction is ordered early in each block. No role is needed. A full stop of withdrawals would need the sender to be first in every block, which the public record does not show to be possible.

What can be done: Core has no setting for this. Users can resubmit, pay a higher priority fee if the sequencer orders by fee, or use the L1 deposit path, whose transactions are placed at the start of a block in the OP Stack design. That placement was not confirmed in the MegaETH node source, and a deposit is not exempt from the state growth test itself. The chain operator can raise the block limit above the transaction limit or price new state entries in minimum-size buckets.

Second reviewer's corrections: The gas figure differs slightly with my own init code (22,559,391 against 22,559,525), which does not change the cost. The specification says later candidates are skipped when they depend on the exhausted resource, while the reference limiter skips every candidate. The finding follows the limiter, and that is the stricter reading. The precondition is stated too narrowly. The finding says a full stop needs the sender to be first in every block. If the sequencer serves pending transactions in arrival order, a run of N such transactions submitted together from several accounts would close N consecutive blocks for every transaction that arrives after the run, at 0.0000226 ETH per block. If the sequencer orders by priority fee, the same run with a priority fee of 1 wei outranks the current traffic, whose median priority fee is zero, until users pay more. This extension follows from the limiter logic and was not observed on chain. The severity stays low for the confirmed part, which is a delay inside one block. It would be medium under the scale if the multi-block case is confirmed, because liquidity operations would be blocked without loss for as long as the sender pays. The operator of the single sequencer can exclude such transactions, so the stall would last until the operator reacts.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:145-184`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/megaeth-labs/mega-evm/main/crates/mega-evm/src/block/limit.rs (pre_execution_check rejects a candidate when block_state_growth_used is at or above block_state_growth_limit, and only the data availability tests are skipped for deposits)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/megaeth-labs/mega-evm/main/crates/mega-evm/src/constants.rs (TX_STATE_GROWTH_LIMIT and BLOCK_STATE_GROWTH_LIMIT are both 1000)
- https://docs.megaeth.com/spec/megaevm/resource-limits.md (The first transaction that meets or exceeds a block-level runtime limit is included and later candidates are skipped for the current block)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/megaeth-labs/mega-evm/main/crates/mega-evm/src/external/gas.rs (SSTORE storage gas is the base of 20,000 times (multiplier minus 1), which is zero at multiplier 1)
- rpc https://mainnet.megaeth.com/rpc, eth_call with from set to the PoolManager and with state overrides, eth_getCode, eth_getBalance, eth_estimateGas and eth_getBlockByNumber at blocks 32558000 to 32563302 on 2026-11-22 (eth_estimateGas returned 22,559,525 gas for a creation writing 999 new slots, a creation writing 1,000 slots reverts, and five sampled blocks report a base fee of 1,000,000 wei)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/megaeth-labs/mega-evm/main/crates/mega-evm/src/block/executor.rs (byte identical to v1.7.4; a rejected candidate leaves executor state untouched and goes back to the caller)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/megaeth-labs/mega-evm/main/crates/mega-evm/src/block/chain.rs (mainnet schedule MiniRex to Rex6 (timestamp 1787626800), nothing later)
- https://github.com/megaeth-labs/mega-evm/releases (v1.7.3 removed REX7 (pull request 402); v1.7.4 of 24 November changes no execution file)
- https://mainnet.megaeth.com/rpc (eth_getBlockByNumber latest on 2026-11-25: block 32,821,732, baseFeePerGas 0xf4240, gasLimit 10,000,000,000; web3_clientVersion mega-reth/v2.3.1-4499c42@mnet-ash-rpc-2)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/megaeth-labs/mega-evm/v1.7.4/crates/mega-evm/src/block/limit.rs (byte identical to main)
- https://docs.megaeth.com/spec/megaevm/resource-limits (skip rule for later candidates, no receipt, may be reconsidered in a later block, both state growth limits 1,000)

## F-044. A HyperCore transfer makes the node call a linked contract with 200,000 gas ahead of every signed transaction in the block

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: HyperEVM.

What differs on the network: HyperCore credits a spot transfer to the EVM through a system transaction that calls transfer(recipient, amount) on the contract linked to the token, with the token's system address as sender, gas price 0 and 200,000 gas. The docs state that the linked contract may have arbitrary bytecode and give no guarantee about what its transfer function does. Deploying and linking a spot token is open to anyone; the deploy auction stood at 500 on 2026-11-22. System transactions are placed before all signed transactions of the block and their gas does not count toward the block gas limit. On Ethereum only the block builder decides which code runs first.

How it meets the code: An account deploys a spot token on HyperCore, links it to a contract it wrote and sends the token to the token's system address. The node then calls that contract's transfer function at the top of an EVM block, before any signed transaction. Inside the call the contract is an ordinary caller of the PoolManager. It can call unlock, which calls unlockCallback on it (src/PoolManager.sol:104-114), swap, and settle with tokens or ERC-6909 claims it holds (src/PoolManager.sol:187-227 and 322-336). The PoolManager gives the system address and the linked contract no role, and transient state is cleared after the system transaction like after any other, so nothing carries into the signed transactions. What the account gains is position: it reaches every pool before transactions that paid a priority fee, for the price of one HyperCore action.
