## F-139. Under Jenner BNB Chain routes two CAS20 token ranges and three fixed addresses to native code, an address there with no token reverts every call, and no pool can hold funds in such a currency

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: BNB Smart Chain.

What differs on the network: From 2026-12-08 02:30 UTC (JennerTime 1796697000 in params/config.go line 256 at v1.8.2) BNB Smart Chain routes an address to native CAS20 code when bytes 0 and 1 are 0xCA52, bytes 2 to 9 are zero and byte 10 is 0x00 (Asset) or 0x01 (Stablecoin), and also routes the factory 0xCA5F00..00 and the registries 0x7020..01 and 0x7020..02. A token address whose code is not the one byte marker 0xEF reverts every call. Addresses in the same space with byte 10 from 0x02 to 0xff are ordinary accounts. The time precompile 0x70 answers any call with the block time in milliseconds. Ethereum has none of these. The cycle 19 text of this finding said every address in the CAS20 space reverts; that holds only for the two ordinals and the fixed addresses.

How it meets the code: core/vm/evm.go lines 46-59 (v1.8.2) first consults the static precompile map and, when IsJenner holds, calls resolveCAS20. resolveCAS20 (core/vm/cas20.go lines 122-135) returns the three singletons by exact address and otherwise, for an address that passes IsCAS20Address (bytes 2 to 9 zero, lines 50-60), looks up cas20Variants[addr[10]] (lines 107-119), which has entries only for 0x00 and 0x01. For any other ordinal the lookup returns ok = false and EVM.Call treats the address as an ordinary account (evm.go lines 318-360). For a routed token address runCAS20Token reverts unless the code hash equals keccak256(0xEF) (cas20.go lines 62-70 and 189-200; cas20_factory.go line 35). The cycle 19 probe that F-139 and lead L-907 rest on called 0xca52...0001, 0xca52...0000 and 0xca52...00ff, and all three have byte 10 equal to 0x00, so the reverts it saw for 'byte 10 = 0x02 or 0xff' were in fact ordinal 0x00 addresses. This cycle called byte 10 in {0x00, 0x01, 0x02, 0xff} on four endpoints with web3_clientVersion before and after each batch: ordinals 0x00 and 0x01 revert under the block time override 0x6b176e00 and ordinals 0x02 and 0xff return empty success, as the tag says, on Geth v1.8.2-4e1c8247 (dataseed and dataseed1), Geth v1.8.2-9b93ce23 and v1.8.2-132104cd (48 Club answered both builds in one batch) and reth-bsc v0.1.4-hotfix-23b510f on every cell it answered. For the PoolManager the effect is the one F-139 describes: for a routed address X with no token, take(X, to, 0) succeeds and moves nothing today and reverts after the fork (src/types/Currency.sol:64-86); sync(X) reverts before and after because the high level balanceOf call needs 32 bytes of return data (src/types/Currency.sol:90-96, src/PoolManager.sol:279-288). For an unrouted ordinal nothing changes at the fork: take succeeds and moves nothing, sync reverts. With no sync there is no settle credit (src/PoolManager.sol:349-364), so no delta, claim or liquidity in such an X can exist.

What is at risk: None. No pool can be funded in a CAS20 space currency that has no token, before or after the fork, and the fork changes nothing for the unrouted ordinals.

What it takes: Jenner activates as scheduled. Token creation stays closed until a governance appointed administrator enables it (F-076). A token address can only become a token through the factory, because the marker code 0xEF cannot be deployed by CREATE or CREATE2 (EIP-3541) and bytes 0 to 9 fix 80 bits of the address.

What can be done: None needed. Integrators that cache pools by currency should not assume that a routed address which answered as an empty account before the fork keeps doing so.

This entry adds to F-076.

Code: `src/types/Currency.sol:64-86`, `src/types/Currency.sol:90-96`, `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`.

Sources:

- https://github.com/bnb-chain/bsc/releases (v1.8.2 schedules Jenner at 1796697000 with BEP-702 CAS20 precompiles, BEP-703 and the 0x70 precompile of BEP-706)
- rpc: eth_call with blockOverrides time 0x6b176e00 on bsc-dataseed1.bnbchain.org, rpc-bsc.48.club and bsc-rpc.publicnode.com, 2026-11-25, research/network-risk/cycles/019/established-l1.work/raw/cas20_probe.json (CAS20-range addresses revert every call shape under Jenner rules and answer empty success today; 0x70 returns 0x1a25385b000)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bnb-chain/bsc/v1.8.2/core/vm/cas20.go (IsCAS20Address checks bytes 0-1 = 0xCA52 and bytes 2-9 zero (lines 50-60); resolveCAS20Token indexes cas20Variants by byte 10 with entries only for 0x00 and 0x01 (lines 107-119); resolveCAS20 adds the three singletons (lines 122-135); runCAS20Token reverts unless the code hash is that of the marker (lines 62-70, 189-200); DisabledPrecompile makes dispatch treat the address as an ordinary account (lines 38-48))
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bnb-chain/bsc/v1.8.2/core/vm/evm.go (EVM.precompile consults the map, returns not-a-precompile for DisabledPrecompile, and routes CAS20 only when IsJenner holds (lines 46-67); EVM.Call runs a precompile or the account's code (lines 299-360))
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bnb-chain/bsc/v1.8.2/core/vm/cas20_factory.go (CAS20MarkerCode is the single byte 0xEF (line 35) and the factory writes it at token creation (lines 161-164))
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bnb-chain/bsc/v1.8.2/params/config.go (BSC mainnet JennerTime 1796697000, 2026-12-08 02:30 UTC (line 256))
- rpc: eth_call and eth_estimateGas from the PoolManager to CAS20 space addresses with byte 10 in {0x00,0x01,0x02,0xff}, singletons and 0x70, with and without blockOverrides time 0x6b176e00 and with and without a code override, on bsc-dataseed1.bnbchain.org, bsc-dataseed.bnbchain.org, rpc-bsc.48.club and bsc-rpc.publicnode.com, 2026-11-25, research/network-risk/cycles/020/theme-reserved-address-routing.work/raw/cas20_probe.json and raw/cas20_reth_retry.json (ordinals 0x00 and 0x01 and the singletons revert under the override, ordinals 0x02 and 0xff answer empty success with gas 21000, all four builds agree on every answered cell; publicnode refused part of the calls with a Cloudflare challenge (HTTP 403), recorded as refusals)
- rpc: eth_call on https://bsc-dataseed1.bnbchain.org, 2026-11-25, from 0x28e2ea090877bf75740558f6bfb36a5ffee9e9df, balanceOf calldata, blockOverrides time 0x6b176e00 and none (byte 10 = 0x00, 0x01 and the factory revert under the override; byte 10 = 0x02 and 0xff return empty success; all return empty success without the override)

## F-144. Celo pays the maximum gas refund to a CIP-64 transaction whose main call refills a fee-currency slot that the debit cleared

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Celo.

What differs on the network: On Ethereum the refund counter cannot be negative when a transaction ends. On Celo, the CIP-64 debit can clear a slot before the main call runs. The main call prices its SSTOREs against values from before the debit, so refilling that slot takes back a clearing refund that was never counted. The counter ends negative. celo-reth v1.0.10 passes it unchanged to set_final_refund, which casts it to u64 and caps it at one fifth of gas used. The celo-kona main branch clamps the counter to zero only from Karst on.

How it meets the code: A sender pays fees in a fee currency, and the debit takes its balance of that currency to exactly zero. The sender's main call then credits the sender's balance slot in the same currency again, for example through a transfer to itself from another holder or a take of that currency from the PoolManager. That SSTORE carries the -4,800 take-back, and the transaction ends with a negative counter. The handler's refund step on v1.0.10 does nothing to the counter (handler.rs:1213-1215 at the tag), and revm then grants a refund of gas_used / 5. The refund is applied after execution is complete. It changes no opcode result and no return data. Gas readings inside the main call do differ from Ethereum, because SSTOREs on slots the debit wrote are priced as already written (verifier's correction); the PoolManager forwards all gas and never branches on a GAS value (src/types/Currency.sol:48 and 73, src/libraries/Hooks.sol:134). So no line of src/ computes or decides anything differently, and the PoolManager's balances and deltas are the same as they would be on Ethereum.

What is at risk: Only the fee: the sender pays up to 20 percent less gas, and the base fee and tip recipients receive that much less. No PoolManager funds, LP positions or ERC-6909 claims are affected.

What it takes: The sender pays in a registered fee currency, the debit empties the sender's balance slot, and the main call refills it. The fork is before Karst, which is the case on Celo today.

What can be done: None needed for the PoolManager. Celo's clamp at Karst removes it.

Second reviewer's corrections: The mechanism says the CIP-64 path changes no GAS reading inside the call. That holds for the refund wrap. The celo-kona main comment at handler.rs lines 1295-1297 also says that main-call SSTOREs on slots the debit wrote stay dirty-priced, so the main call can be over- or undercharged by up to about 2,800 gas per such slot, which does shift GAS readings inside execution. src/ does not branch on remaining gas, so the conclusion stands. The actor value unprivileged is correct and severity info is correct.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:104-114`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/celo-org/celo-kona/refs/tags/celo/reth-v1.0.10/crates/celo-revm/src/handler.rs (v1.0.10 refund() only forwards to the OP handler, with no clamp (lines 1213-1215))
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/celo-org/celo-kona/main/crates/celo-revm/src/handler.rs (main clamps a negative counter to zero from Karst; its comment and tests describe the wrap to the cap before Karst)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/celo-org/revm/bd28f9420c250df2a5c4a6d8103b51ae06b5c222/crates/interpreter/src/gas.rs (set_final_refund casts refunded() to u64 and takes min with gas_used / 5 (lines 199-205))

## F-149. A transaction that failed validation in Base's block builder halted every exit on Base for 116 minutes in June 2026, and the fault is fixed in every builder and node handler read

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: Base.

What differs on the network: Ethereum clients drop the journal of a transaction that fails validation. Base's builder kept it, so the warm accounts and slots of the excluded transaction leaked into the next one.

How it meets the code: On 25 June 2026 an invalid transaction reached the builder of block 47806543 and failed validation. The builder did not clear the journal, the next valid transaction ran against the dirty access set and was charged different gas, and the receipts root of the sealed block differed from what validators computed. Validators rejected the block and the chain stopped at 47806542 for 116 minutes, and for 20 minutes on 26 June. Any account can send a transaction that fails validation in the builder; the PR's tests use a sender with code and a stale nonce. Because validation fails before any frame runs, no TSTORE of the failed transaction existed, so the PoolManager's lock, deltas and synced currency (src/libraries/Lock.sol, src/libraries/CurrencyDelta.sol, src/libraries/CurrencyReserves.sol) could not have been carried into the next transaction. Had a leak reached state, validators would have rejected that block too. The fix adds discard_tx to the non-deposit branch of catch_error (base/base PR 3806), present in v1.5.6 at handler.rs:385-387 and in v1.6.0-rc.3; op-revm, which the other six chains and op-rbuilder use, discards the journal on both the transaction error and other error paths, and revm's inspector run path calls the same catch_error.

What is at risk: While halted no PoolManager exit on Base could be included, so every liquidity provider and claim holder on Base was frozen for the duration. No funds were lost and no wrong state was accepted.

What it takes: A builder whose error path keeps the journal. None of the builds read today has that fault.

What can be done: None needed in core. Deployers on single-sequencer chains should expect temporary halts triggered by public transactions and keep withdrawals free of deadlines shorter than such halts.

Second reviewer's corrections: The postmortem describes the bad transaction as failing during execution; that no frame ran is inferred from PR 3806's tests, not stated by Base. The actor is right for the historical event: a stale nonce transaction is something any account can send.

Code: `src/libraries/Lock.sol:13-25`, `src/PoolManager.sol:104-114`.

Sources:

- https://www.base.org/blog/postmortem-june-25th-block-production-outage.md (root cause: stale journal after a transaction validation failure; block 47806543 rejected by validators; 116 and 20 minute halts)
- https://patch-diff.githubusercontent.com/raw/base/base/pull/3806.diff (the fix adds discard_tx in catch_error and tests EIP-3607 and nonce failures)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/base/base/v1.5.6/crates/common/evm/src/handler.rs (the running build carries the fix at lines 385-387)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum-optimism/optimism/85f7c13d7470a66254081f0177fc297201c07459/rust/op-revm/src/handler.rs (the op-revm revision op-rbuilder pins discards the journal on every non-deposit error)

## F-151. X Layer runs PoolManager swaps and liquidity changes at zero gas price through two whitelisted shared carriers

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: any account. Networks: X Layer.

What differs on the network: X Layer waives the fee of a zero-priced transaction when its gasless whitelist allows the transaction's target and calldata prefix and the gas limit is within the allowance. The whitelist allows EntryPoint v0.7 for any non-empty calldata up to 30,000,000 gas and the contract at 0x7c5bee2a for any non-empty calldata up to 50,000,000 gas. Ethereum has no such rule.

How it meets the code: The executor queries getGaslessAllowance(to, input) before each transaction whose max fee per gas is zero and marks it gasless when the answer is true and the gas limit fits; it then executes the transaction normally with only the base fee check relaxed. An ordinary account can submit handleOps to EntryPoint v0.7 carrying its own user operation with zero fees, or a call to 0x7c5bee2a, and so reach the PoolManager without paying gas. In three thousand recent blocks 101 zero-priced transactions from more than 80 senders emitted PoolManager logs. The PoolManager reads neither GASPRICE nor BASEFEE (no use in src/), so every amount it computes and every settlement check in unlock (src/PoolManager.sol:104-114) is the same as in a paid transaction. The effect is cost only: dust tick filling of F-038 and other spam against pools no longer costs gas.

What is at risk: No funds move and nobody is left unable to withdraw. Griefing that F-038 records loses its gas cost on X Layer; the dust payment it needs still applies.

What it takes: Whitelist entries as read on 2026-11-26 and a sequencer that admits zero-priced transactions from any sender. The per block gasless budget and any per sender limit of the sequencer were not read, and no transaction was sent to test admission.

What can be done: None needed for funds. Pool creators who rely on gas cost as a deterrent against tick filling should not do so on X Layer.

Second reviewer's corrections: The client passes the full transaction input to getGaslessAllowance (encode_get_gasless_allowance copies all of tx.input), so "prefix" means the whole calldata. "Any non-empty calldata" is shown only for the prefixes tested. Whether 0x7c5bee2a (13,322 bytes, has an owner function; one sender is an EIP-7702 account delegated to 0xe40ccb2d) accepts calls from any account was not read; the EntryPoint v0.7 route alone carries the claim. Sequencer admission policy, the per block gasless budget and per sender limits were not read, as the finding states. The finding supersedes the cycle 11 cleared item that said no gasless path reaches the PoolManager, which tested direct targets only; that item did not concern funds.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:104-114`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:166-171`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/okx/optimism/refs/tags/xlayer/v0.1.7/rust/alloy-op-evm/src/block/xlayer_gasless_contract.rs (is_gasless requires allowed == true and tx.gas_limit <= returned gasLimit; mainnet whitelist at 0x19787404b0c70021b4752028f7e3a92313885B27)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/okx/optimism/refs/tags/xlayer/v0.1.7/rust/alloy-op-evm/src/lib.rs (a gasless transaction only sets disable_base_fee for its own execution)
- https://rpc.xlayer.tech (eth_call getGaslessAllowance, out/gasless.json) (EntryPoint v0.7 allowed with 30,000,000 gas and 0x7c5bee2a with 50,000,000 gas for any non-empty prefix; PoolManager and periphery not allowed)
- https://rpc.xlayer.tech (receipts of blocks 76,927,161-76,930,160, out/gasless_scan.json) (101 mined zero-priced transactions emitted PoolManager logs, for example 0x14ba5174ce2bf8b682ac926064813eee4a9087bb449346b1500f91659056339d and 0xda396c963d30a6f9774ed5dad69e64ff0b5204432331d4accc2ed88462b42ad8)

## F-003. A Stylus program used as a hook or token stops answering calls on expiry or a Stylus version change, and activation is blocked

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: Arbitrum One.

What differs on the network: On Ethereum, code at an address keeps answering calls. On Arbitrum One a contract can be a Stylus WASM program. A call to it fails unless the program was activated at the current Stylus version and its last activation or keepalive is less than 365 days old. ArbOS 61, active since 2026-08-20, moved the Stylus version from 2 to 3. On chain, ArbWasm.activationGas() is 2^64-1, which makes activateProgram fail for everyone, so a program that has expired or needs the new version cannot be brought back by its users.

How it meets the code: Hook path: the PoolManager calls the hook with call() and reverts the whole action when the call fails. For a hook address with the before or after remove liquidity flag, modifyLiquidity with a negative delta calls the hook, so a hook that no longer answers blocks every liquidity removal in its pools. Hooks with swap or donate flags block those actions the same way. Token path: transfer and balanceOf on a Stylus token that no longer answers revert, so take, sync and settle revert for that currency in every pool. Step 1: a pool is created with a Stylus hook or a Stylus token while the program is active. Step 2: the program reaches 365 days without a keepalive, or an ArbOS upgrade changes the Stylus version. getActiveProgram returns ProgramExpired or ProgramNeedsUpgrade and the call fails. Step 3: anyone who tries activateProgram is charged activationGas first, which exceeds any gas limit, so the call fails. Keepalive works only while the program is still active and at least 31 days old. ArbOS 61 also removed WASM multi-value support, so a program that relies on it cannot be reactivated even when activation reopens. I did not enumerate which pools on Arbitrum One use a Stylus hook or token, and I did not establish the date on which activation gas was raised, so I cannot say how many programs missed the move to version 3.

What is at risk: LP principal and fees in pools whose hook gates liquidity removal are stuck for as long as activation stays closed. For a Stylus token, every balance of that token in the PoolManager is stuck. No funds move to an attacker. Pools without Stylus hooks or tokens are unaffected.

What it takes: A pool must use a Stylus program as its hook or as one of its currencies. Then either nobody pays for keepalive within the 365 day window, or governance ships a Stylus version change. No attacker action is required and none can speed it up. The Arbitrum Foundation or Security Council can reopen activation.

What can be done: Pool creators on Arbitrum One should prefer EVM hooks. LPs in a pool with a Stylus hook should check ArbWasm.programVersion and programTimeLeft for the hook and for each token, and anyone can pay codehashKeepalive once the program is 31 days old. A hook author can leave the remove liquidity flags unset so that removals never depend on the hook.

Second reviewer's corrections: The mechanism and the chain facts hold. The scope needs two changes. First, no pool on Arbitrum One has a Stylus program as its direct hook or currency today, so no funds are frozen through the direct path at present. Indirect use through an EVM proxy or an EVM hook that calls a Stylus program was not checked. Second, the date the finding could not establish is 2026-10-02, 43 days after the version change. The same mechanism reaches Robinhood Chain. Its ArbWasm precompile emitted 26 ProgramActivated events between 2026-07-25 and 2026-10-02, all at version 3, and activation gas there is also 2^64-1. Those programs expire from 2027-07-25 unless someone pays keepalive, and a Stylus version change would stop them at once. On Robinhood Chain the PoolManager emitted 1,169,661 Initialize events up to block 120.6M. 411,764 of them name a hook, across 32,935 distinct hook addresses and 745,518 distinct currency addresses. None of the 26 activated program addresses is a hook or a currency of any pool. I read the code of the 1,500 most used hook addresses, which cover 377,624 of the hooked pools, and none starts with 0xeff0. I did not read the code of the remaining hooks or of the currencies, so a contract that shares its code hash with an activated program is not excluded there. The severity stays medium because the freeze is limited to pools that depend on a Stylus program and needs either a missed keepalive or a governance upgrade.

Code: `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-137`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:195-206`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:209-245`, `src/PoolManager.sol:145-184`, `src/types/Currency.sol:55-87`, `src/types/Currency.sol:90-96`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OffchainLabs/nitro/v3.11.3/arbos/programs/programs.go (CallProgram calls getActiveProgram, which returns an error when the program version differs from the Stylus version or the program is older than ExpiryDays. ProgramKeepalive requires an active program at least KeepaliveDays old.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OffchainLabs/nitro/v3.11.3/arbos/arbosState/arbosstate.go (The ArbOS 59 upgrade step calls UpgradeToVersion(3) on the Stylus parameters.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OffchainLabs/nitro/v3.11.3/precompiles/ArbWasm.go (ActivateProgram burns the configurable activation gas before activation. CodehashKeepalive does not.)
- https://arb1.arbitrum.io/rpc eth_call ArbWasm.activationGas(), stylusVersion(), expiryDays(), keepaliveDays() at 0x71; queried 2026-11-22 (activationGas is 0xffffffffffffffff, the Stylus version is 3, expiry is 365 days and keepalive needs 31 days.)
- https://docs.arbitrum.io/notices/stylus-activation-pause-notice (New activations and reactivation of expired programs are paused on Arbitrum One. Active programs run until they expire and can be renewed with keepalive.)
- https://docs.arbitrum.io/run-a-node/arbos-releases/arbos61 (ArbOS 61 activated on Arbitrum One on 2026-08-20 and programs that rely on WASM multi-value can no longer be activated or reactivated.)
- https://docs.arbitrum.io/stylus/gentle-introduction (Stylus contracts must be reactivated once per 365 days or after any Stylus upgrade, and otherwise become uncallable.)
- https://forum.arbitrum.foundation/t/constitutional-aip-stylus-activation-contract/31605 (The proposal to reopen activation through an Arbitrum Foundation process was put on hold on 2026-11-18, and a later ArbOS proposal plans the ability to deactivate a Stylus contract.)

## F-004. A pool's hook can block or reprice every liquidity removal in that pool, and core has no path that skips the hook

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: all 21 networks.

What differs on the network: None. The code path is the same on every network. Networks where the code behind an address can change after pool creation (A7) add ways for a hook that was harmless at creation to start doing this.

How it meets the code: modifyLiquidity calls the hook library's beforeModifyLiquidity before it touches the pool (src/PoolManager.sol:156). For liquidityDelta <= 0 the library calls beforeRemoveLiquidity when address bit 9 is set (src/libraries/Hooks.sol:203-204). callHook reverts the whole call when the hook reverts (Hooks.sol:134-137) and when the return data is shorter than 32 bytes or carries the wrong selector (Hooks.sol:152-154). A hook address with no code returns empty data, so it also reverts. After the pool update, afterModifyLiquidity calls afterRemoveLiquidity when bit 8 is set, and when bit 0 is also set it reads a BalanceDelta from the return data, subtracts it from the caller's delta without any bound and credits it to the hook (Hooks.sol:232-243, src/PoolManager.sol:177-183). The hook call is skipped only when msg.sender is the hook itself (Hooks.sol:171-175, 217). No other function removes liquidity. The swap path has the same shape: beforeSwap and afterSwap deltas move value between swapper and hook (Hooks.sol:248-315), and the hook of a dynamic fee pool can set the LP fee to 100 percent (src/PoolManager.sol:339-346, src/libraries/LPFeeLibrary.sol:25, 37-45). Cycle 21 addition: Ethereum has a live case. Pool 0x462175dbedc82a59909309d8d93c8ff5283fc3ce41e9dcbea28aea89709ab870 (ETH and tCURV 0x0b5702a3000a3ae3e80c3dfd6be547c4325f7eb9, fee 100, tick spacing 1) names the hook 0xe16af6e33266e572bbcadb288bf3893942cd8220, which has no code, nonce 0 and balance 0 at block 26,495,270 and sets bits 9 (before remove liquidity) and 5 (before donate). The pool was initialized at block 25,072,940 in the transaction that also minted PositionManager token 265,123 over the full range with liquidity 150,557,992,383,766,460,021. A read-only eth_call of modifyLiquidities with a full decrease from the token owner reverts with 0x1e048e1d (InvalidHookResponse) on two endpoints, because the call to the empty address returns no data and Hooks.sol:153 needs 32 bytes. The test at Hooks.sol:202 is liquidityDelta <= 0, so the owner cannot collect fees through a zero change either, and donate to this pool reverts the same way. A control position (token 292,697) in a pool whose empty hook sets only the after swap flag passes the decrease. The position holds about 0.0054 ETH and 4,199,619 tCURV. Its owner created the pool and chose the hook, so no other account is affected today. The case was missed before because the cycle 17 hook census read transaction hashes as pool ids. This behaves the same on every network where an empty address answers a call with success. Cycle 22 addition: a census of every Initialize row on all 21 networks, with pool ids recomputed from the pool keys, found hook addresses without code on 12 networks. The call to an address without code returned success with no data in all 199 test calls on the 21 networks, so the freeze has the same shape everywhere. One address stands out. 0xf8c3f4243c4d8c02831a718a7978fd869f094300 (bits 9 and 8) was deployed through the CREATE2 deployer 0x4e59b44847b379578588920ca78fbf26c0b4956c and has 2,488 bytes of code on Ethereum, OP Mainnet, Base, Unichain and Arbitrum One. It has no code on Polygon, BNB Smart Chain, World Chain and Avalanche, where pool creators named it anyway. On Polygon, pools 0x4accc6ab…4116 and 0xc5a3738f…a184 hold positions of accounts other than the creator (PositionManager tokens 58461 and 69169, about 0.019 POL and 0.0015 USDC together). On Avalanche, pools 0x07ec041c…4f30 and 0x3752b3c6…4d8f hold six such positions (about 0.045 AVAX and 0.32 USDt together). A full removal through the PositionManager reverts with 0x1e048e1d (InvalidHookResponse) for each of them. The same call passes when a stub that returns its selector is placed at the hook address by state override, so the missing code is the only obstacle. The verifier repeated the code, storage and call readings and confirmed them. It corrected three points. The donate flags (bits 5 and 4) freeze no position, so the two Robinhood Chain pools on 0x…dead0030 hold no frozen principal. The Ethereum pool 0x992c0ab6 is on a delegated hook that has code and belongs to F-013. The difference between networks is one of deployed state, with no difference in how a call to an empty address behaves. The owners in the Polygon and Avalanche pools also create pools on the same hook, so they may be one operator; that link was not established.

What is at risk: Principal and uncollected fees of every position in the affected pool. A reverting hook freezes them. A hook with the return delta bit can take part or all of what a removal would pay out, up to what the LP's router accepts. Funds in other pools are not reachable this way, because the caller delta plus the hook delta always equals the pool delta (Hooks.sol:230, 242, 312) and each party must net to zero before unlock returns (src/PoolManager.sol:112).

What it takes: The pool's hook address has bit 9 (0x200) or bit 8 (0x100) set, and for taking funds also bit 0. The hook code reverts, returns a bad selector, returns a hostile delta, or is replaced or removed after LPs deposit. LPs must have chosen that pool. This is how the hook design works; I list it because it is a path to a freeze or loss that the text of core does not limit.

What can be done: LPs and position managers can read the low 14 bits of the hook address before depositing and treat bits 9, 8 and 0 as custody risk unless the hook code is immutable and reviewed. Routers should enforce minimum amounts on removal so a hostile return delta reverts. The deployer cannot change this without adding a removal path that skips hooks.

Second reviewer's corrections: The difference field presents changing code as a property of some networks. EIP-7702 delegation and upgradeable proxy hooks make it possible on every network that has activated EIP-7702, Ethereum included, so the exposure does not depend on the network. The freeze case needs nothing from the LP. The taking case needs the LP's router to accept the reduced amount, as the finding says. The add liquidity path has the same shape through bits 11, 10 and 1, which can block or surcharge deposits and cannot hold existing funds. Severity stays medium because the loss is limited to pools whose LPs chose that hook address, and the tests show this is the intended trust model.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:145-184`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-155`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:171-175`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:195-245`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:248-315`, `src/PoolManager.sol:339-346`.

## F-005. Uncollected fees above the int128 maximum make every later update of a liquidity position revert

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: all 21 networks.

What differs on the network: None. The condition is the same on every network. It needs a token whose supply in base units is larger than 2^127 - 1, about 1.7e38. The comment at src/libraries/Pool.sol:403 says the code assumes token supply stays within uint128.

How it meets the code: Every modifyLiquidity call, including a zero delta poke and a removal, runs Position.update and then casts both fee amounts with feesOwed0.toInt128() and feesOwed1.toInt128() (src/libraries/Pool.sol:184-192). Position.update computes the fee as (feeGrowthInside - feeGrowthInsideLast) * liquidity / 2^128 for the whole position (src/libraries/Position.sol:92-97). SafeCast.toInt128(uint256) reverts for values of 2^127 or more (src/libraries/SafeCast.sol:56-59). Because the whole call reverts, the position's fee checkpoint is never updated. The amount owed therefore never goes down, and every later call for that position reverts the same way, whatever liquidityDelta is used. Fees reach a position through swaps (Pool.sol:401-407) and through donate (Pool.sol:466-480). Each single donate or swap is capped at int128, so the threshold is crossed over two or more calls. I confirmed the arithmetic with a Python transcription of Pool, Position and SafeCast: after two donations of 2^126 + 10 units to a range with one position, updates with delta 0, -1, -1e18 and +1 all revert.

What is at risk: The principal in both currencies and the uncollected fees of that one position stay in the PoolManager with no way out. Other positions in the pool and other pools are not affected.

What it takes: One position must be owed 2^127 base units or more of one currency in fees that it has not yet collected. Fees owed are real tokens paid by swappers or donors, so the token's supply in base units must exceed 1.7e38 and a payer must give up that amount. For a token with a supply below that bound the state cannot be reached. A party that controls such a token's supply can already drain the other side of the pool by selling, so this adds a way to freeze but no extra profit.

What can be done: LPs can avoid pools with a token whose base unit supply exceeds 2^127, and can collect fees with a zero delta call before the amount owed gets near the bound. Pool creators can check totalSupply and decimals. A code change that caps or splits the fee credit would remove the condition.

Second reviewer's corrections: Severity raised from low to medium. The scale reserves low for hazards with no direct loss path, and this is a direct and permanent freeze of a position, limited to pools with a token whose base unit supply exceeds 2^127 - 1. The actor is any holder of 2^127 base units who gives them up as a donation or as swap fees, or ordinary swap volume against a passive position that never collects. The bound is 2^127, half of the uint128 supply that the comment at src/libraries/Pool.sol:403 names as the assumption, so a token inside the stated assumption with a supply between 2^127 and 2^128 can still reach it. The finding's point that an issuer with unlimited supply can already empty the pool by selling holds, and it does not cover a third party token with a large fixed supply, where the attacker gains nothing and the LP still loses access to both currencies of the position.

Code: `src/libraries/Pool.sol:184-192`, `src/libraries/Position.sol:76-102`, `src/libraries/SafeCast.sol:53-59`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:466-480`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:401-407`.

Sources:

- src/libraries/Pool.sol:192 (both fee amounts are cast to int128 on every position update)
- src/libraries/Position.sol:92-97 (fees owed are computed for the full position liquidity and cannot be collected in parts)
- src/libraries/SafeCast.sol:56-59 (toInt128(uint256) reverts at 2^127)
- src/libraries/Pool.sol:403 (the code comment states the assumption that token supply does not exceed type(uint128).max)

## F-013. EIP-7702 lets the code behind a hook address be replaced or removed after a pool is created

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: Ethereum, Base, Arbitrum One, Robinhood Chain, BNB Smart Chain.

What differs on the network: Since Pectra (EIP-7702) an EOA can point its code at any contract through a delegation indicator, repoint it in a later transaction, or clear it. Since Dencun (EIP-6780) an ordinary contract cannot delete its code after the creation transaction. On mainnet today a delegated EOA is therefore the one kind of account whose code can change or vanish at a fixed address without a proxy pattern in its bytecode.

How it meets the code: initialize accepts a hook address on its low 14 bits alone and never looks at code (src/PoolManager.sol:126, src/libraries/Hooks.sol:109-127). An EOA whose address ends in chosen flag bits is cheap to find because only 14 bits must match. While the EOA delegates to honest hook code the pool behaves normally. If the account holder clears the delegation, a call to the hook succeeds with empty return data, and callHook reverts with InvalidHookResponse because the result is shorter than 32 bytes (src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-155). With the before or after remove liquidity flag set, every liquidity removal by anyone other than the hook address then reverts (src/PoolManager.sol:156, 178; src/libraries/Hooks.sol:195-206, 232-243), so LPs cannot withdraw until the holder delegates again. If the holder repoints the delegation to hostile code and the address carries a returns-delta flag, the new code can assign itself part of each withdrawal or swap, because the hook delta is credited to the hook and subtracted from the caller (src/libraries/Hooks.sol:233-243, 298-313; src/PoolManager.sol:181-183, 224-226). The hook address is skipped when it is itself the caller (src/libraries/Hooks.sol:171-175, 217, 253, 293), and a delegated EOA can originate transactions. The holder can therefore delegate to code that answers the unlock callback for its own calls and returns invalid data to hook calls made for everyone else. Cycle 22 addition: the census found seven hook addresses that are delegated accounts, live on Ethereum, Base, Arbitrum One, Robinhood Chain and BNB Smart Chain. The same hook address points at different targets on different networks and the accounts have nonces up to 11, so their holders have set and changed the delegation per network. Fifteen pools with liquidity sit on these hooks, all created by one account (0xec08eff7). Two of them, both on Ethereum, hold a position of another account. Pool 0x992c0ab6…cf9c (hook 0x52bf1a80…258b, bits 0x258b, which include after remove liquidity and its return delta) holds PositionManager token 517545 of 0x3d4e4f78 with about 0.0035 ETH. Pool 0x08834f72 (hook 0xa8531805, bits 0x2488) holds token 514342 of the same account with about 0.0055 ETH and 0.038 USDC; its hook sets no removal flag, so that position is exposed only through the add and swap callbacks. The verifier confirmed the code, nonce, liquidity and owner readings and corrected one statement: the hook's share of a removal is bounded by what the remover's own transaction accepts, because the subtraction is checked and a router with a minimum output reverts first.

What is at risk: Funds of LPs and swappers in pools that use such a hook. Other pools are not affected because pool math never reads another pool's state. The same outcome is already possible with an upgradeable proxy hook; the change is that inspecting the code at the hook address at one point in time no longer shows it.

What it takes: The attacker creates the pool with a delegated EOA as hook and LPs deposit into it. Cycle 22 scanned all 21 networks for such pools; the result is in the mechanism.

What can be done: Interfaces, routers and LPs can read the code at the hook address and reject hooks whose code is the 23 byte delegation indicator starting with 0xef0100, or that have no code. The deployer could add a code check in initialize, but the same account could delegate afterwards, so the check belongs in periphery and off-chain listing.

Second reviewer's corrections: I raise the severity from low to medium. The finding describes a direct loss and freeze path for funds in pools that use such a hook, which is the medium definition, and it matches the rating of the general hook finding in the core-code-accounting unit, of which this is one variant. The statement that inspecting the code no longer shows the risk is too strong. A delegated account has 23 bytes of code starting with 0xef0100 and an undelegated one has none, and either result is a visible warning for a hook address. The freeze also covers swaps and donations when the matching before or after flags are set, since those calls go through the same callHook.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:126`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:109-127`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-155`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:171-175`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:195-245`, `src/PoolManager.sol:156-183`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum/EIPs/master/EIPS/eip-7702.md (an authority's code is set to 0xef0100 || address, can be changed by a later authorization and cleared by delegating to the zero address; delegated accounts may originate transactions; msg.sender == tx.origin no longer implies an account without code)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum/EIPs/master/EIPS/eip-6780.md (SELFDESTRUCT no longer deletes code except in the transaction that created the contract)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum/EIPs/master/EIPS/eip-7600.md (EIP-7702 is included in Pectra, with mainnet activation timestamp 1746612311)
- https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com (eth_config at block 26470380 reports fork id 0x07c9462e with activation time 1767747671, which EIP-7607 lists as BPO2, a fork later than Pectra)
- rpc: eth_getCode and eth_getTransactionCount of every empty or delegated hook on all 21 networks, file research/network-risk/cycles/022/theme-empty-hook-pools.work/raw/xchain.json (delegation indicators, targets and nonces per network (cycle 22))
- rpc: ModifyLiquidity logs and PositionManager ownerOf, files research/network-risk/cycles/022/theme-empty-hook-pools.work/raw/detail_<chainId>.json (live positions and their owners in pools on delegated hooks (cycle 22))

## F-053. Activated Stylus programs exist on Robinhood Chain as well, and a copy of an activated program runs without a new activation

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: Arbitrum One, Robinhood Chain.

What differs on the network: Nitro chains run WASM contracts next to EVM contracts. A WASM contract answers calls only while its code hash is recorded as activated at the current Stylus version and the record is younger than the expiry period. Ethereum has no contract whose callability ends with time or with a protocol version. Cycle 1 recorded this for Arbitrum One and cleared Robinhood Chain on the ground that activation is closed there, without checking for earlier activations. Robinhood Chain has 26 activated code hashes, and the activation record is per code hash on both chains.

How it meets the code: The client looks a program up under the code hash of the called contract and fails the call when the record is missing, expired or at another Stylus version. Activation costs activationGas, which is 2^64-1 on both chains, so no new code hash can be activated and an expired one cannot be reactivated. A record can be extended with codehashKeepalive once it is 31 days old, by any account. An ordinary account can still deploy a new contract with the bytecode of one of the activated programs, at an address whose low 14 bits form a valid hook flag set, and name it as the hook or a currency of a pool. When the record of that code hash expires after 365 days without a keepalive, or when a later ArbOS release raises the Stylus version, every call to the contract fails. A failed hook call makes callHook revert, so each pool action that reaches the hook reverts, including liquidity removal when the address carries a remove flag. A failed transfer call makes take revert for that currency.

What is at risk: Funds in pools that use a Stylus program as hook or token. Liquidity providers cannot remove liquidity through a hook that no longer answers, and nobody can take a token that no longer answers. Pools without such a hook or token are not affected. On Robinhood Chain the 26 programs were activated between 2026-07-25 and 2026-10-02, so the first records expire in July 2027 unless someone extends them. I did not check whether any of the 26 programs or a copy of them is used by a pool.

What it takes: The pool creator must choose a Stylus program, or a copy of an activated one, as hook or currency, and the victims must use that pool. The freeze then needs the record to lapse or the Stylus version to change, which needs time or a chain upgrade.

What can be done: Liquidity providers should avoid pools whose hook or token is a Stylus program while activation is closed. Interfaces can read ArbWasm.programVersion and programTimeLeft for a hook or token address and warn when the answer is a live program. Anyone can call codehashKeepalive to extend a record that pools depend on.

This entry adds to F-003.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor and the severity stand. The actor is pool-creator because the victims must use a pool whose hook or currency the creator chose. The creator cannot bring about the lapse at will: it needs 365 days without a keepalive, which any account can pay while the record is live, or a Stylus version change by chain governance. Four additions. First, the two Arbitrum documents cover Arbitrum One and Nova. For Robinhood Chain the closed activation rests on the on-chain value of activationGas() and on the date of the last activation, and I found no statement by the chain operator. Second, a copy shares the record of its code hash. One keepalive extends the original and every copy, and a copy deployed late has only the time the original has left. Third, I checked the 26 program addresses on Robinhood Chain. None answers balanceOf(PoolManager) as a token would: 25 revert, and 0xd6cf26df204184fc9c3f2c47c36b04eb631ecf1f returns 64 bytes of hash output for any input and appears in no Initialize event as a currency between block 77,000,000 and the head. The low bits of 16 of the 26 addresses pass isValidHookAddress. The hook is not an indexed field of Initialize, so I did not establish whether a pool uses one of them, a copy, or an EVM contract that forwards to one. Fourth, the finding lists Hooks.sol:194-245 for liquidity changes. The same failure blocks swaps and donations for hooks with those flags, which the mechanism text implies and the references omit.

Code: `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-155`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:194-245`, `src/types/Currency.sol:55-87`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`.

Sources:

- https://rpc.mainnet.chain.robinhood.com eth_getLogs on 0x0000000000000000000000000000000000000071 and eth_call expiryDays(), keepaliveDays(), stylusVersion(), activationGas(), programTimeLeft(address), queried 2026-11-22 (26 ProgramActivated events with 26 distinct code hashes at Stylus version 3 on Robinhood Chain, expiry of 365 days, keepalive age of 31 days, activation gas of 2^64-1)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OffchainLabs/nitro/v3.12.4/arbos/programs/programs.go (CallProgram takes the code hash of the called contract and fails unless getActiveProgram finds a live record for it (lines 206 to 219 and 559 to 580))
- https://docs.arbitrum.io/notices/stylus-activation-pause-notice (new and expired programs cannot be activated while the pause lasts, active programs keep running until they expire and can be renewed with keepalive)
- https://forum.arbitrum.foundation/t/security-council-emergency-action-2-10-2026/31530 (the pause was made by setting the activation gas to 2^64-1 on 2026-10-02)

## F-086. Token contracts have burned or moved the PoolManager's balance of their own token on six networks, and hookless pools with active liquidity now record tokens the PoolManager does not hold

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: Ethereum, BNB Smart Chain, Polygon, Unichain, Elysium, Arbitrum One.

What differs on the network: The cause is token code written by the token's author, on the networks where pool creation is cheapest and most used. These tokens can change balanceOf(PoolManager) without any call from the PoolManager: a transferFrom that skips the allowance for chosen senders, an allowance from the PoolManager that the token writes by itself, a controller contract that rewrites balances, or an owner burn. F-069 recorded tokens whose balance falls with time. These fall in one transaction, to zero in 20 of the 53 confirmed cases on Ethereum.

How it meets the code: 1. The PoolManager can only call transfer and balanceOf on a currency (src/types/Currency.sol:60-73, 94). It never approves anyone. A fall of its balance in a transaction where it emits no log is therefore the token's own doing. 2. Search: every ERC-20 Transfer log with the PoolManager as sender and the zero address, 0x...dEaD, 0xdead000000000000000042069420694206942069 or address 1 as recipient, over the block ranges in the sources. For each token, receipts of up to six such transactions were read, and for tokens with a hit where the PoolManager emitted no log, every hit. Each flagged token was then checked by balanceOf(PoolManager) one block before and at the block of one example. 3. Results. Ethereum: %s rows over %s tokens, %s tokens flagged, %s confirmed by a balance fall and %s that only emit a log (spoof tokens). BNB Smart Chain: %s rows, %s tokens, %s flagged, %s confirmed. Polygon: 5 flagged, 4 confirmed, of which PolySata and SNI have pools and two are spam tokens without a pool. Unichain: 13 flagged, 1 confirmed (ELX), 12 spoof. Elysium: 3 flagged and confirmed (CLU, EMBR, BLOB). Arbitrum One: 5 flagged and confirmed, one of them the issuer burn of PUSD that is filed as its own finding. 4. Forms seen. (a) A helper contract reads balanceOf(PoolManager) and calls transferFrom(PoolManager, 0xdead, amount) on the token; the token logs Approval(PoolManager, helper, 0) afterwards, so it had written an allowance in the PoolManager's name. Ethereum helper 0xd8e11826e82619bf49c58c05f26c8e00b0b64ea4 with selector 0x73ed6b13 did this for %s of the confirmed tokens, and the same selector appears for %s confirmed tokens on BNB Smart Chain (15 of them through 0xa25c886dced0e8fc0129082bf597381ea073c925). (b) An externally owned account calls transferFrom(PoolManager, 0xdead, amount) on the token directly. For CLU on Elysium I read the runtime bytecode: after the balance update the transfer routine loads a flag for tx.origin and, when it is set, returns true at once, which skips the allowance spending in transferFrom. A read-only transferFrom(PoolManager, other, 1000) reverts with ERC20InsufficientAllowance from an unrelated address and succeeds from the original sender 0xd27d278469aa24b5e8ed3af8675d5f6c2548f884. (c) TRUMP MAGA on Ethereum (verified source): transfer() only calls _beforeTokenTransfer, which emits the log and forwards to an unverified controller (lines 505 to 508 and 652), and cap() lets that controller emit any Transfer log (lines 680 to 688). In transaction 0xa6ce9d53504d62a54600498895c8671eb13fb8f312cdbb9d3a4fa345f0d89003 a holder's transfer of 90,100 MAGA made the controller call token function 0x20bd4fca, and the PoolManager's balance went from 776,894.03 MAGA to 0. Nine such events since block 21,818,333 removed 25,231,970 MAGA. (d) Owner functions such as burn(address,uint256) and burnFrom(address,uint256). 5. Effect in core: take pays out with transfer and reverts when the balance is too low (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:60-86). A holder of a positive delta in that token can still close it with mint or clear (src/PoolManager.sol:310-329), so a removal of liquidity can succeed when the caller gives up or defers the token side.

What is at risk: Pools that still have active liquidity while the PoolManager holds none of the token: TRUMP MAGA on Ethereum (balance 0; pools 0x4686f189f970... with liquidity 52,818,877,641,898,785 and 0x03762bf00ac1... with 1,320,363,208,123,135,774, both paired with ETH and without a hook), MEME 0x05f226755b11cb3666340551ebbaa615cdfefdd6 and BITCOIN 0x37d299d9900209c3566254cfe59bfe6ff8f8c295 on Ethereum, ELX 0x6836567f23a137f2ed07576826b0d2579b416b6d on Unichain, and SNI 0xdc685acc53ca2a760029cab2f6a587482aa9cf57 on Polygon with a balance of 1 base unit. For the other confirmed tokens I checked the balance fall and not the pools. Buyers of such a token cannot be paid, and liquidity providers cannot take the token side. Whoever holds the token outside the PoolManager can still sell it into the pool for the other currency. The amounts are in tokens without a price source, so I did not value them. The shortfall stays inside each token's own currency id; pools of other tokens are not affected. The victims chose a pool with a token written by the party that gains.

What it takes: A pool in a token whose author kept a path to the PoolManager's balance. The actor is the token author or an account the token treats as privileged. For form (c) the trigger was an ordinary holder's transfer, and the rule sits in a controller whose code is not public. On BNB Smart Chain the Initialize query failed (limit exceeded), so the number of pools per token is not known there. The Base, World Chain and Robinhood Chain searches cover recent windows only (blocks from 57,500,000, 34,000,000 and 109,000,000) and X Layer 400,000 blocks. Monad, OP Mainnet, Avalanche, Ink, Celo, Arc, Soneium, Zora, MegaETH, Tempo and HyperEVM were searched over the whole history and have no confirmed case. The search sees burns only. A forced transfer to an ordinary recipient shows up only in the census windows of this unit (0.5 to 30 days per network), where none was found.

What can be done: Core cannot stop a token from changing its own ledger. Interfaces and routers can flag a token when balanceOf(PoolManager) falls in a transaction without a PoolManager log, which the scripts scan_burns.py, classify_burns.py and validate_burns.py in the work directory detect. Liquidity providers in such a pool can still recover the other currency with a router that closes the token side by mint or clear. A deployer who wants a hard guarantee needs per currency reserve accounting, which this code does not have.

This entry adds to F-069.

Second reviewer's corrections: Actor pool-creator is right: in every case I checked the balance moved through a path the token's author built (a controller that answers balanceOf, a transferFrom that skips the allowance for one account, a helper with a self-written allowance). The mechanism text was published with unfilled placeholders (%s) in the Ethereum and BNB Smart Chain counts. The values in the researcher's files are: Ethereum 54,048 rows over 1,277 tokens, 78 flagged, 53 with a balance fall and 25 spoof; BNB Smart Chain 29,326 rows over 1,801 tokens, 97 flagged, 91 with a balance fall; helper 0xd8e11826e82619bf49c58c05f26c8e00b0b64ea4 for 12 Ethereum tokens (selector 0x73ed6b13 for 18 through four helpers); selector 0x73ed6b13 for 18 BNB Smart Chain tokens, 15 of them through 0xa25c886dced0e8fc0129082bf597381ea073c925. The MAGA record on Sourcify is a match and not an exact match. For CLU the read-only test sets the caller and the origin to the same address, so it cannot tell whether the flag is read for tx.origin or for msg.sender; the finding's reading of the bytecode on that point is unconfirmed. Two of the four Polygon tokens and one of the five Arbitrum One tokens have no pool, so they show the token behaviour and no pool loss. Who loses is narrower than the text suggests: after such a burn a purchase of the token reverts, holders can still sell into the pool, and the party short of tokens is whoever provided liquidity, which for these tokens is often the author. Medium is the upper bound.

Code: `src/types/Currency.sol:60-86`, `src/types/Currency.sol:90-96`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:310-319`, `src/PoolManager.sol:322-329`.

Sources:

- https://mainnet.gateway.tenderly.co (eth_getLogs with topics Transfer, PoolManager, four burn recipients over blocks 21,688,329 to 26,476,425: 54,048 rows, 1,277 tokens. Receipts, balanceOf(PoolManager) before and at the block for the 78 flagged tokens, Initialize logs per token, StateView.getLiquidity and getSlot0 for the MAGA, MEME and BITCOIN pools.)
- https://eth.drpc.org (debug_traceTransaction for 0xb8a295249493a05eb8b86202111683ad838bbd1a32b970fcfa7629394155d9ef (helper 0xd8e11826... calls balanceOf(PoolManager) and transferFrom(PoolManager, 0xdead, 5,000,000,000) on SPCX) and for 0xa6ce9d53504d62a54600498895c8671eb13fb8f312cdbb9d3a4fa345f0d89003 (MAGA transfer, controller 0xa8b0cdcb011c82ca0b3c981868d78f84f06e0c65 calls token function 0x20bd4fca).)

- https://rpc.elysium.kinetiq.xyz (CLU 0x13c83fd2df0ae8c7b462d344a458275d1f6b5b82: transaction 0x0d470f854fb59f4ad2b4c8bcb8dfcafc55a63bcd3f6083cbaf983209d48ed41d is transferFrom(PoolManager, 0xdead, 30,639,051.89 CLU) sent by an externally owned account; balanceOf(PoolManager) 30,639,996.93 at block 4,589,427 and 945.04 at block 4,589,428; allowance(PoolManager, sender) 0; eth_getCode (1,823 bytes) and the two eth_call tests of transferFrom. EMBR and BLOB went from 113,252,552.87 and 113,247,370.33 to 0 in the same way.)
- https://bsc-mainnet.nodereal.io/v1/64a9df0874fb4a93b9d0a3849de012d3 (eth_getLogs for the burn recipients over blocks 45,369,483 to head on BNB Smart Chain: 29,326 rows over 1,801 tokens.)
- https://bsc-mainnet.public.blastapi.io (balanceOf(PoolManager) before and at the block for the 97 flagged tokens on BNB Smart Chain: 91 fell.)
- https://unichain.gateway.tenderly.co (ELX 0x6836567f23a137f2ed07576826b0d2579b416b6d: balance 2,253,186,633,332 to 0 in transaction 0xe276007551f292449d6db401530312f9fc55143bf84228ece62ca57ffe4f10dc, one hookless pool with liquidity 996,133,074,871 today.)
- research/network-risk/cycles/005/theme-unbacked-claims.work/raw/burns_1_validated.json:1 (Per token rows for Ethereum: balance before and after, sender, called contract, selector, number of pools. The files burns_56, burns_137, burns_130, burns_99901 and burns_42161 hold the other networks.)
- src/types/Currency.sol:60-73 (The only state changing call the PoolManager makes on a currency is transfer(address,uint256).)
- src/PoolManager.sol:310-329 (clear and mint close a positive delta without a token transfer.)

## F-094. The wider census finds more tokens that moved the PoolManager balance themselves: 4 new ones on Ethereum in 14 days and 2 on Robinhood Chain in 5 days, and over the whole history of Elysium only the three known ones

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: Ethereum, Robinhood Chain, Polygon, Elysium.

What differs on the network: These token contracts lower the balance of the PoolManager from inside their own code, in a call that the PoolManager did not make. The main assets of Ethereum pools cannot do that.

How it meets the code: 1. The PoolManager can only call transfer and balanceOf on a currency and never approves anyone (src/types/Currency.sol:60-80, 90-96). A fall of its balance without a transfer call from it is the token acting on its own. 2. The census rule: per transaction and token, the logged outflow plus claims minted minus inflow minus claims burned must not exceed the Swap and Donate amounts of the transaction. Each flagged row was then read with balanceOf one block before and at the block, the Transfer logs of the block and the call trace. A row is in this class when the balance fell as the logs say and the trace holds no transfer call from the PoolManager to the token. 3. Ethereum, blocks 26,377,966 to 26,478,434: 0x2bb84fd8f7ed0ffae3da36ad60d4d7840bdeeada in 3 rows, for example 0x0528ed0ea31f1e4aa0a07c5119834fa408acdff1f7ad3fbfc85880dcf48fa958; 0x1f426c51f0ef7655a6f4c3eb58017d2f1c381bff in 3 rows, for example 0x13d30827134d5d4a798a588ace8b83a192d0ac60828151a8dce7f86301bc846f; 0x741cdf7f730c259fe50bdf8c54e6b197eabd76cc in 3 rows, for example 0x38ed1d1871c3190dd78c371728d864c6bf170635527a9314ad2f2f8c8eaf603c; 0x39b8b6385416f4ca36a20319f70d28621895279d in 2 rows, for example 0x218002fef949f405326bfee360af4acc931a618d43c8010866b6681e925532a5, and one more row of MAGA 0xf211b655431c10e72c1caeae37688ae9f7f7a549, which F-086 already names. 4. Robinhood Chain, blocks 117,163,727 to 121,483,727: 0x79b661c0d27026f9a4266901a0ac88936c0849d6 in 1 row, for example 0x93e4a5307a0bcc9fecca0c6a0e3b81aaa1e54da9ca35c132dfebf8b5c9488c60; 0x93d6e67bd9b499b0af7776ef5c109b1457543907 in 1 row, for example 0xa9a21eabeb4c291b6239cd56d3eaf05af745ad596f2a115b3b9e0ac59ca3147f. 5. BNB Smart Chain, blocks 134,715,087 to 135,290,767: no row of this class. 6. Polygon, 30 days: 0xdc685acc53ca2a760029cab2f6a587482aa9cf57 in 1 row, for example 0xeba5a802345f6569cc02ddb56285230bccd4f72e7054249d0cb2127c7e6abae7, which cycle 5 already recorded. 7. Elysium from the deployment block: five rows over the three tokens that cycle 5 recorded (0x13c83fd2df0ae8c7b462d344a458275d1f6b5b82, 0x5cf58da30b62aca1c7a735a680279fbedaa7189c, 0xa425daa2cc467378ab4368496f1d8027c69915f3). The Elysium endpoint serves no trace, so those rows rest on the balance fall in a transaction without a pool event. 8. I did not read the source of the new tokens, so I cannot say for each who may start the move.

What is at risk: Pools of these tokens record balances that the PoolManager no longer holds. The loss falls on whoever provided or bought the token in those pools. The other currency of each pool is not touched, because accounting is per currency (src/PoolManager.sol:368-376).

What it takes: A pool of a token whose code can move balances of other holders.

What can be done: Monitors should compare balanceOf(PoolManager) with the sum of pool records per currency. Interfaces should hide pools of tokens that have moved the PoolManager balance.

This entry adds to F-086.

Second reviewer's corrections: actor: pool-creator is right for the three real cases, which are tokens whose own code moves balances of other holders. The count of new tokens is 2 on Ethereum and 1 on Robinhood Chain, where the finding has 4 and 2. 0x1f426c51..., 0x39b8b638... and 0x79b661c0... are a back end contract of a pooled token, the second address of EURe and a balance tracker of a pooled token. The test that the trace holds no transfer call from the PoolManager to the token must use the pooled currency address and every contract it calls. The address that emits the log is the wrong reference.

Code: `src/types/Currency.sol:60-80`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:368-376`.

Sources:

- https://mainnet.gateway.tenderly.co (Ethereum census logs, receipts, old balances and traces for the rows listed.)
- https://rpc.mainnet.chain.robinhood.com (Robinhood Chain census logs; old balances and traces through https://robinhood.drpc.org and the endpoints listed in rule.py.)
- https://rpc.elysium.kinetiq.xyz (Elysium census from block 256,083 to 9,953,762: 3,344,473 transactions, 5 flagged rows.)
- research/network-risk/cycles/006/theme-currency-lists.work/raw/census_summary.json:1 (Per network classes with token, row count and one example transaction each.)

## F-018. On MegaETH a caller can make every block field and oracle read revert inside hook and token sub-calls, and one such read caps the rest of the transaction at 20 million compute gas

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: MegaETH.

What differs on the network: MegaETH treats block fields, the beneficiary account and Oracle storage as volatile data. Two rules follow that Ethereum does not have. First, any contract can call disableVolatileDataAccess() on the MegaAccessControl system contract at 0x6342000000000000000000000000000000000004. From then on every volatile read in that frame and in all frames below it reverts with VolatileDataAccessDisabled, uses no gas, and cannot be switched back on by a descendant. Second, when a volatile read does succeed, the whole transaction, including parent frames, may spend only 20,000,000 more compute gas, and the cap survives a revert of the frame that read.

How it meets the code: 1. The PoolManager source uses no block field (a search of src/ outside src/test found none), so its own code never triggers either rule. 2. A swapper's contract calls disableVolatileDataAccess() and then unlock (src/PoolManager.sol:104-114). The PoolManager, the hook it calls at src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-155 and every contract the hook or a token calls are descendants of that frame. 3. A hook that executes TIMESTAMP or NUMBER in its own frame reverts, the call at src/libraries/Hooks.sol:134 fails and the PoolManager reverts at src/libraries/Hooks.sol:137. The swapper only stops its own transaction. 4. A hook that wraps the read in an external call and tolerates failure behaves differently. Examples are a price feed read with a staleness check, a read of the MegaETH Oracle or high-precision timestamp contract, or a call into a rate limiter, each inside try/catch with a default branch. The swapper can now force the default branch at will and at no gas cost. If that branch skips a circuit breaker, applies a base fee where a surge fee was due, or accepts a stale price, the swapper trades against liquidity providers on terms the hook was written to prevent. The same applies to tokens that catch failures of time-based accrual inside transfer. 5. Under the second rule a hook or token that reads a block field early leaves the PoolManager 20,000,000 compute gas for the rest of the transaction. The swap loop at src/libraries/Pool.sol:344-437 does a cold bitmap read per word and a tick update per initialized tick, so a single swap behind such a hook can cross several hundred initialized ticks before the budget ends. That figure is an estimate from Ethereum compute gas costs and was not measured. The swap can stop at a price limit (src/libraries/Pool.sol:344) and continue in another transaction, and liquidity removal touches two ticks (src/libraries/Pool.sol:146-238), so no funds are stuck.

What is at risk: Funds of liquidity providers in pools whose hook, or whose token, has a failure-tolerant path around a volatile read. No such hook was identified in this cycle, so no loss path is confirmed. Hookless pools and hooks that read block fields directly are unaffected apart from the 20,000,000 compute gas bound on work after the read.

What it takes: A pool must use a hook or token that catches a failed sub-call which reads a block field, the beneficiary or Oracle storage, and the fallback branch must be more favourable to the caller. Any account can then trigger it. The compute gas bound needs no precondition beyond a volatile read in the transaction.

What can be done: Hook authors on MegaETH should call isVolatileDataAccessDisabled() on MegaAccessControl at entry and revert when it returns true, or should never continue after a failed read. Reviews of hooks ported from other chains should list every try/catch and unchecked low-level call. Routers should set price limits so that large swaps behind time-reading hooks finish inside the detention budget.

Second reviewer's corrections: 1. Forcing the catch branch of a try around a sub-call is possible on any chain by starving the call of gas. MegaETH adds a way that costs nothing and always works, since the blocked frame returns all its gas. The finding should say this so the difference is not overstated. 2. The estimate of several hundred tick crossings inside 20,000,000 compute gas was not measured by the finding or by me. 3. A plain account cannot call disableVolatileDataAccess before unlock by itself. The caller has to be a contract or an account with delegated code. 4. Low stands. No loss path is shown, and the hazard sits in hooks and tokens outside this repository.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:104-114`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-155`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:344-437`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:146-238`.

Sources:

- https://docs.megaeth.com/spec/system-contracts/mega-access-control.md (disableVolatileDataAccess covers the caller's frame and all descendants; blocked reads revert without consuming gas; descendants get DisabledByParent)
- https://docs.megaeth.com/spec/megaevm/gas-detention.md (List of volatile reads, the 20,000,000 relative cap, transaction scope, persistence across reverted frames)
- https://docs.megaeth.com/spec/system-contracts/overview.md (MegaAccessControl address and Rex4 activation)
- rpc https://mainnet.megaeth.com/rpc, queries at blocks 32548426 to 32549054 on 2026-11-22 (397 bytes of code at 0x6342000000000000000000000000000000000004)

## F-040. Avalanche C-Chain folds two storage slot indexes into one, which lets a hook author hide a write path that Ethereum does not have

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: Avalanche.

What differs on the network: On the Avalanche C-Chain the node clears the lowest bit of the first byte of every storage slot index before SLOAD and SSTORE touch the account trie. Slot S and slot S with bit 248 flipped are therefore one slot. The set bit is reserved for balances of imported X-Chain assets. Ethereum, Polygon and BNB Smart Chain treat the two indexes as different slots.

How it meets the code: 1. The rule is in the client: partitionStateKeysHook.TransformStateKey clears bit 0 of byte 0, and libevm applies it in GetState, GetCommittedState and SetState. Transient storage is not rewritten. 2. The live PoolManager shows it. eth_getStorageAt for slot 0x01 followed by 31 zero bytes returns the owner address stored in slot 0, and extsload(bytes32) for that index returns the same word (src/Extsload.sol:10-15). 3. The PoolManager itself is not harmed. Its fixed slots are 0 to 6 (owner, protocolFeesAccrued, protocolFeeController, isOperator, balanceOf, allowance, _pools; src/libraries/StateLibrary.sol:11), all with a zero first byte. Every other slot is a keccak256 output or a keccak256 output plus an offset below 7 (src/libraries/Pool.sol:83-91, src/libraries/TickBitmap.sol:66-73, src/libraries/StateLibrary.sol:324-326). Two such slots collide only if two hashes agree in 255 bits, which costs about 2^127 hash evaluations to find even for a party that controls both inputs. Raw writes in src use slots derived this way (src/libraries/Pool.sol:544-557, src/libraries/TickBitmap.sol:73), and extsload only reads. 4. A hook or token is different, because its author picks constants. A contract can keep a guarded value, for example an admin or a fee recipient, at a hand-picked slot C and expose an innocent looking setter that writes slot C with bit 248 flipped. On Ethereum, and in any audit or fork test that runs standard EVM rules, the two slots are unrelated. On the C-Chain the setter overwrites the guarded value. 5. The PoolManager calls hooks with all gas and accepts their deltas (src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-155), so a hook that gains control this way can do whatever its privileged path allows with the liquidity of its own pools.

What is at risk: No funds of pools without a hook are reachable. Liquidity in pools whose hook or token was reviewed on Ethereum semantics can be exposed to a storage alias that exists only on Avalanche. The size of the loss is whatever the aliased slot controls in that hook.

What it takes: The attacker must be the author of a hook or token that victims choose to use, and must place two constants that differ only in bit 248. A scan on 2026-11-24 (tools/opdiff/avaxslots.py, opcodes/avalanche-slot-constants.json) read the bytecode of the ten official v4 contracts on Avalanche and of all 300 hooks named by an Initialize event up to block 101,267,314 (296 with code). None holds two 32 byte constants that differ only in bit 248. Two hooks use the OpenZeppelin reentrancy guard slot 0x9b77...5f00, whose bit 248 is set, so on Avalanche the guard lives at 0x9a77...5f00; nothing else in those hooks uses that slot, so the guard works as written. The scan sees constants only, so a slot computed at run time is outside it.

What can be done: Reviewers of hooks and tokens for Avalanche should compare every constant storage slot after clearing bit 248 and should run fork tests against a C-Chain node. Interfaces can warn when a hook uses assembly sstore with constant slots. Core needs no change.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor pool-creator and the severity low are right. Three details. First, the mitigation that asks for fork tests against a C-Chain node is incomplete. A local EVM that loads state over RPC and executes SLOAD and SSTORE itself follows standard rules and would treat the two indexes as two slots. Only execution on an avalanchego node shows the alias. This is my reasoning from the source read and I did not run such a test. Second, the alias adds no power that a hook author lacks. A hook author can already write any privileged path into the hook. The alias only lets that path pass a review done on Ethereum rules. Third, eth_getStorageAt applies the same rewrite, so off-chain tools that read a hook's slot with bit 248 set see the value of the cleared index and cannot tell the two apart from the outside.

Code: `src/Extsload.sol:10-15`, `src/libraries/StateLibrary.sol:11`, `src/libraries/StateLibrary.sol:324-326`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:83-91`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:544-557`, `src/libraries/TickBitmap.sol:66-73`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-155`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ava-labs/avalanchego/v1.15.3/vms/saevm/cchain/libevm/extstate/statedb.go (TransformStateKey calls storageKey, which clears bit 0 of byte 0. assetBalanceKey sets the same bit for imported asset balances.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ava-labs/libevm/d398bc4dbe41/core/state/statedb.go (GetState, GetCommittedState and SetState pass the slot index through transformStateKey at lines 349, 359 and 422.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ava-labs/libevm/d398bc4dbe41/core/vm/eips.go (opTload and opTstore use the slot index unchanged.)
- rpc https://api.avax.network/ext/bc/C/rpc, eth_getStorageAt and eth_call extsload(bytes32) on 0x06380c0e0912312b5150364b9dc4542ba0dbbc85, block 101078679 on 2026-11-22 (Slot 0x0100...00 returns 0x47eb0cf11a1626462da3c830bcde64c3f582b5a6, the value of slot 0.)
- rpc https://polygon.drpc.org and https://bsc-dataseed1.bnbchain.org, eth_getStorageAt on the PoolManager for slot 0x0100...00 on 2026-11-22 (Both return zero while slot 0 holds the owner, so the alias is specific to Avalanche within this unit.)

## F-051. A delegated session credential with a TIP-20 spending limit on Tempo can move the account's ERC-6909 claims and pool positions without limit

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: Tempo.

What differs on the network: Tempo accounts can authorise additional signers in the AccountKeychain precompile, with an expiry, optional call scopes and a spending limit per TIP-20 token. A transaction signed by such a signer runs with the account as msg.sender. The limit is enforced inside the TIP-20 precompile and only when the address that moves or approves tokens is tx.origin. Tempo's documentation states that assets other than TIP-20 tokens do not count and that a signer without scopes can call any contract. Ethereum has no protocol-level delegated signer.

How it meets the code: 1. A user holds ERC-6909 claims in the PoolManager, or liquidity positions through a periphery contract, and authorises a session signer for an application or agent with a limit such as 100 OUSD per day and no call scopes. 2. The holder of that signer sends a transaction as the user that calls transfer(receiver, id, amount) or setOperator(operator, true) on the PoolManager (src/ERC6909.sol:25-33 and 58-64). The PoolManager sees the user as msg.sender. No TIP-20 leaves the user's account, so AccountKeychain.authorize_transfer is never reached for the user. 3. The receiver or operator opens an unlock, burns the claims (src/PoolManager.sol:332-336, src/ERC6909Claims.sol:13-22) and takes the tokens (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297). The TIP-20 transfer has the PoolManager as from address, and authorize_transfer returns early because that address is not tx.origin. 4. The spending limit is never charged for any of these steps.

What is at risk: All ERC-6909 claims of that user in the PoolManager, and positions the user holds through contracts the signer may call, can be moved by a signer whose TIP-20 limit suggested a small bound. On 22 November 2026 the PoolManager held about 426,000 USD of TIP-20 tokens for all users together. Other users are not affected.

What it takes: The victim must have authorised a session signer without call scopes, or with scopes that include the PoolManager or a position contract, and the attacker must control that signer. The actor value is the nearest category: the attacker holds no chain role and needs a credential the victim chose to grant, comparable to a hook the victim chose to use. I confirmed the rule in the precompile source and the documentation and did not send a transaction.

What can be done: Users who keep claims or positions should give session signers explicit call scopes that exclude the PoolManager and position contracts. Wallets on Tempo can warn that a spending limit does not cover claims and positions.

Second reviewer's corrections: No actor value in the schema fits exactly. pool-creator is the nearest, because the victim must have granted the credential to the attacker, and the item is out of scope for the unprivileged goal. The 426,000 USD figure is the whole PoolManager balance and is an upper bound. I did not establish whether any account on Tempo holds ERC-6909 claims. The behaviour is documented by Tempo and is a property of its delegated credentials, which the PoolManager code cannot change. The same documentation sentence means that an allowance given to Permit2 before the credential was issued is also outside the limit.

Code: `src/ERC6909.sol:25-33`, `src/ERC6909.sol:58-64`, `src/ERC6909Claims.sol:13-22`, `src/PoolManager.sol:332-336`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/tempoxyz/tempo/697ea97/crates/precompiles/src/account_keychain/mod.rs (authorize_transfer and authorize_approve return without a check when the account is not tx.origin or the transaction uses the root signer)
- https://tempo.com/developers/docs/accounts/access-keys/spending-limits.md (a spending limit covers TIP-20 transfers, approvals, fees, precompile pulls and burnAt, and assets other than TIP-20 tokens do not count)
- https://tempo.com/developers/docs/accounts/access-keys/scopes.md (a signer without scopes can call anything, and a call outside the scopes reverts with CallNotAllowed)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/tempoxyz/tempo/697ea97/crates/evm/src/handler.rs (execute_batch runs every call of the transaction with the account as caller and origin)

## F-059. Two pooled tokens make their transfer rules depend on the PoolManager address: ELO on Elysium needs a budget set by its hook, and v4CRCL on X Layer pays out only to permitted recipients

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: Elysium, X Layer.

What differs on the network: No chain rule is involved. These are token designs that exist on these two networks among the tokens the PoolManager holds. ELO at 0xe107e5d99910f544f88ef961e3ca6d88f06f29dd on Elysium stores the PoolManager address and has functions poolManagerGateEnabled, poolManagerTransferBudget, authorizePoolManagerTransfer, setPoolManagerGate, setHook and setBlockedVenue. v4CRCL at 0xd4f64a67b2c1295bd6c25b3a306b2a049e392397 on X Layer has POOL_MANAGER, PERMISSIONED_TOKEN, allowListChecker, allowedHooks, allowedWrappers, wrapToPoolManager and swappingEnabled, which is the shape of a wrapper for a permissioned token.
