How it meets the code: 1. take(X, to, amount) debits the caller and makes the PoolManager call X.transfer(to, amount) (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:55-75). 2. ELO: a transfer of zero from the PoolManager succeeds. A transfer of 1 base unit or of 10^18 reverts with UnauthorizedPoolManagerTransfer(bool,uint256,uint256). authorizePoolManagerTransfer succeeds when called from the address returned by hook() and reverts for any other caller, including the PoolManager. Both budgets read zero outside a transaction and the bytecode contains TLOAD and TSTORE, so the budget appears to live in transient storage. ELO can therefore leave the PoolManager only in a transaction in which its hook has authorized the amount. A pool that names ELO with another hook or with no hook can receive ELO and can never pay it out, and an ERC-6909 claim on ELO can be burned for a take only when the hook takes part (src/PoolManager.sol:322-336). The owner of ELO can switch the gate and replace the hook. 3. v4CRCL: every transfer from the PoolManager to the four recipients I tried reverts with TRANSFER_FAILED, for a zero amount as well, and transfers by other senders revert with a custom error. Payouts depend on a permission list kept outside core. 4. In both cases the failed transfer makes take revert with ERC20TransferFailed (src/types/Currency.sol:82-86), and the same holds for collectProtocolFees (src/ProtocolFees.sol:56). I found no way for an ordinary account to use these rules against another currency: the checks act on the token's own balance, a zero amount poke changes nothing, and each take is debited in full.

What is at risk: The PoolManager holds 60,839 ELO on Elysium (about 46,000 USD of pool reserves on the tracker) and 65.98 v4CRCL on X Layer (about 1,800 USD). Holders of these tokens through pools or claims depend on the token owner and on the hook or permission list for every payout. Other currencies and other pools are not affected.

What it takes: A user must hold liquidity or claims in one of these tokens. The token owner, the hook or the permission list must refuse or stop authorizing. I could not obtain verified source for either token, so the function names come from the public signature database and the behaviour from read only calls.

What can be done: Front ends can flag currencies whose transfer from the PoolManager fails in simulation for a plain recipient. Liquidity providers should treat such a token like a hook that can block removal, which the state already records for all chains.

Second reviewer's corrections: The sentence that a pool with another hook or with no hook can receive ELO and can never pay it out is wrong for the receiving half. A transfer of 1 base unit to the PoolManager from each of four ELO holders reverts with UnauthorizedPoolManagerTransfer(true, 1, 0), so ELO can neither enter nor leave the PoolManager without a budget from the hook. The statement that the owner can replace the hook did not hold in simulation: setHook from the owner address reverts with 0xbef9156a, which the signature database names HookAlreadySet(). setPoolManagerGate(false) from the owner succeeds in simulation and reverts for another caller, so the owner can open the gate. The v4CRCL balance is now 68.54 and the finding quotes 65.98. Not confirmed: verified source for either token, the logic of the ELO hook and who controls it, and a payout of v4CRCL to a permitted recipient, because I did not find an address on the permission list. The actor value pool-creator fits ELO. For v4CRCL the party that decides payouts is the keeper of the permission list of the underlying security token, which is closer to issuer. Neither is an ordinary account. Severity low stands: the payout dependence on the hook is the hazard the state already records for hooks, and the sums are about 46,000 USD and under 2,000 USD.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:55-86`, `src/PoolManager.sol:322-336`.

Sources:

- https://rpc.elysium.kinetiq.xyz (rpc eth_call at block 9,697,391 and later on 2026-11-22: ELO transfer(recipient, 0) from the PoolManager returns true; transfer of 1 and of 10^18 revert with 0x206e5f37; poolManagerGateEnabled() is 1; poolManager() is the PoolManager; hook() is 0x34a6e76a10881d215abb5872f584bef6e3103dcd; authorizePoolManagerTransfer(true, 10^18) succeeds from the hook address and reverts with 0x67373552 from two other senders; balanceOf(PoolManager) is 60,838.98 ELO; a linear scan of the 7,456 byte code finds 5 TLOAD and 3 TSTORE)
- https://rpc.xlayer.tech (rpc eth_call at block 76,621,774 and later on 2026-11-22: v4CRCL transfer from the PoolManager to the Universal Router, the PositionManager and two plain addresses reverts with TRANSFER_FAILED for amounts 0 and 1000; balanceOf(PoolManager) is 65,979,861 with 6 decimals)
- https://api.4byte.sourcify.dev/signature-database/v1/lookup?function=0xe7d0c09e,0x206e5f37 (selector 0x206e5f37 is UnauthorizedPoolManagerTransfer(bool,uint256,uint256); the same database resolves the ELO and v4CRCL function selectors named in this finding)
- https://api.geckoterminal.com/api/v2/networks/elysium/dexes/uniswap-v4-elysium/pools?page=1&include=base_token,quote_token (ELO is among the twelve largest currencies of Uniswap v4 pools on Elysium by tracked reserve)

## F-069. Tokens whose balance falls with time leave the PoolManager short of what pools record: TURING on Ethereum has lost 73 percent of its pooled balance in 263 days

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: Ethereum, Robinhood Chain, Arbitrum One.

What differs on the network: These are token designs with a balance that decays by a time formula. Nobody has to send a transaction for the balance to fall, and no Transfer log is emitted.

How it meets the code: 1. Pool accounting records amounts and never reads balanceOf again. take pays out recorded amounts with a plain transfer (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:40-87). 2. Thirteen tokens named TURING on Ethereum lost 0.5 percent of their PoolManager balance in the one day window with no log. For 0x11fb7c5e6fe7e42fb9e28929b673d3eee20048d4 getDecayInfo(PoolManager) returns a stored amount of 999,999,999.99 tokens, a current balance of 267,590,219.70 and 263 days of decay. The contract has checkIn(uint256,bytes), lastCheckIn and hook() among its selectors, so holders who check in keep their balance and the PoolManager cannot. 3. Once withdrawals and swaps have paid out the 267.6 million that exist, every later take of this token reverts in the transfer, and the last positions cannot be closed in that currency. 4. On Robinhood Chain three tokens named PYRE fell 5.3 to 5.6 percent in the window and SMK2 fell 0.39 percent. On Arbitrum One DDL fell 3.7 percent. All without logs.

What is at risk: Each TURING token has a supply of 1,000,000,000 and the PoolManager holds about 26.8 percent of it after decay, 5.3 percent for two of them. The stored amount for the token I read equals almost the whole supply, so the liquidity is the deployer's own. The other balances are small: 9.34 PYRE, 2.12 SMK2, 3.34 DDL. I found no third party funds at risk today. The pattern matters for any future pool in a token with demurrage.

What it takes: A pool in a token whose balance decays. No attacker is needed. The TURING source is not verified; I read selectors from bytecode and state through its getters. I did not read the pool positions, so the split between the deployer and others is inferred from the stored amount.

What can be done: Do not pool tokens with a decaying balance unless the token exempts the PoolManager address. Interfaces can detect them with the reconcile script.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor value pool-creator is acceptable: the hazard comes from the token and hook that the pool's creator wrote, and only pools in that token are affected. Nobody takes anything, so the effect is a freeze of the last withdrawals in that currency. One detail to add: isExcluded(PoolManager) returns true on the token I read while the balance still decays, so that flag does not exempt the PoolManager from decay. The statement that the liquidity is the deployer's own is inferred from the stored amount, as the finding says, and I did not check it either.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:40-87`.

Sources:

- src/PoolManager.sol:291-297 (take debits the caller and transfers before anything is paid in.)
- src/types/Currency.sol:40-87 (The payout is a transfer call that reverts with ERC20TransferFailed when the token refuses it.)
- https://mainnet.gateway.tenderly.co (eth_call on 0x11fb7c5e6fe7e42fb9e28929b673d3eee20048d4: getDecayInfo(PoolManager) returns 999999999999999999999997062, 267590219702701977999999213, 263, 732409780297298021999997849, 0. balanceOf(PoolManager) at blocks 26,467,827 and 26,475,001 for the thirteen tokens is in research/network-risk/cycles/004/theme-balance-reconcile.work/raw/mismatch_1.json.)
- https://robinhood.drpc.org (eth_call balanceOf(PoolManager) at blocks 120,299,181 and 121,117,362 for PYRE (three addresses) and SMK2, read twice; no Transfer log naming the PoolManager between them. Rows in research/network-risk/cycles/004/theme-balance-reconcile.work/raw/mismatch_4663.json.)

## F-074. More pooled tokens gate their own transfers on the PoolManager address: AGL on eight networks, PLAYR, MOON, INFX, A3K, LFNX, FIN and four more Securitize wrappers

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: Ethereum, Optimism, BNB Smart Chain, Polygon, Base, Arbitrum One, Avalanche, Celo, Robinhood Chain, Elysium, X Layer.

What differs on the network: No chain rule is involved. These tokens store the PoolManager address and allow a transfer to or from it only inside an operation of their own hook or for permitted parties. AGL (AgroLastro, 0x0000000000109de411eabc9f04906d79f06fda0e, the same address on Ethereum, OP Mainnet, BNB Smart Chain, Polygon, Base, Arbitrum One, Avalanche and Celo) calls its hook contract PoolAGL on every transfer that touches the PoolManager, and the hook keeps a signed quota in transient storage that only its afterSwap and its own fee collection open. PLAYR on Robinhood Chain reverts with V4NotAuthorized. MOON on Elysium reverts with selector 0x971d2ee1 and the PoolManager address for every nonzero amount. INFX on Avalanche accepts transfers into the PoolManager only up to an allowance that its tax hooks credit in the same transaction. A3K on OP Mainnet marks limited accounts, which receive only with an exception that its own contracts grant, and a transfer back to the PoolManager reverts with AccountIsLimited. LFNX on OP Mainnet and FIN on Polygon revert with the same selector on the return leg, and I did not read their source. v4MSTR, v4COIN, v4HOOD and v4VOLOROST2 on X Layer have the selector set of v4CRCL, which F-059 describes.

How it meets the code: 1. take(X, to, amount) and a payment before settle both end in X.transfer or X.transferFrom with the PoolManager as one party (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297 and 349-364, src/types/Currency.sol:60-75). 2. AGL: _update calls pool.consumirSaida(amount) when the sender is the PoolManager and pool.consumirEntrada(amount) when the recipient is. Both revert with ForaDaCota(amount, quota) unless the quota that afterSwap opened in this transaction covers the amount. From the PoolManager address a transfer of 1 AGL reverts with ForaDaCota(1e9, 0) on Ethereum, Base, Polygon and Celo, and cota() reads 0. The PoolManager holds between 99.9999 and 100 percent of the 1e21 AGL minted on each of the eight networks. 3. A take of AGL therefore works only in a transaction that also swaps in the official pool, and an ERC-6909 claim on AGL can be burned for a take only then (src/PoolManager.sol:331-336). The free round trip of this unit is not available for these tokens, because the return leg is refused. 4. The quota of AGL is one transient value for the whole transaction. It stays set when an unlock ends with claims minted in place of a take. I did not test what a later user of the same transaction meets, which belongs to the unit on shared transactions. 5. For the other tokens the failed transfer makes take revert with ERC20TransferFailed (src/types/Currency.sol:82-86) in the same way.

What is at risk: Holders of ERC-6909 claims and any pool that names one of these tokens without the hook the token expects cannot pay the token out. For AGL the liquidity belongs to the issuer and third party liquidity is refused by the hook, so the exposed parties are claim holders and integrators. No path for an ordinary account to block other users was found.

What it takes: A user holds a claim or a position in such a token outside the flow the token author intended. The token author or hook author sets the rules.

What can be done: Interfaces should not offer ERC-6909 claims or hookless pools for tokens that read the PoolManager address in their transfer logic. Pool creators should simulate a transfer from the PoolManager address before pairing a token.

This entry adds to F-059.

Second reviewer's corrections: Actor pool-creator and severity low stand. The statement that no ordinary account can block other users holds across transactions, because every gate is transient. Inside one transaction the AGL value is shared: a buyer who ends an unlock with claims leaves a negative value, and a later sale of AGL in the same transaction then fails consumirEntrada, which requires a value that is at least the amount. The research file records this as a lead for the shared transaction unit. The entries for MOON, LFNX, FIN and the four X Layer wrappers rest on the researcher's calls alone.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:322-336`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-86`.

Sources:

- https://celo.blockscout.com/api/v2/smart-contracts/0x0000000000109de411eabc9f04906d79f06fda0e (Verified source of AGL: _update calls pool.consumirEntrada or pool.consumirSaida when the PoolManager is a party; the same address is used on eight networks.)
- https://celo.blockscout.com/api/v2/smart-contracts/0x0000000456b76bc801253ef2366664750f602840 (Verified source of PoolAGL: the quota lives in transient storage, afterSwap adds the AGL delta, consumirEntrada and consumirSaida revert with ForaDaCota, beforeAddLiquidity refuses third parties.)
- rpc eth_call on the endpoints of chain ids 1, 8453, 137 and 42220 at blocks 26,475,539, 61,094,554, 97,802,546 and 83,508,526 (transfer(recipient, 1e9) from the PoolManager address reverts with 0x7c467237 (ForaDaCota) and quota 0; cota() returns 0. eth_getCode of the AGL address has 15,125 bytes on chain ids 1, 10, 56, 137, 8453, 42161, 43114 and 42220 and balanceOf(PoolManager) is about 1e21 AGL on each.)
- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/4663/0x775fd5a987bf3ff5e205deabde6634ddcee3a3fc?fields=sources,proxyResolution,compilation (Verified source of PlayrToken: _enforceTradeGates reverts with V4NotAuthorized when the PoolManager is a party and no authorisation is set.)
- https://api.routescan.io/v2/network/mainnet/evm/43114/etherscan/api?module=contract&action=getsourcecode&address=0xda7ad1b3423713dc9b4275a00d59b82024b3eb0c (Verified source of InferenceToken (INFX): transfers into the PoolManager are limited to a transient allowance credited by its tax hooks, error NoHookAllowance.)
- https://explorer.optimism.io/api/v2/smart-contracts/0x6F3852B4622497EEE563cBA022977B8162bFD3fa (Verified source of IndexTokenV4 behind A3K: limited accounts receive only with an exception, error AccountIsLimited.)
- rpc eth_call with state override on https://rpc.elysium.kinetiq.xyz, https://rpc.mainnet.chain.robinhood.com, https://rpc.xlayer.tech, https://mainnet.optimism.io and https://polygon.drpc.org, 2026-11-23 (Observed reverts from the PoolManager address or back to it: MOON 0x971d2ee1 with the PoolManager address, PLAYR 0x19e7dcd0, v4MSTR, v4COIN, v4HOOD and v4VOLOROST2 TRANSFER_FAILED, A3K, LFNX and FIN 0xb8b82b49 on the return leg.)

## F-103. A pool id that resembles another pool's id can be mined through the hooks field of the PoolKey, and core gives a reader nothing to tell the two pools apart (Trail of Bits finding 6, unresolved)

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: all 21 networks.

What differs on the network: None found. The report's method places a hook at a mined address with CREATE2. An account with a mined address and an EIP-7702 delegation serves the same purpose where delegation is live, which the ledger records as F-013.

How it meets the code: 1. The pool id is the keccak256 hash of the five words of the PoolKey (src/types/PoolId.sol:11-15) and the hooks address is the fifth word (src/types/PoolKey.sol:11-22). 2. initialize constrains the hook address only through its low 14 bits and the dynamic fee rule (src/libraries/Hooks.sol:109-127, src/PoolManager.sol:126), so 146 bits of the address are free to vary while currencies, fee and tick spacing equal those of a known pool. 3. Core stores every pool under its full 32 byte id (src/PoolManager.sol:93), so two pools never share state. The hazard is in displays that shorten the id: a mined id can match the first and last characters of the known pool's id. 4. The attacker's pool carries the attacker's hook, which has the powers listed in the hook permissions note of this repository. 5. The fix review marks the finding unresolved, and nothing in src addresses it.

What is at risk: A user who picks a pool by a shortened id, or an integration that matches pools by a prefix, can be led into a pool whose hook takes the swap input or blocks removal. Funds in the original pool are not touched.

What it takes: The victim must choose the attacker's pool. The attacker must author the hook.

What can be done: Interfaces should show and compare the full PoolKey with the hook address. Integrations should match pools by the full 32 byte id.

Code: `src/types/PoolId.sol:11-15`, `src/types/PoolKey.sol:11-22`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:109-127`, `src/PoolManager.sol:117-134`.

Sources:

- docs/security/audits/TrailOfBits_audit_core.pdf:39 (Printed pages 39 and 40, finding 6: the four requirements of the lookalike pool and the recommendation to document it)
- docs/security/audits/TrailOfBits_audit_core.pdf:62 (Printed page 62: unresolved, the team decided not to resolve the issue)
- src/types/PoolId.sol:11 (The id is the hash of all five PoolKey words)
- src/libraries/Hooks.sol:109 (Only the low 14 bits and the dynamic fee rule constrain the hook address)

## F-113. Ten unpriced pooled tokens on Ethereum charge the sender on top of every transfer, so each take lowers the PoolManager balance by more than the caller is debited

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: author of a hook or token the victims use. Networks: Ethereum.

What differs on the network: This is a token behaviour that the existing mainnet PoolManager meets today. WHITE on Ethereum and ARGUS on Arc (F-092, F-109) charge the sender only when the recipient is a listed pair. The ten tokens of this finding charge the sender on a transfer to any recipient, and none of them has a price at coins.llama.fi, which is why the priced sweep of cycle 7 did not list them. They were present in the stored data of the one third transfer sweep of cycle 5 and were not written up.

How it meets the code: 1. take debits the caller by amount and then calls transfer(to, amount) on the token (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297). The transfer routine accepts the call when it does not revert and returns nothing or true (src/types/Currency.sol:60-80). No statement compares the fall of the PoolManager balance with the amount. 2. Simulation at block 26,480,246 with a forwarding stub at the PoolManager address and an unrelated transaction origin. The stub sends one third of the balance to an ordinary account A and the balances are read before and after. The PoolManager balance falls by 150 percent of the amount for SFC, 102 percent for XIN, 101 percent for YZNC, 100.5025 percent for NoMM and Novium, 100.5 percent for Novium+ and NOMM, 100.3003 percent for USDX, 100.1001 percent for MOOP and 100.1 percent for EURX. A receives the full amount in nine cases and 98 percent for XIN. Example: the PoolManager holds 501,793.14 NoMM, sends 167,264.38 and its balance falls by 168,104.90. 3. So every payout of such a token by take, which is how a swap output, a liquidity withdrawal, a claim redemption and a protocol fee collection are paid (src/ProtocolFees.sol:54-56 uses the same transfer), removes the charge from the shared balance in addition to the amount. Pools and claims keep recording the old amounts. 4. Second simulation: A sends 90 percent of what it received back to the PoolManager. A is charged at the same rate (its balance falls by the same percentage of the amount) and the PoolManager receives the amount sent, or 98 percent of it for XIN. A caller who takes and returns a token in one unlock therefore pays the same charge that the PoolManager pays. The statement of lead L-331 that a caller could grind the balance down at no cost does not hold for these tokens. For SFC the return failed, because the account cannot cover 150 percent of the amount.

What is at risk: Liquidity providers and claim holders of the pools of these ten tokens. The PoolManager balance of the token ends below the sum that pools, claims and protocol fees record, and the last withdrawals of that token revert in the transfer. Other currencies are not touched, because each token has its own balance. Balances at the PoolManager at block 26,480,246: 600 SFC, 2,084.19 XIN, 2.14 YZNC, 501,793.14 NoMM, 4.75e12 Novium, 9,942.19 Novium+, 1,402,836.84 NOMM, 8,155.51 USDX, 22.30 MOOP and 2,083.67 EURX. None has a price at coins.llama.fi, so I cannot state a value and I expect it to be small. The tokens sit in 23 pools, all without a hook, 14 of them with active liquidity.

What it takes: A pool of such a token. Any swap, withdrawal or take of the token triggers the shortfall, with no role and no hook. A caller who wants to enlarge the shortfall on purpose pays the same charge on the way back. I did not read the source of the ten tokens, so whether the rate can be changed by an owner, and whether some senders are exempt, is not confirmed. The same stored sweep lists six tokens on Polygon and one on Arbitrum One with a sender charge of 0.2 to 98 percent, which I did not retest.

What can be done: Core cannot detect the charge, because take does not read the balance after the transfer. Interfaces and routers should not list pools of tokens whose sender pays on top. LPs should avoid such pools or expect that the last units cannot be withdrawn. A periphery check that compares balanceOf before and after a payout would show the difference.

This entry adds to F-092.

Second reviewer's corrections: actor: pool-creator. The shortfall comes from the token's own transfer rule, which its author chose, and the victims are LPs and claim holders who chose a pool of that token. No ordinary account gains from it: as the finding itself shows, a deliberate take and return costs the caller the same charge. This is a token hazard outside the opcode focus of cycle 8 and should stay a one-line record. The pool count of 23 rests on the research file except for the 2 NoMM pools that I recounted.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-80`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:54-56`.

Sources:

- https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com (eth_simulateV1 at block 26,480,246 with forwarding stubs at the PoolManager address and at account A: balances before and after the outgoing transfer and after the return transfer for the ten tokens. Script and raw result: research/network-risk/cycles/008/ethereum-reference.work/charged.py and raw_charged.json.)
- research/network-risk/cycles/005/theme-assigned-balance.work/raw/third_1.json.gz:1 (Stored result of the one third transfer sweep of cycle 5 over 56,331 Ethereum currencies: 36 rows where the sender lost more than the amount, ten of them by 0.1 percent or more.)
- https://mainnet.gateway.tenderly.co (eth_getLogs of Initialize by currency topic and StateView.getLiquidity: 23 pools of the ten tokens, all with hook address zero, 14 with active liquidity.)
- https://coins.llama.fi/prices/current/ethereum:0xed4794d6c7f397f8e07922187074843d472abe2e (The price query for the ten addresses returned an empty coins object.)

## F-002. Robinhood Stock Tokens and USDG let the issuer block, pause, burn or upgrade the tokens held by the PoolManager

High severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Robinhood Chain.

What differs on the network: The assets this chain is built for are issuer-controlled in a more concentrated way than a typical Ethereum token. All Robinhood Stock Tokens are beacon proxies behind one contract, 0xe10b6f6b275de231345c20d14ab812db62151b00, which is both the upgrade beacon and the access registry with isBlocked, blockAccounts, pause and upgradeTo. The shared logic contract has adminBurn(address,uint256) and pause. USDG is an upgradeable Paxos token with isFrozen and decreaseSupplyFromAddress. The PoolManager already holds both.

How it meets the code: Step 1, block or pause: if the registry blocks the PoolManager address or a token is paused, the token's transfer call fails. Currency.transfer treats a failed call as an error and reverts with ERC20TransferFailed, so every take and every collectProtocolFees in that token reverts. Settling needs a transfer into the PoolManager, which a pause or a block on the recipient would stop as well. One registry entry covers every stock token at once, and A6 applies because one address holds all pools. An LP can still remove liquidity, take the other currency of the pair and call mint to hold the frozen side as an ERC-6909 claim, because mint moves no tokens. The claim cannot be turned into tokens until the block is lifted. Step 2, burn: adminBurn against the PoolManager lowers balanceOf(PoolManager) with no matching delta. Pool reserves, claims and accrued fees then exceed the balance. Takes keep succeeding until the balance runs out, and the last LPs and claim holders bear the shortfall. Step 3, upgrade: upgradeTo on the beacon changes the code of every stock token in one transaction, which removes the A4 expectation that token code is fixed. I read function selectors and view results from chain. I did not read verified source, so the exact transfer check against the registry and the role holders are unconfirmed. Stock token splits and dividends do not create a balance change, because they move uiMultiplier and leave balanceOf fixed.

What is at risk: At the time of the query the PoolManager held 3,134.87 ANT stock tokens and 16,984,261 USDG. A block or pause freezes the stock token side of every pool that uses the affected tokens. A burn creates a loss equal to the burned amount, carried by whoever withdraws last. The other currency in each pair stays withdrawable.

What it takes: The issuer (Robinhood Assets (Jersey) Limited for stock tokens, Paxos for USDG) or a holder of the relevant role must act. isBlocked(PoolManager) and isFrozen(PoolManager) are false today and both tokens are unpaused. No unprivileged party can trigger any of these functions.

What can be done: The deployer can ask both issuers to confirm in writing how the PoolManager address is treated and can monitor the registry and beacon for block, pause and upgrade events. Pool creators and LPs should size stock token and USDG positions with a freeze and a shortfall in mind. A later cycle should read the verified source of the logic and registry contracts.

Second reviewer's corrections: The transfer check is now confirmed from verified source and it also covers the recipient, so a take of a stock token to a blocked recipient reverts for that caller only. The role holders are single accounts with no code, which the finding did not establish. For USDG the direct burn path is narrower than stated: decreaseSupplyFromAddress works on another address only for a supply controller flagged to burn from any address, and it rejects frozen addresses. The seizure path for a frozen address is wipeFrozenAddress in the facet, whose source I did not read. USDG is a claimable rewards token. Its balanceOf excludes unclaimed rewards and a claim moves tokens from a claim source to a destination set per payout group, so the PoolManager balance does not grow with time. I did not check whether the PoolManager is registered in a payout group. Balances moved slightly since the finding was written: 3,134.38 ANT and 16.84M USDG.

Code: `src/types/Currency.sol:55-87`, `src/types/Currency.sol:90-96`, `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:322-329`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-365`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57`.

Sources:

- https://rpc.mainnet.chain.robinhood.com eth_getStorageAt (beacon slot), eth_getCode and eth_call on 0xbbB6c29bCc3a1028A2B54310DF73F4F7A3bcB373, 0xe10b6f6b275de231345c20d14ab812db62151b00, 0xb35490d6f9163de4f80d88dc75c3516eb64c5ae2 and 0x5fc5360D0400a0Fd4f2af552ADD042D716F1d168; queried 2026-11-22 (Beacon and registry are one contract, the selectors listed are present in the bytecode, isBlocked and isFrozen return false for the PoolManager, paused() is false, and the PoolManager balances are as stated.)
- https://api.openchain.xyz/signature-database/v1/lookup?function=0x6abf7081,0xfaed47fd&filter=true (Selector names blockAccounts(address[]) and unblockAccounts(address[]). The same service resolved the other selector names quoted.)
- https://docs.robinhood.com/chain/building-with-stock-tokens/ (Stock tokens are 18 decimal ERC-20s. The ERC-8056 multiplier changes on corporate actions while balanceOf and totalSupply stay fixed.)
- https://docs.robinhood.com/chain/stock-tokens/ (Stock Tokens are debt securities issued by Robinhood Assets (Jersey) Limited, which also acts as tokenizer.)
- https://docs.robinhood.com/chain/contracts/ (Canonical WETH and USDG addresses on Robinhood Chain.)
- https://cdn.robinhood.com/assets/robinhood/legal/rhj_final_terms_for_tokenised_debt_securities_linked_to_anthropic_pbc.pdf (0xbbB6c29bCc3a1028A2B54310DF73F4F7A3bcB373 is the securities ledger contract of a Robinhood stock token. Read through a search result excerpt only.)

## F-011. One issuer blocklist entry on the PoolManager address freezes or wipes a stablecoin for every pool

High severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Ethereum.

What differs on the network: This is a property of assets on mainnet, and mainnet protocol rules are the reference. USDC, USDT and PYUSD each let one issuer role block a single address. USDC blocks transfers from and to a blacklisted address. USDT blocks transfers sent by a blacklisted address and lets the owner call destroyBlackFunds, which sets that address's balance to zero. PYUSD blocks transfers from and to a frozen address and lets the asset protection role call wipeFrozenAddress, which sets the balance to zero.

How it meets the code: All pools, all ERC-6909 claims and all accrued protocol fees for a currency sit in one token balance at the PoolManager address. take calls currency.transfer and reverts with ERC20TransferFailed when the token call fails (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:60-86), so once the address is blocked nobody can withdraw that token from any pool. collectProtocolFees fails the same way (src/ProtocolFees.sol:56). For USDC and PYUSD a transfer into the PoolManager also fails, so settle can no longer be funded in that token (src/PoolManager.sol:349-364). For USDT the blocked address can still receive, so users can keep paying USDT in while nothing can leave. If the issuer then zeroes the balance, the pools keep their accounting but the tokens behind it are gone, which is the undercollateralised state described in A2. LPs can still remove liquidity, take the other currency of the pair, and convert the blocked currency's delta into ERC-6909 claims with mint, which does no token transfer (src/PoolManager.sol:322-329). The claims stay unredeemable while the block lasts.

What is at risk: At block 26470360 the existing PoolManager holds 79,953,224 USDT, 63,053,277 PYUSD and 30,404,253 USDC. One blocklist transaction per token freezes that token for every LP, every claim holder and the protocol fee balance at once. A wipe by Tether or Paxos turns the freeze into a loss of the full balance. The other currency of each affected pool stays withdrawable.

What it takes: The issuer role must act: the USDC blacklister (0x0a06be16275b95a7d2567fbdae118b36c7da78f9), the USDT owner (0xc6cde7c39eb2f0f0095f41570af89efc2c1ea828) or a PYUSD ASSET_PROTECTION_ROLE holder. The PoolManager is not blocked on any of the three today. I found no way for an unprivileged party to cause the block directly. I did not research what legal or policy triggers each issuer applies to contract addresses.

What can be done: The deployer cannot remove this from a singleton. Interfaces and LPs can treat issuer-controlled tokens as carrying whole-protocol exposure instead of per-pool exposure. Monitoring isBlacklisted, isBlackListed and isFrozen for the PoolManager address gives early warning. Routers can fall back to ERC-6909 claims for settlement when token transfers to the PoolManager fail.

Second reviewer's corrections: The balances moved slightly since the researcher's block: 30,394,358 USDC, 79,798,549 USDT and 63,112,992 PYUSD at block 26470956. The finding lists only the blocklist roles. All three tokens also gate transfer on a global pause (FiatTokenV1.sol line 288, TetherToken.sol line 336, PaxosTokenV2.sol line 139), and USDC and PYUSD sit behind upgradeable proxies, so the pauser and the upgrade admin of each token are further single actors who can stop every pool in that currency. Severity stays high because the freeze or wipe needs the token issuer to act.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-86`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/PoolManager.sol:322-329`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57`.

Sources:

- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/1/0x43506849d7c04f9138d1a2050bbf3a0c054402dd?fields=sources,compilation (FiatTokenV2_2 implementation behind the USDC proxy: transfer and transferFrom carry notBlacklisted modifiers for caller, sender and recipient (FiatTokenV1.sol lines 257 to 290), and blacklist is restricted to the blacklister (Blacklistable.sol line 71))
- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/1/0xdAC17F958D2ee523a2206206994597C13D831ec7?fields=sources,compilation (TetherToken: addBlackList and destroyBlackFunds are onlyOwner (lines 277 to 293), transfer requires the sender not to be blacklisted (lines 336 to 337), destroyBlackFunds sets balances[user] to 0)
- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/1/0x8c35caa5fd5bdc64b6b11344ad57594a3676256a?fields=sources,compilation (PaxosTokenV2: _transfer reverts with AddressFrozen if from or to is frozen (lines 444 to 445), freeze and wipeFrozenAddress are restricted to ASSET_PROTECTION_ROLE (lines 278 to 292), wipeFrozenAddress sets balances[addr] to 0)
- https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com (eth_call at block 26470360: balanceOf(PoolManager) for USDC, USDT and PYUSD; USDC.isBlacklisted, USDT.isBlackListed and PYUSD.isFrozen all return false for the PoolManager; USDC.blacklister and USDT.getOwner; implementation slots of the USDC and PYUSD proxies)

## F-015. Role holders on GHO, syrupUSDC, apxUSD, shMON and mUSD can pause, burn, move or replace balances that the Monad PoolManager holds

High severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Monad.

What differs on the network: Five further tokens with issuer controls sit at the single PoolManager address on Monad, next to the dollar tokens that F-015 already lists. The PoolManager holds 2,729,903 GHO, 1,645,837 mUSD, 1,561,546 syrupUSDC, 299,944 apxUSD and 269,214 shMON.

How it meets the code: The PoolManager records what each pool is owed and trusts balanceOf to stay equal to the sum. GHO, apxUSD, shMON and mUSD are proxies, so the party that controls the proxy can replace the token logic. The apxUSD and mUSD implementations contain pause, and a paused token makes every take of that currency revert at src/types/Currency.sol:73-86, which stops removals and swaps in every pool that uses it. The syrupUSDC and apxUSD code contains burn(address,uint256), and the mUSD implementation contains forceTransfer(address,address,uint256) and freeze. If the role holder applies one of them to the PoolManager address, the balance falls while the pool accounting stays unchanged, and the last liquidity providers to withdraw cannot be paid. This reading rests on the selectors found in the deployed bytecode. Which accounts hold the roles, and whether burn(address,uint256) needs an allowance, was not confirmed.

What is at risk: All pools on Monad that use the affected token. The amounts at the time of the query are listed above: about 6.2 million units of dollar tokens and 269,214 shMON.

What it takes: The issuer or the holder of the relevant role acts against the PoolManager address or pauses or upgrades the token. An ordinary account cannot trigger it.

What can be done: Liquidity providers can weigh the issuer controls of each token before they supply it. The deployer cannot remove the dependency, because one address holds the balances of every pool.

Second reviewer's corrections: The mechanism says a paused token stops removals and swaps in every pool that uses it. The code stops take and settle for that currency. Removals and swaps that are closed with mint and burn of ERC-6909 claims continue (src/PoolManager.sol:322-336), so the effect of a pause is that the token cannot be paid out or paid in. The balances at block 124498672 are 2,640,693 GHO, 1,650,963 mUSD, 1,561,546 syrupUSDC, 299,944 apxUSD and 268,856 shMON. shMON has a proxy admin at 0x00b16590295092f12f4cec655296b6129c49489c in addition to the owner named in the finding. The actor value issuer is correct. No step is open to an ordinary account.

Code: `src/types/Currency.sol:60-86`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`.

Sources:

- rpc https://rpc.monad.xyz, eth_call with from set to the PoolManager, eth_getCode, eth_getBalance and eth_getStorageAt at blocks 124455000 to 124466634 on 2026-11-22 (balanceOf(PoolManager) for the five tokens, the EIP-1967 implementation and admin slots, paused() false on apxUSD and mUSD, owner() of shMON, BURNER_ROLE() on syrupUSDC, isFrozen(PoolManager) false on mUSD, and the PUSH4 selector constants found in each implementation)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/monad-crypto/token-list/main/tokenlist-mainnet.json (Token addresses, symbols and decimals on Monad mainnet)

## F-019. USDC and USDT0 issuers can block the PoolManager address, which stops every pool in that currency, and USDT0's owner can burn the blocked balance

High severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: HyperEVM.

What differs on the network: Ethereum has the same class of issuer control for USDC, so this is a property of the assets pools hold on HyperEVM and is listed for completeness of the per network picture. On HyperEVM the two main stablecoins are native Circle USDC at 0xb88339CB7199b77E23DB6E890353E22632Ba630f and USDT0 at 0xB8CE59FC3717ada4C02eaDF9682A9e934F625ebb. USDC has a blacklister role (0x33aa4e959132e49552cb754fb6585bdcdf874beb) and a pauser, and a blacklisted address can neither send nor receive. USDT0 has an owner (0xb64a89ad247a2d691a728bb6822a85eedd7fc541) that maintains a blocklist and can call destroyBlockedFunds on a blocked address. Both tokens sit behind upgradeable proxies. USDC is also the asset HyperCore treats specially under AQAv2, which raises the share of pool liquidity likely to sit in it.

How it meets the code: 1. All pools, all ERC-6909 claims and all protocol fees in one currency share one token balance at the PoolManager address. 2. Every payout runs take, which calls transfer on the token with the PoolManager as sender and reverts with ERC20TransferFailed when the token call fails (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:55-86). collectProtocolFees uses the same transfer (src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57). 3. If the USDC blacklister lists the PoolManager, or the USDC pauser pauses the token, every USDC transfer from or to the PoolManager reverts. take of USDC fails for all pools and inbound payment fails too, so the balance check in settle never sees new funds (src/PoolManager.sol:349-365). 4. If the USDT0 owner blocks the PoolManager, transfers out revert while transfers in still succeed, so users can pay in and nobody can take out. The owner can then call destroyBlockedFunds and burn the whole PoolManager balance. Pool state still says the tokens are owed, and every later take of USDT0 fails for lack of balance. 5. modifyLiquidity and swap only move deltas (src/PoolManager.sol:145-184 and 187-227). An LP can still remove liquidity, take the other currency and call mint to hold the blocked currency as an ERC-6909 claim (src/PoolManager.sol:322-329). The claim is redeemable only if the block is lifted.

What is at risk: All USDC or all USDT0 held by the PoolManager across every pool, plus claims and protocol fees in that currency. A block freezes them. destroyBlockedFunds on USDT0 removes them for good. The other currency of each affected pool stays withdrawable.

What it takes: The token issuer acts against the PoolManager address: Circle's blacklister or pauser for USDC, the USDT0 owner for USDT0, or the proxy admin of either token through an upgrade. No unprivileged party can trigger this. On-chain reads at block 51,941,800 show isBlacklisted(PoolManager) false, paused() false and isBlocked(PoolManager) false.

What can be done: The deployer cannot remove the control. LPs can limit exposure by choosing pool pairs with that risk in mind. Front ends can watch for Blacklisted and BlockPlaced events that name the PoolManager and tell LPs to exit the other side of each pool with take and to hold the blocked side with mint. Integrators should not treat ERC-6909 claims in these currencies as equal to the token while a block is in force.

Second reviewer's corrections: The exposure today is small: the PoolManager holds 997.75 USDC and 0.000002 USDT0, so the impact text describes the structure and not a large current balance. The hazard exists in the same form on Ethereum, as the finding says, so it is a property of the pooled assets and not of the HyperEVM execution layer. The severity stays high because the scale assigns that level to loss or freeze by a token issuer. The suggested monitoring should also watch Upgraded events on both proxies and the Pause event on USDC, since a block is one of three routes.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:322-329`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-365`, `src/types/Currency.sol:55-86`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57`.

Sources:

- https://github.com/circlefin/stablecoin-evm/blob/master/doc/tokendesign.md (A blacklisted address cannot call transfer or transferFrom and cannot receive tokens. The blacklister role controls the list and the pauser can stop all transfers.)
- https://docs.usdt0.to/technical-documentation/token-features/additional-behavior (The USDT0 contract owner maintains an on-chain isBlocked list, blocked addresses cannot send tokens, and the tokens sit behind upgradeable proxies.)
- https://www.openzeppelin.com/news/usdt0-audit (The USDT0 owner can block accounts and burn tokens from blocked accounts. Blocked accounts can still receive transfers.)
- https://rpc.hyperliquid.xyz/evm eth_call on USDC 0xb88339CB7199b77E23DB6E890353E22632Ba630f: blacklister(), paused(), isBlacklisted(PoolManager), balanceOf(PoolManager) (USDC on HyperEVM has a blacklister set, is not paused, has not blacklisted the PoolManager, and the PoolManager held 997.75 USDC.)
- https://rpc.hyperliquid.xyz/evm eth_call on USDT0 0xB8CE59FC3717ada4C02eaDF9682A9e934F625ebb: owner(), isBlocked(PoolManager), addToBlockedList(address), destroyBlockedFunds(address) (USDT0 on HyperEVM exposes isBlocked and owner gated addToBlockedList and destroyBlockedFunds. The simulated calls from a non owner revert with Ownable: caller is not the owner.)

## F-027. B20 precompile tokens on Base give the issuer seize, pause and policy control over the PoolManager balance, and their rules change at hard forks

High severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Base.

What differs on the network: Since the Beryl upgrade on 25 June 2026, Base has a native token standard called B20. Anyone can create a token through a factory precompile at 0xB20f000000000000000000000000000000000000, and all B20 tokens share logic that runs natively in the node. Each token has role gated mint, burn and seizeWithMemo, a pause switch per feature, and policy slots that are checked on every transfer against the sender, the receiver and msg.sender. The policies are allowlists or blocklists stored in a shared Policy Registry precompile, and one policy can back many tokens and be administered by a third party. Base changes the shared logic at hard forks: Cobalt added seize, and Denim on 5 November 2026 started to enforce the executor policy on plain transfer calls. Ethereum has no enshrined token logic of this kind.

How it meets the code: 1. A pool is created with a B20 token as one currency. Users pay in with sync, a token transfer and settle, which credits balanceOf(PoolManager) minus the synced reserves (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288 and 349-364). 2. Every payout calls transfer on the token with the PoolManager as msg.sender (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:55-86). For a B20 token that call passes through the TRANSFER pause check, then TRANSFER_EXECUTOR_POLICY on msg.sender, TRANSFER_SENDER_POLICY on from, and TRANSFER_RECEIVER_POLICY on to. msg.sender and from are both the PoolManager. 3. If the token admin pauses TRANSFER, binds a policy that does not authorise the PoolManager, or the admin of a referenced policy removes the PoolManager from an allowlist or adds it to a blocklist, the transfer reverts and take reverts with ERC20TransferFailed. Every pool, every ERC-6909 claim and the protocol fee balance in that currency are stuck together because they share one holder address. collectProtocolFees uses the same transfer path (src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57). 4. If the issuer has configured SEIZE_EXEMPT_POLICY so that the PoolManager is not exempt, an account with SEIZE_ROLE can call seizeWithMemo(PoolManager, to, amount, memo). The seize skips allowances and the three transfer policies. The PoolManager balance falls without any delta being recorded, so the pools hold less than their accounting says, and the last LPs and claim holders to withdraw cannot be paid. 5. A hard fork can produce the freeze in step 3 with no action by the issuer after pool creation. Before Denim a token with a restrictive executor allowlist still allowed holder initiated transfer, so the PoolManager could pay out. Since Denim the same token rejects transfer from any msg.sender that is not on the executor allowlist. 6. A seize or pause in the middle of a settlement cannot be turned into credit: a lower balance makes reservesNow minus reservesBefore underflow and revert (src/PoolManager.sol:360).

What is at risk: All liquidity, ERC-6909 claims and accrued protocol fees held in the affected B20 token on Base. A seize is a direct loss for LPs of that token. A pause or policy change freezes the token side of every pool that uses it. The other currency of each pool is not affected. Which B20 tokens the Base PoolManager holds today, and in what amounts, could not be listed in this cycle because the public RPC rate limited log queries and the Base explorer blocked the request. The PoolManager's ETH, WETH and USDC on Base are not B20 tokens: the factory returns false for isB20(USDC).

What it takes: A B20 token with market value is used in v4 pools on Base. For a freeze, the token admin, the pauser, or the admin of a policy the token references must act, or a Base hard fork must tighten enforcement for a configuration the token already has. For a seize, the issuer must hold SEIZE_ROLE and must have set SEIZE_EXEMPT_POLICY to a policy that does not authorise the PoolManager. With the slot unset no account is seizable. An unprivileged attacker cannot do any of this to a token issued by someone else. An attacker who issues their own B20 token gets no more than a hostile ERC-20 already gives on any chain.

What can be done: Pool creators and LPs should read, for every B20 currency, the three transfer policy slots, the seize policy slots, the holders of DEFAULT_ADMIN_ROLE, SEIZE_ROLE and PAUSE_ROLE, and the admin of each referenced policy, and should treat a non renounced admin as able to freeze or seize. Front ends and routers on Base can call isAuthorized on the Policy Registry for the PoolManager under each bound policy and warn when it is false or when the policy admin is live. LPs caught by a freeze can still remove liquidity and keep the frozen side as ERC-6909 claims through mint (src/PoolManager.sol:322-329), which does not call the token, and take the other currency. The deployer should track each Base upgrade changelog for B20 changes before activation.

Second reviewer's corrections: 1. The finding misses a second loss path. burnBlocked(from, amount) has existed since Beryl and is still callable. It destroys the balance of a holder that is not authorised under TRANSFER_SENDER_POLICY, and it needs BURN_BLOCKED_ROLE and an unpaused BURN feature. It does not read SEIZE_EXEMPT_POLICY. The statement that no balance can be removed while the seize slot is unset is therefore too strong: an issuer who blocks the PoolManager under the sender policy and holds BURN_BLOCKED_ROLE can burn its balance. OUSD has a sender policy bound, so this path applies to it if that role is granted. 2. The exposure is no longer hypothetical. The PoolManager holds about 5.34 million OUSD, and one account without code administers the policy that decides whether the PoolManager may send it. 3. take reverts with WrappedError that carries ERC20TransferFailed as context. 4. The claim based exit needs a caller whose unlock callback can call mint. Whether the deployed position manager offers that is outside this repository and was not checked. 5. The fork driven freeze in step 5 is a documented behaviour change. Neither of the two tokens found at the PoolManager has an executor allowlist that excludes it, so no instance of that freeze was observed. 6. PAUSE_ROLE, not the admin role, is what pauses TRANSFER. The admin can grant it.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:322-329`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/types/Currency.sol:55-86`, `src/types/Currency.sol:90-96`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57`.

Sources:

- https://docs.base.org/specifications/b20/introduction (B20 runs as precompiles in the Base node, is upgraded through hard forks, is created by anyone through the factory, and has roles, pause vectors and policies.)
- https://docs.base.org/specifications/b20/concepts/policies (Policy scopes and the account each one checks: sender, receiver, msg.sender as executor, mint receiver, seize exempt and seize receiver. Policies are shared and updated by a policy admin.)
- https://docs.base.org/base-chain/specs/reference/b20/changelog/02-cobalt-b20-seize (seizeWithMemo moves a holder balance to a destination, skips allowances and transfer policies, and works only for holders not authorised under SEIZE_EXEMPT_POLICY.)
- https://docs.base.org/base-chain/specs/reference/b20/changelog/03-denim-b20-transfer-executor-enforcement (Denim enforces TRANSFER_EXECUTOR_POLICY on msg.sender for transfer as well as transferFrom, a breaking change for tokens with a restrictive executor policy.)
- https://docs.base.org/specifications/b20/reference/constants (Precompile addresses of the factory, the Policy Registry and the Activation Registry, and the role and policy identifiers.)
- https://docs.base.org/upgrades (Activation dates of Beryl, Cobalt and Denim on Base mainnet.)
- https://mainnet.base.org/ (On chain check: eth_getCode at the factory returns empty code while eth_call isB20(PoolManager) and isB20(USDC) return false and getB20Address returns an address starting 0xb2. policyExists(2) returns true. balanceOf and transfer on an uninitialised 0xb2 address revert.)

## F-028. Blacklist and freeze roles on the pooled stablecoins can stop every pool that holds them, because one address holds all balances

High severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Optimism, Base, Unichain, Worldchain, Ink, Soneium.

What differs on the network: The issuer model matches USDC on Ethereum, but the role holders are set per chain and several of the largest PoolManager balances in this unit are tokens that Ethereum pools do not hold in this form. On Soneium the bridged USDC.e returns one address, 0x8be79275fcfd08a931087ecf70ba8a99aee3ac59, for owner, pauser and blacklister. On Ink and Unichain USD₮0 exposes isBlocked. On Ink USDG and on Soneium USDSC expose isFrozen. Circle USDC on OP Mainnet, Base, Unichain, World Chain and Ink each has its own blacklister and pauser address.

How it meets the code: 1. All pools, ERC-6909 claims and protocol fees for a currency sit in one token balance at the PoolManager address. 2. FiatToken transfer carries the modifiers whenNotPaused, notBlacklisted(msg.sender) and notBlacklisted(to). When the PoolManager pays out, it is msg.sender (src/types/Currency.sol:55-86, called from src/PoolManager.sol:291-297 and src/ProtocolFees.sol:56). 3. If the blacklister lists the PoolManager address, or the pauser pauses the token, every take of that currency reverts with ERC20TransferFailed. sync still works because balanceOf is a view call. 4. The freeze covers every pool on the chain that uses the token, regardless of which pool or user caused the listing. 5. The freeze functions on USD₮0, USDG and USDSC were confirmed to exist by eth_call. Their effect on transfers and any balance destroying function were not read from source in this cycle.

What is at risk: At read time the PoolManager held about 12.25 million USDC on Base, 978,360 USDC and 180,746 USD₮0 on Unichain, 1.52 million USD₮0, 767,333 USDG and 676,274 USDC on Ink, 1.90 million USDSC and 517,001 USDC.e on Soneium, 323,556 USDC on OP Mainnet and 66,644 USDC on World Chain. A listing of the PoolManager freezes the whole balance of that token for all LPs, claim holders and the protocol. isBlacklisted, isBlocked or isFrozen returned false for the PoolManager on every token checked.

What it takes: The token's blacklister, freezer or pauser acts against the PoolManager address or pauses the token. This is a privileged issuer action. An unprivileged attacker cannot trigger it directly. A realistic trigger is a compliance response to tainted funds that were routed through a pool.

What can be done: LPs can exit the non frozen currency normally and hold the frozen currency as ERC-6909 claims through mint (src/PoolManager.sol:322-329) until the listing is lifted. Integrators should monitor the blacklist and pause state of each large pooled token for the PoolManager address on each chain. For Soneium USDC.e, the deployer should identify who controls 0x8be79275fcfd08a931087ecf70ba8a99aee3ac59 before treating it like Circle issued USDC.

Second reviewer's corrections: 1. The effect is understated for USD₮0. The USD₮0 implementations on Unichain (0xaf37e8b6c9ed7f6318979f56fc287d76c30847ff) and Ink (0x06d886ff518b5e642e7e6aad3ce797b7dabd8e9a) contain the selectors of addToBlockedList(address) and destroyBlockedFunds(address). A blocked PoolManager could therefore lose its whole USD₮0 balance, about 1.53 million on Ink and 180,760 on Unichain, which is a loss for LPs and claim holders. I matched selectors in bytecode and did not read the source or the access control. USDSC on Soneium has freeze and unfreeze and none of the wipe selectors I tried. 2. The difference from Ethereum is thin. The Ethereum PoolManager is exposed to the same issuer roles. What is specific to this unit is the set of role holders and balances, and the single account behind all three roles of Soneium USDC.e. 3. The claim based exit depends on the contract that owns the position being able to call mint in its unlock callback. That was not checked. 4. Severity stays high: the actor is a token issuer and the frozen amount is the full balance of the token across all pools.

Code: `src/types/Currency.sol:55-86`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:322-329`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/circlefin/stablecoin-evm/master/contracts/v1/FiatTokenV1.sol (FiatTokenV1.transfer has the modifiers whenNotPaused, notBlacklisted(msg.sender) and notBlacklisted(to).)
- https://rpc.soneium.org (eth_call on USDC.e 0xbA9986D2381edf1DA03B0B9c1f8b00dc4AacC369: owner(), pauser() and blacklister() all return 0x8be79275fcfd08a931087ecf70ba8a99aee3ac59, isBlacklisted(PoolManager) is false, balanceOf(PoolManager) is about 517,001. USDSC isFrozen(PoolManager) is false, balance about 1,898,135.)
- https://rpc-gel.inkonchain.com (eth_call on Ink: USD₮0 isBlocked(PoolManager) false and balance about 1,515,475. USDG isFrozen(PoolManager) false, paused false, balance about 767,333. USDC blacklister 0x2163ed19c381a39cf9c564ee73badc35a95d7417, balance about 676,274.)
- https://mainnet.unichain.org (eth_call on Unichain: USDC blacklister 0x15dab8b3c0cab0e4d1f96259420b1d42fd3ca55a, balance about 978,360. USD₮0 isBlocked(PoolManager) false, balance about 180,746.)
- https://mainnet.base.org/ (eth_call on Base USDC: blacklister 0x1f2e3a640175d20ac31ed523b6733b977173e277, isBlacklisted(PoolManager) false, balance about 12,253,276.)
- https://mainnet.optimism.io (eth_call on OP Mainnet USDC: blacklister 0x551e855b7355c98534175879a300972856c04d7c, isBlacklisted(PoolManager) false, balance about 323,556.)
- https://worldchain-mainnet.g.alchemy.com/public (eth_call on World Chain USDC: blacklister 0xcb4f5150c828164d78e3a7c67165d2cbbcce3f50, isBlacklisted(PoolManager) false, balance about 66,644.)

## F-031. TIP-20 issuer roles can burn, freeze or pause the PoolManager balance of a Tempo stablecoin

High severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Tempo.

What differs on the network: On Tempo every stablecoin is a TIP-20 precompile with issuer roles built into the protocol. burnAt(from, amount), live on mainnet since T12 on 13 October 2026, lets a BURN_AT_ROLE holder burn any balance with no transfer policy check, unless the holder is on a protected list. burnBlocked lets a BURN_BLOCKED_ROLE holder burn the balance of an address that fails the sender policy. The protected list is FeeManager, StablecoinDEX, TIP20ChannelReserve, ReceivePolicyGuard, the token's own address and Zone Portal addresses. The PoolManager is not on it. pause stops every transfer of the token. The admin of a TIP-403 policy can block an address for every token bound to that policy, and pathUSD and OUSD are both bound to policy 2, a blacklist administered by 0x251d2711ebeb0a09fdb8992f5506f3d949175246. Ethereum has no protocol-level burn of another account's ERC-20 balance. Each token there has whatever controls its own contract code contains.

How it meets the code: 1. All pools, ERC-6909 claims and protocol fees for a token sit in one TIP-20 balance at the PoolManager address. 2. A BURN_AT_ROLE holder calls burnAt(PoolManager, x). The TIP-20 precompile debits the PoolManager and reduces total supply. No PoolManager function runs. 3. The PoolManager tracks no per-currency reserve in storage. sync reads balanceOf (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288) and take pays out with transfer (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:60-75), so the claims recorded in pool state and ERC-6909 balances now exceed the tokens held. Withdrawals succeed until the balance runs out, then transfer reverts with InsufficientBalance and the remaining LPs and claim holders cannot exit. 4. The freeze variant: the policy admin adds the PoolManager to blacklist policy 2, or a PAUSE_ROLE holder pauses the token. Every take and every inbound payment for that token then reverts with PolicyForbids or ContractPaused, and settle cannot complete because the payment transfer fails. Once the PoolManager fails the sender policy, a BURN_BLOCKED_ROLE holder can also burn its balance. 5. If a burn lands between sync and settle inside one transaction, the checked subtraction at src/PoolManager.sol:360 reverts that transaction and nothing else changes.

What is at risk: At block 50,261,382 the PoolManager held about 406,197 OUSD and 19,727 pathUSD. A burn destroys part of the shared balance and the shortfall falls on whoever withdraws last, across every pool that uses the token. A blacklist entry or a pause freezes the full balance of that token, and policy 2 covers pathUSD and OUSD at once.

What it takes: A holder of BURN_AT_ROLE, BURN_BLOCKED_ROLE or PAUSE_ROLE on the token, the token admin, or the admin of the TIP-403 policy must act. An unprivileged attacker has these roles only on tokens they created through the TIP-20 factory, which harms only pools that chose to hold that token. I could not list the current holders of BURN_AT_ROLE and BURN_BLOCKED_ROLE on OUSD and pathUSD, because the public RPC limits log queries to 100,000 blocks. I confirmed that both role constants exist on both tokens, that both tokens use policy 2, and that the PoolManager is authorized under policy 2 today.

What can be done: LPs should treat every TIP-20 as an asset its issuer can burn or freeze at the PoolManager address, and check role holders and the policy admin before providing liquidity. The deployer can ask Tempo to add the PoolManager to the protected address list for burnAt and burnBlocked, and can ask issuers to confirm in writing that the PoolManager will not be blacklisted. Interfaces can warn when a pool token has a BURN_AT_ROLE holder.

Second reviewer's corrections: The finding could not list role holders. I read every RoleMembershipUpdated log on both tokens from block 0 to block 50,274,300 in 100,000 block windows. There are 16 events, and none grants BURN_AT_ROLE, BURN_BLOCKED_ROLE, PAUSE_ROLE or UNPAUSE_ROLE. hasRole returns false for those roles on every account that appears in the logs. So nobody holds a burn or pause role on OUSD today. pathUSD had one admin set at genesis without an event, so I cannot exclude a genesis holder there. The roles are one transaction away. 0x79c6631f...4a4e holds DEFAULT_ADMIN_ROLE on both tokens, getRoleAdmin returns DEFAULT_ADMIN_ROLE for all of them, and the simulation shows it can grant itself a role and use it. That account and the policy 2 admin both carry 23 bytes of code, the EIP-7702 designator 0xef0100 followed by 0x000000fb7702036ff9f76044a501ac1aa74cbab16b. They are signer-controlled accounts and I did not find out how many signers control them. Step 4 says every pool operation stops. The transfers stop. Swaps and liquidity changes that settle with ERC-6909 mint and burn (src/PoolManager.sol:322-336) still run, so holders can trade a frozen token for another token inside the PoolManager while other reserves last. The severity stays high under the scale: a token issuer or policy admin must act, and the whole balance of the token is in reach.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-75`, `src/types/Currency.sol:90-96`.

Sources:

- https://tempo.com/developers/docs/protocol/tip20/spec.md (burnAt and burnBlocked semantics, the protected address list, pause behaviour and the role list)
- https://tempo.com/developers/docs/protocol/upgrades/t12.md (burnAt was added in T12, active on mainnet since 13 October 2026)
- https://tempo.com/developers/docs/protocol/tip403/spec.md (policy types, policy admin powers and the shared registry at 0x403c000000000000000000000000000000000000)
- https://rpc.mainnet.tempo.xyz (eth_call at block 50,260,093: pathUSD and OUSD transferPolicyId() = 2, policyData(2) = (BLACKLIST, 0x251d2711ebeb0a09fdb8992f5506f3d949175246), isAuthorized(2, PoolManager) = true, BURN_AT_ROLE() and BURN_BLOCKED_ROLE() return constants on both tokens, balanceOf(PoolManager) for both tokens)

## F-032. A pause of the USDC token on Arc stops the ERC-20 face and leaves native transfers open over the same PoolManager balance

High severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Arc.

What differs on the network: The pause flag of USDC on Arc lives in the token contract and guards transfer, transferFrom, mint, burn and the signature-based entry points with whenNotPaused. Native value transfers are checked by the EVM against the blocklist only. On Ethereum a paused USDC has no second way to move.

How it meets the code: 1. The pauser (0x51fa31e029abf0ce307bca9ee6bf48aed6345212) calls pause on the USDC proxy. 2. take of currency 0x3600 calls transfer and reverts with 'Pausable: paused' (src/types/Currency.sol:55-86), and no payer can move ERC-20 USDC to the PoolManager, so settle for 0x3600 credits nothing (src/PoolManager.sol:358-360). collectProtocolFees for 0x3600 reverts as well (src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57). 3. take of address(0) still pays with a value call (src/types/Currency.sol:44-52) and settle with msg.value still works (src/PoolManager.sol:352-354). 4. Claims and pool balances are recorded per currency id, so holders of 0x3600 claims cannot leave while holders of address(0) claims on the same balance can. A pool that pairs the two faces becomes the only exit for 0x3600 holders, at whatever price that pool reaches.

What is at risk: Every pool and claim denominated in 0x3600 is frozen for the length of the pause. At block 32,584,426 the PoolManager held 2,445,327.82 USDC in total for both faces. I did not split that figure between the two ids. Native-denominated pools keep working.

What it takes: The USDC pauser, a Circle role, must pause the token. An ordinary account cannot reach this path. The asymmetry was reproduced with eth_call and a state override of the paused bit and no transaction was sent.

What can be done: Liquidity providers who want an exit during a token pause can hold USDC positions under address(0). Interfaces should state which face a pool uses.

Second reviewer's corrections: The statement that settle for 0x3600 credits nothing during a pause holds for ERC-20 payments. sync still works because balanceOf is not paused, and Arc keeps a SELFDESTRUCT that moves the balance to its target (crates/evm/src/opcode.rs), so native value forced into the PoolManager after sync(0x3600) would still be credited under 0x3600. This opens no exit for 0x3600 holders and does not change the verdict. The actor issuer is right: only the pauser role can trigger it.

Code: `src/types/Currency.sol:44-52`, `src/types/Currency.sol:55-86`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57`.

Sources:

- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/5042/0xc6ad664ac6679f4ce74e10e91449c93ec1ae3ca6?fields=sources,abi,compilation (whenNotPaused on transfer, transferFrom, mint, burn, transferWithAuthorization and receiveWithAuthorization of NativeFiatTokenV2_2)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/circlefin/arc-node/main/crates/evm/src/evm.rs (check_blocklist_and_create_log tests the zero address, the blocklist and self-destructed recipients on value-bearing frames and reads no pause flag)
- https://rpc.mainnet.arc.io (eth_call at block 32,584,904 with slot 1 of 0x3600 overridden to set the paused bit: USDC.transfer from and to the PoolManager reverts with 'Pausable: paused', a native send from the PoolManager succeeds, fee adapter collect succeeds for address(0) and reverts for 0x3600)

## F-036. Issuer block lists on USDC, USDT0, PYUSD and cbBTC cover the single PoolManager address on Arbitrum One

High severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Arbitrum One.

What differs on the network: This is the same issuer power that exists on Ethereum, and cycle 1 did not record it for Arbitrum One. Native USDC, USDT0, PYUSD and cbBTC on Arbitrum One each have a role that can block an address, and USDT0 has an owner function that destroys the balance of a blocked address. The PoolManager holds every pool's balance of each token at one address.

How it meets the code: Step 1: the role holder lists the PoolManager address. Step 2: the token's transfer then fails when the PoolManager is the sender. Currency.transfer reverts on a failed call, so every take and every protocol fee collection in that token reverts. Where the token also rejects a listed recipient, a settle cannot be funded either. I did not test which of the four tokens check the recipient. Step 3: liquidity providers can still remove liquidity and keep the blocked side as ERC-6909 claims through mint, which moves no tokens, but the claims cannot be turned into tokens while the block stands. Step 4, USDT0 only: the owner calls destroyBlockedFunds for the PoolManager, which lowers balanceOf(PoolManager) with no matching delta. Pool reserves, claims and accrued fees then exceed the balance and the last withdrawers bear the shortfall.

What is at risk: All pools and claims in the listed token. At the time of the queries the PoolManager held 8.99M USDC, 0.98M USDT0, 28,236 PYUSD and 14.8 cbBTC.

What it takes: The blacklister of USDC (0xac5b4946a8c29eafb42e1742b3ab82e6d5771329), the owner of USDT0 (0x4dff9b5b0143e642a3f63a5bcf2d1c328e600bf8), the PYUSD role holder or the cbBTC blacklister must act. All four tokens report the PoolManager as not blocked today. An arbitrary caller is rejected by each of these functions.

What can be done: None in the PoolManager. Liquidity providers can watch the block list views for the PoolManager address. A blocked side can be held as claims until the block is lifted.

Second reviewer's corrections: The balances moved since the research queries. For USDC the block applies to transfers in both directions. The actor and the severity stand: only the role holders can act, and the scale puts a freeze that needs a token issuer at high.

Code: `src/types/Currency.sol:53-87`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:322-329`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`.

Sources:

- https://arb1.arbitrum.io/rpc eth_call blacklister(), isBlacklisted(PoolManager), blacklist(PoolManager) from an arbitrary sender on USDC 0xaf88d065e77c8cC2239327C5EDb3A432268e5831, queried 2026-11-22 (USDC has a blacklister, the PoolManager is not listed, and only the blacklister can list it.)
- https://arb1.arbitrum.io/rpc eth_call isBlocked(PoolManager), owner(), addToBlockedList(PoolManager) and destroyBlockedFunds(PoolManager) from an arbitrary sender on USDT0 0xFd086bC7CD5C481DCC9C85ebE478A1C0b69FCbb9, queried 2026-11-22 (USDT0 has an owner gated block list and an owner gated function that destroys a blocked balance, and the PoolManager is not blocked.)
- https://arb1.arbitrum.io/rpc eth_call isFrozen(PoolManager) and freeze(PoolManager) on PYUSD 0x46850aD61C2B7d64d08c9C754F45254596696984, isBlacklisted(PoolManager) and blacklister() on cbBTC 0xcbB7C0000aB88B473b1f5aFd9ef808440eed33Bf, and balanceOf(PoolManager) on all four, queried 2026-11-22 (PYUSD freeze is role gated, cbBTC has a blacklister, the PoolManager is not listed on either, and the balances are as stated.)

## F-056. The GoodDollar owner has blocked the PoolManager address on Celo, so 550 million G$ held for pools cannot leave

High severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Celo.

What differs on the network: G$ on Celo is a Superfluid Super Token with an owner controlled list of accounts that can neither send nor receive it, and with an owner function that burns from any account. After the Superfluid incident of September 2026 the owner put the Uniswap v4 PoolManager on that list and has not removed it. Ethereum's PoolManager holds no token in this state.

How it meets the code: 1. On 11 September 2026 at 13:48:08 UTC (Celo block 77,233,730, transaction 0x22cd34a5f179dfa342fd3659d029e4d40469ca8fa02abd032145f162b0b8dd4d, sent by 0x6823a04a6fd4148167567d5ef6dcca1f34db0494 to the contract 0xa2521e8f5a990903fdd8afa6570c52526e7e0d5e) the token emitted BlockedUpdated with value true for eleven addresses, one of which is the PoolManager 0x288dc841A52FCA2707c6947B3A777c5E56cd87BC. setBlocked is restricted to the owner role. isBlocked(PoolManager) returned true at block 83,475,285 on 22 November 2026. 2. Every ERC-20, ERC-677 and ERC-777 movement of G$ runs _processFees, which reverts with SUPER_GOODDOLLAR_BLOCKED (0xe7d0c09e) when the sender or the recipient is blocked. An eth_call of transfer from the PoolManager reverts with that selector for amounts 1 and 0. A transfer of 1 unit from the owner account to the PoolManager reverts the same way, and the same transfer to another address returns true. 3. take debits the caller and then calls transfer on the currency (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297). A failed transfer makes take revert (src/types/Currency.sol:64-86). Every take of G$ therefore reverts, including a take of zero. The PoolManager's 550,022,104.456 G$ stay where they are. 4. A position owner who removes liquidity from a G$ pool receives a positive G$ delta. The owner can take the other currency. For the G$ side the remaining choices are mint, which turns the delta into an ERC-6909 claim (src/PoolManager.sol:322-329), and clear, which gives it up (src/PoolManager.sol:310-319). A swap that buys G$ ends the same way. 5. A payment of G$ into the PoolManager by transfer reverts as well, so a seller needs a G$ claim to burn (src/PoolManager.sol:332-336). The token source says a blocked account can still be credited through Superfluid agreements. Such a credit would raise balanceOf(PoolManager), and sync followed by settle would credit it to the settler (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288, 349-364). I did not simulate that path. 6. The owner can also call adminBurn(account, amount), which skips allowance, hooks, pause and the block list. A burn of the PoolManager's balance would leave pool liquidity and G$ claims without tokens behind them. Superfluid's report says 544 million G$ was burned elsewhere during the recovery.

What is at risk: 550,022,104 G$, which is 13.7 percent of the G$ supply and about 60,000 USD at the price Blockscout shows (0.00010924 USD). GoodDollar names one position holder with two wallets as the depositor. Because all pools share one address, the block also covers every other G$ pool in this PoolManager, every G$ claim and any protocol fee accrued in G$. The paired currencies of those pools can still be withdrawn.

What it takes: None remain. The block is in force. It ends when an account with the G$ owner role calls setBlocked with false for the PoolManager. owner() returns 0x495d133b938596c9984d462f007b676bdc57ecec. GoodDollar states it will do so once the position holder contacts Good Labs or moves the position to the Good Labs Safe.

What can be done: The deployer cannot lift the block. Interfaces should stop routing through G$ pools on Celo and should show G$ positions as withdrawable only as claims. Liquidity providers can remove liquidity, take the paired currency and mint the G$ side as a claim. Integrations should not value G$ claims or G$ pool liquidity at the market price while the block and the admin burn function exist. New deployments should treat any token with a per address block list as able to freeze every pool of that token at once.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor value issuer is right. The block was set by the G$ owner role, an ordinary account can neither set nor lift it, and no step lets an ordinary account take funds. The mitigation that tells liquidity providers to mint the G$ side as a claim does not hold for positions held through the PositionManager. In core the position owner is the msg.sender of modifyLiquidity (src/PoolManager.sol:161), so only the contract that opened a position can remove it. The runtime code of the Celo PositionManager 0xf7965f3981e4d5bc383bfbcb61501763e9068ca9 (23,877 bytes) contains the selectors of take, clear, settle, sync, modifyLiquidity and unlock and does not contain the selector of mint(address,uint256,uint256) (0x156e29f6) or of burn(address,uint256,uint256) (0xf5298aca). A holder of such a position who removes liquidity must close the G$ delta with take, which reverts, or with clear, which forfeits the G$. I did not establish whether the PositionManager can move the G$ delta into another position inside the same unlock. setBlocked takes an array of accounts. At today's Blockscout price the balance is worth about 63,000 USD. The statement that no later unblock event exists was not checked by a full log scan here.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:64-86`, `src/PoolManager.sol:310-336`, `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`.

Sources:

- https://forno.celo.org eth_call isBlocked(address) on 0x62B8B11039FcfE5aB0C56E502b1C372A3d2a9c7A with the PoolManager address, block 83475285 (isBlocked(PoolManager) returns true)
- https://forno.celo.org eth_call transfer(address,uint256) on 0x62B8B11039FcfE5aB0C56E502b1C372A3d2a9c7A from the PoolManager and from the owner account, block 83475323 (transfers from and to the PoolManager revert with 0xe7d0c09e, and a transfer between two other accounts returns true)
- https://celo.blockscout.com/api?module=logs&action=getLogs&fromBlock=0&toBlock=latest&address=0x62B8B11039FcfE5aB0C56E502b1C372A3d2a9c7A&topic0=0x148a86796e0fc2ba3270f94c00ce0af947ccc7a4db4269c66c9dd1eb883e90a5 (one BlockedUpdated event for the PoolManager, at block 77,233,730 with value true, and no later event that unblocks it)
- https://celo.blockscout.com/api/v2/smart-contracts/0xb4419a3df33b9aa6fbe452df8816389e599c29ef (verified source of SuperGoodDollar with isBlocked, setBlocked, adminBurn, _processFees and the agreement guards)
- https://discourse.gooddollar.org/t/gooddollar-incident-post-mortem-september-4-attack/9164 (the Celo Uniswap v4 pool holds 550 million G$ and remains blocked, and the condition GoodDollar sets for unblocking)
- https://forum.superfluid.org/t/security-council-preliminary-disclosure-of-a-security-incident-that-impacted-g-liquidity-on-celo-network/1743 (cause of the incident, the unbacked amount and the burn of 544 million G$)

## F-057. Issuers of the tokens that make up most pool value on Celo and X Layer can pause, block, burn or upgrade the PoolManager's balance

High severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Celo, X Layer.

What differs on the network: The pooled value on these two networks sits in issuer controlled tokens that are small or new: wBRL and wARS on Celo, and USDT0, USDG, two USDC contracts and tokenized stocks on X Layer. On Celo the PoolManager holds 83.7 percent of all wBRL and 59.0 percent of all wARS. Each of these tokens has roles that act on a single holder address.

How it meets the code: 1.
