All pools keep a currency in one balance at the PoolManager address, and that balance leaves only through transfer inside take (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:64-86). A role that stops transfers for that one address stops every pool of the token. 2. wBRL and wARS are proxies of LatamStable. Its compliance role can switch the blocklist on and block the PoolManager, after which _update reverts for it as sender and as recipient. Its pauser role stops all transfers. Its upgrader role can replace the implementation through UUPS and with it the balance logic. The blocklist is switched off today and neither token is paused. 3. On X Layer USDT0 has addToBlockedList and destroyBlockedFunds, USDG has freeze and wipeFrozenAddress, both USDC contracts have a blacklister and a pause, and xETH has a deny list and a pause. A wipe or a destroy sets the PoolManager balance to zero while pool accounting and ERC-6909 claims still count the tokens. 4. BMNRx and ANTx report balanceOf as shares times a multiplier that a multiplier updater role sets, and a fee per period can lower the multiplier over time. A lower multiplier lowers balanceOf(PoolManager) without a take, which leaves pools with less than their accounting says (assumption A2). A higher multiplier adds tokens that no settle credits. The multiplier is 1.0 and the fee is 0 today. 5. On Celo the owner of USDm, which is Celo Governance, can name operators and burners that move or burn the balance of any account. 6. core has no function that reacts to any of these actions. Liquidity providers who exit first after a partial loss take the remaining tokens, and later exits revert when the balance runs out.

What is at risk: On Celo about 522,000 USD of wBRL and 129,000 USD of wARS at the prices Blockscout shows, plus the USDC paired with them. On X Layer about 5.97 million USDT0, 459,000 USDG and 97,700 USDC, plus the 4.67 million USDe that trade against USDT0 in the largest pool. USDe itself has no block or pause function in its code. A freeze stops withdrawals of the affected token for every pool. A wipe, burn or lower multiplier is a loss shared by all liquidity providers and claim holders of that token.

What it takes: An action by a role holder of the token: compliance, pauser or upgrader for wBRL and wARS, the owner of USDT0 (0x4dff9b5b0143e642a3f63a5bcf2d1c328e600bf8), the asset protection role of USDG, the blacklister or pauser of each USDC contract, the multiplier updater or owner of the stock tokens, and the owner of USDm, which is Celo Governance (0xD533Ca259b330c7A88f74E000a3FaEa2d63B7972). No such flag is set for the PoolManager on either chain at blocks 83,476,610 (Celo) and 76,608,329 (X Layer).

What can be done: Liquidity providers should size positions in these tokens with the issuer roles in mind, and pool interfaces should show them. Monitoring should watch the PoolManager address in each token's block, freeze and pause state and the stock token multiplier. Pools in rebasing stock tokens should use the wrapped ERC-4626 versions, which keep a fixed balance. The deployer cannot remove any of these roles.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor value issuer is right. Every step needs a role holder of the token, and for USDm it needs Celo Governance. wARS does not point at the implementation 0xfbab5dcb1647787ec8402a6509add256fa6c7f9d that the finding cites. It points at 0xba0030bba7112171a8a5bcc417ee1994051321b9, a second deployment with the same verified source. The USDG balance has fallen from 459,142 to 220,723 since the finding was written. The severity follows the scale entry for a freeze or loss that needs a token issuer. This is the same hazard class as the issuer findings of earlier cycles on other chains and should be merged with them as a per chain token table.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:64-96`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`.

Sources:

- https://celo.blockscout.com/api/v2/smart-contracts/0xfbab5dcb1647787ec8402a6509add256fa6c7f9d (verified source of LatamStable with the pauser, minter, compliance and upgrader roles and the blocklist check in _update)
- https://forno.celo.org eth_call totalSupply(), balanceOf(PoolManager), enforceBlocklist(), isBlocked(address), paused() on wBRL and wARS, block 83475210 (PoolManager share of supply and the current flag values)
- https://rpc.xlayer.tech eth_getCode selector scan of USDT0, USDG, USDC, xETH, BMNRx and USDe implementations and eth_call isBlocked, isFrozen, isBlacklisted, paused, denyList, multiplier, feePerPeriod, block 76608329 (which control functions each token has and that none is set for the PoolManager)
- https://api.openchain.xyz/signature-database/v1/lookup (function names for the selectors found in the token bytecode)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/mento-protocol/mento-core/develop/contracts/tokens/StableTokenV3.sol (sendToPool for operators and burn(address,uint256) for burners in the USDm implementation)
- https://celo.blockscout.com/api/v2/addresses/0x288dc841A52FCA2707c6947B3A777c5E56cd87BC/tokens?type=ERC-20 (token list and prices used for the Celo amounts)

## F-061. A two signature Safe can pause ten wrapped stock tokens or list the PoolManager as sanctioned, which would stop pools that hold 7.9 million dollars on Monad

High severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Monad.

What differs on the network: On Monad the second largest asset class at the PoolManager is a set of wrapped stock tokens (wSPYx, wSPCXx, wTSLAx, wQQQx, wEWYx, wSMHx, wNVDAx, wSKHYx, wMUx, wANTx). The PoolManager holds between 89.4 and 99.8 percent of the supply of each. Every transfer of these tokens checks a pause flag and a sanctions list that the issuer controls. cbBTC on Monad adds freeze, pause and burn roles. The exposure comes from the tokens that make up the pooled value on this network. The Monad client plays no part in it.

How it meets the code: 1. All pools of a currency share one balance at the PoolManager address, and take pays out with currency.transfer and reverts when the token call fails (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:60-86). 2. The wrapper's transfer hook requires that isPaused is false and that neither sender nor recipient is on the sanctions list of the underlying stock token (WrappedBackedTokenImplementation.sol lines 178 to 193). 3. setPause can be called by the pauser, a Safe with threshold 2 of 4. The sanctions list at 0x615dd3b9445a94334c1579f68115042d77cc7c44 has addToSanctionsList and is owned by a Safe with threshold 2 of 3, which also owns the wrappers and the proxy admin and can replace the wrapper code. 4. If the pauser pauses a wrapper, or the owner lists the PoolManager address, every take of that wrapper reverts, so no liquidity removal and no swap that pays out the wrapper can settle in any pool of that token. Transfers into the PoolManager revert as well, so settle cannot be funded. 5. isPaused is false for all ten wrappers and isSanctioned(PoolManager) is false today. 6. cbBTC (40.0 coins at the PoolManager, 43 percent of supply) has freeze(address), pause() and burn(address,uint256) behind FREEZER_ROLE, PAUSER_ROLE and BURNER_ROLE. A freeze of the PoolManager or a pause has the same effect, and a burn would leave pool accounting above the balance. 7. A live example of this class exists at small size. Curvance has paused the market manager 0xbc4cd7bbd8d38027a88838b88ba04561fa778c35, and a transfer of cWBTC or ceBTC from the PoolManager reverts today with MarketManager__Paused(). Five pools use each of the two tokens, and the PoolManager holds about 83 dollars of each. 8. No ordinary account can trigger any of these steps. A third party cannot move the PoolManager's wrapper shares, because redeem and withdraw spend an allowance of the owner (lines 265 to 273) and delegatedTransfer needs an ECDSA signature that recovers to the owner address (lines 112 to 131).

What is at risk: A pause or a sanctions entry freezes about 7.9 million dollars of wrapped stock tokens together with the other side of those pools while it lasts, for every liquidity provider in them. A freeze or pause of cbBTC does the same for about 2.5 million dollars of cbBTC. A burn of the PoolManager's cbBTC would leave the last providers unpaid. The Curvance pause already locks about 170 dollars.

What it takes: Two signers of the pauser Safe or of the owner Safe act, or the holder of a cbBTC role acts. No action by an ordinary account leads there.

What can be done: Liquidity providers should treat the issuer's pause and sanctions rights as a condition of these pools. The deployer can list these tokens in user documentation as issuer controlled. Core cannot route around a token that rejects transfers from the PoolManager.

This entry adds to F-015.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor value issuer is right and the severity high fits the scale. The impact statement is too wide at one point. A pause or sanctions entry does not freeze the other side of those pools at the level of the PoolManager. A liquidity provider who can call the PoolManager directly can remove liquidity, take the other currency and mint ERC-6909 claims for the blocked wrapper (src/PoolManager.sol:322-329), and a swap that pays out the wrapper can be closed the same way. What is frozen is the wrapper itself: no take and no settle of it works until the issuer lifts the block. Whether providers who hold positions through the deployed PositionManager can use this route depends on that contract, which is outside this repository and was not read. Two of the ten wrappers (wNVDAx and wEWYx) were not located by me, so the statement that all ten share the roles rests on eight read here and on the research file for the other two. The burn right on cbBTC was confirmed as a selector and a role check only. I did not read which accounts hold the cbBTC roles.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-86`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/backed-fi/backed-token-contract/main/contracts/WrappedBackedTokenImplementation.sol (Transfer hook with pause and sanctions checks, setPause by the pauser, delegatedTransfer with ECDSA recovery, redeem spending the owner's allowance)
- rpc https://rpc.monad.xyz eth_call owner(), pauser(), isPaused(), asset(), sanctionsList(), isSanctioned(PoolManager), getThreshold(), getOwners(), balanceOf(PoolManager), totalSupply() and EIP-1967 slots for the ten wrappers at block 124,616,006 (Roles, Safe thresholds 2 of 3 and 2 of 4, pause state false, PoolManager share of supply 89.4 to 99.8 percent)
- https://api.geckoterminal.com/api/v2/simple/networks/monad/token_price/0xe7e553cd128f0011777323a0b44a7b96ea1cb540 (Prices used for the 7.92 million dollar total and for cbBTC at 61,511 dollars)
- rpc https://rpc.monad.xyz eth_call from the PoolManager address: transfer on cWBTC 0xdb3e888c3b50771821226d30ab6ec14eb5ba85ba and ceBTC 0x2840772e14ffbe337ab966727b7d1dd09bdc76e4 returns error 0xf47323f4 (Transfers of the two Curvance tokens from the PoolManager revert with MarketManager__Paused() today)
- rpc https://rpc.monad.xyz eth_getCode of cbBTC implementation 0xd1611e6e93ec512bfbd2b6428a26c463c1dce6f9 and eth_call paused(), isFrozen(PoolManager) (cbBTC selectors freeze, pause, burn(address,uint256) and the three roles, current state not paused and not frozen)

## F-065. A TIP-20 issuer has already frozen a pooled token on Tempo by setting the always reject transfer policy

High severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Tempo.

What differs on the network: A TIP-20 token carries a transfer policy id that its admin can change at any time. Policy 0 is built in and rejects every sender and recipient.

How it meets the code: 1. GABAGOOL at 0x20c000000000000000000000030a4c7f7b8f3586 was used as a currency in two pools of the PoolManager, which still holds 600,000 base units (0.6 tokens). 2. transferPolicyId() on the token now returns 0, and isAuthorized(0, PoolManager) is false. 3. take calls transfer on the token (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:55-86). The precompile reverts with PolicyForbids and the take reverts with the wrapped ERC20TransferFailed error. I simulated a take of 1 unit and of the full balance. Both revert. 4. A holder of a position in those pools can still remove liquidity, take the other currency and turn the GABAGOOL delta into an ERC-6909 claim or clear it (src/PoolManager.sol:310-329). The GABAGOOL itself cannot leave. 5. Of the 73 currencies that Tempo pools name, this is the only one where a take of the PoolManager balance fails today.

What is at risk: This instance concerns 0.6 tokens of a token with a supply of 310.6. It shows that the issuer control described in F-031 is used in practice on this network. The same single call by the policy admin of OUSD or pathUSD would stop takes of 406,692 OUSD and 19,629 pathUSD.

What it takes: The admin of the token calls changeTransferPolicyId, or the admin of the policy changes its lists. No action by an ordinary account produces this state.

What can be done: List the transfer policy id and its admin for each pooled TIP-20 in the deployment notes and monitor changes. Liquidity providers should treat TIP-20 tokens with an active admin as revocable.

This entry adds to F-031.

Second reviewer's corrections: Both GABAGOOL pools carry the hook 0x55aeb9ebfa15c28ff9d9a675e8574a5bbe186aa8 (21,648 bytes of code). Its address has the beforeInitialize, beforeAddLiquidity, beforeRemoveLiquidity, beforeSwap, beforeDonate and beforeSwapReturnsDelta flags, so step 4 of the mechanism also depends on that hook accepting the removal (src/libraries/Hooks.sol:195-206). The finding does not mention the hook. TIP20Factory.create_token has no role check (crates/precompiles/src/tip20_factory/mod.rs lines 98 to 149), so the admin of GABAGOOL is whoever created it. For a token that an attacker issues and that victims then choose to pool, the schema class is pool-creator. The class issuer stays right for pathUSD, OUSD and the other stablecoins. The cited registry file holds the interface only. The checks that a transfer runs and the admin check for a policy change are in crates/precompiles/src/tip20/mod.rs. pathUSD and OUSD share policy 2, a blacklist with one admin, so one entry for the PoolManager stops takes of both tokens at once. The OUSD balance is 408,832 at my block, where the finding has 406,692.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:55-86`, `src/PoolManager.sol:310-329`.

Sources:

- https://rpc.mainnet.tempo.xyz (eth_call transferPolicyId() returns 0 on the token, isAuthorized(0, PoolManager) returns false, balanceOf(PoolManager) returns 600000, and a simulated take through unlock reverts with 0x54cfe659 inside the WrappedError of the PoolManager)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/tempoxyz/tempo/697ea97/crates/contracts/src/precompiles/tip403_registry.rs (the registry interface with WHITELIST, BLACKLIST and COMPOUND policies and the authorisation checks a transfer runs)

## F-080. Three issuers have blocked the Ethereum PoolManager address and two pooled tokens are paused, so yUSD, yETH, xSolvBTC, KITE and NTX held for pools cannot leave today

High severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Ethereum.

What differs on the network: This is an observed state of the hazard that the stablecoin findings describe for Ethereum. The full balance transfer test of this unit ran over 60,024 currencies of the Ethereum PoolManager and 1,119 refused. Six of the refusals carry a price above 100,000 USD at coins.llama.fi. Five of them have an issuer cause, a block on the PoolManager address or a pause of the token. The sixth, VIBESTR 0xd0cc2b0efb168bfe1f94a948d8df70fa10257196 (about 150,000 USD), rejects every nonzero transfer made outside its own trading flow, which belongs to the author gated class recorded in earlier cycles.

How it meets the code: 1. take pays with transfer from the single PoolManager address (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:60-75). 2. eth_call with from set to the PoolManager at block 26,476,678. yUSD 0x19ebd191f7a24ece672ba13a302212b5ef7f35cb and yETH 0x8464f6ecae1ea58ec816c13f964030eab8ec123a revert with the message blacklisted for every amount including zero, in both directions, while a zero transfer between two other accounts succeeds. xSolvBTC 0xd9d920aa40f578ab794426f5c90f6c731d159def reverts with error 0xbb70159d carrying the PoolManager address, and isBlacklisted(PoolManager) returns true while it returns false for another account. KITE 0x904567252d8f48555b7447c67dca23f0372e16be reverts with EnforcedPause (0xd93c0665) and NTX 0xf0d33beda4d734c72684b5f9abbebf715d0a7935 with the message ERC20Pausable: token transfer while paused, for every sender. 3. Every take of these five currencies reverts, and for the three blocked tokens a payment into the PoolManager reverts as well, so settle cannot complete. A position holder can still remove liquidity and turn the delta of the stuck currency into an ERC-6909 claim (src/PoolManager.sol:322-329) and take the other currency.

What is at risk: Balances at the Ethereum PoolManager and their value at coins.llama.fi prices: 677,988 yUSD (about 677,000 USD), 62.69 yETH (about 102,000 USD), 3.2104 xSolvBTC (about 197,000 USD), 3,755,211 KITE (about 530,000 USD) and 95,177,682 NTX (about 163,000 USD). The sum is about 1.67 million USD that liquidity providers and claim holders of these pools cannot withdraw until the issuer lifts the block or the pause. I did not check since when each state holds or how many pools are affected.

What it takes: An issuer or token admin decision. No unprivileged party can cause or lift it. Whether the yUSD and yETH block is an entry for the PoolManager address or a rule that covers a class of addresses is not confirmed, because the tokens answer none of the usual list getters. The prices come from one service and were not cross checked.

What can be done: None in core. Interfaces should mark pools whose currency rejects transfers from the PoolManager. The new deployment has a new address, so these blocks do not carry over, and the same issuers can block it in the same way.

This entry adds to F-011.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor issuer is right. No ordinary account can cause or lift these states. This is an observed state of the hazard already recorded in F-011 and F-056 and should be merged into the table of live cases that the cycle 4 critic asked for. The xSolvBTC value is about 194,000 USD at the price I fetched, against 197,000 USD in the finding.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-75`, `src/PoolManager.sol:322-329`.

Sources:

- src/types/Currency.sol:60-75 (take calls transfer on the token with the PoolManager as sender and reverts when the token reverts.)
- https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com (eth_call at block 26,476,678 with from set to the PoolManager and to two other accounts: the revert data and the isBlacklisted readings quoted in the mechanism; balanceOf(PoolManager) of the five tokens.)
- research/network-risk/cycles/005/theme-assigned-balance.work/raw/xfer_1.json.gz:1 (Full balance transfer test at blocks 26,476,612 to 26,476,613: 60,024 currencies tested, 1,119 refused.)
- https://coins.llama.fi/prices/current/ethereum:0x19ebd191f7a24ece672ba13a302212b5ef7f35cb (Prices used for the five values, fetched in bulk through research/network-risk/cycles/005/theme-assigned-balance.work/prices.py.)

## F-088. Issuer controls on USDG and Dinari USD+ cover 2.78M more in the Arbitrum One PoolManager, and the USD+ operator can lower the pooled balance by a rebase

High severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Arbitrum One.

What differs on the network: F-036 lists USDC, USDT0, PYUSD and cbBTC. A ranking of all priced pool currencies shows two more issuer controlled tokens among the six largest positions. Global Dollar (USDG) has a freeze list and a pause. Dinari USD+ keeps balances as shares times an index, checks every transfer against a restrictor contract, and lets the holder of its operator role raise or lower the index.

How it meets the code: USDG: the issuer freezes the PoolManager address or pauses the token. transfer then fails with the PoolManager as sender, Currency.transfer reverts, and every take and every fee collection in USDG reverts, as F-036 describes for the other tokens. USD+: step 1, the owner of the restrictor 0x2ed5c85d5105abe5d13586c5c3a8f99a837942b4 lists the PoolManager. checkTransferRestricted runs before every transfer, so payouts and payments in USD+ revert. Step 2, separately, the operator calls rebaseSub. balanceOf(PoolManager) is sharesOf(PoolManager) times balancePerShare, so it falls in proportion while pools, claims and accrued fees keep their recorded amounts. The last withdrawers find less USD+ than the pools record. Step 3, in the other direction rebaseAdd raises the balance. The index stands at 1.0707, so about 70,678 USD+ of the 1,070,678 held came from index growth after deposit, on the assumption that the 999,999.89 shares were deposited near an index of 1. No settle is credited for that growth, because a sync and a settle in one transaction read the same index, and the rebase functions revert for an ordinary account. The surplus belongs to no pool. I read rebaseSub in the public Dinari repository, whose current version takes one argument. The deployed version 6.0.0 takes a second bytes32 argument and rejects ordinary callers with AC: unauthorized. The implementation 0xff5bff8081eaee966bd74e9c0c059949df2345e1 is not verified on Sourcify and the Blockscout API was not reachable, so the deployed body of rebaseSub is unconfirmed.

What is at risk: All pools and claims in USDG and USD+. The PoolManager holds 1,712,030.85 USDG and 1,070,677.64 USD+, which is 34.4 percent of the USD+ supply. A freeze stops withdrawals in the token. A downward rebase takes a share of the pooled USD+ from the last liquidity providers to withdraw.

What it takes: The Paxos asset protection role for USDG. For USD+ the owner of the transfer restrictor (0x269e944ad9140fc6e21794e8ea71ce1afbfe38c8) or the holder of the operator role of the token. No ordinary account can act.

What can be done: Liquidity providers should treat a USD+ pool as exposed to the issuer's index and restrictor. Interfaces can label both tokens as issuer controlled. The deployer cannot remove the exposure in core, because one address holds every pool's balance.

This entry adds to F-036.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor is right: every lever needs a Paxos role, the Dinari restrictor role, or a signature that 0xb7541f225385104347539397a4e01abc1d4ea8bd accepts. Four statements need correcting. First, the mechanism says about 70,678 USD+ of the pooled balance is index growth that belongs to no pool. That is wrong. The whole balance arrived in one transfer at block 425,967,321, after the last BalancePerShareSet event (block 425,539,068). Transfer logs give a net inflow of 1,070,699.885347 and the balance is 1,070,677.639195, so the PoolManager is 22.25 USD+ short of what the pools were credited, and there is no surplus. Second, the lever that moved the index is a raw storage write to the token, authorised by an off-chain signature and submitted through a public contract. It can write the index slot and, by its shape, any other slot named in the call. The finding describes only the role gated rebase functions, and the statement that the index did not move rests on an event that this route does not emit. The call is open to any sender: an eth_call of the first transaction's calldata from an unrelated address at block 425,966,863 succeeds, both through Multicall3 and directly. A tampered signature or payload reverts, a zero signature reverts with ECDSAInvalidSignature, and both used calls revert from every sender after their first execution, so they cannot be replayed. I could not read the contract (no verified source for 0xb7541f..., its implementation 0x1b5a16e81ab8154559ec2f457fa61062a6b8876f or the token implementation), so the signer, the digest format and its other functions are unconfirmed. Third, USDG on Arbitrum One is the Paxos claimable rewards build, and the asset protection role also holds wipeFrozenAddress, so the issuer can burn a frozen balance as well as block it. On the restrictor, restrict(address) needs RESTRICTOR_ROLE, which the admin 0x269e... grants. Fourth, the value of the USD+ position is face value. All of it sits in one hookless USDC/USD+ pool (id 0x12d6df73..., fee 0.3 percent), in one position of the account 0xcd04ccbf1cd5d18c9735b59f5e41f03a2941ebad between ticks 1080 and 3540, and the pool price is at tick 3539, which is 0.70 USDC per USD+. The title's 2.78M is 1.69M USDG plus 1.07M USD+ at par today.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-365`, `src/types/Currency.sol:53-87`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57`.

Sources:

- https://arb1.arbitrum.io/rpc (eth_call on 2026-11-23: balanceOf(PoolManager), totalSupply, balancePerShare, sharesOf, transferRestrictor, isBlacklisted, publicVersion and the reverts of rebaseAdd, rebaseSub, mint and burn from an ordinary account on 0xfc90518d5136585ba45e34ed5e1d108bd3950cfa; isFrozen, paused, owner and balanceOf on 0x004b506865409877c9fa29bfb1eba929984b9bbc; eth_getLogs showing no index event in the last 40,000,000 blocks)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/dinaricrypto/usdplus-contracts/main/src/UsdPlus.sol (rebaseAdd, rebaseMul and rebaseSub are restricted to OPERATOR_ROLE and set balancePerShare; _beforeTokenTransfer calls checkTransferRestricted; setTransferRestrictor and setIssuerLimits are restricted to the admin role; burn(address,uint256) spends an allowance)

## F-107. The block lists that gate OUSD and AMDc transfers on Base are each administered by one externally owned account

High severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Base.

What differs on the network: B20 tokens on Base check up to three policies of the chain's policy registry on every transfer: executor, sender and receiver. A policy is changed by its admin alone. Ethereum has no registry of this kind in the protocol.

How it meets the code: A take of a B20 token is a transfer with the PoolManager as executor and sender (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:60-75), and a payment before settle is a transfer with the PoolManager as receiver. OUSD binds its sender scope to block list 0x7a, whose admin is 0x251d2711ebeb0a09fdb8992f5506f3d949175246. AMDc binds sender, receiver, executor and mint receiver to block list 5, whose admin is 0xec0f05c174e54fbf0fe16ad930a8afebce612812. Both admins are accounts without code. One updateBlocklist call from such an account that names the PoolManager makes every later take of that token revert with PolicyForbids, and for AMDc every payment into the PoolManager as well. The same call from an account that is not the admin reverts with Unauthorized, and no path in the registry source lets another account become admin without a stageUpdateAdmin call of the current admin.

What is at risk: The PoolManager held 5,319,114.88 OUSD (6 decimals) and 32.15 AMDc (8 decimals) at block 61,314,282. After a block list entry none of it can leave by take, for any pool or claim holder.

What it takes: The policy admin, or whoever holds the private signing secret of that one account, sends one transaction. No token role is needed, because the policy admin is separate from the roles of the token.

What can be done: Issuers can move policy administration to a multi-signature account with stageUpdateAdmin and finalizeUpdateAdmin. Liquidity providers should treat the policy admin of each bound policy as a party that can freeze the pooled balance, next to the token roles recorded in F-027.

This entry adds to F-027.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor issuer is right: every step needs the admin account of a policy that the token's default admin bound, and an ordinary account is refused. Balances have moved since the finding was written, to 5,318,720.45 OUSD and 32.74 AMDc at block 61,342,065. For AMDc the sender check is skipped because the executor and sender scopes hold the same policy and the caller is the source, so the revert names the executor scope. The outcome is the same.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-75`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-365`.

Sources:

- https://mainnet.base.org/ (eth_call of policyId for the six scopes of both tokens, policyAdmin, pendingPolicyAdmin and isAuthorized on 0x8453000000000000000000000000000000000002, eth_getCode of the three admin addresses (empty), and updateBlocklist(0x7a, true, [PoolManager]) from an ordinary account reverting with 0x82b42900, at block 61,314,282.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/base/base/v1.5.6/crates/common/precompiles/src/policy/logic/v3.rs (require_admin compares the stored admin with the caller for membership updates, staging of a new admin and renouncing; finalize_update_admin needs the pending admin; built in policies have admin zero.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/base/base/v1.5.6/crates/common/precompiles/src/b20_stablecoin/logic/v3.rs (transfer_inner checks executor, sender and receiver policies through the registry before move_balance.)

## F-108. One USDC blocklist entry for the PoolManager on Arc stops native USDC in both directions, the token face and the bridged WETH token

High severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Arc.

What differs on the network: On Arc the USDC blocklist lives in the Native Coin Control precompile and the EVM checks it on every value-bearing call, so a listed address can neither send nor receive the native asset. The ERC-20 face of USDC moves the same balance and fails the same check. The bridged WETH token of the CrossChainTokenService asks the same list on every transfer through an adapter contract. On Ethereum a USDC blacklist entry stops USDC only, and ETH and WETH keep moving.

How it meets the code: 1. The USDC blacklister (0x2a2b7ff330f15f9a8c52af5fec27d775f5138467) lists the PoolManager address. In the simulation this is one storage entry of 0x1800000000000000000000000000000000000001, the entry for the PoolManager address in the mapping at slot 2, set to 1 by state override. 2. A native payout, which take performs as a value call from the PoolManager (src/types/Currency.sol:44-52), reverts with 'Blocked address', so take of address(0) reverts, and collectProtocolFees for address(0) uses the same call (src/ProtocolFees.sol:56). 3. USDC.transfer from the PoolManager reverts, so take of 0x3600000000000000000000000000000000000000 reverts (src/types/Currency.sol:60-86). 4. A value call into the PoolManager reverts before its code runs, and USDC.transfer to the PoolManager reverts, so settle can credit nothing under either id (src/PoolManager.sol:349-364). 5. WETH.transfer from the PoolManager and to the PoolManager reverts with AccountDenylisted(PoolManager), because the token calls isDenylisted on the adapter 0x3ebe1f103151890346cd7dbcec1c596cc50ef58e, which reads the USDC blocklist. 6. EURC and cirBTC keep moving, since each has its own list. Swaps and liquidity changes that are settled with ERC-6909 claims alone still run, and nothing in the three affected currencies can enter or leave.

What is at risk: At block 32,769,037 the PoolManager held 2,442,363 USDC of native balance for both ids and 9.54 bridged WETH. Of the 286,916 Initialize logs that cycle 4 collected up to block 32,670,244, 229,191 name 0x3600, 53,208 name address(0) and 275 name the WETH token. Every liquidity provider, swapper and claim holder in those pools loses access for as long as the entry stands. The source has no path that pays out without the transfer (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297).

What it takes: The USDC blacklister, a Circle role, must list the PoolManager address. An ordinary account cannot write the list. The effect was reproduced with eth_call and a state override, with the PoolManager as an inner caller, and no transaction was sent. I could not read the WETH implementation source, which is unverified, so the claim about WETH rests on the access list of a transfer and on the simulated reverts.

What can be done: Deployers and interfaces should state that on Arc the bridged WETH token shares the USDC blocklist, so one decision of one issuer covers native USDC, its token face and WETH. Liquidity providers who want an asset outside that list can use EURC or cirBTC, which carry their own issuer lists. Nothing in core can route around a listed PoolManager address.

This entry adds to F-011.

Second reviewer's corrections: actor: issuer. The value in the finding is right. Three points of detail. First, the access list of WETH.transfer from the PoolManager also names the CrossChainTokenService proxy 0x431871229103b780868f8C6BB820cd16ECf942BC with two slots: the EIP-1967 implementation slot, which holds 0xcef229c35cf3b6ba34690295399369412ab9b2b8, and slot 0x86a0e84c7d929f61fb2e716e558792f9ee8d701914df21037b29796118dd8e02, which holds the adapter address 0x3ebe1f103151890346cd7dbcec1c596cc50ef58e. The finding and the L3 profile describe the transfer as a call from the token to the adapter and do not name the service. Every WETH transfer passes through that upgradeable proxy, so whoever controls it is a second party that can stop WETH for the PoolManager without the USDC blacklister. Second, the PoolManager held 8.465 WETH at block 32,779,440. The figure of 9.54 belongs to block 32,769,037. Third, the blacklister address carries no code, so the role is exercised by one signing account today. I could not confirm how that account is held. The severity stays high: the freeze covers about 2.44 million USDC under two currency ids and the WETH pools, it lasts as long as the entry stands, and it needs the token issuer.

Code: `src/types/Currency.sol:44-52`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-86`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57`.

Sources:

- https://docs.arc.io/arc/references/evm-differences.md (The blocklist is enforced at runtime and a value transfer to or from a blocklisted address reverts)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/circlefin/arc-node/main/crates/precompiles/src/native_coin_control.rs (Native Coin Control stores a blocklist status per address and offers blocklist, unBlocklist and isBlocklisted)
- https://arc.drpc.org (eth_call at block 32,768,272 with a measuring stub at the PoolManager address and the PoolManager entry of the blocklist mapping set by state override: native send of 1 wei, USDC.transfer and WETH.transfer from the PoolManager revert, EURC.transfer and cirBTC.transfer succeed; USDC.transfer, a value call and WETH.transfer to the PoolManager revert and EURC.transfer succeeds. Without the override all of these succeed (research/network-risk/cycles/007/stablecoin-l1.work/a3_blocklist.py))
- https://arc.drpc.org (eth_createAccessList at block 32,767,960: a WETH transfer reads the adapter 0x3ebe1f103151890346cd7dbcec1c596cc50ef58e, the USDC proxy and implementation and the precompile 0x1800000000000000000000000000000000000001. isDenylisted(0x3600000000000000000000000000000000000000) on the adapter returns true, and blacklister() on USDC returns 0x2a2b7ff330f15f9a8c52af5fec27d775f5138467)

## F-114. The yUSD, yETH and xSolvBTC blocks are list entries for the PoolManager address itself, dated 2026-01-21 and 2026-07-12, and ankrETH has been paused for every sender since 2026-11-09

High severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Ethereum.

What differs on the network: Additions to F-080 from lead L-363. An issuer list on mainnet can name a single contract address, and here two issuers named the PoolManager singleton. F-080 did not know since when the blocks hold, how many pools are affected and whether the entry is for the address or for a class of addresses.

How it meets the code: 1. take pays with transfer from the one PoolManager address, and settle of an ERC-20 needs a transfer into it (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, 349-364, src/types/Currency.sol:60-80). 2. Bisection with eth_call at historical blocks, a zero amount transfer sent from the PoolManager address: yUSD 0x19ebd191f7a24ece672ba13a302212b5ef7f35cb and yETH 0x8464f6ecae1ea58ec816c13f964030eab8ec123a accept it up to block 24,283,368 and reject it from block 24,283,369. xSolvBTC 0xd9d920aa40f578ab794426f5c90f6c731d159def accepts it up to block 25,518,434 and rejects it from 25,518,435. A zero transfer between two other accounts succeeds at both blocks. 3. Block 24,283,369 holds transaction 0xf46bcb77afadbb0f745982cf8f0629c703b1ff4322421778944f2a44f6ecc3b8, sent by 0x944416e5df03ee4c14ec44c01495005564e6b07e to 0x10472f91f67c49260bdd65f016ea0757bb1fc91e with selector 0xf9911c7d, which is blackListUsers(address[]). The array holds two addresses: 0x9efec24b767b44a46cf16e8aead9624d129a56d1, an account without code, and the PoolManager. The log has topic0 0x8d3f6de1..., which is BlackListed(address,address), with the PoolManager as second topic. 4. Block 25,518,435 holds transaction 0xc00cb1f2c6859155069f001892dac79526ce4d2cb440bb3757aaf228ac6cee6f, a Safe execTransaction on 0xdc3a91d2fa7e1d36b1baa0852f5d8734bd209d02 whose target is xSolvBTC. The token logs topic0 0x44d5fe68..., which is BlacklistAdded(address), with the PoolManager as topic. 5. ankrETH 0xE95A203B1a91a908F9B9CE46459d101078c2c3cb reverts every transfer with the message This action currently paused, also a zero transfer between two unrelated accounts, from block 26,376,473 on.

What is at risk: Balances at the PoolManager today: 677,988.17 yUSD, 62.69 yETH, 3.2104 xSolvBTC and 0.3045 ankrETH. Pools that name the tokens: yUSD 60, of which 5 hold active liquidity and 51 have no hook; yETH 16, of which 3 hold active liquidity; xSolvBTC 16, of which 5 hold active liquidity. Most of these pools carry an LP fee between 49 and 99.98 percent. 8 yUSD pools and 13 yETH pools were initialized after the block began, which works because initialize moves no tokens. The position holders cannot receive these tokens until the issuer removes the entry or the pause.

What it takes: A decision of the issuer or of the token admin. Both lists name the PoolManager address, so a new deployment at another address starts unlisted and would be listed only by a new issuer action. I did not find a public statement of YieldFi or Solv on the reason. The contract 0x10472f91f67c49260bdd65f016ea0757bb1fc91e has no verified source at Sourcify, so the function and event names rest on the selector and topic hashes that I computed.

What can be done: None in core. A position holder can remove liquidity and turn the delta of the blocked currency into an ERC-6909 claim (src/PoolManager.sol:322-329) and take the other currency. Interfaces can warn on pools of tokens whose issuer has listed the singleton.

This entry adds to F-080.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-80`.

- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/1/0xd9d920aa40f578ab794426f5c90f6c731d159def?fields=compilation,proxyResolution (xSolvBTC is a BeaconProxy whose implementation is named SolvBTCYieldTokenV3_1.)

## F-007. Scheduled Jenner fork adds protocol-native tokens whose issuer can seize, pause or block the PoolManager balance

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: BNB Smart Chain.

What differs on the network: BNB Smart Chain has scheduled the Jenner fork for 2026-12-08 02:30 UTC. It ships BEP-702, a token standard named CAS20 that runs as native code behind addresses starting with 0xCA52. Every CAS20 token has a role that can reassign a holder's balance (seizeWithMemo), a per-feature pause, and transfer policies that read shared allowlists and blocklists. BEP-703 lets governance list these tokens as payment assets with reserved block space. Ethereum has no token logic in the protocol.

How it meets the code: 1. The PoolManager keeps every pool's reserves, all ERC-6909 claims and accrued protocol fees for a currency in one balance, read by balanceOf at src/types/Currency.sol:94 and paid out by transfer at src/types/Currency.sol:60-75. 2. A CAS20 token keeps the transfer and balanceOf selectors, so pools can be initialized with it. initialize makes no call to the currency (src/PoolManager.sol:117-142). 3. The token admin points SEIZE_HOLDER_POLICY at a blocklist that contains the PoolManager address. An account with SEIZE_ROLE then calls seizeWithMemo(PoolManager, to, value). The balance moves without any take, and no delta is recorded. Pool accounting still shows the old reserves, so the last liquidity providers and claim holders to exit find transfer failing for lack of balance. 4. If TRANSFER_SENDER_POLICY stops authorizing the PoolManager, or the TRANSFER feature is paused, the token reverts every transfer from the PoolManager. Currency.transfer then reverts at src/types/Currency.sol:82-86, so take (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297) and collectProtocolFees (src/ProtocolFees.sol:56) fail for every pool that uses the token. 5. If TRANSFER_RECEIVER_POLICY stops authorizing the PoolManager, nobody can pay the token in, and settle credits nothing (src/PoolManager.sol:358-360). 6. The Asset variant multiplier does not change balanceOf, so it creates no rebase at the PoolManager. That part was checked and is safe.

What is at risk: All reserves, ERC-6909 claims and protocol fees held in the affected CAS20 token across every pool. Seizure is a loss for liquidity providers of those pools. A policy change or pause is a freeze that lasts until the issuer reverses it. Liquidity providers can still remove liquidity, take the counter-asset and hold the affected token as an ERC-6909 claim through mint (src/PoolManager.sol:322-329), because mint makes no token call.

What it takes: Jenner activates as scheduled, chain governance opens CAS20 creation through the ActivationRegistry, a CAS20 token is created, and pools holding it exist on the PoolManager. The issuer roles of that token must then act. An unprivileged attacker can do this only with a token it issued itself, which harms only those who pool against that token. None of these conditions hold today. The factory and registry addresses have no code at the time of the query. The BEP is still marked Draft and the client implementation was not read, so the final behaviour could differ from the text.

What can be done: Treat CAS20 tokens as issuer-controlled assets in interfaces and routing. Before listing a CAS20 pool, read the token's six policy ids, its pause mask and the holders of SEIZE_ROLE and the admin role. Prefer tokens whose role membership is frozen with no seizer. Liquidity providers should size positions on the assumption that the issuer can take or freeze the pooled balance. After the fork, re-test take and settle against a CAS20 token on a fork node.

Second reviewer's corrections: 1. The client on master names the holder scope SEIZE_EXEMPT_POLICY (core/vm/cas20_admin.go), while the BEP text names it SEIZE_HOLDER_POLICY. The semantics are the same. Tools that watch the PoolManager's seizability must use the getter the client exposes. 2. The finding attributes the hazard to issuer roles only. BEP-702 section 3.8 makes the PolicyRegistry a shared singleton, so the admin of a policy list can change the PoolManager's status for every token that references the list without holding a role on any of them. 3. The finding says the client implementation was not read. It is on bnb-chain/bsc master and agrees with the BEP on transfer, pause, policy checks and seizure. 4. Severity stays medium. The actor is a token issuer, which the scale places at high. No CAS20 token exists today, the PoolManager holds none, and the loss is limited to pools of the affected token. Raise it to high once a CAS20 token with a seizer or a restrictive sender policy has a balance at the PoolManager.

Code: `src/types/Currency.sol:60-75`, `src/types/Currency.sol:82-86`, `src/types/Currency.sol:94`, `src/PoolManager.sol:117-142`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:322-329`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-365`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57`.

Sources:

- https://github.com/bnb-chain/BEPs/blob/master/BEPs/BEP-702.md (CAS20 design: stateful precompiles, address space, seizeWithMemo semantics, policy scopes with ALWAYS_ALLOW defaults, pause features, multiplier leaves balanceOf unchanged)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bnb-chain/BEPs/master/BEPs/BEP-702.md (Full text read for sections 3.6, 3.8, 3.9, 3.12, 3.15 and 6.7)
- https://github.com/bnb-chain/BEPs/blob/master/BEPs/BEP-718.md (Jenner contains BEP-702, BEP-703 and BEP-706, and token creation is opened by a governance-appointed switch)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bnb-chain/bsc/master/CHANGELOG.md (Client v1.8.2 schedules mainnet Jenner for 2026-12-08 02:30:00 UTC)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bnb-chain/bsc/master/params/config.go (Mainnet JennerTime is 1796697000)
- https://github.com/bnb-chain/BEPs/blob/master/BEPs/BEP-703.md (Payment lane lists stablecoins and BEP-702 native tokens by governance vote)
- rpc https://bsc-dataseed1.bnbchain.org, queries near block 135065254 on 2026-11-22 (eth_getCode returned empty code for 0xCA5F000000000000000000000000000000000000, 0x7020000000000000000000000000000000000002 and 0x0000000000000000000000000000000000002007)

## F-043. stHYPE and wstHYPE are two currency ids over one share balance, so a loss in the rebasing face is paid from the shares of pools in the other face

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: HyperEVM.

What differs on the network: The StakedHYPE liquid staking token on HyperEVM has two ERC-20 contracts that read and write the same storage. stHYPE at 0xfFaa4a3D97fE9107Cef8a3F48c069F577Ff76cC1 stores shares with 24 decimals and reports shares times totalSupply divided by totalShares. wstHYPE at 0x94e8396e0869c9F2200760aF0621aFd240E1CF38 keeps no balances: its balanceOf returns stHYPE.sharesOf divided by 1e6 and its transfer calls stHYPE.transferShares. Every holder has both balances at once, and a transfer through either contract changes both. The issuer's docs describe this and warn that multi entry point tokens have caused losses elsewhere. The stHYPE figure rises every second, because totalSupply is interpolated over an interval of 82,800 seconds, and a rebase to a lower supply applies in the same transaction. On Ethereum, stETH and wstETH are separate tokens with separate balances.

How it meets the code: The PoolManager treats the two addresses as unrelated currencies. Deltas, pool reserves and ERC-6909 claims are recorded per currency address in that currency's unit (src/libraries/CurrencyDelta.sol:10-41, src/PoolManager.sol:322-336). A take under either id calls transfer on that contract and moves shares out of the single balance the PoolManager holds (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:55-87). Let S be the shares the PoolManager holds, W the amount owed under the wstHYPE id in shares, T the amount owed under the stHYPE id in stHYPE units, and r the stHYPE per share rate. The PoolManager can pay everyone while S is at least W plus T divided by r. I first checked what an ordinary account can do. A credit under either id needs a rise of that contract's balanceOf between sync and settle (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288 and 349-365). Both balances rise only when shares arrive, since r depends on block.timestamp and is constant inside one transaction (stHYPE.sol lines 495 to 513) and Overseer.rebase reverts for a caller without REBASER_ROLE. One deposit therefore cannot be credited under both ids, and no account can move r inside its own sync window. Rounding between the two units is at most one base unit per settle. Three effects remain. First, T is a fixed number while r grows, so the staking yield on the shares that back stHYPE pools and claims stays in the PoolManager and no pool or claim can withdraw it. Second, when the rebaser reports a lower supply, r falls at once (stHYPE.sol lines 400 to 404). T does not change, so paying T now takes more shares than were paid in. If the PoolManager also holds shares for wstHYPE pools, those takes succeed by using them, and the wstHYPE pools end up short by T times the difference of 1/r after and 1/r before. An account that sees the slash coming (the Overseer exposes pendingSlashExists) can deposit under the stHYPE id before the rebase and withdraw after it, and gains that difference on its deposit. Third, pauseTransfer on stHYPE blocks transfer and transferShares, so one pause stops take under both ids.

What is at risk: No funds are exposed today: sharesOf(PoolManager) is zero. If pools or claims exist under both ids, a slash of x percent moves about x percent of the amount owed under the stHYPE id from the LPs of wstHYPE pools to the accounts that withdraw stHYPE first. LPs of stHYPE pools lose the staking yield on their stHYPE, about 2 percent a year at the current rate. A pause freezes every pool that uses either id.

What it takes: Liquidity or claims under both ids in the PoolManager. The loss needs a rebase to a lower supply, which only a holder of REBASER_ROLE can send and which follows a validator slash; Hyperliquid has no automatic slashing at present. The yield loss needs no actor. I did not read the Overseer implementation at 0x2b8698dd4b8addf7ddcabc46c6aedafe5a70b11c, which is not verified on Sourcify. That no public function other than rebase starts a rebase rests on the simulated revert of rebase for an unprivileged caller and on the last rebase transaction.

What can be done: Interfaces and routers on HyperEVM should offer wstHYPE pools only and refuse pools whose currencies include the stHYPE address. LPs should not provide stHYPE through the rebasing contract. The PoolManager cannot call setSelfDisableTransfer on stHYPE with any of its call shapes, so excluding the stHYPE face needs a change in core or a refusal at the interface level. Monitoring should watch stHYPE for Rebase events whose new supply is below the current supply and for TransferPaused.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor issuer is correct for the loss shift and for the freeze, since both need a call by a holder of REBASER_ROLE or PAUSER_ROLE, and the slash that would lead to a lower supply is not available to an ordinary account. An ordinary account that deposits under the stHYPE id before such a rebase gains only if that rebase happens. Medium fits because the PoolManager holds neither token today and the loss needs pools or claims under both ids. The statement about slashing is backed by the Hyperliquid staking page, which the finding did not cite.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:322-336`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-365`, `src/types/Currency.sol:55-87`, `src/libraries/CurrencyDelta.sol:10-41`.

Sources:

- https://docs.valantis.xyz/stakedhype/integrate/wsthype (Issuer statement that stHYPE and wstHYPE are two interfaces to the same data, that a transfer of one transfers the other, and the warning about multi entry point tokens)
- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/999/0xe71caf5c1fe56d8897c7b604295d23968049e057?fields=sources (Verified stHYPE implementation (exact match): shares, transfer, transferShares, syncSupply with the immediate branch for a lower supply, linear interpolation in _totalSupplyComponents, pauseTransfer, setSelfDisableTransfer)
- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/999/0x104324863cfb2220756c60384efa9bb67a57aaf7?fields=sources (Verified wstHYPE implementation (exact match): balanceOf reads stHYPE.sharesOf, transfer calls stHYPE.transferShares)
- https://rpc.hyperliquid.xyz/evm eth_getLogs Transfer on both tokens in the 1,000 blocks ending near 51,956,900 (transaction hash starting 0x3d982e864a) and eth_call balanceOf of 0x40db8941e90c5a540c52159d9f3b9ad4417364b3 on both tokens (One transfer emitted 106.40 on stHYPE and 102.53 on wstHYPE between the same parties, and the receiver's two balances have the ratio 1.03777 of the two total supplies)
- https://rpc.hyperliquid.xyz/evm eth_call rebase() on 0xB96f07367e69e86d6e9C3F29215885104813eeAE from 0x00000000000000000000000000000000000a11ce and from the PoolManager; eth_getLogs Rebase at block 51,883,107 (rebase reverts with AccessControlUnauthorizedAccount for role 0x5fde63b5...0b75; the last rebase was sent by 0x4eb038eb501045daa520b972fcad48c429531e10 with selector 0xaf14052c and an interval of 82,800 seconds)
- https://rpc.hyperliquid.xyz/evm eth_call sharesOf(PoolManager), balanceOf(PoolManager) on both tokens, syncStart, syncEnd, rewardsToSync, totalSupply (The PoolManager holds no shares; interval 82,800 seconds, 123.56 HYPE of rewards on a supply of 2,275,453 stHYPE)

## F-068. xStocks tokens multiply every balance by a figure the issuer schedules, and the 8 for 1 split of SNDKx raised the PoolManager balance eightfold on Ethereum and X Layer with no transfer

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Ethereum, BNB Smart Chain, Monad, X Layer.

What differs on the network: The Robinhood stock tokens cleared in an earlier cycle keep balanceOf fixed and change a display multiplier. The xStocks tokens (symbols ending in x, one implementation behind every proxy) do the opposite: balanceOf is shares times a multiplier, and the multiplier changes at a time the issuer sets. The PoolManager holds them on Ethereum (5 tokens in the window), BNB Smart Chain (42), Monad (4) and X Layer (7 over 6.9 days).

How it meets the code: 1. BackedAutoFeeTokenImplementation.balanceOf returns shares times getCurrentMultiplier() (lines 254 to 259). getCurrentMultiplier returns lastMultiplier until newMultiplierActivationTime and newMultiplier from that second on, reduced by feePerPeriod for each period that has passed (lines 265 to 283). Only the multiplier updater may schedule a change (lines 445 to 452) and only the owner may set feePerPeriod (lines 392 to 397). 2. SNDKx 0xb63efbc28860c8097e341de1fcf59456161e9d98 has a stored update from 1e18 to 8e18 with activation time 1795167000 (20 November 2026) on Ethereum and on X Layer. On X Layer the block with that timestamp is 76,397,964. The token emitted no log in that block, and balanceOf(PoolManager) went from 137,059,273,049,122,723 to 1,096,474,184,392,981,784 base units, a factor of exactly 8. 3. Pool accounting did not change (src/PoolManager.sol never reads a balance outside sync and settle), so seven eighths of the SNDKx the PoolManager now holds belong to no pool. A provider who had put in one token before the split can take out one token, which is worth one eighth. 4. A pool that still held liquidity at its old price after the activation time would quote eight times the market value for each token sold into it, and any account could sell new tokens there and take the other currency. I did not observe this: the pool that traded SNDKx before the split on X Layer had 1.04 SNDKx removed 21,405 seconds before activation, and later swaps went through another pool at about 26 units of the 6 decimal quote token per SNDKx, against about 267 before. 5. Dividends use the same path in small steps: STRCx has 26 stored updates and a multiplier of 1.1058, and each step raises the PoolManager balance without a transfer. feePerPeriod is 0 on the six token contracts I read on four networks; a value above 0 would lower every balance each period, which would leave pools short. 6. Transfers convert amounts to shares and back, which explains differences of 1 to 8 base units between Transfer logs and balances.

What is at risk: Every pool in an xStocks token on the four networks. Balances today: 569.57 STRCx, 17.34 NVDAx and 1.40 SNDKx on Ethereum; 1.88 SNDKx on X Layer; 42 tokens of up to 9.96 units each on BNB Smart Chain; a few thousand base units on Monad, where 14,932 NVDAx transfers passed through in a day. A split strands the added tokens in the PoolManager and cuts the value of the token side of each position by the split ratio. Each dividend strands the dividend. The other currency of the pool is exposed to whoever trades first after an activation time that is public in advance. I did not measure what share of the 569.57 STRCx is already unaccounted.

What it takes: The issuer schedules a multiplier change, which is a routine corporate action for these tokens, and liquidity stays in the pool past the activation time. An ordinary account cannot bracket the change with sync and settle or with take and repay, because the change happens at a timestamp or inside the updater's own transaction. The arbitrage in step 4 is my reading of the pool mechanics and was not seen on chain.

What can be done: Liquidity providers must remove liquidity before every scheduled activation and should prefer a wrapped form with a fixed balance. Interfaces should read newMultiplierActivationTime and multiplierUpdates and warn when a change is pending. A hook cannot recover the stranded tokens, because core has no payout for an unaccounted balance.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor value issuer is right: only the multiplier updater 0x5f7a4c11bde4f218f0025ef444c369d838ffa2ad or the owner can change the figure, and no ordinary account can place the change between a sync and a settle. Two details need correction. First, the sentence that one implementation sits behind every proxy does not hold on Monad: the SNDKx proxy there points to 0xd865ce1b07540b5ede20e8298f48da69770fe22e, 14,411 bytes, with no Sourcify match and no multiplierUpdates getter. I tested it from the PoolManager's own balance of 8 base units and 1 share: a transfer of 8 or 9 moves the one share, a transfer of 7 moves nothing and a transfer of 16 reverts, so its transfer uses the 8e18 multiplier as balanceOf does. SPYx, QQQx and NVDAx on Monad use the common implementation. Second, the downward case is wider than feePerPeriod. _updateMultiplier requires only a nonzero value (line 684), so the updater can lower the multiplier, for example for a reverse split, at once or at a set time, and the PoolManager would then hold less than pools record. The STRCx balance on Ethereum was 567.49 at my read. The arbitrage in step 4 remains unobserved, as the finding says.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-289`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-365`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`.

Sources:

- src/PoolManager.sol:349-365 (_settle credits the recipient with the balance now minus the synced balance, with a checked subtraction, and resets the synced currency.)
- src/PoolManager.sol:291-297 (take debits the caller and transfers before anything is paid in.)

- https://rpc.xlayer.tech (eth_call balanceOf(PoolManager) on SNDKx at blocks 76,397,963 and 76,397,964: 137059273049122723 and 1096474184392981784. Block timestamps 1795166999 and 1795167000. eth_getLogs for the token in both blocks: none. multiplierUpdates(1) returns old 1e18, new 8e18, activation 1795167000. Swap logs before and after are in research/network-risk/cycles/004/theme-balance-reconcile.work/raw/xlayer_SNDKx_swaps.json.)

STRCx: multiplier 1105774914969160700, multiplierUpdatesLength 26, balance 569.568. feePerPeriod 0 and periodLength 604,800 on all tokens read. Values in research/network-risk/cycles/004/theme-balance-reconcile.work/raw/xstocks_multiplier.json.)

## F-076. Jenner client source and the Chapel testnet show how a CAS20 issuer can block or seize the PoolManager balance, and seizure needs one more step than first recorded

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: BNB Smart Chain.

What differs on the network: BNB Smart Chain activates Jenner at timestamp 1796697000 (2026-12-08 02:30 UTC). The client then runs CAS20 tokens as native code behind addresses that start with 0xCA52. Each token carries roles for mint, burn, seize, pause and metadata, a pause mask per feature, and six policy lanes that point at shared allowlists and blocklists in a policy registry. The standard is live on the Chapel testnet since 2026-10-29. Ethereum has no token logic in the client.

How it meets the code: 1. transfer checks the pause bit, then the sender policy and the receiver policy, then debits the sender and credits the recipient by the amount and returns a 32 byte true (core/vm/cas20_token.go lines 175 to 189 and 239 to 269). The PoolManager accepts that answer (src/types/Currency.sol:64-74). 2. A policy that does not authorize the sender makes transfer revert with PolicyForbids for every amount, zero included. On Chapel the token 0xca52000000000000000001217954ab5467577565 has sender policy 0x0100000000000007, and transfer of 0 and of 1 from an unlisted contract address both reverted with selector 0xa43fec12. If the token admin binds TRANSFER_SENDER_POLICY to a list that leaves the PoolManager out, if the admin of that list removes the PoolManager, or if PAUSE_ROLE pauses the transfer feature, then every take and every protocol fee collection in that token reverts (src/types/Currency.sol:82-86, src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/ProtocolFees.sol:56). 3. A receiver policy that leaves the PoolManager out makes every payment to it revert, so settle credits nothing (src/PoolManager.sol:358-360). The transfer reverts and the amount is not diverted to another account. 4. seizeWithMemo needs SEIZE_ROLE and an unpaused seize feature, and it refuses a source that the SEIZE_EXEMPT policy authorizes (core/vm/cas20_admin.go lines 507 to 553). The default policy id 0 authorizes every account, so with default settings no account can be seized. The admin must first bind SEIZE_EXEMPT_POLICY to a list that leaves the PoolManager out. After that an account with SEIZE_ROLE can move the PoolManager balance to any allowed receiver without a take, and the pools still record the old amounts. The earlier text of this finding named a scope SEIZE_HOLDER_POLICY and a blocklist that contains the PoolManager. The client names the scopes SEIZE_EXEMPT_POLICY and SEIZE_RECEIVER_POLICY, and the test is the inverse: exemption must be withdrawn. 5. balanceOf returns the raw balance. The Asset multiplier is applied only by the UI selectors, so it causes no rebase at the PoolManager. A call that cannot pay for a storage read ends with an out of gas error and does not return a zero balance (core/vm/cas20_errors.go lines 55 to 63), so sync cannot record a false zero. 6. Token creation and policy creation are closed until an administrator appointed through GovHub 0x1007 activates them (core/vm/cas20_activation.go). On Chapel all three features are active.

What is at risk: All reserves, ERC-6909 claims and protocol fees that the PoolManager holds in an affected CAS20 token. A policy change or a pause freezes them until the issuer reverses it. A seizure after the exemption is withdrawn is a loss for the liquidity providers of those pools. None of this applies today on mainnet because no CAS20 token can exist before the fork and before activation.

What it takes: Jenner activates, the activation administrator opens token creation on mainnet, a CAS20 token is created and pooled, and the roles of that token act as described. An ordinary account can do this only with a token it issued itself, which harms only those who pool that token.

What can be done: Before a CAS20 token is listed, read policyId for the six scopes, isPaused for the four features, and the holders of SEIZE_ROLE, PAUSE_ROLE and the admin role. A token whose sender or receiver policy is an allowlist cannot be pooled unless the PoolManager and the routers are on it. Prefer tokens whose SEIZE_EXEMPT policy is the default and whose admin role was renounced. After 2026-12-08 repeat the Chapel checks of this cycle on mainnet.

This entry adds to F-007.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor value issuer is right, and an ordinary account can reach this only with a token it created itself. The correction to the earlier text of F-007 is accurate: the scopes are SEIZE_EXEMPT_POLICY and SEIZE_RECEIVER_POLICY, and an account is seizable only after the admin binds the exemption lane to a policy that does not authorize it. Small differences in my repeat: I tested 18 tokens where the finding names 12, and the Chapel token with the sender policy also has its seizure exemption lane bound. None of this applies on mainnet before 2026-12-08 and before the administrator opens token creation.

Code: `src/types/Currency.sol:60-75`, `src/types/Currency.sol:82-86`, `src/types/Currency.sol:90-96`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-365`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bnb-chain/bsc/master/core/vm/cas20_token.go (dispatch, transfer, transferFrom and move: pause check, sender and receiver policy checks, debit and credit, 32 byte true)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bnb-chain/bsc/master/core/vm/cas20_admin.go (roles, policy scopes, pause, mint, burn, seizeWithMemo with the SEIZE_EXEMPT test, updatePolicy restricted to the admin role)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bnb-chain/bsc/master/core/vm/cas20_asset.go (balanceOf is not overridden by the Asset variant; the multiplier is applied in scaledBalanceOf, balanceOfUI and totalSupplyUI)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bnb-chain/bsc/master/core/vm/cas20_errors.go (finishCAS20Metered returns ErrOutOfGas when the frame ran out of gas, before any return data is used)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bnb-chain/bsc/master/core/vm/cas20_activation.go (features are switched by an admin that only GovHub can appoint; deactivation does not reach existing tokens)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bnb-chain/bsc/master/CHANGELOG.md (v1.8.2 schedules mainnet Jenner for 2026-12-08 02:30 UTC; v1.8.1 renamed the CAS20 seizure exemption policy (pull 3829))
- rpc https://bsc-testnet-dataseed.bnbchain.org (Chapel, chain id 97, Geth v1.8.1, Jenner active since 2026-10-29), eth_call with state override, blocks 144360009 to 144364040 on 2026-11-23 (33 CAS20Created logs; transfers from contract holders of 12 tokens return true and move exactly the amount; PolicyForbids on transfers of 0 and 1 from an unlisted address for a token with a sender policy; isActivated true for three features)

## F-087. The owner of Pleasing USD burned 41,000 PUSD out of the Arbitrum One PoolManager balance with an owner only burnFrom

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Arbitrum One.

What differs on the network: F-036 and F-028 record that an issuer can block or burn the single address that holds every pool's balance. This is a case where an issuer did it. PUSD at 0xc8fb643d18f1e53698cfda5c8fdf0cdc03c1dbec on Arbitrum One is an upgradeable token whose owner can call burnFrom(address,uint256) on any holder.

How it meets the code: 1. On 23 April 2026 the owner 0x43f285d7d53cc3e7bd4914d3e57ec90f58f510ff sent burnFrom(PoolManager, 40,000 PUSD) in block 455,597,235 (transaction 0x077516739f49e38972552537bbc49fe61e542dd6ca0b3d4d0ada8c3cf1de8fe6) and burnFrom(PoolManager, 1,000 PUSD) in block 455,604,741 (transaction 0x86afa98aa0369719d0faeda5dce5a1708fd6acc0e054b75f4287523b4f54e8c9). The PoolManager's balance went from 44,024.91 to 4,024.91 PUSD and from 1,509.05 to 509.05 PUSD. 2. The PoolManager emitted no log in either transaction. Pool state was not changed, so pools kept recording the burned tokens. 3. A read-only burnFrom(PoolManager, 1) from an unrelated address reverts with the text Ownable: caller is not the owner. From the owner it reverts today with the text blacklist, so the burn needs the target on a list that the owner controls. 4. Thirteen pools name PUSD, ten of them created between blocks 455,516,336 and 455,554,073, in the hours before the burns, with fees of 9 to 80 percent. Eight still show liquidity. The PoolManager holds 14.14 PUSD today.

What is at risk: 41,000 PUSD of the PoolManager's balance was destroyed. The first burn took 40,000 of the 44,024.91 PUSD it held. I did not establish who the liquidity providers were or why the owner acted, and I found no price source. Whoever held a position or a claim in PUSD at that time could no longer be paid in full by take. The other currencies of those pools were not touched by the burn.

What it takes: Control of the token owner account, and the PoolManager on the token's blacklist at the time of the call. The implementation behind the proxy was not read; the statements about the owner check and the blacklist rest on the revert texts of two eth_call tests and on the two transactions.

What can be done: None in core. Pool creators and liquidity providers should treat an owner burn function as a risk to every pool of that token, and interfaces can show it. The search of this unit lists such burns after the fact.

This entry adds to F-036.

Second reviewer's corrections: Actor issuer is right. Preconditions: the sentence that the burn needs the PoolManager on the token's blacklist is wrong; the PoolManager was not on the list at either burn and was put on it ten minutes after the second one. Effect should be both: 41,000 PUSD of the PoolManager's balance were destroyed, and the PUSD side of every pool has been frozen since block 455,607,037 because the token rejects transfers from and to the PoolManager. This is a live freeze of the kind recorded in F-036 and F-056 and belongs in the table of pooled tokens that reject PoolManager transfers today. The finding says eight pools still show liquidity; none of them can pay PUSD out. The pools created in the hours before the burns carry fees of 9 to 80 percent and two older ones 88 and 95 percent, which suggests the owner acted against these pools; I did not establish who provided their liquidity. Medium stands for a token with a supply of 644,376 PUSD.

## F-093. The restrictor of HOLD has restricted the PoolManager address on BNB Smart Chain, so 140,774.9 HOLD held for pools cannot leave, and 448 more of the newly listed BNB Smart Chain currencies refuse every transfer from the PoolManager

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: BNB Smart Chain, X Layer, Base.

What differs on the network: HOLD 0xff583dc5ddc7c0bb3625fff39a587a2000e78888 has a restrictor role that can stop any address from sending. One entry for the PoolManager address stops every pool of the token, because all pools share that address.

How it meets the code: 1. isRestricted(PoolManager) returns true. Every transfer from the PoolManager reverts with ACCOUNT_IS_RESTRICTED, for one base unit as for the whole balance. restrict(address) from an ordinary account reverts with ONLY_RESTRICTER. 2. take pays with transfer and reverts when the token refuses (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297, src/types/Currency.sol:60-80), so no pool can pay HOLD out. 3. The full balance transfer test ran on the 68,701 BNB Smart Chain currencies that cycle 5 had not tested. 517 refuse the full balance. For those I repeated the transfer with one base unit, 1 percent, one half and a second recipient: 449 refuse even one unit, 44 accept a smaller amount (transfer or wallet limits) and 24 passed on the second run. The frozen ones with a price are HOLD, LN (325 USD), STBU (218 USD, text Address: should be allowed), BSU (43 USD, paused) and GBCK (8 USD, text Sender is blacklisted). 4. X Layer: 21 of 2,051 refuse the full balance and 16 of those refuse one unit, none with a price. Base: 3,474 of 54,674 tested currencies refuse the full balance. In a random sample of 240 of them, 206 accept a smaller amount, 28 refuse one unit and 6 passed on the second run. None has a price at coins.llama.fi.

What is at risk: 140,774.905 HOLD, 15,626 USD at the coins.llama.fi price, is the largest priced balance among the frozen ones. The other priced ones sum to less than 600 USD. The liquidity providers of those pools cannot withdraw that side while the restriction stands. The other currency of each pool is not affected.

What it takes: A token with a per address stop and a role holder who applies it to the PoolManager. For HOLD this has happened.

What can be done: Interfaces should test a one unit transfer from the PoolManager address by eth_call before they show a pool. Liquidity providers cannot undo the restriction.

This entry adds to F-011.

Second reviewer's corrections: actor: issuer is right. Add that the restriction dates from block 83,149,493 and that the token also refuses transfers to the PoolManager. Two of the 16 pools were created within 1,000 blocks of the restriction, and the pool fees are between 85 and 99.5 percent for most of them, so part of the balance may come from bait pools.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-80`.

Sources:

- https://bsc.api.pocket.network (eth_call on HOLD: isRestricted(PoolManager) returns 1, balanceOf(PoolManager) is 140774905397439540858093, transfer from the PoolManager reverts with ACCOUNT_IS_RESTRICTED and restrict(address) from an ordinary account reverts with ONLY_RESTRICTER.)
- https://bsc-dataseed1.bnbchain.org (Full balance transfer test and the repeat with smaller amounts for the BNB Smart Chain list (eth_call with a measuring stub at the PoolManager address and with from set to the PoolManager).)

## F-035. Elysium USDC is a bridge representation whose backing on HyperEVM sits at one address that the issuer can block

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: Elysium.

What differs on the network: On Ethereum the PoolManager holds USDC issued by Circle directly. On Elysium the token called USDC is a standard gateway representation minted by the Arbitrum token bridge. Its backing is HyperEVM USDC held by one gateway contract on HyperEVM, and HyperEVM USDC has a blacklister role. The Elysium token itself has no block list.

How it meets the code: Step 1: the PoolManager on Elysium holds about 174,155 USDC at 0xb47c4C8F73F009CC0C0d3f92B388d13E293F0B65. The token reports the HyperEVM token 0xb88339CB7199b77E23DB6E890353E22632Ba630f as its parent and the ERC-20 gateway as its minter. Step 2: all 4.03M Elysium USDC are backed by 4.26M HyperEVM USDC held by the gateway 0xd97B30dE0bA0C2eAeE942afce77530e06c5F122c. Step 3: if the blacklister of HyperEVM USDC lists that gateway, every withdrawal from Elysium fails when the gateway tries to pay out, and deposits fail when it tries to pull tokens in. Step 4: on Elysium nothing reverts. sync, settle and take keep working for the representation because its transfer and balanceOf are unaffected, so pools keep quoting a token that can no longer be redeemed. Holders sell it into the pools and liquidity providers end up holding it. The PoolManager code treats the representation as any other ERC-20 and has no way to notice the change.

What is at risk: All pools on Elysium that use USDC, and ERC-6909 claims in it. The PoolManager balance was about 174,155 USDC. Liquidity providers on the other side of USDC pairs lose the assets that are sold to them for the impaired token. No funds are moved out of the PoolManager by this path.

What it takes: The blacklister of HyperEVM USDC, 0x33aa4e959132e49552cb754fb6585bdcdf874beb, must list the gateway. isBlacklisted(gateway) is false today. No unprivileged party can cause it. The same structure applies to every token bridged through the standard gateway whose parent chain contract has a block list.

What can be done: Liquidity providers should treat Elysium USDC as bridge exposure to one HyperEVM address and watch isBlacklisted for the gateway. Interfaces can label the token as a bridged representation. Nothing in the PoolManager can be configured against it.

Second reviewer's corrections: The figures moved since the research queries: the PoolManager balance is 182,936 USDC, and the gateway holds 4.27M against a supply of 4.05M. One further control exists that the finding does not list. The Elysium token is a beacon proxy (beacon 0x659f40efd07d70224b7ac61e4721f6f3e11b625e, implementation 0xbd080eef044a3e6226757487c5cef33568afefd6). The beacon owner is the proxy contract 0xce4ceced412aa9359840c7d52bbd99827a5d42b9, which can replace the logic of the token. I did not identify who controls that contract. This is a governance class control and does not change the actor or the severity.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/types/Currency.sol:53-75`.

Sources:

- https://rpc.elysium.kinetiq.xyz eth_call l1Address(), l2Gateway(), totalSupply(), isBlacklisted(address) on 0xb47c4C8F73F009CC0C0d3f92B388d13E293F0B65 and balanceOf(PoolManager), queried 2026-11-22 (The Elysium token is a gateway representation of the HyperEVM token, has a supply of 4.03M, has no isBlacklisted function, and the PoolManager holds about 174,155.)
- https://rpc.hyperliquid.xyz/evm eth_call balanceOf(gateway), blacklister(), isBlacklisted(gateway) on 0xb88339CB7199b77E23DB6E890353E22632Ba630f, queried 2026-11-22 (The gateway holds 4.26M HyperEVM USDC, the token has a blacklister, and the gateway is not listed today.)
- https://elysium.kinetiq.xyz/docs/bridging-hyperevm-tokens-to-elysium (The canonical bridge locks the token in the standard gateway on HyperEVM and mints a representation on Elysium, and lists the two USDC addresses.)

## F-073. The X Layer PoolManager is enrolled in USDG payout group 2, so every USDG transfer runs reward share accounting for it and the rewards go to a third address

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: token issuer or admin. Networks: X Layer.

What differs on the network: USDG is the Paxos reward token. Each account slot packs balance, shares, payout group id and last update time. On X Layer payoutGroupIdOf(PoolManager) returns 2. The claimer and the destination of group 2 are 0xb1b3d51e4be3fe55006d1ae4bc252dc6aa00fa72 and the group holds 23.18 million USDG in total. On Robinhood Chain, Ink, Arbitrum One and Ethereum the same call returns 0 for the PoolManager. A cleared item of cycle 3 states that enrolment needs the registrar role or the consent of the account. The PoolManager cannot give consent, so a role holder enrolled it on X Layer. I did not re-read the enrolment functions.

How it meets the code: 1. A take or a payment of USDG on X Layer runs _updateWalletWithPayoutGroup for the PoolManager: it recomputes the shares of the PoolManager with reward preservation, writes the timestamp and applies a share delta to the totals of group 2 (ClaimableRewardsBase.sol lines 572 to 619). On the other chains the transfer takes the short path that writes two balances. 2. I traced a full round trip on X Layer. The only slot that differed afterwards was the PoolManager slot, where the timestamp field moved from 0x6b03bb26 to 0x6b03bb81 and balance, shares and group id were unchanged. Twenty five round trips in one call all succeeded and left the balance unchanged. 3. Rounding in calcShares is downward on each step, so a round trip can cost the PoolManager slot up to a few share units of one millionth of a USDG. That reduces rewards of the group destination and does not touch the balance. 4. _applyDelta saturates at zero when it subtracts and the additions are bounded by 64 bit balances, so I found no revert that a caller can cause. 5.
