What is at risk: No PoolManager funds move. A follower or RPC node on the final tag could stall or serve a fork, so users reading it would see stale pool state.

What it takes: An operator runs v0.0.7.3 instead of v0.0.7.3-rc.1.

What can be done: Integrators that read X Layer pool state should use nodes that report f60420a in web3_clientVersion.

Second reviewer's corrections: severity: low. The finding describes an operational hazard (a follower node that may stall or fork and serve stale pool state) with no loss path, which SCHEMA.md places at low rather than info. Actor operator is correct.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:104-114`.

Sources:

- https://github.com/okx/xlayer-reth/releases (v0.0.7.3 (30 October) is synced to okx/reth 81573d2; v0.0.7.3-rc.1 (5 November) is a mandatory patch that integrates the trie correctness fix)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/okx/reth/81573d2c7d806d5df9ff2d512dd000c2bf542b1b/crates/trie/trie/src/node_iter.rs (81573d2c node_iter.rs line 284 uses key.starts_with(&seek_prefix))
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/okx/reth/f60420aaac6457295a38b66bd544bab28ab6ec5d/crates/trie/trie/src/node_iter.rs (f60420aa node_iter.rs line 282 uses key == &seek_prefix and adds a regression test for an inserted leaf before a deeper stored branch)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/okx/xlayer-reth/v0.0.7.3/Cargo.lock (lock file of v0.0.7.3 pins okx/reth 81573d2c7d806d5df9ff2d512dd000c2bf542b1b; all other packages equal those of rc.1)

## F-142. The reth-bsc endpoint on publicnode answers PoolManager log queries below block 135,500,000 with an empty list and no error

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: BNB Smart Chain.

What differs on the network: Ethereum clients that pruned receipts answer a log query for a pruned range with an error. reth-bsc v0.1.4-hotfix on publicnode serves the block body but returns zero logs for every address in blocks below 135,500,000.

How it meets the code: An integration that rebuilds positions, claims or fee state from the PoolManager events Initialize, ModifyLiquidity, Swap and Donate (src/PoolManager.sol:139, 175, 241, 273) through this endpoint sees nothing for older blocks and records no activity. For block 135,483,276 the endpoint returns 0 logs where two other builds return 7 PoolManager logs, and the answer is the same by block number and by block hash. Nothing in the PoolManager depends on logs, so pool state and payouts are unchanged.

What is at risk: No funds move. Indexers, front ends and monitoring that use this endpoint can show wrong positions or miss events for older blocks.

What it takes: A reader uses this endpoint for history. No attacker action is involved.

What can be done: Read history from an archive endpoint and compare a sample of logs with receipts, as gapcheck2.py does.

Second reviewer's corrections: severity: low. SCHEMA.md gives low to an operational or integration hazard with no direct loss path, which is what the impact field describes; info is for a difference that creates no hazard. The actor value operator fits as the nearest value, since only the RPC provider's node choice causes it and no attacker is involved. The finding concerns one provider's client, not a rule of BNB Chain.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:139`, `src/PoolManager.sol:241`.

Sources:

- rpc: eth_getLogs on https://bsc-rpc.publicnode.com and https://rpc-bsc.48.club, block 135483276, file research/network-risk/cycles/021/established-l1.work/l752.log (empty answer on reth-bsc, 7 logs on 48 Club, boundary at 135,500,000)

## F-148. After Lagoon an unsafe reorg on OP Mainnet or Unichain replaces dependent blocks on the other chain with deposits-only blocks

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: Optimism, Unichain.

What differs on the network: From 11 January 2027 OP Mainnet and Unichain form an interop dependency set. The sequencer may include an executing message that consumes an initiating message from an unsafe block of the other chain, and a block whose executing message turns out invalid is replaced by a block that holds only its deposits.

How it meets the code: An account emits a log on OP Mainnet in an unsafe block and, within seconds, sends a Unichain transaction that executes it; other users' swaps, liquidity removals and takes against the Unichain PoolManager follow in the same Unichain block. If the OP Mainnet block is later reorganised (sequencer equivocation, a batch not posted within the sequencing window, or an L1 reorg), cross-chain validation marks the executing message invalid and derivation replaces the whole Unichain block with a deposits-only block. Each removed unlock is atomic (src/PoolManager.sol:104-114), so the PoolManager's balances and deltas stay consistent; what disappears is every user transaction of that block, which users and integrators may already have treated as done from the unsafe receipt.

What is at risk: No PoolManager funds move or freeze. Users whose exits sat in a replaced block must resend them; bridges, exchanges or solvers that released value on the strength of an unsafe receipt of a swap or take can be left short. The width of the effect is one block per invalid message, and a cascade is possible when the replaced block itself held initiating messages consumed on the other chain.

What it takes: Lagoon active with interop between OP Mainnet and Unichain, a sequencer policy that admits messages from unsafe remote blocks, and an unsafe reorg on the remote chain. The last needs the sequencer, the batcher operator or an Ethereum reorg; an ordinary account cannot cause it, and deposits cannot carry executing messages.

What can be done: Integrators should treat only safe heads as final for value released off chain on these two chains after Lagoon. Sequencers can restrict executing messages to safe remote blocks. Nothing in core needs to change.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:104-114`.

Sources:

- https://specs.optimism.io/interop/derivation.html (invalid executing messages invalidate a block, which is replaced by a deposits-only block; deposits are prevented from executing messages)
- https://specs.optimism.io/interop/sequencer.html (the sequencer may include executing messages with any level of confirmation safety, at higher risk of an invalid block)
- https://docs.optimism.io/notices/upgrade-22.md (interop between OP Mainnet and Unichain activates with Lagoon on 11 January 2027, subject to governance)

## F-153. Monad serves balances, call results and PoolManager receipts at latest from a proposed block that has no vote yet

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: Monad.

What differs on the network: Since v0.13.0 the Monad RPC maps latest to the Proposed state, a block the leader has sent and the node has executed speculatively before any quorum certificate exists. Ethereum's latest is a block that already carries proposer weight in the fork choice; Monad's latest has none. safe is Voted and finalized is Finalized.

How it meets the code: monad-rpc v0.16.7 resolves BlockTags::Latest (and pending) to get_latest_proposed_block_key (monad-rpc/src/data/mod.rs:91-93 and 102-106, monad-rpc/src/types/eth_json.rs:328-330 and 373-376). eth_getTransactionReceipt first reads the in-memory buffer and then looks up the transaction location against the latest proposed block key (monad-rpc/src/data/mod.rs:196-225), so a receipt is served as soon as the proposed block is executed. A wallet, router or bridge that releases value on such a receipt, a Transfer log or a balance read at latest acts on a block that consensus can still abandon: a proposal that fails to collect a QC, or one of two blocks an equivocating leader sent, is dropped implicitly when another block finalizes at that height (docs.monad.xyz block states). The PoolManager itself is not affected: every action settles inside one transaction (src/PoolManager.sol:104-114) and core reads no block field, so a dropped block takes its whole PoolManager effect with it.

What is at risk: No loss of pool funds and no locked funds. An off-chain integration that credits a user on a latest receipt of a PoolManager take could pay out for a swap that never finalizes. On the sample taken no block served at latest was replaced.

What it takes: A Monad leader that equivocates or a proposal that misses its vote, and an integration that acts on latest. An ordinary account cannot make a proposal miss its vote with a transaction.

What can be done: Integrations and bridges on Monad should settle on finalized, which lags latest by one to two blocks (about 0.4 to 0.8 s), or compare the receipt block number with the finalized height as the Monad documentation advises.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:104-114`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`.

Sources:

- https://docs.monad.xyz/reference/json-rpc/overview (latest maps to Proposed with no consensus vote, safe to Voted, finalized to Finalized; transaction-hash lookups can match a transaction in a non-finalized block)
- https://docs.monad.xyz/monad-arch/consensus/block-states (a Proposed block may never be Voted; finalizing a number implicitly abandons other proposals at that number)
- https://docs.monad.xyz/developer-essentials/changelog/releases (v0.13.0 moved latest from Finalized to Proposed (monad-bft PR 2675))
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/category-labs/monad-bft/v0.16.7/monad-rpc/src/data/mod.rs (get_latest_block_key returns the latest proposed block key (lines 91-93); Latest and Safe tags resolve to proposed and voted keys (102-106); receipts are looked up against the latest proposed key (196-225))
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/category-labs/monad-bft/v0.16.7/monad-rpc/src/types/eth_json.rs (Latest is commented proposed, Safe voted, pending parses to Latest (lines 328-330, 373-376))
- theme-deferred-verification.work/raw/monad_heads.json and monad_heads_recheck.json (eth_getBlockByNumber on https://rpc.monad.xyz, Monad/0.16.7 at start, middle and end) (2,000 distinct blocks 125,644,933 to 125,648,318 recorded at latest all have the same hash after finality; latest minus finalized was 1 or 2 blocks in 41 of 43 snapshots; 245 requests were refused with HTTP 429 and retried later)
- theme-deferred-verification.work/raw/monad_pm_receipts.json (eth_getLogs and eth_getTransactionReceipt on https://rpc.monad.xyz, Monad/0.16.7) (70 PoolManager transactions were read at latest, 69 of them before their block was finalized; none of the 70 receipts changed block hash, status or log count after finality)

## F-155. MegaETH serves call results and balances at latest from mini-block state that the sequencer can still drop

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: MegaETH.

What differs on the network: On MegaETH the latest block selector returns the streaming state with committed mini-blocks. That state is ahead of the safe head, and finality depends on the containing EVM block being posted to and finalized on L1. On Ethereum, latest is a proposed block that a reorg can also drop, and OP Stack chains serve an unsafe head at latest as well. MegaETH differs in that latest includes mini-block state inside the current EVM block. The lead varies: two reads on 2026-11-26 gave 168 and 416 one-second blocks over safe, and 2,087 and 1,953 over finalized. Monad (F-153) and Avalanche (F-152) have the same class of hazard.

How it meets the code: An integrator that reads a swap result, a take, or an ERC-6909 balance at latest and then acts off chain (pays out on another chain, releases goods) acts on state that only the sequencer has signed. If the sequencer drops or rewrites unsafe blocks, the PoolManager state rolls back with them. No PoolManager code path reads the selector; its accounting inside one transaction (PoolManager.sol:349-365 for settle, :291-297 for take) is unaffected. The hazard is the same shape as the Monad proposed-block finding of theme-deferred-verification and needs the operator to act.

What is at risk: Integrators that treat latest as final can be double spent by whoever controls the sequencer. LPs and claim holders inside the PoolManager lose nothing from this alone.

What it takes: The sequencer drops or reorders unsafe blocks, and an off-chain party acted on latest.

What can be done: Integrators wait for safe or finalized before acting off chain on PoolManager results.

This entry adds to F-153.

Second reviewer's corrections: The lead of latest over safe and finalized varies; at 2026-11-26 it was 416 and 1,953 blocks when re-queried. The comparison with Ethereum holds for OP Stack style chains too, which also serve an unsafe head at latest; the MegaETH difference is that latest includes mini-block state inside the current EVM block.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:349-365`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`.

Sources:

- https://docs.megaeth.com/developer-docs/rpc/types.md (latest is the streaming state including committed mini-blocks; safe and finalized are EVM blocks)
- https://docs.megaeth.com/mini-block.md (a mini-block signature does not prove finality; finality depends on L1 posting)
- rpc:https://mainnet.megaeth.com/rpc eth_getBlockByNumber latest/safe/finalized 2026-11-26 (latest 32,924,634, safe 32,924,466, finalized 32,922,547)

## F-157. From Lagoon a predeploy on OP Mainnet and Unichain holds type(uint128).max wei that the interop bridge releases, so native pools rest on interop message validity

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: Optimism, Unichain.

What differs on the network: Ethereum has no contract that can mint native ETH. OP Stack chains already credit native ETH through deposit transactions from OptimismPortal, so native pools there already rest on a bridge, and ETHLiquidity adds a second source. From Lagoon (11 January 2027, a date that depends on governance approval and can move) the ETHLiquidity predeploy on OP Mainnet and Unichain starts with type(uint128).max wei and pays ETH to SuperchainETHBridge whenever a cross-chain ETH message from the other chain is relayed, or when an expired send is refunded.

How it meets the code: Native pools settle with msg.value and pay out with a plain value call (src/PoolManager.sol:349-364, src/types/Currency.sol:44-52). The PoolManager cannot tell where the ETH it receives came from. A relayETH on a destination chain mints from ETHLiquidity against an initiating sendETH on the source chain, and the protocol checks that link through the CrossL2Inbox access list at sequencing and through derivation. If a relay of a message that was never initiated were accepted into a canonical block, its recipient would hold ETH that no burn backs, could pay it into native pools as a normal settle and take out the other currency of every native pool up to its liquidity. Derivation replaces a block that holds an invalid executing message with a deposits-only block, which also removes the swaps made with the ETH, so in the canonical chain the ETH and the swaps vanish together. An ordinary account can send a relay transaction, but cannot make an invalid one canonical: that needs the sequencer to include it and the derivation or proof system to accept it. Before Lagoon the proxy at 0x4200..0025 has no implementation and a balance of 0 on all four Lagoon chains (queried 2026-11-26). The contract has no receive or fallback, so a native take to it reverts (ETHLiquidity.sol:63-82). F-148 records the other face of the same dependency: an unsafe reorg on one of the two chains replaces dependent blocks on the other.

What is at risk: Up to every native ETH pool on OP Mainnet and Unichain, but only through a fault in interop validation or in derivation. No path for an ordinary account was found.

What it takes: Lagoon active on 11 January 2027 and a fault that lets an unbacked relayETH or refundETH become canonical, or off-chain parties acting on an unsafe block that is later replaced.

What can be done: Treat interop ETH like a bridge: integrations that act on PoolManager payouts should wait for safe blocks after Lagoon. Governance can pause ETHLiquidity through the L1 pause.

This entry adds to F-148.

Second reviewer's corrections: The difference with Ethereum is narrower than stated: OP Stack chains already credit native ETH through deposit transactions from OptimismPortal, so native pools there already rest on a bridge. ETHLiquidity adds a second such source. The Lagoon mainnet date depends on governance approval and can move, as the notice says.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/types/Currency.sol:44-52`.

Sources:

- https://specs.optimism.io/interop/eth-liquidity.html (initial balance type(uint128).max wei and mint only by SuperchainETHBridge)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum-optimism/optimism/op-contracts/v10.0.0/packages/contracts-bedrock/src/L2/ETHLiquidity.sol (burn, mint, fund and setPaused, no receive or fallback)
- https://docs.optimism.io/notices/upgrade-22.md (Lagoon activation dates and ETHLiquidity pause)
- https://specs.optimism.io/interop/derivation.html (a block with an invalid executing message is replaced by a deposits-only block)

## F-162. An account that runs another user's ERC-4337 operation from inside its own unlock makes every PoolManager router call of that operation revert with AlreadyUnlocked

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: all 21 networks.

What differs on the network: EntryPoint v0.6, v0.7 and v0.8 accept handleOps from a contract, so whoever holds a signed user operation first decides the frames around it. On these seven chains the only documented way for an outsider to obtain other users' pending operations is the ERC-4337 shared mempool, which the ERC-4337 documentation lists as live on Ethereum, Arbitrum and Optimism only, at a stage where only whitelisted bundlers propagate operations. No shared mempool is documented for Base, Unichain, Zora, World Chain, Ink or Soneium, where operations sit in the queue of the bundler the wallet chose.

How it meets the code: 1. unlock reverts with AlreadyUnlocked while the transient lock flag is set and clears it only after the callback returns (src/PoolManager.sol:104-114). 2. A contract of the attacker calls unlock and, inside unlockCallback, calls EntryPoint.handleOps with the victim's operation. 3. The victim's router calls unlock and reverts; the EntryPoint records the operation as failed and charges its gas to the victim's account or paymaster, paid to the beneficiary the attacker named. 4. The attacker's callback returns with no delta and its unlock completes. Nothing credits the attacker or lowers a pool balance, because every delta the victim's contracts could open is keyed to their own address (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297). The new evidence narrows step 2: on OP Mainnet the attacker must be a whitelisted shared-mempool bundler, and on the other six chains it must be the bundler the victim submitted to, or obtain operations from it.

What is at risk: No pool funds move and no claim or position is frozen. One user operation fails, its nonce is used and its gas goes to the attacker's beneficiary. The victim resubmits through another bundler.

What it takes: The attacker must hold the victim's signed operation before an honest bundler includes it. On OP Mainnet that requires a whitelisted bundler in the stage 1 shared mempool; on the other six chains the documented sources give no public view of pending operations, so the attacker must be, or be fed by, the victim's chosen bundler, which is an operator role in practice. The operation must target EntryPoint v0.6, v0.7 or v0.8. The cycle 29 recheck covered the seven OP Stack chains with ETH gas. The other 14 networks stay on the record because the same EntryPoint versions are deployed widely, but their bundler arrangements were not read.

What can be done: Wallets can submit PoolManager trades through EntryPoint v0.9, whose handleOps accepts only an externally owned caller, or through a bundler they trust. No core change is proposed.

This entry adds to F-071.

Second reviewer's corrections: actor: operator. The finding's own preconditions say the attacker must be a whitelisted shared-mempool bundler on OP Mainnet, or the bundler the victim chose (or someone it feeds) on the other six chains. The Etherspot post confirms that joining the shared mempool needs permission. A party that holds a victim's pending operation only through permissioned relay infrastructure or the victim's choice of relayer is not an ordinary account under SCHEMA.md. The hazard is out of scope for an unprivileged attacker. Severity low stands: there is no loss or freeze, only a failed operation whose gas goes to the beneficiary.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:104-114`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/libraries/Lock.sol:13-25`.

Sources:

- https://docs.erc4337.io/bundlers/censorship-resistance.html (the shared mempool is live on Ethereum, Arbitrum and Optimism, and the ecosystem is moving from stage 1 (only whitelisted bundlers propagate operations) to stage 2)
- https://etherspot.io/blog/erc-4337-shared-mempool-official-launch-on-ethereum-mainnet-arbitrum-and-optimism/ (launch announcement of 12 November 2024 naming Ethereum, Arbitrum and Optimism)
- https://etherspot.io/blog/eip-7702-infrastructure-launches-to-support-account-abstraction-for-eoas/ (October 2025: the free shared-mempool infrastructure is live on Ethereum and Optimism, with Base, World Chain and Unichain listed as upcoming)
- https://sourcify.dev/server/v2/contract/1/0x433709009B8330FDa32311DF1C2AFA402eD8D009?fields=sources (EntryPoint v0.9 nonReentrant requires tx.origin == msg.sender (recorded in cycle 28, F-162))

## F-117. Six go-ethereum family nodes run a Go toolchain with a published silent dead store miscompile, and no function on the opcode or state path has the triggering shape

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: Ethereum, Arbitrum One, Robinhood Chain, Polygon, BNB Smart Chain, Avalanche.

What differs on the network: Go issue 81863: from Go 1.26.0 through go1.26.10 and from Go 1.27.0 through go1.27.3, the dead auto elimination pass attributes a CondSelect of two local addresses to its first operand only, so a store through a pointer that a branch chose between two non-escaping locals is deleted when the first local is never read, even if the other one is. It applies on every architecture whose compiler emits CondSelect (amd64, arm64, ppc64le, loong64, wasm). The fix is go1.26.11 and go1.27.4, released 2026-11-10. Today Arbitrum One answers as nitro v3.13.0-rc.9 linux-arm64 go1.27.3, Robinhood Chain as the same release on linux-arm64 and linux-amd64 with go1.27.3, Polygon (publicnode) as five bor v2.11.1-private builds with go1.26.10, BNB Smart Chain as Geth/v1.8.2 linux-amd64 go1.26.10, and Avalanche as avalanchego 1.15.3 go=1.26.10. Elysium reports go1.25.12 and the Celo op-geth backend go1.24.13, which predate the faulty pass order. Cycle 17 addition, Ethereum: the official geth v1.17.9 and v1.17.10 builds pin Go 1.27.2 and 1.27.3, both inside the range of the same issue. These releases also run every untraced execution (eth_call, block import, block building) through a new generated switch, execUntraced, while traced calls still use the old table loop, so a step trace no longer shows the loop that produces blocks. Cycle 18 addition: go1.26.10 does not hold the fix. The 1.26 backport (CL 845967) was merged on 2026-10-27, eight days after go1.26.10 was released on 2026-10-19, and go1.26.11 of 2026-11-10 is the first fixed 1.26 release. bor v2.11.1, BNB Chain geth v1.8.2 and avalanchego 1.15.3 therefore run an affected compiler. In go1.26.10 the branch elimination pass runs before dead auto elimination (compile.go lines 479 and 481); in go1.24.13 the order is the reverse, so Celo's op-geth backend is outside the range. Chain 1 is listed for the official geth v1.17.9 and v1.17.10 builds only; the Ethereum endpoint in networks.json answers from reth, a Rust client, and the one geth build tested was reached through the blockpi endpoint in cycle 17.

How it meets the code: The miscompile needs four things in one function after inlining: two stack allocated locals whose address is taken, a pointer that a side effect free if or if-else picks between their addresses, a store, zeroing or copy through that pointer and no load through it (branchelim refuses a CondSelect whose result feeds a load address), and a later direct read of the second local. A scan of holiman/uint256 v1.3.2 and v1.2.4 (all of uint256.go, mod.go, div.go, conversion.go, decimal.go) and of core/vm instructions.go, interpreter.go, stack.go, memory.go, evm.go, eips.go and core/state journal.go, transient_storage.go, statedb.go, state_object.go, access_list.go of the nitro fork 0e44603, bsc v1.8.2, bor v2.11.1 and libevm d398bc4dbe41 found no such function. The only branch selected pointers are: uint256 AddMod (x = &tmpX, y = &tmpY, v1.3.2 lines 240-245), where one operand is the caller's pointer, the pointers are only loaded from, and branchelim therefore keeps the Phi; SMod and SDiv, where the branch calls Neg and so is not side effect free and the pointer is only passed to Mod or Div; DivMod (m = new(Int).Set(m)) with a side effect in the branch; bor's interrupt flag in Run, loaded atomically and passed to runSwitch; the jump table choice in evm.go, which takes addresses of globals; and journal mutation state, which is heap memory. Sqrt in v1.2.4 swaps two pointers in a loop, which branchelim does not touch, and no opcode calls Sqrt. A dropped store in these functions would change the ADDMOD, SDIV or SMOD result, so those operands were run: 6,000 ADDMOD vectors with a modulus of at least 2^192 and each operand above, equal to or below the modulus, 6,112 SDIV and SMOD vectors over all sign pairs, plus the 99,985 battery and 85,544 targeted vectors. On each of the five networks every request was a JSON-RPC batch of web3_clientVersion and eth_call. There was no mismatch on any build. The PoolManager reaches SDIV and SMOD in TickBitmap.compress and Pool.tickSpacingToMaxLiquidityPerTick and MULMOD in FullMath and SqrtPriceMath; ADDMOD appears only in the compiler's metadata trailer. Cycle 17 addition, Ethereum: a scan of geth v1.17.9 core/vm and core/state finds no function with the triggering shape, and the generated loop calls the same handler functions as the table loop. The battery, the targeted vectors and 232 PUSH, DUP and SWAP programs give the specified words on geth v1.17.9 built with go1.27.2 (blockpi endpoint). The verifier corrected two points: the fault also reaches go1.26 releases before its 1.26 backport, and the runs reported for a go1.25.14 build went through a gateway that answered only from reth and erigon in 30 samples, so only one geth toolchain has been tested. Whether go1.26.10, which bor, BNB Chain geth and avalanchego use, holds the backport is lead L-838. Cycle 18 addition: a search of the syntax trees with type information (shapescan, built on go/ast and go/types, with the issue reproducer and three variants as positive controls) covered 849 non-test files in 73 packages: holiman/uint256 v1.3.2 and v1.2.4, geth v1.17.9, bsc v1.8.2, bor v2.11.1, celo op-geth c7ed55b8, libevm d398bc4dbe41, avalanchego graft/evm and graft/coreth v1.15.3, and Nitro's fork at 0e44603. It found no site where a branch picks between the addresses of two locals, no call that passes two local addresses to a pointer selector, and 60 sites of the variant with one local and one other pointer. Each of the 60 fails a precondition: the pointer is loaded through, the arm allocates, or the choice is a loop variable. Compiled with go1.24.7, the 155 functions of uint256 v1.3.2, the 153 of v1.2.4 and the 25 arithmetic, comparison and bitwise handlers of five client trees hold conditional selects of integer type only. 2,340 SIGNEXTEND, ADDMOD and sign extension chain vectors matched on Polygon, BNB Chain, Avalanche, Celo op-geth, Arbitrum One and Robinhood Chain. Not covered: the private fork commit of Nitro rc.9 (lead L-864), and files that only bor or celo op-geth hold outside the fetch list (lead L-865).

What is at risk: No path by which an ordinary account takes or freezes funds was found. If an unread function of the node held the shape, a node built with an affected toolchain would compute another state than one built with a fixed toolchain, which is a consensus fault of the operators and not something an attacker can aim at a pool through the PoolManager.

What it takes: Block producers would have to run an affected build and some function on the state transition path outside the files read would have to contain the shape. Neither is shown. The sequencer and validator builds are not published.

What can be done: Ask the Arbitrum, Robinhood Chain, Polygon, BNB Smart Chain and Avalanche operators to rebuild with go1.26.11 or go1.27.4 or later. Rerun paired.py after their next release.

Code: `src/libraries/TickBitmap.sol:20-27`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:576-578`, `src/libraries/FullMath.sol:24`, `src/libraries/SqrtPriceMath.sol:252`.

## F-118. MegaETH blocks are executed by a closed JIT executor that no public RPC node re-executes, so eth_call evidence speaks for a different EVM than the one that writes PoolManager state

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: MegaETH.

What differs on the network: On Ethereum every node re-executes every block with an interpreter whose source is public. On MegaETH the sequencer runs a parallel, JIT-compiled executor whose source is not published. The default RPC nodes are replicas that apply the sequencer's results without re-execution. Only stateless validators, full nodes and provers re-execute, and the public stateless validator does so with the stock revm 40.0.3 interpreter, mega-evm v1.7.3 and ruint 1.17.2.

How it meets the code: 1. The stateless-validator README says the validator uses a single-threaded executor based on the vanilla revm interpreter, and that, together with the K-semantics executor and the JIT-compiled executor on sequencer nodes, this makes three MegaETH client implementations. 2. The architecture page says replica nodes receive blocks and execution results from the sequencer and apply them without re-execution, and that this is the default RPC mode. eth_call and debug_traceTransaction on such a node therefore run that node's local EVM on state that the sequencer wrote. 3. The only public compiler from megaeth-labs is the evmone-compiler prototype. Its PUSHnJUMPI tests the whole 256 bit condition through intx's explicit operator bool and falls through when the word is zero (evmone_aot_compiler.hpp lines 84-96, intx v0.10.1 line 997). It checks gas only before jumps, but a frame that ends out of gas is undone as a whole, so the late check changes gas use and not state. Nothing ties this prototype to the production build. 4. If the production executor computed MULMOD, a shift, a comparison or a jump differently from the specification, every pool's swap and liquidity amounts in src/libraries (FullMath, SqrtPriceMath, TickMath, TickBitmap) would differ in blocks only. A replica would store the wrong result. A stateless validator would see a state root mismatch. 5. This cycle found no such divergence. 2,885 mined swaps that crossed initialized ticks or took several steps reproduce to the unit. 53,811 mined swaps satisfy the tick relation. For 7,627 mined PoolManager transactions, the receipts the sequencer wrote equal the replica's re-execution in status and in every surviving log. Cycle 24 addition: the 20,000 MegaETH transactions that cycle 23 traced were rerun instruction by instruction with a counter on each side of every conditional jump (117,100 PoolManager frames). Of the 387 conditional jumps of the build, the closed producer executed both sides of 116, one side of 193 and neither side of 78. In 129 of the 193 the missing side reverts. Never executed by the producers of MegaETH, Monad or HyperEVM in the replayed windows: donate (src/libraries/Pool.sol:466-480), clear (src/PoolManager.sol:310-319), settleFor (src/PoolManager.sol:305-307), ERC-6909 transfer, transferFrom and approve (src/ERC6909.sol:25-56), the 512-bit path of four of the five compiled copies of FullMath.mulDiv, and the overflow fallback of getNextSqrtPriceFromAmount0RoundingUp (src/libraries/SqrtPriceMath.sol:43-52). Three of those 512-bit paths cannot return to a caller at all, because the int128 casts at src/libraries/Pool.sol:192, :455 and :459 revert first. The protocol fee branch is also unexecuted, and it needs governance, because the fee controller is zero on all three networks. Every frame that the producers did execute gave the source's words when rerun instruction by instruction. No fault was found; the entry records which sides rest on eth_call and source reading alone. On MegaETH, logs over the whole chain (blocks 0 to 32,890,347) hold no Donate event and no ERC-6909 event other than 47 mints and 140 burns by the owner. Six swaps before the window ended at the MIN or MAX tick clamp, and all six replay exactly.

What is at risk: None found. The record exists so that deployers know where the assurance comes from. A fault in the closed executor would hit every pool, an ordinary transaction could trigger it, and the only checks are the stateless validators and the fault proof path.

What it takes: A fault in the closed production executor or in a future release of it. No fault is known.

What can be done: Run the public stateless validator against MegaETH blocks that contain PoolManager transactions, and rerun tools/opdiff/swaps.py, replay.py and this unit's frames.py after each mega-reth release.

Second reviewer's corrections: The compiler file is lib/compiler/aot_compiler.hpp, and the PUSHnJUMPI macro sits at lines 87-96 of it. The finding names evmone_aot_compiler.hpp lines 84-96.

Code: `src/libraries/FullMath.sol:1-117`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:279-463`, `src/PoolManager.sol:104-114`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/megaeth-labs/stateless-validator/main/README.md (Lines 21 and 25-26: the validator uses the vanilla revm interpreter, and sequencer nodes run a hyper-optimized, parallel, JIT-compiled executor.)
- https://docs.megaeth.com/architecture.md (Replica nodes apply sequencer results without re-execution and are the default RPC mode. Full nodes and provers re-execute.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/megaeth-labs/stateless-validator/main/Cargo.lock (The validator pins mega-evm v1.7.3 (commit 59b8e391), revm 40.0.3, revm-interpreter 37.0.3 and ruint 1.17.2 from crates.io.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/megaeth-labs/evmone-compiler/master/lib/compiler/aot_compiler.hpp (The PUSHnJUMPI macro pops the full word and tests it. The gas check sits before jumps (GAS_CHECK_LOC 1).)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/chfast/intx/v0.10.1/include/intx/intx.hpp (Line 997: uint<N>::operator bool compares the whole value with zero.)
- rpc https://mainnet.megaeth.com/rpc web3_clientVersion on 2026-11-24 (The endpoint reports mega-reth/v2.2.3-72527bc@mnet-ash-rpc-2.)
- https://mainnet.megaeth.com/rpc (eth_getLogs on the PoolManager over blocks 0 to 32,890,347 on 2026-11-26: 0 Donate logs; 187 ERC-6909 logs, 47 mints and 140 burns by the owner (cycles/024/theme-producer-branch-coverage.work/out/megaeth-6909.json).)
- research/network-risk/cycles/024/theme-producer-branch-coverage.work/out/4326/branches.json (Per-side JUMPI counts with source lines for the 20,000 MegaETH transactions.)

## F-119. Arbitrum family validators compute the settled state with a go1.25.9 WebAssembly build that public endpoints do not run

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: Arbitrum One, Robinhood Chain, Elysium.

What differs on the network: On Ethereum the client that answers eth_call also produces the state. On these three chains the state that the parent chain accepts, and so the state behind every withdrawal, is defined by the replay binary compiled by go1.25.9 to WebAssembly (GOOS=wasip1 GOARCH=wasm) and published as WAVM machine consensus-v61. The public endpoints and, as far as anyone can see, the sequencers run a native arm64 or amd64 build of nitro v3.13.0-rc.9 made with go1.27.3. The two paths compile the same go-ethereum fork and holiman/uint256 v1.3.2 with different Go releases and different code generators.

How it meets the code: Every opcode test in this review, including the 185,529 vectors, the 550 scripts and the reruns of this cycle, ran on the native build. If the WebAssembly build of uint256 or core/vm computed one MULMOD in FullMath.sol:24 or 48, one SDIV in TickBitmap, or one JUMPI differently, a swap would produce one state on the sequencer and another in the replay. The sequencer's state is what users see and what the next transactions build on; the replay's state is what an honest validator asserts on the parent chain. An ordinary account cannot cause such a split unless a codegen difference exists, and none is known. The comparison of confirmed assertions with node block hashes over 31 assertions on Arbitrum One, 57 on Robinhood Chain and 6 on Elysium found no split, which covers every PoolManager transaction in those ranges.

What is at risk: No loss path. A split would stall confirmations and withdrawals to the parent chain for all users until the operators reconciled the two paths, and pool funds would stay where they are.

What it takes: A difference between the go1.25.9 WebAssembly build and the go1.27.3 native build on an instruction the PoolManager uses. None is known.

What can be done: Deployers can watch confirmed assertions against their own node, as done here. Operators can build the native node with the Go release of the consensus machine.

Second reviewer's corrections: The statement that the assertion comparison covers every PoolManager transaction as an independent check holds only for Arbitrum One, whose rollup returns validatorWhitelistDisabled() = 1. The Robinhood Chain rollup returns validatorWhitelistDisabled() = 0 with two whitelisted validators (0xa0a1d8cd6f867a583a39232a477ff231afd8ec4c, 0x992d8d50548fcccaf11147a9a692c496011c8aa0), and the Elysium rollup returns 0 with one whitelisted validator 0x8e87f7c65750c25c6708de33823124cf457676b2, an EOA that sent the confirming transaction 0x249e9127...4269 itself. On those two chains the match shows that the operator's own validators agreed with the operator's node, not that an independent replay of consensus-v61 accepted it. A minor point: arm64 also has no Div64 intrinsic in go1.25.9, so the native arm64 path and the wasm path share the generic Div64 code. The actor value operator is acceptable: with no known codegen difference nobody can trigger a split, and if one existed the effect would be a stalled confirmation that operators resolve.

Code: `src/libraries/FullMath.sol:24-73`, `src/libraries/TickMath.sol:104`, `src/libraries/UnsafeMath.sol:14`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OffchainLabs/nitro/v3.12.4/Makefile (replay.wasm is built with GOOS=wasip1 GOARCH=wasm (lines 477-480))
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OffchainLabs/nitro/v3.12.4/Dockerfile (go1.25.9 is pinned for the wasm build (line 73) and consensus-v61 root 0xc10cd7ec... is downloaded (line 206))
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OffchainLabs/nitro/consensus-v61/Dockerfile (the consensus-v61 tag pins go1.25.9)
- https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com eth_call wasmModuleRoot() on 0x4DCeB440657f21083db8aDd07665f8ddBe1DCfc0 and 0x23a19d23e89166adedbdcb432518ab01e4272d94 (both rollups pin 0xc10cd7ec6acaf1c441a3f6bd0900ad20f15855ba775a96f1939118cbc629dc97)
- https://arb1.arbitrum.io/rpc web3_clientVersion (public endpoint is nitro v3.13.0-rc.9 built with go1.27.3)

## F-120. No public client checks the storage that the closed HyperEVM validator writes, because block headers carry a zero state root and nanoreth compares its own root as zero

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: both. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: HyperEVM.

What differs on the network: On Ethereum every full node recomputes the state root of each block and rejects a block whose root differs. HyperEVM blocks are produced by the closed hl-node validator, and the headers it publishes have stateRoot 0x00..00. The public archive client nanoreth (the build served at rpc.purroofgroup.com reports reth/v0.1.0-f0cad31) replaces its computed state root with B256::ZERO before the comparison with the header, so the comparison always passes. hyper-evm-sync computes a private blake3 hash of its state for snapshots and compares it with nothing. Both clients do re-execute every transaction and stop on a different receipt: nanoreth checks gas used, the receipts root and the logs bloom against the header, and hyper-evm-sync asserts that its computed receipts equal the validator's receipts.

How it meets the code: The PoolManager keeps pool state, positions, ERC-6909 balances and protocol fees in persistent storage (src/libraries/Pool.sol, src/libraries/Position.sol, src/ERC6909.sol, src/ProtocolFees.sol). A state root would commit to exactly those slots. Transient storage is cleared at the end of each transaction and is in no state root on any network, so the missing check has no bearing on it. A validator fault that changed a persistent storage write without changing gas, status or logs of that transaction would not stop either public client. It would show up only once a later transaction reads the slot and its receipt differs, and then the client halts instead of correcting anything. The validator's receipts are therefore checked publicly, its storage writes only indirectly. This needs a fault or a deliberate change in the validator, which only its operator controls. No mismatch was observed: 2,762 mined PoolManager transactions over the whole history agree in status and logs with nanoreth's re-execution, 485 storage slots from prestateTracer agree with nanoreth's state at the block, and 222 touched PoolManager slots read at latest agree between the official endpoint and nanoreth. Cycle 24 addition: all 2,833 PoolManager transactions in HyperEVM's history to block 52,268,606 were rerun instruction by instruction with a counter on each side of every conditional jump (20,018 PoolManager frames). Of the 387 conditional jumps of the build, the closed validator executed both sides of 121, one side of 184 and neither side of 82. In 131 of the 184 the missing side reverts. Never executed by the producers of MegaETH, Monad or HyperEVM in the replayed windows: donate (src/libraries/Pool.sol:466-480), clear (src/PoolManager.sol:310-319), settleFor (src/PoolManager.sol:305-307), ERC-6909 transfer, transferFrom and approve (src/ERC6909.sol:25-56), the 512-bit path of four of the five compiled copies of FullMath.mulDiv, and the overflow fallback of getNextSqrtPriceFromAmount0RoundingUp (src/libraries/SqrtPriceMath.sol:43-52). Three of those 512-bit paths cannot return to a caller at all, because the int128 casts at src/libraries/Pool.sol:192, :455 and :459 revert first. The protocol fee branch is also unexecuted, and it needs governance, because the fee controller is zero on all three networks. Every frame that the producers did execute gave the source's words when rerun instruction by instruction. No fault was found; the entry records which sides rest on eth_call and source reading alone. HyperEVM has in addition never run an exact-output swap: SwapMath.computeSwapStep lines 88-104, getNextSqrtPriceFromOutput (src/libraries/SqrtPriceMath.sol:158-178) and the subtracting sides of both next-price functions have 0 executions. It took the MAX tick clamp 8 times and never the MIN side. The official endpoint answers eth_call like revm behind reth's RPC types on all 38 probes where client families differ, and it agrees with a nanoreth replica on 8,867 PoolManager storage items. Whether that endpoint runs the code that produces blocks is still unstated by Hyperliquid.

What is at risk: No loss path for an ordinary account. The hazard is on evidence: clearances of HyperEVM that rest on storage, as opposed to receipts, are backed by nanoreth's own state and by the official endpoint, not by a check against the validator's commitment.

What it takes: A fault or change in the closed hl-node validator that alters a storage write but not the receipt of the same transaction. Only the validator operator can cause it.

What can be done: The deployer can run nanoreth or hyper-evm-sync and keep it at head, since a later receipt mismatch halts it, and can compare PoolManager slots between the official endpoint and nanoreth at regular intervals with eth_getStorageAt.

Second reviewer's corrections: The mechanism lists the transient storage libraries (Lock.sol, CurrencyReserves.sol, CurrencyDelta.sol, NonzeroDeltaCount.sol) among the storage that a state root check would cover. Transient storage is cleared at the end of each transaction and is never committed to any state root, so a missing state root check has no bearing on it; a validator fault in transient semantics would show in the same transaction's receipt or not at all. The relevant slots are the persistent ones in Pool, Position and the ERC-6909 and protocol fee mappings. Line numbers for the two comparisons are 565 and 627 in payload_validator.rs; 562 and 626 are the assignments. Actor operator and severity info are correct.

## F-121. BNB Chain's own RPC nodes run a geth build whose commit is not in the public repository

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: BNB Smart Chain.

What differs on the network: bsc-dataseed1.bnbchain.org and bsc-dataseed2.bnbchain.org answer web3_clientVersion with Geth/v1.8.2-4e1c8247-20261104. The v1.8.2 tag is commit 54afaff4 of 2026-11-03. GitHub returns not found for the compare view v1.8.2...4e1c8247 and HTTP 422 for the commit, while the tag commit and the later pre-release commit 4bd1c730 resolve. The geth version string appends the first 8 characters of the build commit and the build date, so the official endpoints run code built one day after the release from a commit the public repository does not hold.

How it meets the code: Every BSC reading of cycles 8 to 12 (interpreter, jump destination cache, keccak opcode, state) used the public v1.8.2 tag. A build from an unpublished commit can differ in any of these. The vectors and scripts of this cycle ran against that build and gave the specified results, so whatever differs is outside what 185,529 opcode vectors, 8,415 MULMOD sequence vectors and the PoolManager scripts reach. The most likely explanation is a private security or operational patch on top of v1.8.2, as BNB Chain has shipped before, which nobody outside can read.

What is at risk: No loss path. The difference is that the code serving BNB Chain's own endpoints, and possibly its validators, cannot be read, so the source readings of this review cover the public release only.

What it takes: None for the fact. A harmful difference would need BNB Chain or a validator to run a faulty private build.

What can be done: Ask BNB Chain which commit 4e1c8247 is and whether validators run it, and rerun the vector and script sets after Jenner on 2026-12-08 against both dataseed and reth-bsc.

Second reviewer's corrections: The 20261104 field is the commit date of 4e1c8247 taken by git show --format=%ct (internal/build/env.go lines 164-176), not the build date. The statement that a private security patch is the most likely explanation is a guess without a source. I could not open the GitHub commit or compare pages (HTTP 403); the absence of 4e1c8247 rests on the raw.githubusercontent.com 404 and on the research agent's report.

Code: `src/libraries/FullMath.sol:14-116`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-364`.

Sources:

- https://bsc-dataseed1.bnbchain.org (web3_clientVersion returned Geth/v1.8.2-4e1c8247-20261104/linux-amd64/go1.26.10 on 2026-11-24, and dataseed2 the same)
- https://github.com/bnb-chain/bsc/releases (v1.8.2 is commit 54afaff, released 2026-11-03; tmp-master-4bd1c730-20261117 is a later pre-release)
- https://github.com/bnb-chain/bsc/commit/54afaff (the tag commit resolves (54afaff4da31ac9e49ba646a4f65104edac5a2f3, 2026-11-03), while https://github.com/bnb-chain/bsc/commit/4e1c8247 returned HTTP 422 and https://github.com/bnb-chain/bsc/compare/v1.8.2...4e1c8247 returned not found)

## F-122. Withdrawals on OP Mainnet, Unichain and Ink now settle against kona-client on the Cannon MIPS64 machine, a Rust build that no RPC endpoint runs

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: Optimism, Unichain, Ink.

What differs on the network: On Ethereum the client that answers eth_call also produces the state that matters. On these three chains the state that L1 accepts for withdrawals is fixed by the respected game type of the AnchorStateRegistry. A read on Ethereum at block 26,486,262 returns respectedGameType 9 on all three, which the OP contracts name SUPER_CANNON_KONA. The game implementation 0x19af533c... (SuperFaultDisputeGame 0.8.0) carries absolutePrestate 0x03e5a548...37c9 and the MIPS64 VM 0xacc005dc... version 1.10.1. The superchain registry lists that prestate as the cannon64-kona-interop build of release 1.9.0. The monorepo tag kona-client/v1.9.0 pins revm 43.0.2, revm-interpreter 43.0.1, op-revm 20.0.0, ruint 1.20.0 and alloy-primitives 1.7.3, compiled with Rust 1.95 for the big-endian MIPS64 target of Cannon. The nodes behind the public endpoints are op-reth builds on x86-64 or arm64. Cycle 7 cleared the kona proof issues on the ground that no chain used kona as its respected proof; that ground no longer holds for these three chains.

How it meets the code: If the MIPS64 build of revm computed one opcode differently from the x86 node for some input, a transaction hitting that input would give one post-state on L2 and another in the proof program. The PoolManager paths where such an input could arise are mulmod(a, b, not(0)) and mulmod(a, b, denominator) in src/libraries/FullMath.sol:24 and :48, the sar and signextend in src/libraries/TickBitmap.sol:38, the shifts and byte lookups of src/libraries/BitMath.sol:16-22 and :40-45, div(not(0), price) in src/libraries/TickMath.sol:104 and the rounding mulmod in src/libraries/SqrtPriceMath.sol:252. Any account could then disprove the honest output root by running the public program, so proposals covering that block would lose and L1 withdrawals of every user would stall until governance changed the prestate. Pool balances inside L2 would not move. This review found no such opcode difference. ruint 1.20.0 has one architecture-specific path, the borrow chain behind limb comparison (src/algorithms/mod.rs:144-185), with a portable fallback of two overflowing_sub calls (src/algorithms/add.rs:24-35). Its division uses the MG10 reciprocal on u64 and u128 products, and u128 products on MIPS64 lower to DMULTU, whose on-chain semantics in MIPS64Instructions.sol:794-798 are the exact 128-bit product. The same targeted vectors were run through a transcription of the Go library that World Chain's program uses with DMULTU as the multiplier and gave no mismatch (see cleared). The Rust path itself was read, not executed.

What is at risk: No loss path found. A divergence would freeze L1 withdrawals of all users of the three chains, including withdrawals of tokens taken out of the PoolManager, until operators replaced the prestate. Funds held by the PoolManager on L2 stay where they are.

What it takes: An opcode result that differs between revm 43 on x86-64 and the same code compiled for Cannon MIPS64. None is known. Matching the prestate hash to the kona-client/v1.9.0 tag rests on the registry entry; the build was not reproduced.

What can be done: Deployers can run kona-client from the same prestate against blocks that contain PoolManager transactions, or watch for output roots that a SUPER_CANNON_KONA game rejects. Operators can add the opcode battery of this review to the kona test suite on the MIPS64 target.

Second reviewer's corrections: The cited MIPS64Instructions.sol is the develop branch; the line numbers there need not match the source of the deployed 1.10.1 VM, though version() on chain is 1.10.1 as claimed. The link from the prestate hash to the kona-client/v1.9.0 tag rests on the registry entry, as the finding says. If an opcode divergence between the MIPS64 kona build and op-reth existed, both the triggering transaction and the challenge would be open to an ordinary account under permissionless type 9 games, and the effect would fall on L1 withdrawals rather than on PoolManager funds; with no divergence known, actor operator and severity info stand.

Code: `src/libraries/FullMath.sol:24`, `src/libraries/FullMath.sol:48`, `src/libraries/TickBitmap.sol:38`, `src/libraries/BitMath.sol:16-22`, `src/libraries/TickMath.sol:104`, `src/libraries/SqrtPriceMath.sol:252`.

Sources:

- https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com eth_call respectedGameType() on AnchorStateRegistry 0x23b2c629..., 0x27cf508e..., 0xee018baf... (from OptimismPortal.anchorStateRegistry()) (respected game type 9 on OP Mainnet, Unichain and Ink (cycles/012/theme-proof-builds.work/op/dgf.json))
- https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com eth_call gameImpls(9), gameArgs(9), gameAtIndex(gameCount-1), absolutePrestate(), vm() on DisputeGameFactory 0xe5965ab5..., 0x2f12d621..., 0x10d7b350... (implementation 0x19af533c... version 0.8.0, prestate 0x03e5a54835d39c1d83b198314473e1f111c3539c04f65c3e8a9b8ccebe3737c9, VM 0xacc005dc... version 1.10.1 on the newest game of each chain (dgf2.json))
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum-optimism/optimism/develop/packages/contracts-bedrock/src/dispute/lib/Types.sol (game type 9 is SUPER_CANNON_KONA, 1 PERMISSIONED_CANNON, 5 SUPER_PERMISSIONED)

## F-123. Zora, World Chain and Soneium settle withdrawals on a permissioned proposer, and Zora and Soneium name no proof program at all

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: Zora, Worldchain, Soneium.

What differs on the network: Zora and World Chain respect game type 1 (PERMISSIONED_CANNON). Zora's implementation args carry the absolute prestate 0xdead0000...0000, so no step can ever be proven and the outcome rests on the permissioned proposer and challenger. World Chain's carries 0x033c0009...4dbb6e, the cannon64 build of op-program 1.9.0 (op-geth v1.101604.0 pseudo-version of 2025-12-08, holiman/uint256 v1.3.2, Go 1.24.10, GOARCH=mips64 GOMIPS64=softfloat). Soneium respects type 5 (SUPER_PERMISSIONED): the newest game 0xfe326897... was created and resolved DEFENDER_WINS in the same second by proposer 0x49cc743a..., and absolutePrestate() and vm() revert on it.

How it meets the code: For these chains the state behind a withdrawal is what the operator's proposer asserts. An ordinary account cannot make that assertion differ from the L2 state that the PoolManager's users see, because no proof program is consulted on Zora and Soneium, and on World Chain only the permissioned challenger can play the program. A wrong opcode in the proof build therefore cannot be turned against users by an unprivileged party on these three chains. It also means no program checks the operator's state root.

What is at risk: No loss path for an unprivileged account. A dishonest or faulty proposer could finalise any state, which is an operator risk to every bridged asset, including tokens withdrawn from the PoolManager.

What it takes: Operator action only.

What can be done: Deployers should list these chains as operator-settled in the deployment checklist.

Second reviewer's corrections: Zora does name a VM in gameArgs (0xacc005dc...) but with the placeholder prestate 0xdead..., so the statement that it names no proof program holds for the program, not the VM. State cleared.json cycle 10 (op-stack-eth) describes World Chain's proof client as kona with revm 42.0.1, while the respected prestate read here is the cannon64 op-program build; the earlier entry concerns a different or older proof path and should not be read as the current respected one.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/types/Currency.sol:48`.

Sources:

- https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com eth_call respectedGameType(), gameImpls(1|5), gameArgs(1|5), gameAtIndex(gameCount-1) on the DisputeGameFactory and AnchorStateRegistry of each chain (respected types 1, 1 and 5; Zora prestate 0xdead..., World Chain prestate 0x033c0009..., Soneium game resolved at creation with status 2 (cycles/012/theme-proof-builds.work/op/dgf2.json))
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum-optimism/superchain-registry/main/validation/standard/standard-prestates.toml (0x033c000916b4...4dbb6e is cannon64 of release 1.9.0)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum-optimism/optimism/op-program/v1.9.0/go.mod (toolchain go1.24.10, holiman/uint256 v1.3.2, op-geth replace v1.101604.0-synctest.0.0.20251208094937)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum-optimism/optimism/op-program/v1.9.0/op-program/Makefile (op-program-client64.elf is built with GOOS=linux GOARCH=mips64 GOMIPS64=softfloat (line 34))

## F-124. Arc nodes from v0.8.0 to v0.8.4 could read stale storage of an account without code that a block deleted and created again, which cannot reach PoolManager storage

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: Arc.

What differs on the network: Arc v0.8.0 to v0.8.4 build on reth v2.2.0, whose execution cache handled an account without code that a block destroyed and created again by removing the account entry and keeping its cached storage words. A later block on that node read the old words. v0.8.5 moved to circlefin/arc-reth v2.2.0-arc.2 with the upstream fix (reth pull request 28148). The v0.8.4 release fixed a separate state root computation fault of the same base.

How it meets the code: 1. The cache code runs after each block. For an account marked destroyed it checks whether the account had code at the start of the block. With code it clears the whole cache, which is safe. Without code it removed only the account entry and continued, so storage words cached for that address before the block stayed in the cache. 2. Any account can reach the second branch. The v0.8.5 release note names the trigger: a block deletes an empty account under EIP-161 and a later transaction of the same block creates it again and writes storage. A contract destroyed in its own creation transaction and created again in the same block is marked destroyed in the same way. On a node with the fault, a later read of such a word returns the value from before the block, and that node computes a different result from nodes with the fix. 3. The PoolManager has had code since deployment, so a block that touched it in this way would take the first branch and clear the cache. Its slots are keyed by its own address (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288, 349-364 and the transient libraries such as src/libraries/CurrencyDelta.sol:21-39 index slots under the PoolManager address), so no stale word of another address is read as PoolManager state. 4. Tokens and hooks of pools that do not use attacker-made contracts also have code at the start of any such block. The only contracts whose reads could go stale are contracts created in that pattern, which are attacker-made by construction, and such a contract can return any value to the PoolManager anyway (src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-160, src/types/Currency.sol:40-95).

What is at risk: No PoolManager funds move. On a lagging node, a block that uses the pattern makes that node disagree with the rest, which is a liveness and consistency matter for that node's operator and its RPC users. The official endpoint reports v0.8.6-rc1 and publicnode v0.8.5, both with the fix.

What it takes: A validator or RPC node running arc-node v0.8.0 to v0.8.4. Which releases the validators run is not public.

What can be done: Before deployment, confirm with Circle that every validator runs v0.8.5 or later. Integrations that read PoolManager state through RPC should use an endpoint whose arc_getVersion reports v0.8.5 or later.

Second reviewer's corrections: The Arc v0.8.5 release note describes the trigger as an account that a block deletes under EIP-161 and then recreates in the same block; mechanism step 2 describes only the SELFDESTRUCT-in-creation route and its first step (funding the fresh address) would keep the account non-empty for EIP-161. Both routes mark the account destroyed in the bundle, so the conclusion is unchanged. The actor value operator is right: harm needs a node operator who runs v0.8.0 to v0.8.4, and the harm is that node's divergence, with no PoolManager funds at stake.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-288`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/libraries/CurrencyDelta.sol:21-39`.

Sources:

- https://patch-diff.githubusercontent.com/raw/paradigmxyz/reth/pull/28148.patch (the fix: a destroyed account without original code that has current info is no longer just removed from the cache)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/paradigmxyz/reth/v2.2.0/crates/engine/execution-cache/src/cached_state.rs (reth v2.2.0 removes the account and continues for a destroyed account without code (lines 861-882))
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/circlefin/arc-reth/508cbdf7d6ba49b4ce735d678863897067fa381a/crates/engine/execution-cache/src/cached_state.rs (arc-reth 508cbdf, pinned by v0.8.5 and v0.8.6, carries the fixed code)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/circlefin/arc-node/v0.8.6/CHANGELOG.md (v0.8.4 and v0.8.5 release notes naming both faults)
- https://rpc.mainnet.arc.io (arc_getVersion returned v0.8.6-rc1 commit 37812c61 on 24 November 2026; arc-rpc.publicnode.com returned v0.8.5)

## F-129. The released reth binary carries an LLVM compiler for EVM code that validates and builds Ethereum blocks when an operator enables it, and no simulated call can reach it

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: Ethereum.

What differs on the network: reth v2.11.0, the client behind the endpoint in networks.json, links the evm2 JIT and its LLVM backend by default. With --jit, a contract that has been looked up 8 times is compiled to native code with no size limit, and that native code then runs inside payload building and payload validation. eth_call, eth_estimateGas and debug_traceCall run the interpreter. On Ethereum a block is accepted only if the execution clients of a supermajority of validators agree, and reth is a small minority client on every published census.

How it meets the code: The PoolManager is called in many blocks, so on a reth node with --jit it becomes resident compiled code within minutes. From then on every swap that node executes runs FullMath.mulDiv (MULMOD with modulus 2^256 - 1 and with the pool denominator), the SAR and SIGNEXTEND unpacking of Slot0 and BalanceDelta, the BYTE and shift lookups of BitMath and the signed division of TickBitmap.compress in code generated by LLVM. If that code computed one of these differently, the reth node would compute a different post-state root for the block. As a builder it would publish a block that geth, Nethermind, Besu and Erigon reject. As a validator it would reject the canonical block and fall off the chain. Neither outcome changes the balances that the canonical chain records for LPs, swappers or claim holders, because the majority clients decide the canonical state. An ordinary account cannot switch the compiler on, cannot choose which node builds its block, and gains nothing from a divergence on a minority client. The vectors, scripts and mined swap checks of this review never reach this path: simulated calls do not use it, and mined swaps are the output of whichever client built the block, which was rechecked here against the source on 30,321 swaps of blocks 26,485,859 to 26,487,858 without a mismatch.

What is at risk: No loss to users of the canonical chain. A reth operator who enables --jit risks missed slots or rejected blocks if the compiler is wrong. Only if reth with --jit came to build and attest for a supermajority could a compiler fault reach canonical pool state.

What it takes: A node operator starts reth v2.11.0 or later with --jit, and the generated code for one of the opcodes above differs from the specification for an input the pool math produces.

What can be done: Track client share and the --jit flag in reth releases. Before relying on reth block production, read or test the JIT code generation for MULMOD, SAR, SIGNEXTEND, BYTE, SDIV and SMOD, as was done for Monad. The deployer needs no change to the PoolManager.

Second reviewer's corrections: The JIT can be switched on at run time without the --jit flag: node.rs build_evm_config with jit.enabled false still builds a JitBackend and its comment says reth_jit enable starts it, and crates/rpc/rpc-api/src/reth.rs defines reth_jit with actions Enable, Disable, Pause, Unpause and Clear. The reth namespace is not exposed on the networks.json endpoint (rpc_modules). An operator who exposes the reth namespace publicly would let outside callers toggle the JIT on that node, which still affects only that node. The actor operator and severity info stand.

Code: `src/libraries/FullMath.sol:14-117`, `src/libraries/TickBitmap.sol:16-64`, `src/libraries/BitMath.sol:12-49`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/paradigmxyz/reth/v2.11.0/bin/reth/Cargo.toml (jit and gmp are default features of the reth binary (lines 89-104))
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/paradigmxyz/reth/v2.11.0/crates/node/core/src/args/jit.rs (--jit defaults to false, hot threshold 8, max bytecode length 0 meaning no limit)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/paradigmxyz/reth/v2.11.0/crates/engine/tree/src/tree/payload_validator.rs (payload validation calls with_jit_support (line 1033) and can use resident compiled code (lines 233-238))
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/paradigmxyz/reth/v2.11.0/crates/ethereum/payload/src/lib.rs (the payload builder calls with_jit_support (line 205))
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/paradigmxyz/reth/v2.11.0/crates/ethereum/evm/src/factory.rs (the JIT runner is installed only when jit_support is set (lines 376-386, 456-470))
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/paradigmxyz/reth/v2.11.0/crates/ethereum/node/src/node.rs (the node logs the backend as experimental (line 565) and builds the executor through build_evm_config (lines 606-610))
- https://clientdiversity.org/ (reth is listed at 3 to 7.67 percent of execution clients; the page marks its data as stale)
- https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com (web3_clientVersion returns reth/v2.11.0-b9abbcc and rpc_modules lists no JIT namespace)

## F-130. Monad nodes that serve call traces execute blocks with the interpreter only, so no public data shows compiled execution of the PoolManager

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: sequencer, validator or bridge committee. Networks: Monad.

What differs on the network: Monad v0.16.7 runs two execution engines. The execution daemon builds its VM as InterpreterOnly when it is started with --trace-calls and as Dual (interpreter plus native x86 compiler) otherwise (cmd/monad/main.cpp lines 146, 214 and 484-488). Call frames are recorded and committed only when tracing is enabled (runloop_monad.cpp lines 233-245 and 330-337). Every RPC method that executes code uses a second, InterpreterOnly VM (rpc/monad_executor.cpp lines 1306-1309, used at 1525, 1547, 1824 and 1969; monad-rpc debug_replay.rs lines 61-64 and 236-237). Ethereum has one execution semantics per client and every serving node runs it.

How it meets the code: Every public Monad endpoint that answers debug_traceTransaction with the call tracer (rpc.monad.xyz, rpc1, rpc2 via eRPC, rpc-mainnet.monadinfra.com, monad-mainnet.gateway.tatum.io) returns stored frames, so those nodes ran their block execution with --trace-calls and therefore with the interpreter. Their receipts and logs, among them every Swap and ModifyLiquidity event earlier cycles and this unit checked, are interpreter output. The only link between those nodes and validators that may run compiled code is the delayed merkle root of the state, checked D = 3 blocks later. On chain this is visible: in blocks 125,115,001 to 125,118,000 all 689 out-of-gas frames of 1,582 failed transactions, 19 of them PoolManager frames (swap with 94,784 gas, take with 1,844 to 18,922 gas), carry status 3 "out of gas", which is what the interpreter writes (interpreter/stack.hpp line 44); compiled code would end a static gas shortfall through error_label_ with StatusCode::Error (emitter.cpp lines 636, 679, 1054-1069), which debug.rs lines 381-388 map to "error". A compiled-only fault in a PoolManager branch that no block has taken yet (the overflow fallback of src/libraries/SqrtPriceMath.sol:52, the amount above 2^160 branch at src/libraries/SqrtPriceMath.sol:97 and 105, the 512 bit branch of the Q128 mulDiv at src/libraries/Position.sol:94-96) could be triggered by an ordinary swap or removal, and nobody can test it in advance through RPC. If two thirds of validators ran such a build, the canonical PoolManager state would follow the compiled result and the tracing RPC fleet would fall out of consensus. No such fault was found: the compiled paths for MULMOD, shifts, comparisons, sign extension and jumps were transcribed in cycles 9 to 13 without a mismatch, and the state roots of the tracing nodes agree with consensus at the finalized tip (block 125,124,132 on 2026-11-24). Cycle 24 addition: the 20,000 Monad transactions that cycle 23 traced (blocks 125,440,776 to 125,484,771) were rerun instruction by instruction with a counter on each side of every conditional jump (188,569 PoolManager frames). Of the 387 conditional jumps of the build, mined transactions executed both sides of 110, one side of 206 and neither side of 71. In 136 of the 206 the missing side reverts. Never executed by the producers of MegaETH, Monad or HyperEVM in the replayed windows: donate (src/libraries/Pool.sol:466-480), clear (src/PoolManager.sol:310-319), settleFor (src/PoolManager.sol:305-307), ERC-6909 transfer, transferFrom and approve (src/ERC6909.sol:25-56), the 512-bit path of four of the five compiled copies of FullMath.mulDiv, and the overflow fallback of getNextSqrtPriceFromAmount0RoundingUp (src/libraries/SqrtPriceMath.sol:43-52). Three of those 512-bit paths cannot return to a caller at all, because the int128 casts at src/libraries/Pool.sol:192, :455 and :459 revert first. The protocol fee branch is also unexecuted, and it needs governance, because the fee controller is zero on all three networks. Every frame that the producers did execute gave the source's words when rerun instruction by instruction. No fault was found; the entry records which sides rest on eth_call and source reading alone. On Monad the window also holds no swap that took the MIN or MAX tick clamp, and the 249,215 Swap logs of the 200,000 blocks before it hold none ending near a price bound. Public Monad endpoints refuse log ranges over 100 blocks, so the earlier 125 million blocks were not searched. The compiler harness of cycle 23 entered 899 of the 913 compiled blocks with test inputs, so the compiled code of these sides was run in the harness and matched the interpreter; it was never run by a validator.

What is at risk: No loss path was found. The finding corrects the evidence base: OPCODES.md says the Monad mined swaps "ran as compiled code", and cycle 11 says call frames are what block execution recorded. Both are true only of an interpreter run on the tracing node. Compiled execution is covered by reading and by state root agreement, not by any served receipt, log or frame.

What it takes: A fault in the native compiler of the release that validators run, on an input that no mined block has produced yet. It needs no role from the attacker, but it needs a client defect that no reading has found.

What can be done: Before liquidity arrives, run one non-tracing Dual mode node (a local monad build replaying mainnet blocks without --trace-calls) and compare its receipts with a tracing node for transactions that take each untaken branch. Ask Monad which execution mode validators run. Treat Monad state root agreement, not RPC answers, as the producer evidence.

Second reviewer's corrections: Minor: the main.cpp VM selection is at line 488 with the comment at 485-487, inside the cited 484-488 range. The "all 689 out-of-gas frames report out of gas" observation is supporting evidence only, since an out-of-gas frame that a compiled run reported as "error" would not be counted among them; the source reading is the proof. Actor operator is right: a mismatch would only reach canonical state if a supermajority of validators ran a faulty compiled build.

Code: `src/libraries/SqrtPriceMath.sol:42-52`, `src/libraries/SqrtPriceMath.sol:94-106`, `src/libraries/Position.sol:94-96`, `src/libraries/FullMath.sol:21-38`.

Sources:

- rpc https://rpc.monad.xyz web3_clientVersion and debug_traceTransaction 0xd5d7b9bf9dc21f66a1186a11f4637a0c0acff3bb859e0e698d01ed891a027ebd on 2026-11-24 (Monad/0.16.7; stored frames returned by rpc.monad.xyz, rpc1, rpc2, rpc-mainnet.monadinfra.com and monad-mainnet.gateway.tatum.io; rpc3 and rpc.ankr.com refuse the method)
