## F-026. PoolManager ownership on Celo and X Layer rests on the upgradeable L2CrossDomainMessenger, and on X Layer a 2 of 3 Safe of EOAs can upgrade it

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: owner, fee controller or chain governance. Networks: Celo, X Layer.

What differs on the network: On Ethereum the owner is the governance timelock itself. On both chains the owner is a cross-chain account contract that accepts a call when msg.sender is the L2CrossDomainMessenger predeploy and that predeploy reports the Ethereum timelock as the cross-domain sender. The predeploy is a proxy. On Celo its admin is controlled by the alias of an L1 2 of 2 Safe of two 6 of 8 Safes. On X Layer its admin is controlled by a Safe on X Layer with threshold 2 and three EOA owners.

How it meets the code: Whoever controls the messenger implementation can make it call the owner contract while reporting the timelock as sender. The owner contract then forwards a call to the PoolManager as owner. The only owner function in src/ is setProtocolFeeController (src/ProtocolFees.sol:29-32). The Owned base contract is missing from this checkout because lib/solmate is empty, so its ownership transfer functions could not be read. A controller chosen this way can set a protocol fee of at most 1000 pips per direction on any pool (src/ProtocolFees.sol:35-41, src/libraries/ProtocolFeeLibrary.sol:8 and 25-32) and can withdraw accrued protocol fees to any recipient (src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57). The fee is taken from swap input before the LP fee (src/libraries/Pool.sol:385-396). collectProtocolFees subtracts from protocolFeesAccrued with checked arithmetic, so it cannot pay out more than was accrued.

What is at risk: Up to 0.1 percent of swap input per direction on every pool for as long as the hostile controller stays, paid by swappers, plus any accrued protocol fees. Both are zero today because no controller is set. Pool reserves, ERC-6909 claims and LP principal are not reachable through the owner or the controller role.

What it takes: On X Layer two of the three Safe owners must sign an upgrade of the messenger predeploy. On Celo both 6 of 8 Safes must sign. Uniswap governance can undo the controller change only through the same messenger.

What can be done: Monitor ProtocolFeeControllerUpdated and ProtocolFeeUpdated on both chains and Upgraded events on the messenger proxy. The 0.1 percent cap in the code bounds the loss.

Second reviewer's corrections: 1. Severity raised from low to medium. Low requires that no direct loss path was found. This finding has one: whoever controls the messenger can appoint a controller, add up to 0.1 percent per direction to every later swap and take what accrues. The actor is a chain level upgrade authority and the reach is capped at protocol fees. The same finding on the ETH gas OP Stack chains was set to medium in this cycle. 2. The owner role includes transferOwnership on the deployed contracts. A forged message can move ownership to another address, after which Uniswap governance cannot undo the change through the messenger. The finding says governance can undo it. 3. The guard in collectProtocolFees checks the synced currency and skips address(0). It does not check the lock state (src/ProtocolFees.sol:49). On Celo a native fee collection is therefore allowed while the CELO token is synced, which the research file already lists as a lead. 4. The same signers hold wider powers than the finding covers. On X Layer the proxy admin of the L1 OptimismPortal is owned by a Safe at the same address on Ethereum with threshold 2 and the same three owners, and the SystemConfig owner is one of those three accounts acting alone. On Celo the Security Council in the governance hotfix path is the same aliased Safe that owns the ProxyAdmin. The portal route is listed as a lead. 5. Accrued fees were checked for native CELO and the CELO token on Celo, where both are zero. Other currencies and X Layer were not checked.

Code: `src/ProtocolFees.sol:29-57`, `src/libraries/ProtocolFeeLibrary.sol:8`, `src/libraries/ProtocolFeeLibrary.sol:25-32`, `src/libraries/Pool.sol:385-396`.

Sources:

- https://rpc.xlayer.tech eth_call owner() on 0x360e68faccca8ca495c1b759fd9eee466db9fb32; l1Owner(), messenger() on 0x044aAF330d7fD6AE683EEc5c1C1d1fFf5196B6b7 (The owner is the cross-chain account with l1Owner 0x1a9C8182C09F50C8318d769245beA52c32BE35BC and messenger 0x4200000000000000000000000000000000000007.)
- https://rpc.xlayer.tech eth_getStorageAt EIP-1967 admin slot of 0x4200000000000000000000000000000000000007; owner() on 0x4200000000000000000000000000000000000018; getThreshold(), getOwners() on 0xC290be56089bCC83C6993583ce2cF51a7951D45A; eth_getCode per owner (The messenger proxy admin is the ProxyAdmin predeploy, whose owner is a Safe with threshold 2 and three owners without code.)
- https://forno.celo.org eth_call owner() on 0x288dc841A52FCA2707c6947B3A777c5E56cd87BC; l1Owner(), messenger() on the owner; eth_getStorageAt admin slot of 0x4200...0007; owner() on 0x4200...0018 (Celo uses the same owner contract. The ProxyAdmin owner is 0x51a3a77bAF58FEf0309452ceacB09221E556F223.)
- https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com eth_call getThreshold(), getOwners() on 0x4092A77bAF58fef0309452cEaCb09221e556E112 and on its two owners (The unaliased Celo admin is a 2 of 2 Safe on Ethereum and each owner is a 6 of 8 Safe.)
- https://docs.optimism.io/notices/archive/upgrade-19 (Karst adds the L2 Contract Manager, a consensus level mechanism for upgrading L2 predeploys, which becomes one more route to change the messenger once a chain adopts it.)

## F-029. PoolManager ownership rests on an upgradeable messenger predeploy on five chains and on the portal alias on two, and a takeover reaches protocol fees only

Medium severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: owner, fee controller or chain governance. Networks: Optimism, Base, Unichain, Zora, Worldchain, Ink, Soneium.

What differs on the network: On Ethereum the owner is a contract that acts directly. On OP Mainnet, Base, Zora, World Chain and Soneium the owner is a CrossChainAccount that accepts a call only when msg.sender is L2CrossDomainMessenger at 0x4200000000000000000000000000000000000007 and xDomainMessageSender() equals the Ethereum timelock 0x1a9C8182C09F50C8318d769245beA52c32BE35BC. The messenger is a proxy whose admin is the ProxyAdmin predeploy, owned by an aliased L1 upgrade multisig, and since Karst predeploys are also upgraded by network upgrade deposits at fork activation. On Unichain and Ink the owner is the bare alias 0x2bad8182c09f50c8318d769245bea52c32be46cd, which only a deposit from the timelock through that chain's OptimismPortal can produce.

How it meets the code: 1. Whoever controls the messenger implementation can make xDomainMessageSender() return the timelock address and call CrossChainAccount.forward, which then calls the PoolManager as owner. On the alias chains the same result needs a change to deposit derivation or to the portal. 2. The owner can call setProtocolFeeController (src/ProtocolFees.sol:29-32). 3. The controller can call setProtocolFee for any pool, limited to 1,000 pips per direction by isValidProtocolFee (src/ProtocolFees.sol:35-41, src/libraries/ProtocolFeeLibrary.sol:25-32), and can call collectProtocolFees to any recipient. 4. collectProtocolFees subtracts the amount from protocolFeesAccrued[currency] with checked arithmetic before the transfer (src/ProtocolFees.sol:54-56), so it cannot pay out more than the fees accrued for that currency. Pool reserves and ERC-6909 claims are out of reach of the owner and the controller. 5. On OP Mainnet and Base the current controller forwards collections to a fixed token jar and lets anyone trigger collect, so the present setup does not let a caller redirect fees.

What is at risk: Accrued protocol fees, about 0.030 ETH of native fees on OP Mainnet and 0.034 ETH on Base at read time, plus whatever has accrued in ERC-20 currencies, which was not read, and up to 0.1 percent per direction of future swap volume on every pool of the chain. No LP principal. On the five chains without a controller nothing has accrued.

What it takes: Control of the chain's L2 predeploy upgrade path: the aliased ProxyAdmin owner, a hard fork upgrade bundle, or a compromise of the L1 timelock itself. Each of these parties can already do more damage to bridged assets than to the PoolManager.

What can be done: Uniswap governance can monitor ProtocolFeeControllerUpdated and OwnershipTransferred events on each chain. Swappers and LPs can read the protocol fee of a pool from slot0 before trading. No code change in core is needed because the owner's reach is already capped by src/ProtocolFees.sol:54-56.

Second reviewer's corrections: 1. Severity raised from low to medium. The definition of low requires that no direct loss path was found. This finding has one: a party that controls the predeploy upgrade path or a fork bundle can appoint itself controller, take accrued fees and add up to 0.1 percent per direction to every later swap. The actor is chain governance, which would place it at high, and the reach is capped at protocol fees, so medium fits. 2. The list of actors is incomplete. Network upgrade deposits are written by the fork authors and can carry any sender. The Ecotone and Isthmus specifications define upgrade deposits sent from the zero address. A fork bundle could therefore call the PoolManager as the alias owner directly, or as the zero address controller on Unichain, Zora, World Chain, Ink and Soneium, without touching the messenger. 3. The statement that nothing has accrued on the five chains was checked for the native currency only. 4. Fee figures move: Base native fees were 0.0508 ETH at my read against 0.034 in the finding.

Code: `src/ProtocolFees.sol:29-32`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:35-41`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57`, `src/libraries/ProtocolFeeLibrary.sol:25-32`.

Sources:

- https://worldchain-mainnet.explorer.alchemy.com/api/v2/smart-contracts/0xcb2436774c3e191c85056d248ef4260ce5f27a9d (Verified CrossChainAccount source on World Chain: forward requires msg.sender to be the messenger and messenger.xDomainMessageSender() to equal l1Owner. Constructor arguments are 0x4200…0007 and 0x1a9C8182C09F50C8318d769245beA52c32BE35BC.)
- https://soneium.blockscout.com/api/v2/smart-contracts/0x044aaf330d7fd6ae683eec5c1c1d1fff5196b6b7 (Verified CrossChainAccount source on Soneium with immutable messenger and l1Owner and the same constructor arguments.)
- https://mainnet.optimism.io (eth_getStorageAt on the OP Mainnet owner 0xa1dd330d602c32622aa270ea73d078b803cb3518: slot 0 is the messenger predeploy, slot 1 is the timelock. The same two values were read on Base, Zora and World Chain. The messenger admin slot is 0x4200…0018 on all seven chains.)
- https://mainnet.unichain.org (eth_call owner() on the Unichain and Ink PoolManager returns 0x2bad8182c09f50c8318d769245bea52c32be46cd, which has no code and equals the timelock address plus the alias offset.)
- https://docs.optimism.io/op-stack/protocol/privileged-roles (The L2 ProxyAdmin owner is the aliased L1 ProxyAdmin owner and can upgrade L2 system contracts. For Optimism governed chains the L1 owner is a 2-of-2 Safe of the Foundation and the Security Council.)
- https://specs.optimism.io/protocol/overview.html (Network upgrade transactions at fork activation call L2ProxyAdmin.upgradePredeploys from the depositor account.)
- https://explorer.optimism.io/api/v2/smart-contracts/0xd85393784234fb2ee4f077867a8b78e1ad4ce56b (V4FeeAdapter source on OP Mainnet: collect sends to the immutable TOKEN_JAR and is callable by anyone.)

## F-045. The MRB hard fork raised the price of seven precompiles by 5 to 40 times, which can push a hook past the 3,000,000 gas fast block

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: owner, fee controller or chain governance. Networks: HyperEVM.

What differs on the network: From block 50,988,232 HyperEVM charges 5 times Ethereum's price for ECRECOVER, 10 times for MODEXP, BN254 add, multiply and pairing and BLAKE2F, and 40 times for the KZG point evaluation. Measured on 2026-11-22: ECRECOVER 15,000 gas, BN254 add 1,500, multiply 60,000, pairing with empty input 450,000. The archive client release that carries the change was published on 10 November 2026 and asked operators to upgrade before 11 November 0:00 UTC. The official HyperEVM docs page still describes Cancun without any note on precompile prices.

How it meets the code: Core calls no precompile: the only raw calls in src are the native payout, the ERC-20 transfer and the hook call (src/types/Currency.sol:48 and 73, src/libraries/Hooks.sol:134). Pools without hooks are not affected. A hook is called with all remaining gas and a failed hook call reverts the pool action (src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-155). A hook that checks a signature or a proof now pays the multiplied price. By the multiplier a pairing check with four pairs costs 1,810,000 gas, where Ethereum charges 181,000, and each BN254 multiplication costs 60,000. Fast blocks hold 3,000,000 gas. If the hook's check runs in beforeRemoveLiquidity or afterRemoveLiquidity and no longer fits, modifyLiquidity reverts in fast blocks (src/PoolManager.sol:145-184) and the LP has to switch the sending account to big blocks through a HyperCore action before it can exit. A second repricing could put the cost above the 30,000,000 gas of a big block, and then the position could not be removed.

What is at risk: LPs of pools whose hook uses a repriced precompile in the removal path. I found no such hook on HyperEVM. No pool without a hook is affected.

What it takes: A hook that calls ECRECOVER, MODEXP, a BN254 precompile, BLAKE2F or the KZG precompile on liquidity removal, and a gas need above the block limit after the repricing. The repricing itself is decided by the validators through a node release.

What can be done: Hook authors should keep signature and proof checks out of the remove liquidity callbacks. LPs in hook pools should know the evmUserModify action that directs their transactions to big blocks. Integrators should follow the node release notes, since this change did not appear in the HyperEVM docs.

Second reviewer's corrections: Nothing in the facts. The hazard needs a hook that calls a repriced precompile on the removal path and the finding names none on HyperEVM, so this is a note for hook authors. The actor governance stands for the repricing itself, which validators adopt through a node release.

Code: `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-155`, `src/PoolManager.sol:145-184`, `src/types/Currency.sol:48`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/hl-archive-node/nanoreth/master/src/node/evm/mrb.rs (Mainnet activation block 50,988,232 and the multiplier table: 5 for ECRECOVER, 10 for MODEXP, BN254 add, multiply, pairing and BLAKE2F, 40 for KZG)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/hl-archive-node/nanoreth/master/src/node/evm/factory.rs (Lines 79 to 137: V2 replaces MODEXP with the Osaka version and wraps the seven precompiles with the multiplier)
- https://github.com/hl-archive-node/nanoreth/releases/tag/nb-20261110 (Release of 10 November 2026: reprice precompiles at the MRB hard fork, upgrade before 11 November 0:00 UTC on mainnet)
- https://rpc.hyperliquid.xyz/evm eth_call creation probe 0x5a5f5f5f5f61<address>5afa5a60405260205260005260605ff3 for 0x01, 0x06, 0x07, 0x08 and the same probe on https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com (Gas differences 15,115, 1,615, 60,115 and 450,115 on HyperEVM against 3,115, 265, 6,115 and 45,115 on Ethereum, of which 115 is call overhead)
- https://hyperliquid.gitbook.io/hyperliquid-docs/for-developers/hyperevm/dual-block-architecture (Fast blocks have a 3M gas limit, slow blocks 30M, and evmUserModify directs an account's transactions to big blocks)

## F-052. Arc keeps a denylist contract whose owner can keep transactions that name a listed sender or target out of the mempool

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: owner, fee controller or chain governance. Networks: Arc.

What differs on the network: Besides the USDC blocklist, Arc has a Denylist system contract at 0x3600000000000000000000000000000000000004. Every node's transaction pool rejects a transaction whose sender, to address or EIP-7702 authority is flagged there. The client makes the check mandatory and derives the contract address from the chain specification. Ethereum has no protocol-defined admission list.

How it meets the code: 1. The owner of the Denylist proxy (0x49ec36db19623e4dadc1aa821cba2d1476f8e859) flags an address. 2. If the address is a user's account, that user's transactions are no longer admitted by nodes that run the stock client, so the user cannot call unlock, burn claims or remove liquidity (src/PoolManager.sol:104-114 and 145-184). 3. If the address is the PoolManager, a router or the PositionManager, transactions sent directly to it are refused. A call that reaches the same contract from inside another contract is not checked, because the list is read at admission only and the execution layer has no denylist check. 4. Arc has no forced inclusion path, so a listed account depends on a validator that includes its transaction outside the public mempool.

What is at risk: A listed user loses access to funds held in the PoolManager for as long as the entry stands. A listed router or PositionManager can no longer be the target of a transaction, so its users must reach it through another contract that they deploy or approve. No funds move.

What it takes: The Denylist owner must act. The denylist slots of the PoolManager, the PositionManager, both Universal Routers, Permit2 and the fee adapter were zero at block 32,584,426. I read the admission rule in the arc-node main branch and could not confirm which release mainnet validators run.

What can be done: Users can hold positions directly or through contracts they control, so a listing of one shared periphery contract does not strand them. Deployers should monitor the Denylist contract for entries that touch the deployment.

Second reviewer's corrections: The actor governance is right for the owner of the Denylist proxy. The severity low is consistent with the scale because the list acts on single accounts at admission, is not enforced during execution and moves no funds.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:104-114`, `src/PoolManager.sol:145-184`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/circlefin/arc-node/main/crates/execution-txpool/src/validator.rs (check_for_denylisted_addresses tests the sender, the to address and recovered EIP-7702 authorities and rejects the transaction on a match)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/circlefin/arc-node/main/crates/execution-config/src/chainspec.rs (DENYLIST_ADDRESS_MAINNET is 0x3600000000000000000000000000000000000004 and the denylist is a protocol requirement)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/circlefin/arc-node/main/crates/execution-config/src/addresses_denylist.rs (ERC-7201 base slot of the denylist mapping and the slot formula)
- https://github.com/circlefin/arc-node/issues/513 (report that mempool admission is the only enforcement point of the denylist and that the payload builder and the EVM do not check it)
- https://rpc.mainnet.arc.io (eth_call and eth_getStorageAt at block 32,584,426: owner() of 0x3600...0004, isDenylisted(PoolManager) = false, zero denylist slots for six Uniswap addresses)

## F-134. Polygon's Salvador hardfork rewrites the code of five accounts at block 98,688,600 through BlockAlloc, a mechanism that can give any address new code outside a transaction

Low severity, confirmed. Effect: freeze. Who can trigger it: owner, fee controller or chain governance. Networks: Polygon.

What differs on the network: Bor applies a per-block code table (BlockAlloc) during block finalisation. For every listed address it calls SetCode and keeps the storage, and it sets the balance only when the balance is zero. Ethereum has no rule that replaces an account's code. Polygon has used the table on 0x1001 and 0x1010, and at block 62,278,656 on WPOL (0x0d500B1d8E8eF31E21C99d1Db9A6444d3ADf1270), a token that was deployed by an ordinary transaction and that the PoolManager holds. Salvador, scheduled for 2026-12-08, applies it to five more ordinary accounts.

How it meets the code: At block 98,688,600 the five Polymarket and Binance accounts get a 344 byte sweeper. A call with value reverts, and sweep() sends the native balance to a fixed recovery Safe. For the PoolManager, an address that changes code under BlockAlloc behaves differently through each of the calls it makes. A native take to that address (src/types/Currency.sol:44-52) reverts afterwards. A token take or sync on it (src/types/Currency.sol:53-80 and 90-95) calls code the pool creator never saw. A hook at that address (Hooks.sol permission bits at src/libraries/Hooks.sol:29) answers with other code. The PoolManager's own code could be replaced the same way, and noDelegateCall (src/NoDelegateCall.sol:19-25) does not detect that. The five Salvador targets hold no ERC-6909 claims at the PoolManager in currency 0 (native), USDC, USDC.e, WPOL, USDT or WETH, and no PositionManager position (eth_call at block 97,906,958), so the scheduled rewrite touches no PoolManager funds. Cycle 32 placed the BlockAlloc code at each of the five targets by state override and called it from the PoolManager address as Currency.transfer does: a native payment with empty calldata reverts at all five, so a take of native POL that names one of them reverts with its whole unlock after the fork. sweep() moves only the account's own POL. At block 98,042,343 none of the five holds an ERC-6909 claim in native POL, WPOL, USDC, USDC.e, USDT or WETH.

What is at risk: None for the Salvador entries as scheduled. In general, a BlockAlloc entry for a pooled token, a hook or the PoolManager would decide every pool that uses that address. On 2026-11-24 the PoolManager holds 3,211,373 POL and 7,903 WPOL.

What it takes: A bor release with a BlockAlloc entry, adopted by the validator set through Polygon governance (a PIP and a coordinated release). No ordinary account can add an entry.

What can be done: At each bor release, read params/config.go BlockAlloc for the PoolManager, PositionManager and pooled currencies and hooks. After block 98,688,600, compare the code at the five addresses with the table.

Second reviewer's corrections: Two details are wrong. First, the statement that Salvador is the first use of BlockAlloc on accounts that are not system or genesis contracts is contradicted on chain: eth_getCode for WMATIC/WPOL 0x0d500B1d8E8eF31E21C99d1Db9A6444d3ADf1270 at polygon.drpc.org returns 0x at blocks 1 and 4,915,200 and code at 6,250,000, so WPOL was deployed by an ordinary transaction and its code was already replaced by the 62278656 entry. That precedent is a token the PoolManager holds (7,903 WPOL per the finding). It is the reason for low rather than info: chain governance has already rewritten a pooled currency once. Second, src/libraries/Hooks.sol:27 is ALL_HOOK_MASK; the permission flag constants start at line 29. The actor value governance is right.

Code: `src/types/Currency.sol:44-52`, `src/types/Currency.sol:90-95`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:29`, `src/NoDelegateCall.sol:19-25`.

Sources:

- https://github.com/0xPolygon/bor/pull/2546 (Salvador purpose, the five addresses, recovery addresses, activation blocks and dates, no new consensus rule, and transfers to the addresses reverting)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/0xPolygon/bor/develop/params/config.go (BorMainnetChainConfig SalvadorBlock 98688600 and the BlockAlloc entry with the sweeper code. Earlier BlockAlloc entries replace 0x1010, 0x1001 and WMATIC 0x0d500B1d...)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/0xPolygon/bor/develop/consensus/bor/bor.go (changeContractCodeIfNeeded: SetCode on each listed address, balance set only if zero, called in finalisation (lines 1289, 1381-1398, 1441, 1501))
- eth_call PoolManager.balanceOf(address,uint256) and PositionManager.balanceOf on https://polygon-bor-rpc.publicnode.com, block 0x5d5eb62 (the five targets hold no claims in six currency ids and no position NFTs)

## F-058. BNB Smart Chain runs a call from the system address with its stated value and moves no balance, and no ordinary sender can be that address

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: siphon. Who can trigger it: owner, fee controller or chain governance. Networks: BNB Smart Chain.

What differs on the network: On Ethereum as the PoolManager code models it, a frame that reports CALLVALUE v exists only after v was moved from the caller to the callee. The BNB Smart Chain client treats the caller 0xfffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffe as a system call: EVM.Call skips the balance check and skips the transfer for that caller, and still builds the callee frame with the stated value (core/vm/evm.go lines 311 to 316 and 342 to 347 on master). The same text is in go-ethereum master (lines 288 to 309), in op-geth on the optimism branch (lines 268 to 303) and in the head of the Celo fork of op-geth (lines 285 to 321). Bor, the Nitro fork of go-ethereum, libevm (Avalanche), revm and the Monad client perform the check and the transfer for every caller. A read-only probe on the 21 public endpoints showed the rule active on BNB Smart Chain only: a call from that address with value 7 and an overridden balance of 1000 left the sender at 1000 and the target at its old balance, while the target frame read CALLVALUE 7. The other 20 endpoints moved the 7 wei or rejected the transaction.

How it meets the code: 1. settle and settleFor credit msg.value as paid when no ERC-20 is synced and read no balance (src/PoolManager.sol:300-307 and 352-354). 2. A frame entered from the system address on BNB Smart Chain reports a value that nobody paid. If such a frame reached settleFor(recipient) while the manager is unlocked, the recipient would hold a native credit backed by nothing and could take the PoolManager's BNB (src/PoolManager.sol:291-297). 3. The client itself calls with that caller in three places, the beacon root call, the parent hash call and the request queue call, each with value zero and a fixed target (core/state_processor.go lines 353 to 429). Parlia system transactions use the block's coinbase as sender (consensus/parlia/parlia.go lines 1389, 2069, 2096, 2110, 2131 and 2137), so they pay value from a real balance. 4. A signed transaction or an EIP-7702 authorization from that address needs a signature for an address with no known signing secret. 5. A system call starts a new transaction context, so the manager is locked. A probe call of settle() and settleFor(address) with value 1 from the system address returned ManagerLocked (0x54e3ca0d) on all 21 networks. 6. A contract that the system address calls sees the value but receives no balance, so it cannot pass the value on to the PoolManager. No step lets an ordinary account produce the frame.

What is at risk: None today. The PoolManager on BNB Smart Chain holds about 3,794 BNB plus the tokens of every pool that has BNB on one side. Those funds would be exposed only if a later client release or hard fork added a system call that carries value into contract code chosen by users, or let a public transaction type run with the system address as caller.

What it takes: A consensus rule change on BNB Smart Chain (or on a network whose client carries the same rule) that makes the system address the caller of user-chosen code with nonzero value while the PoolManager is unlocked. Client developers and the validator set must act. No ordinary account can meet this.

What can be done: The deployer can keep this rule on the checklist for every BNB Smart Chain hard fork, starting with Jenner on 2026-12-08, and rerun the probe from this finding after each one. A core change that removes the dependency would have sync(address(0)) record address(this).balance and have the native branch of _settle credit the smaller of msg.value and the balance rise. LPs and pool creators cannot act on this.

Second reviewer's corrections: The mechanism text says the settle and settleFor probe returned ManagerLocked on all 21 networks. On Tempo the endpoint rejects any call that carries value with 'value transfer not allowed', also when the sender balance is set by override, so the count is 20 and one rejection. With value 0 the Tempo PoolManager returns ManagerLocked. The op-geth line range begins at 247 and the Celo range at 264, where isSystemCall is defined. forno.celo.org answered from two client families during my runs (reth/v2.2.0-74cc8bb and Geth/v0.1.0-untagged-c7ed55b8-20260817) and both moved value for the system address in 12 of 12 runs. The actor value governance and the severity info are right: no party can trigger the hazard today and it needs a consensus rule change.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:300-307`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`, `src/types/Currency.sol:44-52`.

Sources:

- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bnb-chain/bsc/master/core/vm/evm.go (isSystemCall at lines 291 to 293. Call skips CanTransfer at 314 and skips Transfer at 345 to 347 when the caller is the system address, and passes value to the callee frame.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bnb-chain/bsc/master/core/state_processor.go (The three calls made with From params.SystemAddress pass common.U2560 as value (lines 353 to 363, 380 to 390, 420 to 429).)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/bnb-chain/bsc/master/consensus/parlia/parlia.go (getSystemMessage at line 2137 is always called with header.Coinbase as sender. distributeIncoming at lines 2022 to 2052 moves the system address balance to the coinbase before the reward deposit.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum/go-ethereum/master/core/vm/evm.go (Same rule in go-ethereum master, lines 268 to 309.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum/go-ethereum/master/core/state_processor.go (The go-ethereum system calls at lines 387 to 487 pass value zero.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum-optimism/op-geth/optimism/core/vm/evm.go (Same rule in op-geth, lines 268 to 303.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/celo-org/op-geth/HEAD/core/vm/evm.go (Same rule in the head of the Celo fork, lines 285 to 321. The Celo mainnet endpoint (build c7ed55b8 of 2026-08-17) did not show it in the probe.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/0xPolygon/bor/master/core/vm/evm.go (Bor checks CanTransfer at line 326 and calls Transfer at line 357 for every caller.)
- rpc https://bsc-dataseed1.bnbchain.org eth_call from 0xfffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffe, value 7, balance override 1000, to a probe placed by state override (Returned sender balance 1000, target balance 10 before its own transfer of 5, and CALLVALUE 7 in the target frame. The same call on the other 20 endpoints returned sender balance 993 and target balance 17, or was rejected (Tempo).)
- rpc eth_call of settle() and settleFor(address) with value 1 from 0xfffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffffe to the PoolManager on all 21 endpoints (Every call reverted with 0x54e3ca0d (ManagerLocked).)

## F-089. No test fails when the unsettled delta check of unlock is removed, and under the --isolate flag of the CI no test pins the lock close or the reset of the synced currency; the source has all three statements

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: owner, fee controller or chain governance. Networks: all 21 networks.

What differs on the network: None. This is a property of the repository's tests and it is the same on every network. The network features that each unpinned statement stands against are named in the mechanism field.

How it meets the code: I read every guard and accounting statement in the assigned lines together with every test under test/ and every helper under src/test, and asked which test fails when the statement is removed or its condition is inverted. Nothing could be compiled, so the answer comes from reading. Three statements carry the whole custody model and are weakly pinned. First, src/PoolManager.sol:112 reverts with CurrencyNotSettled when the count of nonzero deltas is not zero at the end of unlock. No file under test/ names CurrencyNotSettled. Outside the interface and PoolManager.sol the name occurs only in the copy of unlock at src/test/ProxyPoolManager.sol:66. Every expectRevert that is reached through a router expects another error, and the bare expectRevert at test/PoolManager.t.sol:917-922 is satisfied by the failing token transfer. Inverting the condition fails every test that completes an unlock. Removing the line fails no test. The line is what makes take, mint and a swap output a loan that must be repaid (A5), and it reads a transient counter (A1). Second, src/PoolManager.sol:113 closes the lock. Without --isolate the second unlock of one test transaction would revert with AlreadyUnlocked, for example in the setup helper at test/utils/Deployers.sol:199-211, which adds liquidity twice. The CI runs forge test --isolate (.github/workflows/tests-pr.yml:37, .github/workflows/tests-merge.yml:31), and in that mode every top level call of a test is its own transaction, so transient storage is empty at the next call and no test notices a lock that stays open. I found no test that performs two unlocks inside one top level call and no assertion that the manager is locked after an unlock returns. Third, src/PoolManager.sol:361 resets the synced currency after a token settle. Without the reset a second settle would credit the same balance rise again, because the stored reserves are not updated. test_settle_twice_doesNotApplyDelta (test/Sync.t.sol:341-374) does not detect that: it takes the credited amount between the two settles, so the balance equals the stored reserves again and the second settle pays zero with or without the reset, and its last assertion reads the delta of address(0), which is zero in both cases. Without --isolate the reset is pinned by the setup helper, where a token settle is followed by a native settle with value in the same transaction and a stale token sync would raise NonzeroNativeValue (test/utils/Deployers.sol:203-206, src/PoolManager.sol:356). With --isolate nothing pins it. Further statements that no test pins in either mode: the onlyWhenUnlocked modifier on settleFor and on clear (src/PoolManager.sol:305, 310; the locked tests cover take, settle, mint and burn at test/PoolManager.t.sol:962-999); the toInt128 limit as applied in take, clear, mint, burn and _settle (src/PoolManager.sol:294, 313, 326, 334, 364; the library function is pinned by test/libraries/SafeCast.t.sol:85-99, and no manager test uses an amount of 2^127 or more); the use of _burnFrom with its allowance and operator check in burn (src/PoolManager.sol:335; the check is pinned on the mock at test/ERC6909Claims.t.sol:15-48, and every manager test sets the router as operator before it burns); the truncation of the id in mint and burn (src/PoolManager.sol:324, 333); the checked subtraction for a balance that fell between sync and settle (src/PoolManager.sol:360); the acceptance of empty return data, the minimum of 32 bytes for a returned value and the absence of a code check in the token transfer (src/types/Currency.sol:64-74; the tests use tokens that return true, a token that returns false and a contract that reverts); the amount of gas forwarded with a native payout (src/types/Currency.sol:48); the minimum of 32 bytes for a hook answer and the exact 64 bytes for a hook that returns a delta (src/libraries/Hooks.sol:152, 166; the mock hooks always return full words); and the absence of a lock check in collectProtocolFees (src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57, recorded in F-006 and F-014). For the increment branch at src/PoolManager.sol:375 I found no test that moves one delta from a nonzero value to another nonzero value and then asserts the count; I did not trace every hook test for that case. I then read each of these statements as if it were the only changed line. Each does what its comment, its error name and the surrounding tests require: line 112 reads NonzeroDeltaCount and runs before the lock is closed; line 113 writes false to the lock slot; line 361 writes zero to the currency slot after the balance was read and before the delta is booked; take, mint and clear negate a value that toInt128 has limited to 2^127 - 1 and book it to msg.sender; burn credits msg.sender and burns from the named account through _burnFrom; settleFor credits the recipient. The deployed PoolManagers are other bytecode than a build of this checkout, so a read-only test of them says how each network executes these paths and nothing about this build. With that limit, three eth_call probes with a forwarder stub placed by state override gave the same words on all 21 networks, the Unichain predeploy included: a callback that only mints leaves the unlock reverting with 0x5212cba1 (CurrencyNotSettled); the lock slot reads zero and a take reverts with ManagerLocked right after unlock returns in the same call; the currency slot reads the synced address before settle and zero after it; settleFor, clear, settle, take, mint and burn revert with ManagerLocked outside an unlock; amounts of 2^127 revert with SafeCastOverflow in mint, take and burn; a burn of claims that another address holds does not credit the caller.

What is at risk: No funds are at risk from the source as it is written. The observation matters for the review method: a build in which line 112 is missing would let any account take every token without paying, a build without line 113 would let any call that follows an unlock in the same transaction take without an end check, and a build without line 361 would let one payment be credited twice, and the repository's tests as the CI runs them would pass in all three cases. Earlier cycles relied on the tests to confirm the source. For these statements the confirmation comes from reading the text and, for the deployed contracts only, from the probes.

What it takes: None for the observation itself. A loss would need a build in which one of the listed statements is missing or changed. The text of this checkout shows each statement in place, and nobody in this review could compile it.

What can be done: Before deployment, compile this checkout and run the three probes in research/network-risk/cycles/006/core-code-test-pins.work against the built bytecode placed at a test address by state override on each network, or add tests: an unlock whose callback leaves a debt must revert with CurrencyNotSettled; a take right after an unlock in the same call must revert with ManagerLocked; two settles in a row after one payment must credit once; settleFor and clear must revert while locked; a burn by an account without approval must revert at the manager.

Second reviewer's corrections: actor: governance. The finding names no step that an ordinary account can take against the source as written. The loss it describes needs a build that lacks one of the statements, and only the party that compiles and deploys the PoolManager can bring that about. It is a confirmed info item and it is outside the unprivileged scope. Second, the wording on --isolate is dated. On the Foundry master branch the default configuration has isolate: true and a --no-isolate flag exists, so with a current forge a plain forge test also runs every top level call as its own transaction. Lines 113 and 361 are then pinned by no test in any default run, and the setup helper pins them only with --no-isolate or with an older forge that does not isolate by default. I did not check the default of v1.3.6, the version the CI installs. Third, the finding names one bare expectRevert in front of a router call. Five more are in test/utils/SwapHelper.t.sol. They fail in the helper's require statements before any router is called, so the conclusion stands. Fourth, the impact field says earlier cycles relied on the tests to confirm the source. 37 of the 598 items in state/cleared.json cite a test file, and I found none that rests on a test for line 112, 113 or 361. Those items cite the source text. Fifth, the sentence that the deployed PoolManagers are other bytecode than a build of this checkout is an assumption taken from the brief. Nobody compiled the checkout. The consequence the finding draws from it is the safe one: the on-chain probes describe the networks and the contracts deployed today and say nothing about this build.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:104-114`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-336`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-378`, `src/types/Currency.sol:44-87`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-168`, `src/ProtocolFees.sol:44-57`, `src/ERC6909Claims.sol:13-22`.

## F-126. The LLVM MIPS shift lowering that kona-client 1.9.0 was compiled with miscompiles 128 bit shifts by 64 to 127 under some constant folds, and a scan of the respected prestate finds no bad shape on any fall-through path

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: owner, fee controller or chain governance. Networks: Optimism, Unichain, Ink.

What differs on the network: OP Mainnet, Unichain and Ink settle withdrawals on kona-client 1.9.0 compiled by Rust nightly-2026-08-22 for the big-endian mips64 target with cpu mips64 (no R6). LLVM issue 235261 (opened 2026-10-24, label miscompilation) shows that MipsTargetLowering::lowerShiftLeftParts and lowerShiftRightParts use (shamt AND 64) as an ISD::SELECT condition although MIPS booleans are 0 or 1, so a DAG fold such as select(c, 0, 1) into xor(c, 1) returns 65 instead of 0 for 128 bit shifts on MIPS64. The fix, commit 0212d77 of 2026-11-14, is on LLVM main only: the MipsISelLowering.cpp of llvmorg-21.1.8, llvmorg-22.1.0, llvmorg-22.1.8, llvmorg-23.1.0 and the release/22.x and release/23.x branches all still use the bare AND, so every Rust toolchain of 2026 including the kona one carries the fault. ruint 1.20.0 implements U256 wrapping_shl and wrapping_shr on two u128 halves with unbounded_shl and unbounded_shr (bits.rs:463-520 and 604-641 of the crate), so every EVM SHL, SHR and SAR and the masks of SIGNEXTEND go through exactly this lowering. On x86 and arm64 nodes the lowering is not used.

How it meets the code: A shift amount in [64, 127] sets the AND result to 64. Where LLVM folded the select into arithmetic, the MIPS build would return a different word from the node for those amounts. In this bytecode BitMath.mostSignificantBit shifts by the running result r, which reaches 64 to 127 for every input of 64 bits or more, TickBitmap flips a bit with shl(and(tick, 0xff), 1), and the TickMath logarithm shifts by f and by 127 and 128. A changed word there changes the tick, the price or the bitmap slot that a swap writes, so the proof program would compute a different post-state root from the one the op-reth nodes report. The games of type 9 are permissionless, so an ordinary account could post one swap that hits the input and then win the dispute with the program's root against the honest root, delaying withdrawals, or defend a root in which its own withdrawal is larger. Three checks say this does not happen with the present build. First, the revm-interpreter 43.0.1 handlers over ruint 1.20.0 were compiled with rustc 1.97.0 (LLVM 22.1.6, which also lacks the fix) for kona's own mips64-unknown-none target spec, with -mno-check-zero-division and opt-level 3, and run instruction by instruction in an emulator of Cannon's MIPS64Instructions.sol semantics: all 85,544 targeted and 96,876 battery vectors, EXP included, give the specified word, and 3,794 mulDiv and bit scan cases and 2,283 ticks of the price functions match. Second, a planted fault that makes MOVN test bit 0 only, which is the effect the bad fold has, is caught by 624 of 4,278 sampled targeted vectors. Third, the 4,928,204 byte text segment of the respected prestate 0x03e5a548...37c9 itself, rebuilt from the published state file, holds 66 instructions of the form andi rX, rY, 0x40; every later read of rX before it is redefined is the condition of a MOVN or MOVZ (124), a comparison with zero in a branch (10), or one srl by 6 that turns it into 0 or 1. None feeds xori, ori, addu or a store, which is the shape of the miscompile.

What is at risk: None with the respected prestate as built. A future prestate built with a toolchain older than LLVM commit 0212d77 can contain the shape, and would then let an unprivileged account make the proof disagree with the nodes on the price, tick and bitmap arithmetic of every pool on these three chains. The scan follows straight-line code for at most 47 instructions after each site and does not follow branch targets, so a bad shape on a taken branch is not excluded.

What it takes: Governance adopts a new absolute prestate whose code folds the shift-parts select into arithmetic. The present prestate does not.

What can be done: Build future kona prestates with a toolchain that contains LLVM commit 0212d77, or rerun scan_prestate.py of the work directory on each new prestate before it is adopted, and rerun the vector set through the MIPS build.

Second reviewer's corrections: The scanner follows straight-line code for at most 47 instructions and does not follow branch or jump targets, so the prestate check covers uses on the fall-through path only; a use reached through a taken branch would be missed. The use list in the finding omits one scanner entry (dclz at text offset 0x3de7d0), which is a false positive. The value 65 in place of 0 is an extrapolation to MIPS64 of the MIPS32 example in the commit log (0x2100000001 instead of 1). The actor governance is right: adopting a new absolute prestate is a governance act, and with the present prestate there is nothing to exploit.

Code: `src/libraries/BitMath.sol:16-22`, `src/libraries/TickBitmap.sol:73`, `src/libraries/TickMath.sol:142-151`, `src/libraries/TickBitmap.sol:38`.

Sources:

- https://github.com/llvm/llvm-project/issues/235261 (The miscompile: (and shamt, 32 or 64) used as a select condition, folded into xor c,1, affects 128 bit shifts on mips64; fetched with Exa.)
- https://lists.llvm.org/pipermail/llvm-commits/Week-of-Mon-20261109/2098972.html (Fix 0212d77, PR 241685, committed 2026-11-14, adds setcc ne in both lowerings.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/llvm/llvm-project/llvmorg-22.1.8/llvm/lib/Target/Mips/MipsISelLowering.cpp (Release code still uses the bare AND as the select condition; same for llvmorg-21.1.8, 22.1.0, 23.1.0, release/22.x and release/23.x (grep for the fix comment returns 0).)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum-optimism/optimism/kona-client/v1.9.0/rust/justfile (kona-client ELFs are built with RUSTFLAGS -mno-check-zero-division, -Zbuild-std=core,alloc, the nightly of mise.toml and the mips64-unknown-none.json target spec.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum-optimism/optimism/kona-client/v1.9.0/mise.toml (The nightly toolchain is nightly-2026-08-22.)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum-optimism/optimism/kona-client/v1.9.0/rust/kona/docker/cannon/mips64-unknown-none.json (Target cpu mips64, big endian, soft float, n64.)
- https://storage.googleapis.com/oplabs-network-data/proofs/kona/cannon/0x03e5a54835d39c1d83b198314473e1f111c3539c04f65c3e8a9b8ccebe3737c9.bin.gz (The respected prestate state file (sha256 bf144319...3f73); its text segment was scanned by research/network-risk/cycles/013/theme-proof-builds-2.work/kona/scan_prestate.py.)
- https://static.crates.io/crates/ruint/ruint-1.20.0.crate (ruint 1.20.0 source, checksum f5e99bff... equal to the kona-client 1.9.0 lock; shifts on u128 halves in bits.rs.)
- file:research/network-risk/cycles/013/theme-proof-builds-2.work/kona/kona-mips64-vectors.json (0 mismatches on 85,544 targeted and 96,876 battery vectors on the MIPS64 build; planted faults movn_bit0, dsllv5 and dmultu_hi caught.)

## F-128. Nitro's Stylus and JIT compilers use a Cranelift release inside the range of two October 2026 miscompile advisories, and no unprivileged account can reach either fault

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: owner, fee controller or chain governance. Networks: Arbitrum One, Robinhood Chain, Elysium.

What differs on the network: Nitro runs Stylus programs as native code compiled on each node, and its JIT validator runs the WebAssembly replay machine through wasmer. Both compile with cranelift-codegen 0.129.1, which is the wasmtime 42 line. Wasmtime's advisories of October 2026 report that Cranelift before 43 can miscompile functions with more than 2^24 values (CVE-2026-115889, any architecture) and that aarch64 code with a br_table of more than about 256,000 targets jumps to the wrong place (CVE-2026-115893). The public Arbitrum One and Robinhood Chain endpoints run linux-arm64 builds.

How it meets the code: The PoolManager is EVM bytecode and never runs through Cranelift: the interpreter loop of the fork sends only code with the Stylus prefix to ExecuteWASM (core/vm/interpreter.go lines 168-172 at 0e44603). A miscompile could only touch the PoolManager's funds indirectly, through a Stylus program that calls it, or through a validator that computes a different settled state. A Stylus program would need one function with more than 16.7 million SSA values or a branch table with more than about 262,144 targets. The 256 KB limit on a decompressed program rules out the first; it does not rule out the second, since a branch table with one byte targets can come within a few dozen entries of that count. What closes the hazard is that new programs cannot be activated at all: activationGas is 2^64-1 on all three chains. The replay machine that the JIT compiles is fixed by the consensus-v61 root, chosen by governance, and the one-step prover, which interprets WAVM without Cranelift, decides disputes.

What is at risk: None today. If governance reopens activation with a larger size limit, or ships a consensus machine with a function large enough to hit the overflow, nodes compiling with different backends could disagree on a Stylus call's result, which halts or splits the chain without taking any PoolManager funds by itself.

What it takes: Chain governance raising the Stylus size limit or reopening activation, and a crafted program; or a governance-chosen replay machine with an oversized function.

What can be done: Offchain Labs can move wasmer's Cranelift to 0.130 or later (wasmtime 43 and up, or a 48.0.7, 49.0.4 or 50.0.0 line for the aarch64 issue) before Stylus activation reopens. The deployer needs no action.

Second reviewer's corrections: The Robinhood Chain endpoint is load balanced: four web3_clientVersion queries returned linux-arm64 three times and linux-amd64 once, so not all of its nodes are arm64. The statement that the 256 KB limit bounds both faults far below the threshold holds for the 2^24 value overflow but not for the br_table fault: 256 KB is 262,144 bytes, and a br_table whose targets are one byte LEB128 labels can come within a few dozen entries of that count, which is the same order as the about 256,000 target reach limit of a 1 MiB branch with 4 byte table entries. The hazard is closed by activationGas = 2^64-1 and by governance control of MaxWasmSize, not by the size limit alone. The advisories are filed against the wasmtime crate, so the mapping to cranelift-codegen 0.129.1 rests on the wasmtime 42.0.1 dependency listing. The Red Hat CVE page was not fetched by either agent and adds nothing. Actor governance is correct.

Code: `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:134`, `src/types/Currency.sol:73`.

Sources:

- https://crates.io/api/v1/crates/wasmtime/42.0.1/dependencies (wasmtime 42.0.1 depends on cranelift 0.129.1, so cranelift-codegen 0.129.1 belongs to the wasmtime 42 line)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OffchainLabs/nitro/v3.12.4/Cargo.lock (Nitro v3.12.4 locks cranelift-codegen 0.129.1 and wasmer 7.1.0 (the master lock pins the same cranelift))
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OffchainLabs/nitro/v3.12.4/crates/jit/Cargo.toml (the JIT validator links wasmer-compiler-cranelift)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OffchainLabs/nitro/v3.12.4/arbos/programs/native.go (Stylus programs are compiled natively per node with singlepass or Cranelift, with a fallback to the other compiler on failure (lines 365-400) and a Cranelift retry after a native stack overflow (lines 55-60))
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/OffchainLabs/nitro/v3.12.4/arbos/programs/params.go (the decompressed Stylus program size limit is 256 KB from ArbOS 60 (line 43))
- https://thehackernews.com/2026/10/two-critical-wasmtime-flaws-could-let.html (CVE-2026-115889 (24 bit value fields overflow, wrong value references, wasmtime before 36.0.19 and 37 to 42.x) and CVE-2026-115893 (aarch64 br_table with more than about 256,000 targets); secondary source)
- https://access.redhat.com/security/cve/CVE-2026-115889 (CVE record for the Cranelift value index overflow (found by search, its text was not fetched))
- https://rpc.mainnet.chain.robinhood.com (eth_call ArbWasm.activationGas() = 2^64-1 on all three chains, queried 2026-11-24)

## F-131. The LLVM MIPS shift lowering fault does not reach any path of the respected kona prestate, which the control flow scan and a run of its own shift handlers both show

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: owner, fee controller or chain governance. Networks: Optimism, Unichain, Ink.

What differs on the network: OP Mainnet, Unichain and Ink settle withdrawals on permissionless games of type 9 whose program is kona-client 1.9.0 for MIPS64. Its toolchain predates LLVM fix 0212d77 for issue 235261, in which MipsTargetLowering uses (shamt AND 64) as a select condition and a later fold can treat that 0-or-64 value as a 0-or-1 boolean. eth_call on Ethereum (kona/prestate_now.json, Ethereum block 26,488,182) shows all three portals' AnchorStateRegistry return respectedGameType 9, the factories return implementation 0x19af533c (version 0.8.0) whose gameArgs start with absolute prestate 0x03e5a548...37c9, and the newest game of each chain (indexes 21571, 17871 and 2655) returns the same absolutePrestate().

How it meets the code: If a fold had turned the condition into arithmetic, an EVM SHL, SHR or SAR with an amount between 64 and 127 would give the proof program another word than the op-reth nodes. BitMath.mostSignificantBit shifts by amounts in that range, TickBitmap.position and flipTick shift by the low 8 bits of a tick, and TickMath.getTickAtSqrtPrice shifts by 127 and 128, so the post-state root of a swap would differ and an ordinary account could contest an honest root. Two new checks rule this out for the respected prestate. First, kona/cfg_scan.py follows every one of the 66 `andi rX, rY, 0x40` sites of the 4,928,204 byte text segment through fall-through, branch targets, delay slots, j and jal targets and returns, with copies and stack spills tracked, until the value is overwritten on every path (up to 11,034 instructions from one site, nested calls to depth 8, no budget exhausted). The value is read 136 times: 135 as the condition of MOVN or MOVZ or a branch compared with zero, and once by srl 6. No path reads it in xori, ori, addu, daddu, sltu, a multiply, a shift amount or a store. The paths still open end in three ways that read nothing: one return with the value left in $3 by a function that returns through memory, where all 8 jal callers overwrite $3 within 8 instructions (kona/ret_callers.json); 36 indirect calls where the value is a stale $8 to $11 and the caller writes only $4 to $6 for that call (kona/jalr_args.json); and direct calls past depth 8 with no read. Second, kona/run_real.py runs the SHL, SHR and SAR handlers of the prestate image itself, located at 0x12006b1a8, 0x12006b328 and 0x12006ad70 by their stack and spec-byte accesses and identified by their results, in the cycle 13 Cannon-semantics emulator. They return the specified word on all 11,316 targeted, 23,763 battery and 57,792 further vectors (every amount 0 to 300 on 64 values for each opcode). The same harness with a planted MOVN fault that tests bit 0 only (the effect of the bad fold) gives 636 mismatches in 1,132 sampled targeted vectors, and a 5 bit DSLLV mask gives 403.

What is at risk: None with the prestate that all three chains respect today. A later prestate built with a toolchain older than LLVM 0212d77 needs the same two checks, since the fault depends on how the code is folded.

What it takes: Governance adopts a new absolute prestate whose code folds the shift-parts condition into arithmetic. The present prestate does not, on any path.

What can be done: Before a new kona prestate is adopted, run kona/cfg_scan.py and kona/run_real.py of the work directory on its state file, or build it with a toolchain that contains LLVM 0212d77.

This entry adds to F-126.

Second reviewer's corrections: The 36 indirect calls are 36 pairs of site and jalr spread over 13 distinct jalr instructions and 4 sites (jalr_args.json). src/libraries/TickBitmap.sol:38 is the AND that forms bitPos. The shift in position() is sar(8, ...) at line 37, and the shift by the low 8 bits of a tick is at line 73. The depth 8 limit left 142 call paths unfollowed. My rerun to depth 20 found no read on them, but the static scan is still bounded and assumes the n64 calling convention for jalr callees. The shift handlers were found by pattern and identified by their results, and the dispatch table entry that selects them was not read. The static scan of all 66 sites covers any other handler. Actor governance is correct: an ordinary account can drive the permissionless type 9 game, but the fault only reaches it if governance adopts a new prestate whose build folds the condition into arithmetic.

Code: `src/libraries/BitMath.sol:16-22`, `src/libraries/TickBitmap.sol:38`, `src/libraries/TickBitmap.sol:73`, `src/libraries/TickMath.sol:142-151`.

Sources:

- https://storage.googleapis.com/oplabs-network-data/proofs/kona/cannon/0x03e5a54835d39c1d83b198314473e1f111c3539c04f65c3e8a9b8ccebe3737c9.bin.gz (Respected prestate state file, sha256 bf144319...3f73, version 8, 1,244 pages, text segment 0x120000000 of 4,928,204 bytes; scanned and executed by research/network-risk/cycles/014/theme-proof-images.work/kona/cfg_scan.py and run_real.py.)
- rpc eth_call https://ethereum-rpc.publicnode.com, results in research/network-risk/cycles/014/theme-proof-images.work/kona/prestate_now.json (eth_call on Ethereum: respectedGameType 9 and absolute prestate 0x03e5a548...37c9 in gameArgs and in the newest game for chains 10, 130 and 57073.)
- file:research/network-risk/cycles/014/theme-proof-images.work/kona/cfg_scan.json (66 sites, 136 reads (135 zero tests, 1 srl 6), no arithmetic, store or shift-amount read on any path.)
- file:research/network-risk/cycles/014/theme-proof-images.work/kona/real_shift.json (Prestate SHL, SHR and SAR handlers match on 92,871 vectors; real_shift_movn_bit0.json and real_shift_dsllv5.json show planted faults are detected.)
- https://github.com/llvm/llvm-project/issues/235261 (The MIPS shift-parts miscompile (recorded in cycle 13, F-126).)
- src/libraries/BitMath.sol:16-22 (Shifts by running amounts that reach 64 to 127.)

## F-145. Nitro v3.13.0 carries dormant Amsterdam execution rules that a future ArbOS version would switch on, and the PoolManager bytecode has no instruction they reinterpret

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: owner, fee controller or chain governance. Networks: Arbitrum One, Robinhood Chain.

What differs on the network: Ethereum has not scheduled Glamsterdam. The Nitro build that the Arbitrum One and Robinhood Chain endpoints run (commit b72783f, published as the v3.13.0 binary-only image on 25 November 2026) already contains a go-ethereum v1.17.x based interpreter with an Amsterdam instruction set (newAmsterdamInstructionSet and newArbitrumAmsterdamInstructionSet in its function table), the EIP-8037 two-part gas budget (GasBudget with ChargeState, ChargeExecution and Forward; Contract.forwardGas and refundState), handlers for the EIP-8024 opcodes (opDupN, opSwapN, opExchange) and EIP-7843 (opSlotNum). These rules switch on only with an ArbOS version that the chain owner schedules; at ArbOS 61 the endpoints run the Osaka set and no upgrade is scheduled on either chain.

How it meets the code: Two parts of the PoolManager meet these rules. First, the bytecode: walked instruction by instruction, the 24,009 byte PoolManager on Arbitrum One has no byte 0x1e, 0x4b, 0xe6, 0xe7 or 0xe8 at an instruction position and no other opcode that is undefined today; the only 0xfe is the trailing invalid at offset 23,996. So no branch of the PoolManager would decode differently when DUPN, SWAPN, EXCHANGE or SLOTNUM become valid, and the compiled jump destination analysis of the binary treats only PUSH1 to PUSH32 as carrying immediates, as in go-ethereum v1.17.7 analysis_legacy.go. Second, gas: under EIP-8037 the GAS opcode returns only the execution part of the budget, and state creation is paid from a separate reservoir. The PoolManager forwards gas() on three calls: the native payout (src/types/Currency.sol:48), the token transfer (src/types/Currency.sol:73) and the hook call (src/libraries/Hooks.sol:134). In go-ethereum v1.17.7 opCall hands the callee its execution gas plus the caller's whole state reservoir (NewGasBudget(gas, scope.Contract.Gas.StateGas)), so a callee that creates state may draw on a reservoir the caller sized for itself. The outcome of a call is still decided only by success and return data (Currency.sol:48-52 and 73-79, Hooks.sol:134-160), and the PoolManager's own writes are storage updates of existing pools, positions and claims, so the rule can change which transactions run out of gas, not what a successful call computes or who is debited.

What is at risk: No funds move. Once an ArbOS version enables these rules, a transaction that creates many new storage slots (first position in a pool, first ERC-6909 claim balance, first tick of a range) needs more total gas; that is a pricing change of the A9 kind, recorded for the deployment checklist.

What it takes: The chain owner (ArbitrumDAO for Arbitrum One, the two chain owners for Robinhood Chain) must schedule an ArbOS version that activates the Amsterdam rules. No such version is proposed: the v3.13.0 image ships no consensus machine newer than consensus-v61, and getScheduledUpgrade() returns zeros on both chains on 26 November 2026.

What can be done: Before any ArbOS upgrade that names Glamsterdam features, rerun battery.py, targeted.py, pmdiff.py and pmdiff2.py against the upgraded Sepolia or a block time override, and rerun the gas-capped scripts (pmdiff2 script 189) to see which liquidity operations change gas class.

Second reviewer's corrections: The Go build info in the work directory records the go-ethereum module as v1.16.2 replaced by ./go-ethereum, and the function table paths read go-ethereum@v1.16.2; the v1.17.x identification rests on the functions present and their line offsets, not on a version string. The finding states this indirectly; it should say so plainly.

Code: `src/types/Currency.sol:44-52`, `src/types/Currency.sol:60-80`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:131-160`, `src/PoolManager.sol:291-297`.

Sources:

- https://hub.docker.com/v2/repositories/offchainlabs/nitro-node/tags?page_size=30&ordering=last_updated (The v3.13.0-b72783f-stripped image was published on 2026-11-25 for amd64 and arm64, the same commit as the rc.9 build the endpoints report)
- https://registry-1.docker.io/v2/offchainlabs/nitro-node/manifests/sha256:af5059f239fe78ee45db64f6c10211aa67cc3ff7e74e75e36deaa4231b0c4638 (Manifest of the amd64 image whose nitro binary was read (Go build info, pclntab function and file table))
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum/go-ethereum/v1.17.7/core/vm/instructions.go (opCall forwards execution gas plus the caller's whole state reservoir; arithmetic handlers use pop1Peek1)
- https://raw.githubusercontent.com/ethereum/go-ethereum/v1.17.7/core/vm/contract.go (GasBudget accounting, forwardGas, refundState and the jump destination cache keyed by code hash)
- https://coinalerts.org/news/articles/719905/ (Offchain Labs announced v3.13.0 as a binary-only release with source to follow)
- https://forum.arbitrum.foundation/t/constitutional-aip-update-arbitrum-one-and-nova-l1-pricing-parameters-for-ethereum-glamsterdam/31555 (The Glamsterdam change for Arbitrum One is a parameter update that introduces no ArbOS upgrade)
- https://arb1.arbitrum.io/rpc eth_call 0x6b getScheduledUpgrade() and 0x64 arbOSVersion() at block 527,629,490 (No ArbOS upgrade scheduled; ArbOS 61 (0x74 = 116))
- https://arb1.arbitrum.io/rpc eth_getCode 0x360e68faccca8ca495c1b759fd9eee466db9fb32 (PoolManager bytecode walked for opcodes at instruction positions)

## F-158. Elysium's announced ArbOS upgrade adds a HyperCore read precompile and a callback predeploy whose addresses and call shapes are not yet published

Info severity, confirmed. Effect: degrade. Who can trigger it: owner, fee controller or chain governance. Networks: Elysium.

What differs on the network: Kinetiq states that an ArbOS upgrade about four weeks after mainnet adds a native precompile that returns live HyperCore state, balances included, and an ElysiumCoreWriter predeploy that keeps per-contract HYPE escrows and calls contracts back with the result of each HyperCore action. Neither exists on Ethereum, and neither is in the 36 precompiles the node lists today.

How it meets the code: The PoolManager reaches a new address in four shapes only. sync(X) staticcalls X.balanceOf(PoolManager) and settle credits the rise of that word as currency X (src/PoolManager.sol:279-288 and 349-364). take(X, to, amount) calls X.transfer(to, amount) and accepts empty return data or a 32 byte one (src/types/Currency.sol:53-80). take(address(0), X, amount) sends value with empty calldata (src/types/Currency.sol:44-52). A hook address with valid low flag bits receives the IHooks selectors (src/libraries/Hooks.sol:109-124 and 131-160). If the read precompile answered the balanceOf selector with a word that moves, an account could book that movement as a delta of currency X, but X is the precompile address, which no pool of real tokens names, so the booked credit is worth nothing to other users. A value call into the predeploy that it treats as an escrow deposit is paid by the caller of take, who is debited the full amount. A result callback goes to the contract that emitted the intent; the PoolManager cannot emit one, because none of its four call shapes carries caller-chosen calldata to an address other than the hook. The hazard therefore rests on the precompile or predeploy answering one of these selectors in a way the published text does not describe.

What is at risk: None found today. Recorded so that the day-one check of the upgrade calls both new addresses from the PoolManager address in the four shapes above.

What it takes: Elysium governance must ship the upgrade; nothing is scheduled at block 11,231,491.

What can be done: After activation, run the cycle 16 precompile sweep against the new addresses from the PoolManager address, and check whether either address has low 14 bits that form a valid hook flag set.

Code: `src/PoolManager.sol:279-297`, `src/PoolManager.sol:349-364`, `src/types/Currency.sol:44-80`, `src/libraries/Hooks.sol:109-160`.

Sources:

- https://elysium.kinetiq.xyz/docs/trading-on-hypercore-from-elysium (ElysiumCoreWriter predeploy, escrow and callbacks ship in an ArbOS upgrade about four weeks after mainnet)
- https://elysium.kinetiq.xyz/docs/what-is-elysium (HyperCore data precompile as a post-launch upgrade; stock ArbOS 61 today)
- https://rpc.elysium.kinetiq.xyz (getScheduledUpgrade() returns zeros at block 11,231,491 (raw/arbos_state.txt))
